# Startup validation tests: deep research reference

Agent-ingestible reference. 39 tests researched, each with an adversarial verification pass over its numeric claims (verdicts: confirmed / contested / refuted). Every record uses the same schema.

Verdict tally across all claims: confirmed 286, contested 179, refuted 11.

---
### 1. Customer discovery interviews (The Mom Test)

- **Category:** Problem & demand discovery
- **Stage:** problem
- **Definition:** Customer discovery interviews are structured, problem-focused conversations with target users designed to uncover whether a painful, frequent problem exists before any solution is built. The canonical method, codified by Rob Fitzpatrick in The Mom Test (2013) and rooted in Steve Blank's Customer Development framework (2003), requires asking only about past behavior and lived experience -- never about hypothetical future intent -- so that social desirability bias cannot contaminate the signal. A conversation is valid only if it yields behavioral facts or concrete commitments (time, reputation, or money); compliments and opinions are treated as noise.
- **Validates:** Does a specific, named customer segment experience a problem that is painful enough and frequent enough that they have already spent time, money, or effort trying to solve it? (Problem-stage gate; precedes any solution or demand test.)

**Protocol**
1. Segment sharply before recruiting. Define a narrow, behaviorally specific customer profile (e.g., not 'small businesses' but 'US law firms specializing in IP with 5-25 lawyers'). If the segment is too broad, 5 interviews will surface zero-pattern noise. Write down your top three falsifiable hypotheses.
2. Recruit 5-10 initial subjects from the target segment. Use warm intros, LinkedIn, community forums, or direct outreach. Two-person teams work best: one leads questions, one takes verbatim notes on paper (laptops read as rude and distract).
3. Frame the meeting before it starts (Fitzpatrick's 5-component opener): state your vision as a problem you are studying (not a product you are selling), disclose your stage, name a specific knowledge gap you need help with, explain why this person is uniquely qualified, and explicitly ask for their help.
4. Ask only about the past and the specific, never about the future or the hypothetical. Core question arc: 'Walk me through the last time you dealt with X. What did you do? How much did that cost you / how long did it take? What have you tried? Why did that not work?' Fitzpatrick's three rules: talk about their life not your idea; ask about specifics in the past not generics or opinions about the future; listen more than you talk.
5. Listen for three validity signals and three noise signals. Valid: emotionally charged stories, evidence of prior spend or workaround, unsolicited referrals. Noise: compliments ('that sounds great'), future-tense hypotheticals ('I would probably use that'), generic claims ('I always struggle with X').
6. Push for commitment or advancement at the end of every meeting. Ask for something the respondent values: a follow-up meeting with a specific agenda, an introduction to their VP, a signed letter of intent, a deposit, or a pilot agreement. A meeting that ends only with compliments has failed. Fitzpatrick: 'If you don't know what happens next after a meeting, the meeting was pointless.'
7. Debrief within 24 hours as a team. Share verbatim quotes, flag which hypotheses moved, and identify which questions to sharpen. After 5 interviews check for pattern: if you cannot predict what the 6th interviewee will say, your segment is too heterogeneous -- narrow it before continuing.
8. Run until thematic saturation: the point at which you can predict the next interview's answers. Griffin and Hauser (1993) provide the empirical anchor: 20-30 interviews within a well-defined segment uncover 90-95% of that segment's distinct needs. If you have not reached saturation by 20, either the segment definition is too broad or the problem is too weak to generate consistent signal.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Thematic saturation -- share of distinct customer needs uncovered: 20-30 interviews within a well-defined segment uncover 90-95% of all customer needs; 25 interviews alone captures ~90% (src: Griffin, A. & Hauser, J.R. (1993). The Voice of the Customer. Marketing Science, 12(1), 1-27. Also cited in Ulrich & Eppinger (2012), p. 78.)
- Minimum for reliable early-stage problem validation: 15 interviews minimum; patterns should emerge by interview 5 and be fully formed by interview 20 (src: Lean Analytics (Croll & Yoskovitz, 2013) and Customer Development Labs (Justin Wilcox, 2014, customerdevlabs.com))
- Full discovery + validation phase (Steve Blank recommendation): 50-100 customer conversations across discovery and validation phases combined (src: Blank, S. (2003/2013). The Four Steps to the Epiphany. K&S Ranch.)
- Pattern-recognition threshold per Customer Development Labs: 5 interviews: patterns begin repeating. By 20 interviews, 'you'll have learned everything about your customers.' If no pattern after 5, the segment is too broad. (src: Wilcox, J. (2014). 'How Many Customer Discovery Problem Interviews Should I Do?' customerdevlabs.com)
- Startup failure rate attributable to no market need: 42% of startups fail because they built something nobody wanted (src: CB Insights post-mortem analysis of 101 startup failures (widely cited; original CB Insights report))
- Time per interview: 15 minutes (consumer products) to 2 hours (enterprise B2B) (src: Growthramp.io / practitioner consensus; no single primary study)
- Product launch failure rate: 6 out of 10 new product launches fail to meet revenue and adoption expectations (src: Cited across multiple practitioner sources; trace to Nielsen / industry surveys -- treat as approximate industry figure, not a controlled study)

**Origin and evidence:** The method synthesizes two independent lineages. Steve Blank originated Customer Development in The Four Steps to the Epiphany (2003), drawing on his experience across 8 startups and coining the 'get out of the building' imperative. Rob Fitzpatrick operationalized the interview conversation layer in The Mom Test (2013), adding the specific rules about past-tense questions, compliment-filtering, and commitment-as-signal; the book is the de facto field manual. Cindy Alvarez extended it to iterative product teams in Lean Customer Development (O'Reilly, 2014). The 20-30 interview saturation number has an independent academic anchor in Griffin & Hauser (1993), a peer-reviewed Marketing Science paper based on empirical field research on portable food-carrying devices -- the study's domain is product development VoC, not startup discovery, but it is the only controlled study cited for this threshold. YC has embedded the method in Startup School and its application criteria. Evidence strength: the qualitative protocol is high-confidence (convergent across independent practitioners); specific saturation numbers are moderate-confidence (Griffin & Hauser's finding is real but domain-limited; startup-specific replication studies do not exist in peer-reviewed form).

**Canonical sources:** Fitzpatrick, Rob. The Mom Test: How to Talk to Customers and Learn If Your Business Is a Good Idea When Everyone Is Lying to You. Robfitz Ltd, 2013.; Blank, Steve. The Four Steps to the Epiphany. K&S Ranch, 2003 (revised 2013).; Alvarez, Cindy. Lean Customer Development: Building Products Your Customers Will Buy. O'Reilly, 2014.; Griffin, A. & Hauser, J.R. 'The Voice of the Customer.' Marketing Science 12(1), 1993, pp. 1-27.; Y Combinator. 'How to Talk to Users.' YC Startup Library. ycombinator.com/library/Iq-how-to-talk-to-users; Graham, Paul. 'Do Things That Don't Scale.' paulgraham.com/ds.html, July 2013.

**Failure modes**
- Pitching instead of discovering: founders introduce their idea early, priming the respondent to evaluate a solution rather than surface problems. YC explicitly warns against mentioning the idea until the end of the call, or not at all.
- Future-tense questions: asking 'Would you use this?' or 'Would you pay for this?' generates hypothetical intent, not behavioral evidence. Respondents systematically overstate future willingness. The Mom Test's core rule is: only past tense, only specific.
- Underdefined segment: interviewing across heterogeneous customer types (e.g., 'small businesses') produces contradictory signal. No pattern emerges because different problems are being sampled simultaneously. Fix: narrow segment definition before recruiting.
- Zombie leads: prospects who give warm verbal feedback across multiple follow-ups but never commit time, reputation, or money. They consume founder time while providing false demand signal. Fitzpatrick coined the term explicitly.
- Founder-led confirmation bias: the interviewer (usually the founder) unconsciously emphasizes agreeing statements in notetaking and memory. Mitigation: two-person teams with a dedicated notetaker; verbatim quotes only.
- Compliment harvesting: ending meetings that generated only compliments and logging them as 'positive discovery.' Fitzpatrick: 'Compliments are the fool's gold of customer learning.'
- Single-channel recruiting: drawing all interview subjects from the founder's personal network skews toward supporters who already believe in the idea, inflating positive signal.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false positive mechanism is politeness bias combined with hypothetical-intent questions. When a founder asks 'Do you think this is a good idea?' or 'Would you pay for this?', respondents incur near-zero social cost from saying yes -- it makes the founder feel good and ends an awkward interaction. The respondent is not lying maliciously; they are predicting their own future behavior, which humans do poorly. The result is a portfolio of enthusiastic verbal endorsements with zero behavioral backing. Fitzpatrick's key diagnostic: if the conversation produced only opinions and no commitment of time, reputation, or money, the signal is false regardless of how positive it felt. The method is named after the fact that even a mother -- the most supportive possible respondent -- will give a false positive if asked the wrong question ('Do you think my app is a good idea?') and an honest answer if asked the right one ('When did you last have trouble with X and what did you do?').

**Examples**
- Airbnb: Founders Brian Chesky and Joe Gebbia went door-to-door in New York in 2009 to meet hosts in person rather than conduct remote surveys. They observed hosts struggling to create listings and identified that photo quality was the primary conversion bottleneck -- an insight they extracted by watching behavior, not by asking opinions. They personally photographed approximately 40 apartments; New York revenue roughly doubled within a month. They also stayed at hosts' homes to experience their own product, generating behavioral insight no survey could surface.
- Stripe: Patrick and John Collison invented what Y Combinator now calls the 'Collison installation' (documented in Paul Graham's 'Do Things That Don't Scale,' 2013): when any potential user agreed to try Stripe during early YC days, the brothers did not send a link -- they said 'Right then, give me your laptop' and set Stripe up on the spot. This transformed a passive verbal commitment into an immediate behavioral commitment, eliminating the gap between stated intent and actual use, and generating real usage data from the earliest cohort.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is a pattern of behavioral evidence -- not opinions -- appearing consistently across 15-20 interviews within a tightly defined segment: multiple respondents independently describe the same problem unprompted, can recall a specific recent episode with emotional detail, have already spent time or money on workaround solutions, and at least a subset makes a concrete commitment (pilot agreement, letter of intent, deposit, or an introduction to their decision-maker) at the end of the session. Fitzpatrick's predictive test: 'You bet $100 you know what the next interview will say -- and you win.' When combined, these signals indicate a real, recurring, monetizable pain rather than latent or manufactured demand.
- Weak signal: A weak result is one where respondents express enthusiasm and agreement but cannot recall a specific recent episode of the problem, have taken no prior action to solve it, describe the problem only in future or conditional terms ('that would be annoying'), and offer no commitment at session's end beyond 'keep me posted.' Divergent answers across 5+ interviews within a supposed single segment indicate either a poorly defined segment or a weak/inconsistent problem. Weak results are often mistaken for strong ones precisely because the social texture of the conversation feels positive. The correct diagnostic is not how the meeting felt, but what behavioral facts and commitments it produced.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] 20-30 interviews within a well-defined segment uncover 90-95% of all distinct customer needs (Griffin & Hauser, Marketing Science 1993) -- Multiple independent academic and educational sources corroborate this as the central saturation finding from Griffin & Hauser (1993). The paper studied 30 respondents on portable food-carrying devices; secondary literature (MIT course materials, NN/g, qualitative methodology reviews) consistently reports the 20-30 interview / 90-95% figure as the paper's modeled estimate. The specific product category limits direct generalizability, but the finding itself is real and consistently attributed. (src: Griffin, A. & Hauser, J.R. (1993), Marketing Science 12(1):1-27; corroborated at https://www.nngroup.com/articles/interview-sample-size/ and multiple MIT OCW course materials)
- [contested] 25 interviews alone capture approximately 90% of customer needs (Griffin & Hauser 1993, as cited in Ulrich & Eppinger 2012 p.78) -- The 'Ulrich & Eppinger p.78 cites Griffin & Hauser for 25 interviews / 90%' chain is independently confirmed by multiple course-note sources (Tufts engineering handbook, UCSD startup notes). However, the primary Griffin & Hauser paper reports a range (20-30 interviews for 90-95%), not a single '25 interviews for 90%' point estimate. The '25 / 90%' formulation is a secondary-source simplification that differs in precision from the original. Claiming Griffin & Hauser directly said '25 interviews = ~90%' overstates the primary paper's resolution; the Ulrich & Eppinger citation adds one rounding step. (src: Ulrich & Eppinger (2012) p.78 citation chain confirmed at https://sites.tufts.edu/eeseniordesignhandbook/2013/customer-needs-identification-2/ ; primary paper range per https://www.nngroup.com/articles/interview-sample-size/)
- [confirmed] Patterns should begin repeating by interview 5; all major learning should be complete by interview 20 (Customer Development Labs, Wilcox 2014) -- The Wilcox 2014 CustomerDevLabs post exists and is accessible. It states patterns should emerge by interview 5, and that full learning 'takes 15-20' interviews. The claim's phrasing ('all major learning should be complete by interview 20') is a fair paraphrase of the post. Caveat: Wilcox presents this as practitioner rule-of-thumb derived from personal experience, not an empirical study. The post explicitly conditions this on a well-defined segment. (src: https://customerdevlabs.com/2014/07/01/how-many-customer-discovery-problem-interviews-should-i-do/)
- [contested] Steve Blank recommends 50-100 customer conversations across discovery and validation phases combined (Four Steps to the Epiphany) -- No direct Blank quote specifying '50-100 combined across both phases' was found in the book or his blog. What the book does recommend is building 'a list of 50 names' for the customer discovery phase alone. His university programs assign 100 interviews over a full semester. The '50-100 combined' figure appears to be an aggregation by secondary commentators, not a single authoritative Blank recommendation. The book's guidance is more process-oriented and iterative than a fixed number. (src: https://learn.marsdd.com/article/blanks-customer-discovery-method-part-2-the-customer-development-model-in-value-proposition/ ; https://medium.com/@businessbookworm/the-summary-of-the-four-steps-to-the-epiphany-by-steve-blank-75faf78948be)
- [confirmed] Lean Analytics recommends a minimum of 15 interviews for early-stage problem validation -- Multiple independent Lean Analytics summaries and the book's own framework consistently state 'at least 15 prospective customers' at each stage of the Empathy phase. The '15 interviews minimum' is a direct Croll & Yoskovitz recommendation, not a practitioner extrapolation. (src: Croll & Yoskovitz, Lean Analytics (O'Reilly, 2013); confirmed at https://www.alexjhughes.com/books/2019/8/11/lean-analytics-alistair-croll-benjamin-yoskovitz)
- [confirmed] 42% of startups fail because they built something nobody wanted (CB Insights analysis of 101 startup failures) -- The CB Insights report exists, originally published around 2014, based on roughly 101 startup post-mortems. The 42% 'no market need' finding is consistently reported and was replicated in a 2024 update (431 startups, 43% figure). Key methodological caveat not in the original claim: the data is self-reported founder post-mortems, not independent audits, which creates selection and attribution bias. The number is real but the methodology is weak. (src: https://preuve.ai/blog/why-startups-fail-market-fit ; https://www.inc.com/erik-sherman/42-percent-of-startups-that-fail-trip-over-this-one-critical-avoidable-point.html)
- [contested] 6 out of 10 new product launches fail to meet revenue and adoption expectations (industry survey figure, approximate) -- The most traceable NielsenIQ '60%' figure applies specifically to product *relaunches* not recovering from downward trends (NielsenIQ BASES, February 2022), not new product launches. For new product launches, Nielsen's reported failure rates are substantially higher: 76-85% in CPG/FMCG. If the claim's source is the NielsenIQ relaunch figure, it misapplies the statistic. A '~60%' figure for new launches cannot be independently traced to a named primary source; the range cited across credible sources for new products runs from 50% to 85%. (src: https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20220209005150/en/NielsenIQ-60-of-Product-Relaunches-Dont-Pay-Off ; https://www.bevindustry.com/articles/87718-nielsen-85-percent-of-new-products-fail-in-the-marketplace)
- [contested] Airbnb personal photography of approximately 40 apartments correlated with roughly doubling of New York revenue within one month (reported by founders) -- The core story is real and well-documented: Airbnb founders flew to New York, photographed listings personally, and revenue doubled. However, the specific details in the claim are inconsistently reported. The number of apartments photographed: one sourced account (strategybreakdowns.com) says 24 hosts, while '40' appears to be the number of listings *examined*, not photographed. The timeframe: the First Round Review article and several sources say 'a week later,' while GrowthHackers says 'within one month.' The claim's combination of '~40 apartments' and 'within one month' is not consistently supported by primary founder accounts. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-design-thinking-transformed-airbnb-from-failing-startup-to-billion-dollar-business/ ; https://growthhackers.com/growth-studies/airbnb/ ; https://strategybreakdowns.com/p/airbnb-photography)

**Citations**
- Fitzpatrick, R. The Mom Test (book page, Amazon): https://www.amazon.com/Mom-Test-customers-business-everyone/dp/1492180742
- mtlynch.io -- detailed book report on The Mom Test (primary rules and framework): https://mtlynch.io/book-reports/the-mom-test/
- Y Combinator Startup Library -- How to Talk to Users: https://www.ycombinator.com/library/Iq-how-to-talk-to-users
- Customer Development Labs -- How Many Customer Discovery Interviews Should I Do? (Wilcox, 2014): https://customerdevlabs.com/2014/07/01/how-many-customer-discovery-problem-interviews-should-i-do/
- Griffin & Hauser 1993 -- The Voice of the Customer (MIT Sloan PDF): https://mitsloan.mit.edu/shared/ods/documents?PublicationDocumentID=5259
- Paul Graham -- Do Things That Don't Scale (Collison installation / Airbnb): https://educapital.com.br/wp-content/uploads/2022/06/Do-Things-that-Dont-Scale-1.pdf
- The Mom Test Chapter 5 -- Commitment and Advancement (DEV Community summary): https://dev.to/egepakten/the-mom-test-chapter-5-commitment-and-advancement-1k27
- Lean Customer Development summary -- Cindy Alvarez protocol: https://summaries.com/blog/lean-customer-development
- NN/g -- How Many Participants for a UX Interview (saturation literature review): https://www.nngroup.com/articles/interview-sample-size/
- Customer Discovery Interviews -- Ultimate Guide (Cardboard IT, includes Airbnb case): https://cardboardit.com/2023/08/the-ultimate-guide-to-customer-discovery-lessons-from-airbnb-steve-blank-and-the-mom-test/

### 2. Day-in-the-life / Contextual Inquiry / Job Shadowing

- **Category:** Problem & demand discovery
- **Stage:** problem
- **Definition:** Contextual inquiry is a field study method in which a researcher observes and interviews users inside their natural work environment while they perform real tasks, not in a lab or via recall. Developed by Hugh Beyer and Karen Holtzblatt (first published 1988, fully described 1990), it uses a master-apprentice model: the researcher acts as an apprentice watching the expert user work, asking clarifying questions in the moment. The method surfaces tacit, unarticulated behaviors and workarounds that users cannot reconstruct accurately in retrospect -- making it uniquely suited for discovering latent problems before a solution is designed.
- **Validates:** Does the target customer have a real, recurring, high-friction problem in their actual workflow -- one they are currently solving with workarounds -- and is that problem worth solving at all?

**Protocol**
1. Recruit 4-10 participants who currently do the work you are studying -- not aspirational users. For a tightly scoped problem, 4 participants can suffice; for broader discovery, target 8-12 for thematic saturation.
2. Gain access to the natural context: the participant's office, workstation, or physical job site. For enterprise/B2B targets, ask them in advance to save the relevant tasks for when you arrive so you can observe them live, not in simulation.
3. Begin with a 10-20 minute tour and orientation: survey the workspace, tools, artifacts, and environment. Ask 'Walk me through what a normal day looks like' before any task-level questions.
4. Shift into observation mode (the 'contextual interview' body): watch the participant perform real work without steering it. Take handwritten notes on what you see -- actions, hesitations, workarounds, physical artifacts used. Do not describe your product or propose solutions.
5. Use the master-apprentice interrupt technique: when something unexpected happens, pause and ask 'Can you tell me what just happened there?' or 'Why do you do it that way?' Validate your interpretation on the spot ('So it sounds like you do X because Y -- is that right?').
6. Go in pairs when possible: one person watches and takes notes, the other manages rapport and asks follow-up questions. Ensure product, engineering, and design representatives attend so interpretation is cross-functional.
7. Conduct a team debrief within 24 hours of each session (30 minutes): identify surprising observations, quote direct speech, and map the steps the user took. Resist generating solutions during debrief.
8. After all sessions, consolidate into a pattern map: identify recurring pain points, workarounds, and jobs-to-be-done. The signal is behavioral repetition across participants, not individual sentiment.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Minimum participants for single, tightly-scoped problem: 4 users (src: Wikipedia Contextual Inquiry article, citing Holtzblatt & Beyer 1998)
- Recommended participant range for broader contextual design study: 4 to 30+ users depending on scope (src: Wikipedia Contextual Inquiry article, citing Holtzblatt & Beyer 1998)
- Typical session duration per participant: Approximately 2 hours per one-on-one session; can range from 1 hour to several days for longitudinal work (src: Nielsen Norman Group contextual inquiry article (nngroup.com); Wikipedia Contextual Inquiry)
- Intuit 'Follow Me Home' annual investment: 10,000 hours of follow-me-home sessions per year conducted by Intuit across employees (src: MyParticipants Medium article citing Intuit's stated program scale)
- Christensen milkshake study -- morning commuter share of daily milkshake sales: Nearly half (~40-50%) of all milkshakes were sold to solo morning commuters, a pattern invisible in survey data (src: Multiple JTBD sources synthesizing Christensen's original study, including therewiredgroup.com and fullstory.com; exact figure is described as 'nearly half' or 'half' across retellings)
- NNG 5-user usability test problem detection rate: 5 users detect approximately 85% of usability problems on average (range 55-100%); 10 users detect 95% on average -- but this benchmark applies to usability testing, not contextual inquiry (src: Nielsen Norman Group via uxpsychology.substack.com synthesizing Virzi 1992 and Nielsen 1993)

**Origin and evidence:** Hugh Beyer and Karen Holtzblatt originated the formal methodology with the first published reference in 1988 (Whiteside, Bennett, Holtzblatt) and the canonical description in 1990 (Wixon, Holtzblatt, Knox), with the book-length treatment in 1998 ('Contextual Design: Defining Customer-Centered Systems'). IDEO popularized the ethnographic 'deep dive' variant for product design through their widely publicized shopping cart project (ABC Nightline, 1999) and design thinking workshops. Clayton Christensen's milkshake study (Jobs to Be Done framework, 1990s-2000s) is the most-cited empirical demonstration of what observational field research reveals that surveys cannot. Rob Fitzpatrick's 'The Mom Test' (2013) operationalized the behavioral observation principle ('ask them to walk you through the last time that happened') for non-researcher founders, and is taught at Harvard and MIT and used by Shopify and Skyscanner. Evidence quality: the method is well-established in HCI literature with peer-reviewed origins; the startup-facing guidance is practitioner-sourced, not randomized controlled. No RCTs exist comparing contextual inquiry outcomes to other discovery methods.

**Canonical sources:** Beyer, H. & Holtzblatt, K. (1998). Contextual Design: Defining Customer-Centered Systems. Academic Press. -- the founding text.; Fitzpatrick, Rob (2013). The Mom Test. -- operationalizes behavioral observation for founders; principle: 'Talk me through the last time that happened' surfaces actions not opinions.; Holtzblatt, K. & Beyer, H. (2017). Contextual Design: Design for Life. Morgan Kaufmann. -- updated canonical reference.; Nielsen Norman Group -- contextual inquiry definition and four-principle framework (Context, Partnership, Interpretation, Focus).; Clayton Christensen -- Jobs to Be Done framework; the milkshake study is the canonical demonstration that observation reveals the actual 'job' surveys miss.; IDEO -- 'deep dive' ethnographic methodology, popularized in ABC Nightline 1999 shopping cart project.; Intuit -- 'Follow Me Home' program, Scott Cook; the named corporate implementation running at 10,000 hours per year.

**Failure modes**
- Participants revert to 'interview mode': they stop working naturally and begin explaining or demonstrating for you, producing curated behavior rather than real behavior. The observation becomes a performance.
- Researcher steers the session toward confirming the product idea: leading questions ('When you do X, don't you find it frustrating that Y?') introduce demand characteristics.
- Researcher only observes the surface task and misses the upstream and downstream context: the workaround that reveals the real problem often happens before or after the focal task.
- Sessions devolve into complaint sessions about existing tools without surfacing what the user actually does to cope -- frustration is documented, behavior is not.
- The researcher recruits the wrong participant: 'power users' who have already optimized around the problem, or managers who describe their team's work but don't do it themselves.
- Small sample creates an idiosyncratic picture: a single participant's quirky workflow gets pattern-matched into a product requirement because the team fell in love with the observation.
- Recall contamination: if the researcher cannot observe a live task and asks the participant to reconstruct it, they get a rationalized narrative, not the actual sequence -- exactly the failure mode Fitzpatrick identifies as the primary risk of pure interview methods.

**False-positive mechanism:** The core false positive is confusing observed friction with validated demand. A researcher watches a user perform a painful workaround and concludes the user will pay to eliminate it -- but the user has optimized around the friction to the point of tolerance, it is not high enough priority to buy a solution for, or the workaround itself is load-bearing (it produces a side output the user values). The other false positive mechanism is sample bias: if all observed users are early adopters, hobbyists, or unusually sophisticated operators, the observed pain is real for them but absent in the mainstream segment. Contextual inquiry proves a problem exists in the observed population; it does not prove the problem is widespread enough, severe enough, or addressable at a price the user would pay -- those require complementary demand tests (willingness-to-pay probes, concierge tests, or pre-sales).

**Examples**
- Intuit: Scott Cook, Intuit's founder, initiated the 'Follow Me Home' program at product launch: he waited at retail stores for customers to buy Quicken or QuickBooks, then asked to accompany them home and watch them install and use the software. Observers documented exactly when users were confused, when they wanted to call for help, and what workarounds they invented. This revealed that users needed a point-of-sale solution, which led directly to QuickBooks POS. The program scaled to 10,000 hours of in-context observation per year company-wide, and new employees participate as a standard onboarding step.
- McDonald's (Christensen milkshake study): Clayton Christensen's team was engaged to improve milkshake sales after conventional demographic surveys and flavor changes failed. Field observation revealed that nearly half of all milkshakes were purchased before 9am by solo commuters who consumed them during long drives to work. No commuter described 'alleviating boredom on a commute' as a need when surveyed -- they described taste preferences. The observation surfaced a job-to-be-done (one-handed, slow-to-finish breakfast that occupies time) that was structurally invisible in survey data, and led to product and packaging recommendations the demographic analysis could not have generated.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: Across 5+ independent participants from the same segment, you observe: (1) the same workaround appearing with no prompting -- participants independently invented the same coping behavior; (2) the workaround consumes meaningful time or causes visible error that the user notices and acknowledges as a problem; (3) participants cannot clearly articulate why they do it this way, indicating a tacit, non-obvious behavior that surveys would never surface. This trifecta -- repeated, effortful, tacit -- is the strongest signal that the problem is real, broadly shared, and not being solved by the current market. The Intuit milkshake discovery is the archetype: behavior repeated by half the customer base, invisible in survey data, explained only by observation.
- Weak signal: Observations vary significantly across participants (each person has a different workflow), or the pain points surfaced are ones users mention without performing any workaround (pure complaints). A weak result also occurs when participants only exhibit the target behavior when asked to demonstrate it for you rather than as a natural part of their work. A single vivid observation from one participant is not a pattern. Weak results mean either the problem is not consistent enough to build on, the participant sample was wrong, or sessions were too short/shallow to reach the underlying behavior layer.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [contested] Minimum contextual inquiry sample: 4 participants for a single tightly-scoped task (Holtzblatt & Beyer, via Wikipedia) -- Wikipedia's Contextual Inquiry article does state 'as few as 4 users (for a single, small task)' but does not directly cite Holtzblatt & Beyer 1998 for that specific figure. Holtzblatt & Beyer's actual 1998 book recommends 15-20 participants for a full contextual design study; industry practice of 4-6 is commonly noted but is separate from the book's own recommendation. The '4 minimum' appears to be a Wikipedia editorial synthesis rather than a direct Holtzblatt & Beyer citation. The attribution chain is broken. (src: Wikipedia 'Contextual inquiry' article (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contextual_inquiry); Holtzblatt & Beyer 1998 recommendation of 15-20 corroborated via wacclearinghouse.org and uxdesign.cc)
- [contested] Typical upper bound for contextual inquiry sample: 30+ participants for broad contextual design programs (Holtzblatt & Beyer, via Wikipedia) -- Wikipedia does state the range goes 'to 30 or more,' corroborating the upper bound figure. However, Holtzblatt & Beyer's own 1998 book recommends 15-20 participants, not 30+. The '30+' upper bound in Wikipedia does not appear to trace directly to Holtzblatt & Beyer 1998; the attribution is questionable even if the general range is plausible for large programs. (src: Wikipedia 'Contextual inquiry' article (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contextual_inquiry); Holtzblatt & Beyer 1998 actual recommendation of 15-20 via multiple secondary sources)
- [confirmed] Standard session length: approximately 2 hours per one-on-one session (Wikipedia Contextual Inquiry; NNGroup) -- Wikipedia describes contextual inquiry as 'an approximately two-hour, one-on-one interaction.' NNGroup states sessions 'can range from an hour or two to several days' and in NNGroup's own published contextual inquiry study of AI image-generation tools, each session was 2 hours. The 'approximately 2 hours' characterization is well-supported as the typical baseline, even if the full range is wider. (src: Wikipedia 'Contextual inquiry' (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contextual_inquiry); NNGroup 'Contextual Inquiry' article (nngroup.com/articles/contextual-inquiry/); NNGroup AI image-generation contextual inquiry study (nngroup.com/articles/contextual-inquiry-ai-imagegen/))
- [contested] Intuit runs 10,000 hours per year of 'Follow Me Home' observational research (MyParticipants Medium, citing Intuit) -- The MyParticipants Medium article cited as the source does NOT contain the 10,000-hour figure at all -- it discusses the Follow Me Home program generally without any such statistic. The 10,000-hour claim appears in an unsourced Harvard D3 student-submission blog post (aiinstitute.hbs.edu) with no citation to a primary Intuit source. No official Intuit document, Scott Cook speech, or HBS case study corroborating this specific figure was found. The claim may be accurate but is not traceable to the stated source, and the primary source is unverified. (src: MyParticipants Medium article (myparticipants.medium.com) -- does NOT contain the figure; Harvard D3 submission (aiinstitute.hbs.edu) -- contains the figure but cites no primary source)
- [confirmed] Christensen milkshake study: nearly half (~40-50%) of milkshake sales occurred to solo morning commuters -- a finding invisible in surveys -- HBS Working Knowledge (the official HBS publication) states 40% of milkshakes were purchased first thing in the morning by commuters. Other retellings including sources citing Christensen's book 'Competing Against Luck' say 'about half' or 'almost half' sold before 8:30 AM. The range of 40-50% across retellings is an accurate characterization of the spread in how the finding is reported. The core finding -- a disproportionately large share of sales to solo morning commuters, invisible to surveys -- is consistently confirmed. The 'nearly half' framing is a reasonable synthesis, though the precise figure varies by source. (src: HBS Working Knowledge 'Clay Christensen's Milkshake Marketing' (library.hbs.edu/working-knowledge/clay-christensens-milkshake-marketing); Productside, UX Collective, and other JTBD sources for the 'about half' framing)
- [contested] Nielsen's 5-user usability test benchmark: 5 users detect ~85% of usability problems on average (range 55-100%) -- applies to usability testing, not directly to contextual inquiry (Virzi 1992, Nielsen 1993, via uxpsychology.substack.com) -- The 85% average and 55-100% range come from Faulkner (2003), not from Virzi 1992 or Nielsen 1993 directly. Virzi (1992) found that 4-5 users detect approximately 80% of problems (p=0.32-0.42). Nielsen & Landauer's (1993) mathematical model also predicts ~85% at 5 users given their assumed p=0.31, but the empirical 55-100% range is Faulkner's 2003 contribution. Attributing the 85% figure and the 55-100% range jointly to 'Virzi 1992, Nielsen 1993' is imprecise; the range figure in particular postdates those papers by a decade. The caveat that this applies to usability testing, not contextual inquiry, is correct. (src: MeasuringU 'A Brief History of the Magic Number 5' (measuringu.com/five-history/); Faulkner 2003 as the source of the 55-100% range; Virzi 1992 (Human Factors, 34, 457-471) for the 80% / p=0.32 figure)
- [confirmed] Contextual inquiry was first referenced in 1988 and fully described as a methodology in 1990 (Wikipedia, citing Whiteside/Bennett/Holtzblatt 1988 and Wixon/Holtzblatt/Knox 1990) -- Multiple independent sources confirm both milestones. The 1988 paper is Whiteside, Bennett & Holtzblatt, 'Usability Engineering: Our Experience and Evolution,' in the Handbook of Human-Computer Interaction, which introduced the theoretical foundation. The 1990 paper is Wixon, Holtzblatt & Knox, 'Contextual Design: An Emergent View of System Design,' CHI '90 Proceedings pp. 329-336, which is the first full methodological description. Both papers are real, verifiable, and the chronology is consistent across Wikipedia, IxDF, MeasuringU, and EduTech Wiki. (src: Wikipedia 'Contextual inquiry' (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contextual_inquiry); IxDF 'Contextual Interviews' (ixdf.org); CHI '90 paper confirmed at gtubicomp2015grad.pbworks.com and incontextdesign.com)

**Citations**
- Nielsen Norman Group -- Contextual Inquiry definition and four-principle framework: https://www.nngroup.com/articles/contextual-inquiry/
- Wikipedia -- Contextual Inquiry (origin dates, sample size range, session structure): https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contextual_inquiry
- LearningLoop -- Contextual Inquiry protocol steps and real company examples (IDEO, Intuit, Christensen): https://learningloop.io/plays/contextual-inquiry
- MyParticipants Medium -- Intuit Follow Me Home program details including 10,000 hours/year figure: https://myparticipants.medium.com/how-the-follow-me-home-research-approach-helped-intuit-build-a-billion-dollar-customer-centric-e7e8d9e3a317
- The Rewired Group -- Clayton Christensen milkshake study (morning commuter observation): https://therewiredgroup.com/case-studies/milkshakes/
- Maze -- Contextual Inquiry in UX (definition, failure modes, startup application): https://maze.co/blog/contextual-inquiry/
- Looppanel -- The Mom Test and behavioral observation principles (Fitzpatrick): https://www.looppanel.com/blog/customer-interviews
- UX Psychology Substack -- How many participants for qualitative research (NNGroup 5-user benchmark context): https://uxpsychology.substack.com/p/how-many-participants-do-you-need
- Wikipedia -- Contextual Design (Holtzblatt & Beyer methodology origin): https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contextual_design

### 3. Competitor Review Mining (1-star reviews, forums, G2, Reddit)

- **Category:** Problem & demand discovery
- **Stage:** problem
- **Definition:** Competitor review mining is the systematic extraction and analysis of publicly available user feedback -- on platforms such as G2, Capterra, Trustpilot, Reddit, Hacker News, and app stores -- to surface recurring pain points, unmet needs, and pricing objections that competitors have provably failed to resolve. The analyst reads reviews not to understand the competitor's product but to understand the frustrated buyer: what they wanted, what they did not get, and what language they use to describe the gap. It is a desk-research complement to direct customer interviews, providing a large-n signal without requiring access to live customers.
- **Validates:** Does a real, widely-felt problem exist in this market that incumbent products demonstrably fail to solve?

**Protocol**
1. Identify 3-5 direct competitors with the largest review volumes on G2 and/or Capterra. Pull separately on Reddit (subreddits where the buyer persona is active) and Hacker News (search 'Ask HN: alternatives to X'). Use the platform's own filters to sort by recency (last 12 months) and rating.
2. Read all 1-star and 2-star reviews on G2/Capterra first; then read 3-star reviews, which surface partial satisfaction and reveal feature ceilings. Note the 'What do you dislike?' and 'What problems is the software solving for you?' structured fields on G2 -- these are the highest-signal columns. Ignore 5-star reviews for problem discovery.
3. Tag each complaint with a canonical pain-point label (e.g., 'pricing inaccessible for SMB', 'onboarding too complex', 'missing feature X'). Do not invent labels top-down; let the language in the reviews drive the taxonomy.
4. Count frequency: a pain point mentioned in fewer than 3 independent reviews from different reviewers is noise. A pain point appearing across 10+ reviews from users in the same buyer segment is a structural signal worth pursuing.
5. Cross-validate by platform: if the same complaint appears on G2, in a Reddit thread, AND in an 'alternatives to X' HN comment thread, treat it as a confirmed structural gap, not an individual quirk.
6. Distinguish complainers from buyers: per Rob Fitzpatrick's Mom Test, a user venting without having sought any workaround is weaker signal than a user who writes 'we switched to X just for this feature' or 'we wrote our own script to fill this gap' -- behavioral evidence of the pain matters.
7. Map surviving gaps to buyer segments: note which pains cluster in SMB vs. enterprise reviews, which are pricing-driven vs. feature-driven, and which segments the competitor explicitly ignores. The intersection of 'frequent complaint' + 'underserved segment' is the opportunity zone.
8. Synthesize into a 1-page problem brief: name the top 3 pains with evidence count, the buyer segment most affected, verbatim quote examples, and a note on whether the competitor appears to be aware of the gap (roadmap signals, official responses to reviews).

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Minimum frequency threshold for a structural signal vs. noise (independent reviews citing the same pain point): 10+ reviews from distinct reviewers in the same buyer segment (src: Synthesis from foundevo.com competitor review mining framework and G2/Capterra practitioner methodology (hiresteve.ai, 2026))
- G2 total B2B software reviews indexed (scale of available signal pool): 2.5 million reviews as of early 2026, across 2,000+ software categories, averaging 110 new reviews per category per quarter (src: G2 and Capterra competitive intelligence overview (foundevo.com / practitioner synthesis, 2026))
- Capterra total reviews indexed: 2 million+ user reviews across 100,000+ software products in 900 categories (src: Capterra platform coverage figures cited in technologymatch.com comparison, 2026)
- G2 fake review rejection rate (data quality floor for the signal pool): 4,000+ AI-generated reviews rejected monthly via 43-point authentication system (src: CheckThat.ai G2 authentication analysis, 2026)
- Proportion of open-ended feedback responses carrying mixed sentiment (risk of single-label sentiment flattening): 29% of responses contain mixed sentiment; average response contains 4.2 distinct topics (src: Zonka Feedback opinion mining research, cited in TechTarget / ThinkOwl sentiment analysis overview)

**Origin and evidence:** The technique has no single canonical originator. It is a practitioner-developed method that emerged from the broader 'voice of customer' and competitive intelligence traditions. Rob Fitzpatrick's The Mom Test (2013) established the theoretical grounding -- specifically the principle that behavioral evidence of pain (workarounds, switching behavior, money already spent) is stronger than stated complaints -- and warned that 'complainers' who never act are weak signal. The lean startup community (Eric Ries, Ash Maurya via Running Lean) normalized desk research before building as a waste-reduction principle. The specific application to G2/Capterra/Reddit review platforms became widely discussed on Indie Hackers, Lenny Rachitsky's newsletter, and practitioner blogs from roughly 2019 onward, as review platforms reached critical mass. No peer-reviewed study has validated a specific conversion or success rate for the method; the evidence base is practitioner case studies and anecdote. The method is well-documented and widely practiced, but the evidence for its efficacy is observational, not experimental.

**Canonical sources:** Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) -- behavioral evidence of pain, 'complainers vs. customers' distinction; Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011) -- validated learning, eliminate waste before building; Ash Maurya, Running Lean (2012) -- problem interview before solution interview; Indie Hackers community -- practitioner case studies including '5,000+ reviews analyzed, $127K/$284K/$198K successive product revenue' anecdote; G2 and Capterra platform methodology documentation; Lenny Rachitsky newsletter (lennysnewsletter.com) -- problem discovery frameworks for B2B SaaS; TechTarget / Zonka Feedback -- opinion mining and sentiment analysis research base; foundevo.com -- structured competitor review mining framework for SaaS idea validation

**Failure modes**
- Survivorship bias in the review pool: only users who were motivated enough to write a review are represented. Silent churners -- often the most dissatisfied -- leave no trace, and satisfied silent users leave no positive counterweight. The visible review population is not a random sample of buyers.
- Frequency conflation with severity: a pain mentioned in 50 reviews may be a minor nuisance; a pain mentioned in 5 reviews by power users who represent 80% of revenue may be catastrophic. Raw count is a proxy, not a verdict.
- Extreme-rating skew: review distributions are bimodal (very happy and very unhappy). Mid-market, partially-satisfied users -- often the most realistic indicator of a crossable pain threshold -- are underrepresented unless you specifically filter for 2-4 star reviews.
- Competitor-planted reviews: negative reviews of a competitor may be seeded by rivals or disgruntled ex-employees. G2 rejects 4,000+ suspected AI-generated reviews monthly, but the floor is not zero.
- Confirmation bias amplification: founders mining reviews after forming a hypothesis will pattern-match confirming reviews and discount disconfirming ones. The method requires a pre-registered pain taxonomy, not post-hoc clustering.
- The complainer-not-buyer trap (Fitzpatrick): users who write angry reviews may have no actual budget or authority to buy a replacement. A review is a complaint, not a purchase signal.
- Sector mismatch: review platforms are heavily populated by B2B SaaS users. Consumer products, regulated industries (legal, medical, finance), and enterprise software sold through procurement channels are systematically under-reviewed, making the method less reliable in those segments.

**False-positive mechanism:** The core false positive: a cluster of reviews identifies a pain that is real but not commercially actionable. A problem can be (a) genuinely felt, (b) frequently mentioned, and (c) still not the reason buyers switch. Users complain about things they have already accommodated via workarounds and would not pay to fix. Fitzpatrick's test distinguishes a complainer from a buyer: the complainer vents but has taken no action (no workaround built, no budget allocated, no alternative evaluated). If the review pool is full of complainers who remain customers of the incumbent, the pain is confirmed but the demand for a switch is not. A founder who reads 'I hate that X is so expensive' across 40 reviews and builds a cheaper X may discover that those 40 reviewers never churned and never bought the alternative -- the complaint was a protest, not a buying signal. The method validates that a problem exists; it does not validate that buyers will pay to solve it.

**Examples**
- Drift (founded 2015): Drift's founding team analyzed Intercom and Zendesk reviews and observed a consistent pain cluster: B2B buyers resented the ticketing-first, support-first framing of both products when what they actually wanted was real-time sales conversation. G2 data showed Zendesk rated lower (8.6) than Drift (9.1) on live chat specifically. Drift built a product category (conversational marketing) explicitly positioned against the 'open a ticket' paradigm surfaced in competitor complaints. The review signal correctly identified a structural wedge.
- Indie Hackers practitioner case study (anonymous founder): A founder publicly documented on Indie Hackers analyzing 5,000+ customer reviews of competitor products, tracking 10,000+ social posts, and mapping 50+ competitors before building. First three products (built before this research) generated $0. The next three products, built after systematic review mining, generated $127K, $284K, and $198K respectively. This is a single anecdote, not a controlled study, and the causal attribution is the founder's own claim.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is when the same pain point, expressed in nearly identical language by buyers in the same segment, appears independently on at least three platforms (e.g., G2, Reddit, and Hacker News), the reviews include behavioral evidence of the pain (users describe workarounds they built, tools they stitched together, or money they spent on adjacent solutions), AND the incumbent has an official response to the complaint that says 'this is not on our roadmap' or no response at all. This triangulation -- frequency, behavioral evidence, and incumbent non-response -- indicates a structurally unaddressed problem with confirmed buyer motivation.
- Weak signal: A weak result is when pain points appear on only one platform, the reviews are predominantly 1-star (extreme polarity, likely written in anger), the complainers are still customers of the incumbent, the pain is primarily about pricing rather than a feature gap (pricing objections rarely survive the transition from complaint to willingness-to-pay for a new entrant charging similarly), or the pattern only emerges after the founder actively searches for it rather than before. A single platform showing 5-10 mentions of a problem with no cross-platform corroboration should be treated as a hypothesis for interview validation, not a confirmed problem signal.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [refuted] G2 hosts 2.5 million B2B software reviews as of early 2026 -- G2's own announcements confirm it surpassed 3 million reviews in 2025. The '2.5 million' figure is associated with Capterra (now acquired by G2), not G2 itself. The claim misattributes Capterra's figure to G2. (src: https://company.g2.com/news/g2s-2025-year-in-review)
- [contested] G2 rejects 4,000+ AI-generated reviews monthly via a 43-point authentication system -- The 43-point authentication system is confirmed by G2's own documentation. The '4,000+ monthly' rejection figure appears on CheckThat.ai attributing it to G2, but G2's own published materials do not state this specific number. CheckThat.ai itself notes the system's 'proprietary nature prevents independent verification.' G2 also now permits AI-assisted reviews under certain verification conditions, complicating the rejection framing. (src: https://checkthat.ai/brands/g2/reviews — https://learn.g2.com/g2-ai-review-moderation)
- [contested] Capterra covers 100,000+ software products across 900 categories with 2 million+ user reviews -- The 2 million+ reviews figure is confirmed by Capterra's own data page. The product count is ambiguous: Capterra's proprietary data page states 45,000+ reviewed software products but references 100,000+ solutions in its advisor database. The category count is contradicted: Capterra's own page states 1,200+ categories, not 900, though some older Capterra marketing copy does cite 900. The '900 categories' figure appears stale or from a different product-count basis. (src: https://www.capterra.com/resources/proprietary-data-research/)
- [contested] G2 averages 110 new reviews per category per quarter -- This figure traces solely to a content marketing article on hiresteve.ai (March 2026). No G2 official data, press release, or independent analysis corroborates this specific number. G2's own methodology documentation does not publish per-category quarterly averages. It is unverifiable from primary sources. (src: https://hiresteve.ai/articles/g2-capterra-reviews-competitive-intelligence-2026 (now returns 404; figure cited in search snippet only))
- [contested] 29% of open-ended feedback responses carry mixed sentiment; average response contains 4.2 distinct topics -- Both figures originate from Zonka Feedback's self-reported internal analysis of 1M+ feedback responses. Zonka is a feedback software vendor with a commercial interest in this finding. The analysis has not been peer-reviewed or independently replicated, and no methodology details (sampling, industries, time period) are disclosed publicly. The numbers may be accurate but cannot be independently verified. (src: https://www.zonkafeedback.com/blog/what-we-found-inside-1m-customer-feedback-response)
- [refuted] Superhuman's PMF score was 22% at launch and reached 58% within 18 months -- The 22% starting score is confirmed. However, multiple primary sources including First Round Review's canonical article and the SaaS Club podcast with Rahul Vohra state the journey from 22% to 58% took 'three quarters' — approximately 9 months, not 18 months. The '18 months' figure appears to be an inflation of the documented timeline. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [contested] Sean Ellis 40% threshold: startups with >40% 'very disappointed' correlate strongly with PMF, based on comparison across ~100 startups -- The '~100 startups' framing and 40% threshold are widely and consistently reported across secondary sources and trace to Ellis's own pmfsurvey.com. No peer-reviewed study, published dataset, or independent audit of the underlying startup sample exists. The claim is a practitioner rule-of-thumb with strong anecdotal support (including Superhuman's public case) but no independently verifiable empirical basis. 'Correlate strongly' overstates what is essentially a self-reported heuristic. (src: https://pmtoolkit.ai/calculators/pmf-score — https://www.zonkafeedback.com/blog/product-market-fit-survey)
- [contested] Indie Hackers case study: founder analyzed 5,000+ competitor reviews and 10,000+ social posts; first 3 products generated $0; next 3 generated $127K, $284K, $198K respectively -- No corroborating source was found for this specific case study or its revenue figures. Web searches across Indie Hackers, Hacker News, and general search returned no results matching these specific revenue numbers or the described methodology. The claim is labeled 'single anecdote, unverified' in the original text, which is accurate — it cannot be confirmed or refuted without a traceable source. (src: No corroborating source found)

**Citations**
- The Mom Test -- Rob Fitzpatrick (official site): https://www.momtestbook.com/
- Foundevo: How to Find a SaaS Product Idea with Low Competition (review mining framework): https://www.foundevo.com/how-to-find-saas-product-idea-low-competition/
- Indie Hackers: How I Validated My Micro-SaaS Idea Quickly: https://www.indiehackers.com/post/how-i-validated-my-micro-saas-idea-quickly-and-you-can-too-53decf45b9
- CheckThat.ai: G2 Reviews 2026 -- Authenticity and Trust Analysis: https://checkthat.ai/brands/g2/reviews
- TechnologyMatch: G2 vs Capterra vs Trustpilot comparison: https://technologymatch.com/blog/g2-vs-capterra-vs-trustpilot-vs-technologymatch-which-discovery-platform-is-best-for-it-leaders
- Zonka Feedback: Opinion Mining from Customer Feedback: https://www.zonkafeedback.com/blog/opinion-mining-from-customer-feedback
- Startupik: Rahul Vohra and Superhuman -- PMF Framework: https://startupik.com/rahul-vohra-the-superhuman-founder-obsessed-with-product-market-fit/
- Lenny Rachitsky -- How to Validate Your Startup Idea (LinkedIn): https://www.linkedin.com/posts/lennyrachitsky_how-to-validate-your-startup-idea-activity-6929816443035271169-nq62
- G2: Drift vs Zendesk Support Suite comparison: https://www.g2.com/compare/drift-vs-zendesk-support-suite
- TechTarget: Sentiment Analysis / Opinion Mining definition: https://www.techtarget.com/searchbusinessanalytics/definition/opinion-mining-sentiment-mining
- SpringerLink: Detection of False Positive Situation in Review Mining: https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-981-13-3600-3_8
- Simon & Schuster: The Mom Test by Rob Fitzpatrick: https://www.simonandschuster.com/books/The-Mom-Test/Rob-Fitzpatrick/9798893312577
