### 4. Search-volume and intent analysis (Google Trends, keyword volume, autocomplete)

- **Category:** Problem & demand discovery
- **Stage:** problem
- **Definition:** Search-volume and intent analysis uses publicly available search engine data -- primarily Google Trends (relative interest index), Google Keyword Planner (estimated monthly volume), and autocomplete suggestions -- to infer the existence and size of latent demand for a problem or category before a product is built. The core premise is that people search for problems they urgently need solved, so aggregate query volume and intent classification (informational vs. commercial vs. transactional) act as a low-cost proxy for market pull. Google Trends normalizes data to a 0-100 relative interest scale via a three-step process: dividing each data point by total searches for the geography/time period, scaling proportionally across all topics, and sampling from billions of daily queries rather than using the complete dataset.
- **Validates:** Does an active, searchable problem exist at sufficient scale, with searchers expressing commercial or transactional intent rather than mere curiosity?

**Protocol**
1. Define 3-5 seed keywords that describe the problem (not the solution). Use the language a buyer would type when frustrated, e.g. 'HIPAA-compliant CRM behavioral health' not 'CRM software'. Avoid solution-framing terms at this stage.
2. Run each seed in Google Trends (5-year view, global then regional). Score: (a) is the trend line flat/rising/declining? (b) Are there seasonal spikes? (c) Do breakout related queries reveal adjacent problem surfaces? Require at least 4-8 weeks of sustained interest before treating a spike as signal, per Semrush guidance.
3. Classify search intent for each keyword cluster using the four-category framework: Navigational (brand/page lookup), Informational (learning), Commercial Investigation (comparing options), Transactional (ready to buy). Only commercial and transactional clusters confirm near-purchase demand. Informational volume is awareness, not demand.
4. Pull absolute volume estimates from Google Keyword Planner or a secondary tool (Ahrefs, Semrush). Cross-reference at least two tools because individual tool estimates carry substantial noise. Record the 12-month rolling average and note whether tools show zero for sub-3-month emerging topics.
5. Assess competition proxy: note keyword difficulty score and count of existing paid ads (AdWords presence signals that someone is paying for the demand, a positive demand signal). Compare your keyword vs. competitor brand keywords in Google Trends using the comparison feature.
6. Segment by region. Use Trends heatmaps to identify geographic concentration. A keyword that indexes 100 in one metro and 0 elsewhere signals a local market, not a broad one.
7. Autocomplete audit: record what Google and Bing suggest for partial queries around the problem. Note complaint-pattern suffixes ('____ alternative', '____ problems', '____ doesn't work') as pain-signal qualifiers. Do not treat autocomplete rank as volume; it is filtered and can be gamed.
8. Triangulate outputs. Search data is the beginning of the research process, not the end. Gate any conclusion behind at least one behavioral confirmation: a landing-page conversion test, willingness-to-pay interview (Lenny Rachitsky's median of 30 B2B customer conversations), or paid-ad click-through on the exact problem statement.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Minimum monthly search volume worth acting on (niche/local startup): 20-200 searches/month (src: ALM Corp Keyword Search Volume Guide 2026 (almcorp.com/blog/keyword-search-volume))
- General content prioritization threshold: 100-1,000 searches/month (src: ALM Corp Keyword Search Volume Guide 2026)
- High-demand startup idea validation bar (curated startup keyword lists): >100,000 searches/month with keyword difficulty <20 (src: ALM Corp Keyword Search Volume Guide 2026)
- Later (social scheduling app) case: favorable validation threshold used: 9,900 monthly searches, SEO difficulty 31 for 'schedule Instagram post' (src: LearningLoop Trends and Keyword Analysis play (learningloop.io/plays/trends-and-keyword-analysis))
- Traction channel rule of thumb: volume worth targeting: If capturing 10% of searches for a term would be meaningful to your business (src: Traction, Gabriel Weinberg & Justin Mares (Goodreads notes))
- Google Trends relative accuracy for comparative use: 70-90% accuracy for relative comparisons between terms; not usable for absolute volume (src: Semrush Google Trends Guide 2026 (semrush.com/blog/google-trends))
- Sustainability filter before acting on a trend: 4-8 weeks of sustained growth required (src: Semrush Google Trends Guide 2026)
- Multi-signal confirmation: landing page conversion benchmark alongside search signal: >=10% waitlist conversion with paid traffic (src: Y Combinator Startup School validation framework (as summarized by CleverX Blog 2026))
- Median B2B customer interviews required for complementary qualitative validation: 30 conversations (src: Lenny Rachitsky, 'How to validate your B2B startup idea' (lennysnewsletter.com))

**Origin and evidence:** The method was not originated by startup literature; it is adapted from direct-response marketing and SEO practice. Gabriel Weinberg and Justin Mares codified Search Engine Marketing (including keyword demand analysis) as one of 19 structured traction channels in their 2014 book Traction, popularizing it for founders as a systematic demand test rather than a marketing tactic. The Bullseye Framework from Traction instructs founders to allocate 50% of early-stage effort to testing acquisition channels -- of which SEM/keyword demand is one. The primary academic concern about this method's validity was articulated in a 2024 ScienceDirect systematic review (Social Science Research, Mellon et al.) which identified substantial challenges to internal validity, reliability, and generalizability of Google Trends data in research contexts. The evidence base is practitioner-strong but lacks RCT-level validation: no controlled study compares startups that used keyword analysis to validate vs. those that did not.

**Canonical sources:** Traction: How Any Startup Can Achieve Explosive Customer Growth -- Gabriel Weinberg & Justin Mares (2014/2015); Google Trends FAQ -- official Google documentation (support.google.com/trends/answer/4365533); Semrush Google Trends Guide 2026 (semrush.com/blog/google-trends); Lenny Rachitsky, 'How to validate your B2B startup idea' (lennysnewsletter.com); LearningLoop, 'Trends and Keyword Analysis' play (learningloop.io); 'The (mis)use of Google Trends data in the social sciences -- A systematic review, critique, and recommendations' (Social Science Research, ScienceDirect 2024, doi:10.1016/j.ssresearch.2024.103000); ALM Corp Keyword Search Volume Complete Guide 2026

**Failure modes**
- Relative-scale confusion: Google Trends outputs a 0-100 index normalized to peak interest within the selected timeframe and region, not absolute query counts. A score of 50 in one region does not mean 50% of national searches; two identical scores in different regions can represent vastly different absolute volumes.
- Sampling noise on low-volume queries: Google Trends uses random statistical sampling from billions of daily queries. For low-frequency or emerging terms, this introduces 'small and random fluctuations' (Google's own language) that can produce one-off spikes with no actual demand behind them.
- 12-month averaging lag: keyword tools report 12-month rolling averages. A problem that started generating consistent searches 3 months ago may still show zero volume. This systematically undercounts emerging markets and can cause founders to pass on valid opportunities.
- Intent misclassification: high informational volume (people learning about a problem) is often mistaken for commercial demand. 'Meditation techniques' and 'buy meditation app' look similar in raw volume but represent radically different buyer readiness.
- Autocomplete gaming: autocomplete predictions are subject to Google's removal policies and can be artificially inflated by coordinated search campaigns. They cannot be taken as a reliable proxy for organic demand.
- Category-level signal vs. product-level demand: a strong trend for a category keyword (e.g., 'no-code tools') does not validate a specific product. The category might be growing while the specific niche you target is flat or commoditized.
- Audience vs. buyer confusion: B2C curiosity searches and hobbyist traffic inflate volume without representing willingness to pay. The searcher typing a broad problem query may be a student, a journalist, or a competitor, not a buyer.
- Single-platform bias: Google Trends and Keyword Planner reflect only Google search behavior. Markets skewed toward LinkedIn, Reddit, Apple App Store, or enterprise procurement channels are systematically underrepresented.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false-positive mechanism is conflating search curiosity with purchase intent. A founder sees rising Google Trends scores or high monthly search volume for a problem keyword, infers strong demand, and proceeds to build -- only to discover that searchers were informational browsers, not buyers. This is structurally reinforced by three artifacts: (1) Google Trends normalizes to a relative peak, so even a niche topic with 200 absolute searches can show an impressive curve if the prior baseline was near-zero; (2) autocomplete suggestions surface any recurring query cluster regardless of whether those searchers convert, creating an illusion of critical mass; (3) volume tools aggregate 12-month data, smoothing out the difference between a sustained buyer market and a media-driven curiosity spike (e.g., a category that trended because of a viral article, not because people are actively seeking a solution to spend money on). The result: a founding team confidently enters a market with 'proven demand' that turns out to be awareness-stage interest with no monetizable cohort behind it.

**Examples**
- Later (social media scheduler): The founding team identified 'schedule Instagram post' as a target keyword with approximately 9,900 monthly searches and SEO difficulty of 31, which they used as an early demand signal before committing significant engineering resources to the scheduling feature set. The keyword's commercial intent (users seeking a tool to accomplish a specific task) and moderate competition made it a favorable validation signal relative to broader social media keywords.
- DuckDuckGo (Gabriel Weinberg): Weinberg applied the Bullseye Framework from Traction -- which includes SEM/keyword demand as one of 19 channels -- to test which acquisition channels worked before scaling. He ran parallel tests across content marketing microsites, social display ads, and SEM, using keyword click-through and conversion data to determine where demand was most efficiently captured. This made DuckDuckGo one of the canonical case studies for systematic traction channel testing rather than intuition-driven marketing.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result looks like: (a) 3-5 problem-framed keywords with 1,000+ monthly searches and commercial or transactional intent classification; (b) a Google Trends line showing sustained upward trajectory over 6-12 months (not a single spike); (c) presence of multiple paid advertisers bidding on the category (confirming others have already validated conversion economics); (d) geographic correlation across multiple regions rather than one anomalous market; (e) related queries in Trends include complaint-pattern terms ('alternative to X', 'X problems') confirming active dissatisfaction, not just curiosity. This constellation suggests a real, growing, monetizable problem with existing buyer behavior -- a sufficient condition to move to a landing-page or paid-ad conversion test.
- Weak signal: A weak result looks like: (a) high volume concentrated in clearly informational queries with no commercial modifiers; (b) a Trends line with one or two sharp spikes that flatten back to baseline (media-driven curiosity, not sustained demand); (c) zero paid advertisers on adjacent keywords (suggesting no one has found a conversion-positive buyer); (d) volume that vanishes when you shift from broad category terms to specific problem-framing terms (indicating people search the category abstractly but not with buying intent); (e) autocomplete suggestions that surface only educational or news content. A weak result does not disprove the opportunity -- it means the problem is either too new for search data to reflect (early-category markets), too niche for Google's sampling to capture, or the channel to reach buyers is not search at all. Founders should pivot to direct problem interviews (Rob Fitzpatrick's The Mom Test methodology) or a different demand-discovery channel before concluding there is no market.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Google Trends uses a 0-100 relative interest scale normalized to peak search interest within the selected timeframe and geography -- not absolute query counts -- Confirmed directly by the official Google Trends FAQ (support.google.com/trends/answer/4365533). The FAQ states data points are divided by total searches in the selected geography and time range, then scaled 0-100 based on each topic's proportion to all searches, with 100 representing peak popularity. The data explicitly does not reflect absolute query counts. (src: https://support.google.com/trends/answer/4365533?hl=en)
- [refuted] Google Trends achieves 70-90% accuracy for relative comparisons between terms, per Semrush -- The live Semrush Google Trends blog post (semrush.com/blog/google-trends) makes no mention of a '70-90% accuracy' figure. The Glimpse accuracy deep-dive also found no such percentage, calling Trends 'highly accurate' in qualitative terms only. The 70-90% figure does not appear in any independent authoritative source and cannot be traced to Semrush. It appears to be a fabricated or hallucinated attribution. (src: https://www.semrush.com/blog/google-trends/ ; https://meetglimpse.com/google-trends/accuracy/)
- [refuted] 4-8 weeks of sustained trend growth is required before treating a Google Trends spike as a real signal, per Semrush -- The live Semrush Google Trends guide contains no such specific timeframe. Fetching the page directly confirmed it warns about 'small-sample data traps' and recommends pairing Trends with Semrush's Keyword Overview, but gives no '4-8 week' rule. No independent corroborating source for this specific figure was found; it cannot be attributed to Semrush. (src: https://www.semrush.com/blog/google-trends/)
- [confirmed] Keyword tools report 12-month rolling averages, which can show zero volume for topics that only emerged in the past 3 months -- Multiple independent SEO sources (ALM Corp, Search Engine Journal, ContentForce, AgencyDashboard) confirm that keyword tools default to 12-month rolling averages. The consequence -- that topics emerging in the past 3 months can show zero or near-zero volume -- is explicitly stated across these sources as a well-known limitation. (src: https://almcorp.com/blog/keyword-search-volume/ ; https://agencydashboard.io/blog/keyword-search-volume)
- [contested] 20-200 monthly searches is a viable threshold for niche/local startups (ALM Corp) -- The ALM Corp guide (fetched directly) does not state a '20-200' threshold by name. It states that B2B/niche businesses can viably target keywords with '50-200 monthly searches' when conversion value is high, and references a case study of keywords with 'fewer than 20 monthly searches' driving real signups. The claim partially overlaps with the content but the precise '20-200' bracket is not a stated threshold -- the guide emphasizes there is 'no universal threshold.' (src: https://almcorp.com/blog/keyword-search-volume/)
- [confirmed] 100-1,000 monthly searches is the general content prioritization benchmark (ALM Corp) -- The ALM Corp guide (fetched directly) states: 'for new/low-authority sites, targeting keywords with volumes between 100 and 1,000 and moderate-to-low difficulty scores is typically more productive.' This matches the claimed benchmark, though the guide frames it as guidance for new sites rather than a universal content prioritization rule. (src: https://almcorp.com/blog/keyword-search-volume/)
- [contested] 100,000+ monthly searches with keyword difficulty <20 is the high-demand startup validation bar cited in curated startup keyword lists (ALM Corp) -- The ALM Corp guide itself does not frame '100,000+ searches with difficulty <20' as a startup validation bar. The guide mentions the 100k threshold only to warn that competing at that level requires strong domain authority. A Gumroad listing by a third party uses exactly this framing ('30+ keywords with 100k+ monthly searches, difficulty below 20') as a product pitch for curated keyword lists. The claim misattributes a third-party list-seller framing to ALM Corp as a validation benchmark. (src: https://almcorp.com/blog/keyword-search-volume/ ; https://reddysharath.gumroad.com/l/highvolumekeywords)
- [contested] Later validated 'schedule Instagram post' at approximately 9,900 monthly searches with SEO difficulty of 31 (LearningLoop) -- The LearningLoop page (learningloop.io/plays/trends-and-keyword-analysis) does state '9,900 searches/month' and 'SEO difficulty score of 31' for this keyword in a Later case study. The source exists and contains the claim. However, LearningLoop is a third-party playbook site with no disclosed methodology, the figures cannot be independently verified against Ahrefs or Semrush data (which is paywalled), and it is unclear when or from which tool these numbers were pulled. The source is real but not authoritative enough to confirm as fact. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/trends-and-keyword-analysis)
- [confirmed] Traction's rule of thumb: if capturing 10% of searches for a term would be meaningful to the business, the volume is worth targeting (Weinberg & Mares) -- Multiple independent book summaries (mtlynch.io, auresnotes.com, dansilvestre.com, readingraphics.com) consistently quote this rule from the Traction SEO chapter verbatim: 'You want to find terms that have enough volume such that if you captured 10 percent of the searches for a given term then it would be meaningful.' The attribution to Weinberg and Mares is accurate. (src: https://mtlynch.io/book-reports/traction/ ; https://auresnotes.com/book-summary-traction-gabriel-weinberg-justin-mares/)
- [confirmed] Lenny Rachitsky's B2B validation research found a median of 30 customer conversations before validation was considered sufficient -- Confirmed directly from lennysnewsletter.com. The article 'How to validate your B2B startup idea' (published August 2023) explicitly states: 'Founders spoke to a median of 30 potential customers before feeling like their idea was solid.' The figure is from Lenny's own primary research interviewing 20+ B2B startup founders. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-to-validate-your-b2b-startup)
- [confirmed] B2B startup pivot rate is approximately 40%, versus 20% in B2C, per Lenny Rachitsky's research -- Confirmed from lennysnewsletter.com. The article states approximately 40% of B2B startups pivoted at least once, and explicitly notes this rate 'is a lot higher than in B2C, where it's closer to 20%.' A separate Lenny post on X slightly revised the B2B framing to 'one in three' and B2C to 'one in five,' suggesting the 40%/20% figures are rounded estimates from the same dataset, not a precise study. The directional claim is accurate and attributed correctly. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-to-validate-your-b2b-startup ; https://x.com/lennysan/status/1689405499840864256)
- [confirmed] Google processes billions of searches per day; Trends uses a random representative sample rather than the full dataset (Google Trends FAQ) -- Confirmed verbatim from the official Google Trends FAQ (support.google.com/trends/answer/4365533): 'While only a sample of Google searches are used in Google Trends, this is sufficient because we handle billions of searches per day. Providing access to the entire dataset would be too large to process quickly. By sampling data, Google can look at a dataset representative of all Google searches.' The attribution to the Google Trends FAQ is accurate. (src: https://support.google.com/trends/answer/4365533?hl=en)

**Citations**
- Google Trends FAQ -- official sampling and scale methodology: https://support.google.com/trends/answer/4365533?hl=en
- Semrush: How to use Google Trends for SEO in 2026: https://www.semrush.com/blog/google-trends/
- ALM Corp: Keyword Search Volume -- The Complete 2026 Guide: https://almcorp.com/blog/keyword-search-volume/
- LearningLoop: Trends and Keyword Analysis product experiment play: https://learningloop.io/plays/trends-and-keyword-analysis
- Lenny Rachitsky: How to validate your B2B startup idea: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-to-validate-your-b2b-startup
- Traction (Google Books) -- Gabriel Weinberg & Justin Mares: https://books.google.com/books/about/Traction.html?id=A3_MBgAAQBAJ
- ScienceDirect: The (mis)use of Google Trends data in the social sciences -- systematic review 2024: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0049089X24001212
- Traction Lab Podcast: Validation vs. Vibes (interest is not intent): https://zerototraction.substack.com/p/validation-vs-vibes
- StartUs Insights: How to Use Google Trends for Market Research: https://www.startus-insights.com/innovators-guide/how-to-use-google-trends-for-market-research/

---
### 5. The switching interview (current alternatives and switching cost)

- **Category:** Problem & demand discovery
- **Stage:** problem
- **Definition:** The switching interview is a structured qualitative technique, developed by Bob Moesta and Chris Spiek at the Rewired Group under the Jobs-to-Be-Done (JTBD) framework, that reconstructs the full narrative of a real, recent switching decision -- from the first struggling moment that broke a customer's status quo through to ongoing use of a new solution. It maps four competing forces acting on any switch (Push, Pull, Anxiety, Habit) across a six-stage buying timeline to reveal what made a customer leave one solution (including doing nothing), what they actually evaluated as alternatives, what the true switching cost was, and whether Push + Pull finally outweighed Anxiety + Habit. Unlike interviews that ask about hypothetical future behavior, it is grounded entirely in a specific past event the respondent has already lived through.
- **Validates:** What is so painful about the customer's current way of getting this job done that they would bear the cost and friction of switching -- and what does "bearing the cost" concretely mean in time, money, social risk, and process disruption?

**Protocol**
1. RECRUIT RECENT REAL SWITCHERS: Identify 8-12 people who completed an actual switching decision within the past 30-90 days (not hypothetical intenders). Sample across four archetypes: switched-to-you, switched-from-you, switched-between-competitors, switched-from-nothing (prior non-consumption). Avoid recruiting exclusively through your sales team to prevent survivor-bias. Offer modest incentives; request they bring any artifacts (emails, calendar entries, invoices, screenshots) from the decision.
2. OPEN WITH DOCUMENTARY FRAMING: Begin each 30-45-minute session by clarifying you want to reconstruct the movie of their decision, not evaluate your product. Say: 'I want to understand the full story of when you changed how you handle [job]. I am going to ask you to tell me what happened, in your own words, from the very beginning.' Secure consent to record. Suppress any mention of your product's features.
3. START AT FIRST USE, WALK BACKWARD: Anchor on the moment of adoption or purchase, then step backward through the six-stage timeline: (a) Ongoing Use -- what is it like now? (b) First Use -- what happened the first time you used it? What almost made you go back? (c) Deciding -- who else was considered? What was on the table? What tipped it? (d) Active Looking -- when did you start seriously comparing options? What did you try? (e) Passive Looking -- when did you first start noticing alternatives, even casually? (f) First Thought -- what broke the pattern? What was happening in your life at that moment?
4. MAP THE FOUR FORCES IN REAL TIME: As the respondent narrates, tag each statement by force: Push (frustration/inadequacy of the old solution), Pull (appeal of the new solution), Anxiety (fear about the new choice -- switching risk, learning curve, data migration, team adoption), and Habit (inertia, sunk-cost comfort, existing routines). Listen for the specific moment when Push + Pull finally outweighed Anxiety + Habit.
5. PROBE CONCRETELY FOR CURRENT ALTERNATIVES AND SWITCHING COST: Ask: 'What were you using before, and what did that cost you per month/year?' 'What else did you look at?' 'What would you have had to give up if you had stayed with [old solution]?' 'What almost stopped you from switching?' 'Who else in your organization had to agree?' 'How long did the switch actually take?' Collect concrete data: dollar figures, hours spent, headcount involved, approvals required.
6. CLOSE BY COLLECTING ARTIFACTS AND DECISION CRITERIA RANKED: Ask the respondent to rank what mattered most in their decision. Collect any documentary evidence (purchase emails, vendor comparison sheets, calendar invites to demos). Ask: 'If you had to describe to a friend why you switched, what would you say in one sentence?'
7. DEBRIEF WITHIN 15 MINUTES OF EACH SESSION: Write down: the core job (the progress sought), the trigger event (the specific thing that broke the status quo), the dominant force (which of the four was decisive), the actual alternatives evaluated (not category competitors -- sometimes the real alternative was a spreadsheet, inertia, or an intern), and the single biggest switching anxiety.
8. SYNTHESIZE ACROSS 8-12 INTERVIEWS: After the minimum threshold, cluster by repeating patterns -- not by theme, but by causal mechanism. Identify: which triggers most commonly break the status quo, which alternatives customers actually consider (the real competitive set), which switching anxieties appear most frequently, and what combination of forces closed or stalled the switch. Translate into job stories ('When [trigger], I want [progress], so I can [outcome]') and a ranked anxiety inventory for onboarding/sales design.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Minimum interview count before drawing pattern conclusions: 8-12 interviews (src: GoNoGo.team JTBD guide, citing Bob Moesta / Rewired Group practitioner standard: 'one interview gives you a hypothesis, two a coincidence, real JTBD insights emerge across 8-12 switch interviews')
- Recency window for participant recruitment: 30-90 days post-decision (src: CustomerScience.com.au switch interview playbook and thehuman2ai.com JTBD guide, citing Rewired Group recruitment criteria for recall accuracy)
- Session duration: 30-45 minutes each (src: thehuman2ai.com JTBD Switch Interview Guide)
- Interview pace: Maximum 2-3 per day per facilitator (src: thehuman2ai.com JTBD Switch Interview Guide)
- Project timeline: 2-4 weeks total (3-7 days recruitment, 5-8 days interviews, 3-5 days analysis) (src: thehuman2ai.com JTBD Switch Interview Guide)
- Conversion improvement after switch-interview-led redesign (project management SaaS case study): Trial-to-paid conversion: 12% to 21% (+75% relative) (src: thehuman2ai.com JTBD Switch Interview Guide, anonymized case study)
- Participant composition: Approximately 50/50 hirers (new adopters) to firers (churned/lost) (src: thehuman2ai.com JTBD Switch Interview Guide)
- Home builder sales growth via JTBD reframe: 20% sales growth during a market downturn of 60% (src: Intercom blog, Bob Moesta interview Part 2)
- Autobooks sales cycle reduction via timeline-stage alignment: 4x improvement in conversion (src: Bob Moesta, Demand Side Sales 101, referenced in salesbabble.com and wisewords.blog book summaries)
- Quarterly cadence: pilot interviews before full run: 3 pilot interviews in Month 1 before running 8-12 in Month 2 (src: CustomerScience.com.au switch interview playbook)

**Origin and evidence:** Bob Moesta (Rewired Group) and Chris Spiek originated and named the switch interview technique as the primary empirical research method within the Jobs-to-Be-Done school associated with Clayton Christensen (Harvard Business School). Christensen developed the theoretical foundation (JTBD, Four Forces) documented in 'Competing Against Luck' (2016); Moesta operationalized it into the timeline-based interview protocol. Rob Fitzpatrick's 'The Mom Test' (2013) independently converges on the same core discipline -- ask about past behavior and current alternatives, never about hypothetical future intent -- and explicitly states that if a prospect has not already looked for ways to solve the problem, they will not buy yours. Intercom published 'Intercom on Jobs-to-be-Done' (2016) spreading the method to product teams. The evidence base is practitioner-empirical: extensive case studies and 2,300+ trained practitioners (per jobstobedone.org), but no randomized controlled trials exist. The 8-12 interview threshold is practitioner consensus, not a statistically derived sample-size calculation. The conversion improvement examples (12% to 21% for the SaaS case; 4x for Autobooks; 20% growth during a 60% downturn for the home builder) are sourced from published practitioner case studies, not peer-reviewed research.

**Canonical sources:** Bob Moesta and Greg Engle, 'Demand-Side Sales 101: Stop Selling and Help Your Customers Make Progress' (2020); Clayton Christensen, Taddy Hall, Karen Dillon, David Duncan, 'Competing Against Luck' (2016) -- contains the McDonald's milkshake canonical example; Bob Moesta and Chris Spiek, jobstobedone.org -- online course with 4 real recorded switch interviews and 15 published interview examples; Rob Fitzpatrick, 'The Mom Test' (2013) -- Chapter on 'talking about their life' and asking about current behavior and past attempts; Intercom, 'Intercom on Jobs-to-be-Done' (2016), free ebook -- popularized the method for product teams; Des Traynor and Bob Moesta, 'Interview with Bob Moesta on Jobs-to-be-Done' (Parts 1 and 2), intercom.com/blog; Lenny Rachitsky podcast, 'The ultimate guide to JTBD | Bob Moesta (co-creator of the framework)'

**Failure modes**
- Interviewing people who have NOT actually switched: recruiting current users who upgraded a tier, or people who are considering switching but have not acted, yields hypothetical data -- the same politeness bias as any opinion-based interview. The switch event must be real and completed.
- Chasing features instead of narrative: asking 'what features do you need?' instead of 'what happened the day you decided to change?' collapses the method into a standard survey. You get a wish list, not a causal story.
- Sampling only successful adopters (hirers): omitting churned customers and lost prospects removes the Anxiety and Habit signal entirely. The competitive set and switching costs are most visible in the interviews where the switch was hardest or failed.
- Using leading questions that manufacture Pull signal: 'Did our onboarding make it easier to switch?' primes positive attribution. The interview must be documentary, not evaluative.
- Skipping the Deciding beat: most interviewers jump from Active Looking to First Use, missing the alternatives actually considered and the moment of commitment. This hides what nearly stopped the deal.
- Premature synthesis before 8+ interviews: a single compelling story becomes a product roadmap when it should be a hypothesis. Patterns exist in the aggregate, not the individual.
- Conflating multiple switch types in one cohort: mixing 'switched from competitor A,' 'switched from spreadsheets,' and 'switched from doing nothing' obscures the force map because the jobs, triggers, and anxieties are structurally different.
- Ignoring Anxiety and Habit (F3 and F4): teams focus on Push (why the old solution was bad) and Pull (why theirs is good) because those are flattering. The forces that actually stall adoption are Anxiety and Habit, which are uncomfortable to surface and easy to skip.

**False-positive mechanism:** The switching interview's primary false positive is the socially accommodating retrospective narrative: a respondent who switched and stayed with the new product reconstructs their decision as more deliberate and rational than it was, attributing their choice to the product's genuine merits rather than to situational factors (a deadline, a teammate's recommendation, a promotional price). This makes switching appear less costly and the product's Pull appear stronger than they actually are. The mechanism is hindsight rationalization -- the switch already happened, so the respondent edits out the moments of doubt, the Anxiety that was nearly decisive, and the switching cost that was actually significant. This is compounded if the interviewer samples exclusively from happy customers: the cohort self-selects for completed, successful switches, which overrepresents cases where Push was very high and Anxiety was low, creating a distorted picture of the market. The practical consequence: founders emerge believing switching is easy and their competitive advantage is obvious, and then discover in the market that most people do not switch at all. Mitigation requires deliberate sampling of firers and lost prospects (who experienced the switch as hard or abandoned it) and rigorous focus on reconstructing documentary facts ('What email did you send? What did it cost? Who had to approve it?') rather than accepting summarized sentiment.


**Examples**
- McDonald's (with Bob Moesta and Clayton Christensen): McDonald's ran ethnographic JTBD research to understand milkshake sales. Rather than surveying customers about flavor preferences, researchers observed that 40% of milkshake purchases were made in the morning by lone commuters who ordered nothing else. Switch interviews revealed the real job: alleviating boredom during a long, boring drive with one hand on the wheel. The actual competitive set was not other fast-food beverages but bananas, bagels, donuts, and silence. The switch had no financial switching cost but a behavioral one: accepting a less convenient morning routine. Product interventions (thicker shake, thinner straw, fruit chunks for surprise) followed from the job, not from demographic data. This case is the canonical example in Christensen's 'Competing Against Luck' (2016).
- Intercom: Intercom used JTBD switch interviews in their product development process, published the methodology as a free ebook ('Intercom on Jobs-to-be-Done,' 2016), and built their feature prioritization around recurring job statements rather than feature requests. Des Traynor conducted live interviews with Bob Moesta to demonstrate the method. The key insight from their use of switch interviews was that customers' real alternatives were often not direct competitors but prior manual processes (email, spreadsheets, hand-coding customer messages), and that the switching cost was predominantly organizational inertia and retraining -- not technical migration. The interviews shaped Intercom's positioning around the job ('communicate with customers in context') rather than around feature parity with competitors.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is a cluster of 3 or more independent respondents describing: (a) an identical or structurally similar trigger event that broke their status quo, (b) the same set of alternatives evaluated (including non-obvious ones like spreadsheets or inertia), (c) a switching cost that was real but ultimately paid (quantified in hours, dollars, or approvals), and (d) an Anxiety that was nearly decisive but was resolved by a specific product attribute, message, or social proof. This pattern signals that Push is genuine and recurring (not situational), that the switching cost is surmountable at a known price, and that the job is shared across a market segment -- not just one person's idiosyncratic problem. Specifically: if respondents describe having actively sought alternative solutions before finding yours, and if they can name a concrete dollar or time cost they bore to switch, the problem severity and willingness-to-switch are real.
- Weak signal: A weak result presents as: (a) every respondent switched for a different trigger with no common thread, indicating the product is catching random spills from many different jobs rather than serving one well-defined job; (b) respondents cannot name what they were doing before ('I guess we weren't doing anything') -- signaling you are competing against non-consumption and the switching cost is near zero but so is the problem severity; (c) the dominant force across interviews is Habit + Anxiety, meaning Push and Pull exist but are not strong enough to reliably close switches without extraordinary effort; or (d) respondents frame their switching decision entirely in terms of price or features rather than a struggling moment, indicating the purchase was transactional and easily reversible -- a commoditized job with low retention implications. Rob Fitzpatrick's test applies: if the customer had not already sought an alternative solution before encountering yours, the problem is not urgent enough to build a business on.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [contested] 8-12 switch interviews is the practitioner-consensus minimum before drawing pattern conclusions (Bob Moesta / Rewired Group, cited in GoNoGo.team and thehuman2ai.com) -- The 8-12 range is broadly consistent with Moesta's public guidance (he recommends 10-12, sometimes capped at 10 or 12 per round), but the specific framing 'one interview gives you a hypothesis, two a coincidence, real JTBD insights emerge across 8-12 switch interviews' originates from GoNoGo.team's own content (published May 2026) and is not directly attributed to Moesta or a Rewired Group publication. The Rewired Group's own sprint format is built around 10 interviews. Moesta has also stated 'if you conduct over ten interviews, the intent gets lost.' The 8-12 lower bound is a reasonable practitioner consensus but the sourcing to Moesta/Rewired Group specifically is indirect. (src: GoNoGo.team JTBD guide (https://gonogo.team/jobs-to-be-done); Bob Moesta LinkedIn post (https://www.linkedin.com/posts/bobmoesta_jtbd-interviews-how-many-do-i-need-to-get-activity-7102689580667080704-JtOT); Lenny's Podcast notes (https://podpulse.ai/podcast-notes-and-takeaways/lennys-podcast-product-growth-career-the-ultimate-guide-to-jtbd-bob-moesta-co-creator-of-the-framework))
- [confirmed] Participants must have switched within the past 30-90 days to ensure recall accuracy (Rewired Group recruitment standard) -- The 30-90 day recency window is corroborated by multiple independent practitioner sources. CustomerScience.com.au explicitly states 'anchor on an actual switch in the last 30 to 90 days to increase recall accuracy.' Multiple JTBD guides attribute the within-90-days standard to the Rewired Group. The thehuman2ai.com guide also repeats this window. The broader qualitative research literature supports recency as a recall-accuracy driver. The window is widely accepted as Rewired Group practice. (src: CustomerScience.com.au switch interview playbook (https://customerscience.com.au/customer-experience-2/a-practical-playbook-for-switch-interviews/); thehuman2ai.com JTBD guide (https://thehuman2ai.com/research/guides/jtbd-switch-interview))
- [contested] Sessions run 30-45 minutes each, at a pace of 2-3 per day maximum per facilitator -- The 30-45 minute duration and 2-3 per day pace appear on thehuman2ai.com but conflict with the Rewired Group's own documented standard and multiple independent JTBD sources, which consistently cite 60-90 minutes per session. The Rewired Group's coaching program and practitioner guides note '60-90 minutes per participant,' and running 10-20 interviews at that pace requires 15-30 hours total. The 30-45 minute claim appears to be thehuman2ai.com's own compressed estimate, not a verified Rewired Group or community standard. (src: thehuman2ai.com JTBD guide (https://thehuman2ai.com/research/guides/jtbd-switch-interview); Rewired Group coaching program (https://therewiredgroup.com/online-programs/jtbd-coaching-program/); jobstobedone.substack.com (https://jobstobedone.substack.com/p/are-jobs-to-be-done-interviews-relevant))
- [contested] Full switch interview project runs 2-4 weeks (3-7 days recruitment, 5-8 days interviews, 3-5 days analysis) -- These specific sub-ranges (3-7 days recruitment, 5-8 days interviews, 3-5 days analysis) appear only on thehuman2ai.com with no independent corroboration found. The Rewired Group's sprint format is described as week-long intensive processes, not a 2-4 week project. The overall 2-4 week window is plausible for qualitative research of this scope, but the specific phase breakdowns are unverified outside a single source with no cited primary attribution. (src: thehuman2ai.com JTBD guide (https://thehuman2ai.com/research/guides/jtbd-switch-interview); Rewired Group sprint format (https://therewiredgroup.com/online-programs/jobs-to-be-done-interviews/))
- [contested] An anonymized project management SaaS improved trial-to-paid conversion from 12% to 21% (+75% relative) after redesigning onboarding based on switch interview findings (thehuman2ai.com case study) -- This case study appears exclusively on thehuman2ai.com as an anonymized example with no independent corroboration. The thehuman2ai.com guide itself confirms these numbers (12% to 21%, 'in one quarter, without a single new integration') but provides no external citation or attribution. While the conversion numbers are plausible for the SaaS industry (B2B SaaS median trial-to-paid conversion is 18-25%), the specific claim cannot be verified independently. Anonymized case studies with no traceable primary source are unverifiable by definition. (src: thehuman2ai.com JTBD guide (https://thehuman2ai.com/research/guides/jtbd-switch-interview); no independent corroboration found)
- [confirmed] A home builder using JTBD reframing achieved 20% sales growth during a market downturn of 60% (Bob Moesta, Intercom interview) -- Confirmed directly from the Intercom Part 2 interview with Bob Moesta. The article contains his exact words: 'when the market was on its way down by about 60% we were on the way up by about 20%.' This is a self-reported anecdote by Moesta, not an independently audited statistic, but the claim accurately reflects what he stated in the named source. (src: Intercom blog, Interview with Bob Moesta Part 2 (https://www.intercom.com/blog/interview-bob-moesta-part-2-of-2/))
- [contested] Autobooks achieved 4x improvement in conversion by aligning sales approach to buying-timeline stage (Bob Moesta, Demand Side Sales 101) -- The 4x conversion claim for Autobooks is corroborated by Lenny's Podcast notes referencing Bob Moesta (at the 14:46 mark of the episode). Multiple secondary sources cite the Autobooks case as a 4x conversion improvement through buying-timeline stage alignment. However, the claim that this appears in the book 'Demand Side Sales 101' specifically could not be confirmed: book summaries (Wise Words, Sales Babble) do not contain the 4x figure, and only the Lenny's Podcast appearance is the traceable source. The Autobooks VP of Marketing is quoted in the book but without the 4x metric. The claim is likely real but the book citation is contested. (src: Lenny's Podcast / PodPulse notes (https://podpulse.ai/podcast-notes-and-takeaways/lennys-podcast-product-growth-career-the-ultimate-guide-to-jtbd-bob-moesta-co-creator-of-the-framework); Wise Words book summary (https://wisewords.blog/book-summaries/demand-side-sales-101-book-summary/))
- [confirmed] 40% of McDonald's milkshake purchases were made in the morning by lone commuters -- the observation that grounded the JTBD milkshake case study (Christensen, Competing Against Luck) -- The 40% figure is confirmed by the HBS Working Knowledge article (Carmen Nobel, 2011), which is one of the most authoritative secondary sources for this case: 'He discovered that 40 percent of the milkshakes were purchased first thing in the morning, by commuters who ordered them to go.' The figure is consistently cited across multiple independent sources. One divergent source (likely an earlier Christensen lecture) uses 'about half,' but the 40% figure is the dominant and most-cited version and is attributed to the book/case study. (src: HBS Working Knowledge (https://www.library.hbs.edu/working-knowledge/clay-christensens-milkshake-marketing); therewiredgroup.com case study (https://therewiredgroup.com/case-studies/milkshakes/))
- [confirmed] Sean Ellis PMF benchmark: 40% of users responding 'very disappointed' if the product disappeared is the threshold for product-market fit (Sean Ellis, widely cited) -- Widely and independently confirmed. The 40% threshold originates from Sean Ellis's 2010 blog post on startup-marketing.com and was derived from his consulting work with roughly 100 startups. It is documented in his book 'Hacking Growth' and has been extensively validated by practitioners including Rahul Vohra (Superhuman) and many others. The threshold is a practitioner standard, not a peer-reviewed benchmark, and Ellis himself has noted some degree of arbitrariness, but its status as a widely-accepted industry standard is unambiguous. (src: Sean Ellis, startup-marketing.com (2010); FitSignal guide (https://www.fitsignal.com/blog/sean-ellis-40-percent-test); Zonka Feedback (https://www.zonkafeedback.com/blog/product-market-fit-survey))
- [confirmed] Superhuman's initial PMF score was 22% before segmentation, below the 40% Ellis threshold (Rahul Vohra, First Round Review essay, 2019) -- Confirmed directly from the First Round Review article. The exact text states: 'With only 22% opting for the "very disappointed" answer, it was clear that Superhuman had not reached product-market fit.' The article is authored by Rahul Vohra and published on First Round Review. Multiple independent summaries and the SaaSClub podcast episode ('From 22% to 58%') corroborate the figure. (src: First Round Review, Rahul Vohra (https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/); SaaSClub podcast (https://saasclub.io/podcast/rahul-vohra-superhuman-342/))
- [confirmed] jobstobedone.org reports 2,300+ practitioners trained in the switch interview methodology -- Confirmed directly from the jobstobedone.org homepage, which displays '2,300+ practitioners trained' as a site metric in the hero section alongside '57+ articles & episodes' and '15 real recorded interviews.' This is the site's own self-reported figure, not independently audited, but the claim accurately reflects what the site states. (src: jobstobedone.org homepage (https://jobstobedone.org/))
- [contested] Qualitative saturation literature broadly cites 5-50 interviews as the range, with 8-12 sufficient for homogeneous samples (Dworkin 2012, cited in IntoTheMinds.com) -- Dworkin (2012) does cite 5-50 as the range 'most authors suggest,' and the IntoTheMinds.com article correctly references this. However, Dworkin's own recommendation for homogeneous grounded theory studies is 6-12 (not 8-12). The 8-12 lower bound is more closely associated with Guest et al. (2006), which found 80% of themes emerged by interview 12. The IntoTheMinds.com citation of Dworkin is accurate for the 5-50 range but the specific 8-12 figure for homogeneous samples is an overstatement of Dworkin's exact recommendation; it conflates Dworkin with other saturation literature. (src: Dworkin 2012 (https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10508-012-0016-6); IntoTheMinds.com (https://www.intotheminds.com/en/sample-size-qualitative-research); ResearchGate (https://www.researchgate.net/publication/230832973_Sample_Size_Policy_for_Qualitative_Studies_Using_In-Depth_Interviews))

### 6. Past-spend probe (what they already paid to solve it)

- **Category:** Problem & demand discovery
- **Stage:** problem
- **Definition:** The past-spend probe is a customer discovery technique that asks interviewees to recount what they have already paid -- in money, time, or reputational risk -- to address a problem, rather than asking what they would hypothetically pay for a future solution. It is grounded in the behavioral principle, articulated by Rob Fitzpatrick in The Mom Test, that past actions are factual and non-distortable, while future-tense promises are unreliable social-desirability artifacts. By mapping existing expenditure on workarounds, incumbent tools, consultants, and manual labor, a founder identifies whether a problem is severe enough to generate real buying behavior before a product exists.
- **Validates:** Does this problem already cost our target customer enough -- in money, time, or effort -- that they have paid to address it, even imperfectly? (Problem severity and proxy demand, not solution preference.)

**Protocol**
1. RECRUIT: Identify 15-30 people who credibly have the problem -- not friends or family. For B2B, target specific roles (e.g., RevOps leads at 50-200 person SaaS companies). Aim for at least 30 for baseline pattern recognition (Forum Ventures benchmark).
2. OPEN WITH PAST CONTEXT: Begin every interview by asking the subject to narrate a specific recent instance of the problem: 'Walk me through the last time you dealt with X. What triggered it, what did you do, and how did it resolve?' Never introduce your product.
3. ANCHOR TO EXISTING SPEND: Ask directly about current resource allocation: 'How much time does your team spend on this per week?', 'What tools or services do you pay for to handle this today?', 'How much do those cost you per month?', 'Have you ever hired someone -- a consultant, a contractor, a full-time hire -- specifically to address this?' These are factual, non-threatening questions that surface the true cost envelope.
4. PROBE WORKAROUNDS AND FAILED ATTEMPTS: Ask 'What have you already tried to solve this, and why did those approaches fall short?' Fitzpatrick's rule of thumb: if they have never searched for a solution, they are unlikely to buy yours. Active workaround spending is a stronger signal than stated interest.
5. SCORE SEVERITY: Quantify the cost they bear: 'If this problem vanished tomorrow, how much time or money would that free up?' and 'What is the cost to your business if it stays unsolved?' (Running Lean SPIN-adjacent: Implication question.) Responses over $X/month or Y hours/week map your willingness-to-pay ceiling without asking a hypothetical.
6. WATCH FOR COMMITMENT OFFERS: If the subject volunteers to introduce you to colleagues, offers a referral, asks about pricing or timeline, or offers to pay for a pilot, treat these as the strongest confirmation signals. Fitzpatrick categorizes commitments as time, reputation, or financial; strong signals combine multiple categories.
7. PATTERN-MATCH ACROSS INTERVIEWS: After each batch of 5-10 interviews, tabulate: (a) what incumbents they pay and at what price, (b) total hours/cost they absorb per week, (c) how many mentioned the problem unprompted vs. only after prompting. Problems that surface unprompted across multiple interviews and already attract spending are real.
8. SUPPRESS FALSE POSITIVES: Discard any data where the subject used future-tense language ('I would', 'I might', 'I could') without anchoring it to a concrete past episode. Per Fitzpatrick, these fall into the 'fluff' category: generic claims, future-tense promises, and hypothetical maybes are not evidence.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Minimum interviews before pattern judgment: 30 companies (B2B) (src: Forum Ventures Idea Validation Playbook (forumvc.com/playbooks/forum-ventures-playbook-idea-validation))
- Minimum interviews before pattern judgment (lean floor): 5-10 interviews to surface initial patterns; escalate to 30 for conviction (src: Forum Ventures / Running Lean, Ash Maurya, 2nd ed.)
- Skin-in-the-game caliper: email capture signal threshold (Savoia): If fewer than 3 of 1,000 people shown a concept provide a valid email, demand is likely insufficient (src: Alberto Savoia, The Right It / Pretotyping (fourweekmba.com/pretotyping-alberto-savoia))
- Top cause of startup failure attributed to no market need: 42% of post-mortems cite lack of market fit (CB Insights analysis cited across multiple sources) (src: CB Insights failure analysis, cited in customer-validation-subscription-app (revenuecat.com) and B2B discovery guides)
- B2B revenue operations time-cost benchmark (example baseline): RevOps leaders at mid-stage B2B SaaS spend 8-12 hours/week manually preparing reports (illustrative ICP cost anchor) (src: Forum Ventures Idea Validation Playbook)

**Origin and evidence:** Rob Fitzpatrick originated and systematized this technique's articulation in The Mom Test (2013), distilling it from his own failed and successful startups plus the broader customer development movement founded by Steve Blank and Eric Ries. The core behavioral principle -- past actions predict future behavior better than stated intentions -- is well-established in behavioral economics (Kahneman, Thaler) but Fitzpatrick translated it into a founder-executable interview discipline. Ash Maurya reinforced it in Running Lean's Problem Interview chapter (2012), structuring it around the Lean Canvas 'Existing Alternatives' box. Alberto Savoia contributed the 'Skin in the Game Caliper' framing from the pretotyping tradition (2011 Google internal, published as The Right It in 2019). The evidence base is practitioner-experiential and case-documented rather than randomized controlled; no peer-reviewed study isolates past-spend probing as an independent variable against startup outcomes.

**Canonical sources:** Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) -- primary canonical source; Ash Maurya, Running Lean, 2nd Edition (2012) -- Problem Interview chapter, Existing Alternatives block; Alberto Savoia, The Right It (2019) / Pretotyping manifesto -- Skin in the Game Caliper; Steve Blank, The Four Steps to the Epiphany (2005) -- customer discovery foundation; Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011) -- validated learning framing; Forum Ventures, Idea Validation Playbook (2024) -- B2B quantitative thresholds; Lean B2B by Etienne Garbugli -- B2B-specific question bank

**Failure modes**
- Interviewing non-buyers: talking to people who experience the problem but have never been in a position to purchase a solution (e.g., individual contributors without budget authority), which makes their historical spending data irrelevant to your actual sales motion.
- Anchoring on too-narrow spend categories: asking only about direct SaaS tool costs and missing indirect spend (consultant fees, internal headcount, manual labor hours, opportunity cost) that may dwarf tool costs and represent the real budget available.
- Conflating workaround spend with willingness to pay for your solution: a customer paying a $5,000/month consultant to handle a problem does not mean they will pay $5,000/month for software -- the comparison is category-level signal, not price-point confirmation.
- Ignoring the absence of spend as a signal: if interviewees report zero current spending and no attempted workarounds, this can mean (a) the problem is not painful enough to act on, or (b) the interviewee is not the right segment -- founders often over-interpret 'I would love that' and under-weight the silence of no existing spend.
- Interview sample size too small: acting on 3-5 interviews when the pattern is only legible at 15-30; idiosyncratic individual budgets mislead.
- Confirmation-seeking questions: asking 'Don't you find X expensive?' rather than 'What do you pay for X today?' leads the witness and inflates the perceived cost signal.

**False-positive mechanism:** The most dangerous false positive occurs when interviewees describe high historical spending on a superficially similar but categorically different problem. For example, a legal team that spent $200,000 on document review software may report that spend when asked about AI verification tools -- making the founder believe there is budget and precedent for their product -- when in fact the $200K went to a different workflow (e-discovery, not contract review) with a different buyer (IT/legal ops, not general counsel) and a different procurement cycle. The past-spend probe confirms that money has moved in a problem neighborhood; it does not confirm that your specific solution fits the specific workflow, buyer, or budget line. A second false positive path: the spend was episodic or one-time (a consultant engaged for a compliance crisis) rather than recurring, misrepresenting the addressable annual budget.

**Examples**
- Airbnb: Brian Chesky and Joe Gebbia went to New York in person in 2008 and interviewed early hosts. Rather than asking whether hosts would theoretically list their space, they engaged with hosts who were already listing and collected a binder of unsolicited suggestions from one host. The founders also acted as their own photographers, embedding directly in existing host workflows to observe what people were already doing and paying (lost revenue from unused space) to solve the accommodation arbitrage problem. The hosts were already absorbing the cost of empty rooms -- a clear past-spend signal.
- Dropbox: Drew Houston, frustrated by existing solutions (USB drives, email attachments) and already paying in time and friction, built a demo video rather than a product. The 75,000 waitlist sign-ups within 24 hours (against an expected 15,000) validated that a large population was already paying in time-cost to manage file sync across devices -- they had tried and failed with incumbents -- before a line of production code was written.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result: the interviewee spontaneously names what they currently pay for an imperfect solution (a tool, a consultant, internal headcount), can articulate the monthly or annual cost without prompting, has actively sought and tried multiple alternatives, and the problem surfaces unprompted across at least 5-7 independent interviews in the same ICP segment. Bonus confirmation: the interviewee asks about your pricing, timeline, or requests an introduction to their procurement contact during the interview -- Fitzpatrick's 'commitment signals' combining time, reputation, and financial stakes.
- Weak signal: A weak result: interviewees acknowledge the problem only when you describe it, report no existing spend ('we just live with it' or 'we handle it manually but it doesn't cost much'), use exclusively future-tense language ('I would probably pay...'), or the problem only surfaces in 1-2 interviews out of 15. Per Fitzpatrick, if they have not looked for a solution already, they are unlikely to buy yours. Low or zero historical spend combined with absence of active workarounds is strong evidence the problem is real but not painful enough to be a business.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] 75,000 waitlist sign-ups for Dropbox within 24 hours of a demo video, against an expectation of 15,000 (TechCrunch / Drew Houston interview) -- The TechCrunch Founder Stories interview with Drew Houston directly corroborates 75,000 overnight signups against a 15,000 expectation. One nuance: the baseline waitlist before the video was ~5,000 (not zero), and the video in question was posted on Digg during the private beta -- not the original YC application video. The core numbers and source attribution are accurate. (src: https://techcrunch.com/2011/11/01/founder-storie-how-dropbox-got-its-first-10-million-users/)
- [contested] 42% of startup post-mortems cite no market need as the primary cause of failure (CB Insights, cited across multiple validation guides) -- The statistic traces to a real CB Insights report on approximately 101-110 startup post-mortems, so it is not fabricated. However, the methodology is self-selected and self-reported (founders writing their own failure essays), the sample is small and non-random, and an independent academic re-analysis of the same CB Insights data found a materially different distribution of failure factors. The figure circulates heavily in secondary sources, often stripped of these caveats. It is a real number from a real source, but its epistemic weight is lower than the citation pattern implies. (src: https://preuve.ai/blog/why-startups-fail-market-fit)
- [confirmed] Minimum 30 companies interviewed for baseline B2B pattern recognition (Forum Ventures Idea Validation Playbook) -- A direct fetch of the Forum Ventures Idea Validation Playbook (forumvc.com/playbooks/forum-ventures-playbook-idea-validation) confirmed the exact language: 'Aim for at least 30 companies.' The claim and source are accurately cited. (src: https://www.forumvc.com/playbooks/forum-ventures-playbook-idea-validation)
- [confirmed] RevOps leaders at mid-stage B2B SaaS spend 8-12 hours per week manually preparing reports (Forum Ventures illustrative ICP cost anchor) -- A direct fetch of the Forum Ventures Idea Validation Playbook confirmed this exact figure appears in the playbook as an illustrative value hypothesis example. The claim accurately labels it an 'illustrative ICP cost anchor,' which is what the playbook presents it as -- not an independently sourced empirical benchmark. No independent industry study corroborating this specific 8-12 hour figure for report preparation was found; it is the playbook's own example hypothesis. (src: https://www.forumvc.com/playbooks/forum-ventures-playbook-idea-validation)
- [contested] Fewer than 3 email captures from 1,000 concept exposures signals insufficient demand (Alberto Savoia, Skin in the Game Caliper, The Right It) -- Savoia does use 3-from-1,000 as an illustrative example in public lectures and the FourWeekMBA write-up, and the Skin-in-the-Game Caliper concept is real. However, the book PDF excerpt does not contain this specific threshold as a defined calibrated rule, and the FourWeekMBA article frames it as a worked example ('it is probably going to be unlikely') rather than a formal decision threshold. The figure appears to be an illustration that has been retrospectively systematized into a hard rule in secondary sources. (src: https://fourweekmba.com/pretotyping-alberto-savoia/)
- [contested] 5-10 interviews to surface initial patterns; 30 for conviction (Running Lean / Forum Ventures convergent guidance) -- The 30-interview threshold is confirmed in the Forum Ventures playbook. The 5-10 figure is not found as an explicit prescription in Running Lean; Ash Maurya's own recent guidance is 10-20 well-targeted interviews, and the number 30 appears in his writing only as part of a cautionary '30-50 interview' example warning against false confidence. Packaging these as 'convergent guidance' from the same sources overstates the alignment: Forum Ventures names 30, Maurya's current position is 10-20, and the '5-10' floor is not directly traceable to either claimed source. (src: https://www.leanfoundry.com/lean-1-2-3/jul-24-2025)

**Citations**

- Sachin Rekhi: A Primer on Talking to Customers from The Mom Test: https://www.sachinrekhi.com/p/the-mom-test-rob-fitzpatrick

- Forum Ventures: Idea Validation and Customer Discovery B2B Founder Playbook: https://www.forumvc.com/playbooks/forum-ventures-playbook-idea-validation
