### 7. Clickable prototype / mockup usability walkthrough

- **Category:** Solution & concept
- **Stage:** solution
- **Definition:** A clickable prototype or mockup usability walkthrough is a structured research session in which target users attempt predefined tasks using an interactive but non-production simulation of a product (ranging from linked wireframes to high-fidelity Figma flows), while a researcher observes and records task completion, errors, time-on-task, and verbal/non-verbal signals. The session is distinct from a solution interview: it is behavioral observation, not opinion solicitation. Its purpose is to surface usability failures and interaction mismatches in a design before engineering investment is made.
- **Validates:** Does this specific solution design allow target users to accomplish their core jobs without confusion, excessive errors, or drop-off? (Solution-level usability question, not demand or willingness-to-pay.)

**Protocol**
1. Define hypotheses and tasks: Write 3-6 concrete, scenario-framed tasks (e.g., 'You need to book a hotel for next Friday - go ahead') that represent the product's primary use flows. Specify pass/fail criteria upfront (e.g., 'task completed in under 3 minutes without moderator help'). Do not describe how to succeed.
2. Build the minimum-fidelity prototype that can represent the flow: Low-fidelity (paper sketches, linked wireframes) for conceptual flow questions; mid-fidelity (Figma click-through) for navigation and IA questions; high-fidelity (realistic visuals + interactions) for pre-launch confidence testing. Use the lowest fidelity that can test the specific hypothesis.
3. Recruit 5-8 participants per distinct user group matching your target persona. Qualitative rounds require 5-8 users per segment to uncover the majority of salient usability problems (Nielsen-Landauer model). Add users if multiple distinct segments exist (3-4 per group). Run quantitative benchmarking sessions (measuring task success rates, time-on-task) only with 30+ participants.
4. Conduct the session with a think-aloud protocol: Brief participants in 2 minutes, then ask them to narrate thoughts while interacting. Moderator stays silent during tasks - no hints, no leading questions. Note the exact moment, action, and verbalization when confusion or failure occurs.
5. Measure and record: Task completion (binary pass/fail per task per participant), time on task, error count and type, first-click accuracy on key navigation elements, and post-task System Usability Scale (SUS) score. The SUS target for an acceptable product is above 68; above 80 is rated 'good' by Sauro and Lewis (2016, 'Quantifying the User Experience').
6. Debrief after all tasks (not during): Ask open-ended retrospective questions ('What was most confusing?', 'What would you expect to happen when you click X?'). Do not ask whether participants liked the product or would use it - these questions activate social desirability bias.
7. Analyze findings by severity: Categorize each identified problem by frequency (how many participants hit it) and impact (does it block task completion or just slow it). Prioritize blockers (>= 2 users failing the same task) for redesign before the next iteration.
8. Iterate and retest: Run 3 iterative rounds of 5 users each rather than one large study. After each round, fix the highest-severity blockers, update the prototype, and retest. This approach, recommended by Nielsen (2000), surfaces more problems per dollar than a single large-n study.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Usability problems discovered with 5 test users (qualitative, homogeneous group, L=0.31): ~85% (src: Nielsen, J. & Landauer, T.K. (1993). 'A mathematical model of the finding of usability problems.' ACM INTERCHI'93 Proceedings, pp. 206-213; republished as Nielsen (2000) nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/)
- Minimum users for quantitative usability benchmarking (task success rates with statistical reliability): 30-40+ participants (src: Nielsen, J. & Landauer, T.K. (1993); confirmed in Nielsen Norman Group, 'Why 5 Participants Are Okay in a Qualitative Study, but Not in a Quantitative One,' nngroup.com/articles/5-test-users-qual-quant/)
- System Usability Scale (SUS) threshold for 'acceptable' usability: Above 68 (scale 0-100) (src: Sauro, J. & Lewis, J.R. (2016). Quantifying the User Experience. Widely cited in UX literature as the SUS industry benchmark.)
- Problem discovery probability per individual user (L value) assumed in Nielsen-Landauer model: 31% per user (src: Nielsen, J. (2000). 'Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users.' nngroup.com)
- Users needed to find 85% of problems when L=20% (more usable interface): 9 users (src: Nielsen (2000), nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/)
- Users needed to find 85% of problems when L=10% (highly usable interface): 18 users (src: Nielsen (2000), nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/)
- Minimum groups of five that can find as few as 55% of problems (range in validation of the Nielsen model): Some groups of 5 found only 55% of problems vs. average 85% (src: nngroup.com analysis cited in UXArmy: 'Usability Testing: Are 5 participants enough?')
- Stated purchase intent vs. actual trial-to-purchase conversion in one MeasuringU documented case study: Over 90% stated intent to purchase; actual conversion no more than 10% (src: Jeff Sauro / MeasuringU, cited via measuringu.com/ut-bias/ (social desirability bias in usability studies))
- Iterative rounds recommended by Nielsen instead of one large study: 3 rounds of 5 users each (15 users total across rounds) (src: Nielsen (2000), nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/)
- Minimum solution interview participants before stopping (Ash Maurya): At least 10 people (src: Maurya, A. (2012). Running Lean, 2nd ed., Chapter 8 (O'Reilly). oreilly.com/library/view/running-lean-2nd/9781449321529/ch08.html)

**Origin and evidence:** The usability walkthrough method was formalized and popularized through two independent traditions that now overlap in startup practice. The scientific basis comes from Jakob Nielsen and Rolf Molich, who codified usability heuristics in 1990 and from Nielsen and Thomas K. Landauer, whose 1993 INTERCHI study ('A mathematical model of the finding of usability problems') provided the mathematical basis for the 5-user recommendation. Nielsen's 2000 essay at nngroup.com brought this into mainstream product practice. The startup-specific application of prototype demos was systematized by Ash Maurya in Running Lean (2012), which embedded the solution interview/demo walkthrough into the Lean Canvas cycle as the bridge between problem validation and MVP. IDEO's design thinking tradition (David Kelley, Tim Brown) contributed the culture of early, cheap, iterative prototyping. Evidence quality: the Nielsen-Landauer model is peer-reviewed but based on 1990s project data with a variable L parameter; the '85% with 5 users' result is a mean that varies significantly by interface usability. The MeasuringU false-positive evidence (stated vs. actual conversion) is practitioner-documented, not RCT-level. No large-scale RCT exists comparing prototype validation outcomes to cold-launch outcomes.

**Canonical sources:** Nielsen, J. & Landauer, T.K. (1993). 'A mathematical model of the finding of usability problems.' ACM INTERCHI'93 Conference Proceedings, pp. 206-213.; Nielsen, J. (2000). 'Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users.' Nielsen Norman Group. nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/; Nielsen, J. (2012). 'Why 5 Participants Are Okay in a Qualitative Study, but Not in a Quantitative One.' nngroup.com; Maurya, A. (2012). Running Lean: Iterate from Plan A to a Plan That Works, 2nd ed. O'Reilly. Chapter 8: The Solution Interview.; Ries, E. (2011). The Lean Startup. Crown Business. (Build-Measure-Learn cycle; MVP as validation instrument.); Sauro, J. & Lewis, J.R. (2016). Quantifying the User Experience: Practical Statistics for User Research, 2nd ed. (SUS benchmarks.); Brown, T. (2008). 'Design Thinking.' Harvard Business Review. (IDEO prototype philosophy.)

**Failure modes**
- Social desirability / courtesy bias: participants narrate positive reactions and avoid criticizing the prototype to avoid disappointing the founder-moderator. They say 'I'd definitely use this' while behaviorally failing the task. This is the dominant false-positive mechanism.
- Leading task framing: tasks that telegraph the answer ('Click the big blue button to checkout') eliminate the usability failure the test is designed to surface.
- Wrong fidelity for the question: testing visual polish on a low-fidelity paper prototype or testing conceptual flow on a high-fidelity prototype optimized for one path both produce misleading results.
- Non-representative participants: recruiting from the founder's personal network (friends, colleagues, family) systematically selects people with higher motivation to please and higher tolerance for rough interfaces.
- Prototype-specific learning: in sequential-task sessions, early task completion teaches users the prototype's navigation logic, inflating success rates on later tasks.
- Founder presence in the room: even silent moderators who are also the product creator trigger social desirability. Third-party or remote-unmoderated sessions reduce this.
- Confusing behavioral success with demand: a user completing a task in a prototype does not validate willingness to pay, adoption intent, or switching from a current alternative.
- Over-indexing on verbal feedback over observed behavior: what users say they found easy often contradicts where they paused, backtracked, or misclicked.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false-positive mechanism is the gap between stated positive intent and actual behavior caused by social desirability bias. In a documented MeasuringU case, over 90% of users in a usability session stated they would purchase the product from a trial, while actual trial-to-purchase conversion in the live product was no more than 10%. This gap arises because prototype sessions are socially evaluative contexts: participants implicitly understand they are being watched by the product's creators, activating courtesy norms. A secondary mechanism is prototype completion bias: users in a controlled session follow the prototype's scripted flows with more patience and attention than they would exercise as actual users encountering the product cold, unprompted, and in competition with other tools. Both effects inflate perceived usability and intent signals.

**Examples**
- Airbnb (Experiences feature): To validate the redesigned Experiences booking flow, the design team printed paper prototypes and ran guerrilla usability tests with 7 users tasked with finding and booking an experience. Key finding: 100% of users seeking a specific experience in a new city intuitively looked for a search bar that was absent in the prototype - a navigation failure caught pre-engineering. A secondary finding was that a '2 steps left' indicator was not perceived as a clickable button by participants. Both were fixed before development. Source: freecodecamp.org/news/airbnb-experiences-guerrilla-usability-test-on-ios-app-95c2b5186970/
- Dropbox: Before writing the full sync client, Drew Houston validated the core value proposition using a video walkthrough (a non-clickable analog to a prototype demo) showing the product working. Sign-up interest on the waiting list jumped from ~5,000 to ~75,000 overnight. This is a canonical lean startup example of using a simulation artifact (video instead of clickable prototype) to test solution resonance before any engineering - demonstrating the broader principle. Source: Ries, E. (2011). The Lean Startup, and widely cited in lean startup literature.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is when: (a) >= 80% of participants complete the primary task scenario without moderator assistance and without critical navigation errors, (b) failures that do occur cluster on non-core flows rather than the primary value pathway, (c) observed behavior (clicks, pauses, backtracking) is consistent with verbal narration (no positive-talk/failing-behavior split), and (d) errors and confusion points converge across participants (3+ users hitting the same failure indicates a real design issue, not individual noise). A SUS score above 80 in the post-session questionnaire provides additional quantitative support. Iterative rounds where each round surfaces fewer and less severe new problems indicates a design converging on usable.
- Weak signal: A weak or ambiguous result presents as: high verbal positivity ('this is great!', 'I love this') paired with visible struggle - backtracking, wrong-path clicks, or task abandonment. Task completion rates that are high overall but mask a single catastrophic failure on the primary conversion flow (e.g., signup or core action). Feedback that is uniformly positive without a single concrete criticism - this is a social desirability signal, not genuine usability approval. Post-session SUS scores below 68. Any session where participants were recruited from the founder's personal network, were compensated in ways that create reciprocal obligation, or where the founder moderated without a third party reviewing raw recordings. A 'positive' prototype session that cannot be converted into a specific list of identified usability problems is most likely a courtesy conversation, not a usability test.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] 5 users uncover approximately 85% of usability problems in a qualitative study, per the Nielsen-Landauer (1993) mathematical model assuming L=31%. -- The Nielsen-Landauer formula N(1-(1-L)^n) with L=0.31 and n=5 yields approximately 85%. This is documented in the original 1993 ACM INTERCHI paper and reaffirmed in Nielsen's 2000 NNg article. The L=31% figure was derived from an average across 11 projects studied. Multiple independent sources corroborate this. (src: https://www.nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/)
- [confirmed] The 31% L-value is an average across projects studied by Nielsen and Landauer in the 1990s and is not universal; L=20% requires 9 users, L=10% requires 18 users to reach 85% problem discovery. -- The NNg article explicitly states L=31% is an average across projects and that it varies. Applying the formula: at L=0.20, solving 0.85 = 1-(0.80)^n gives n~8.5, rounded to 9; at L=0.10, n~18. These numbers are quoted directly in the NNg article and secondary sources. (src: https://www.nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/)
- [confirmed] Some random groups of 5 users found as few as 55% of problems in empirical validation of the Nielsen model; no group of 20 found fewer than 95%. -- Laura Faulkner's study ('Beyond the 5-user assumption,' Behavior Research Methods, 2003) sampled groups from 60 participants. The range for groups of 5 was 55-100% (average 85%), and no group of 20 found fewer than 95%. This is quoted verbatim in multiple secondary sources and the original study is documented on ResearchGate. (src: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/200553185_Why_and_when_five_test_users_aren't_enough)
- [confirmed] Quantitative usability benchmarking (task success rates intended to predict population behavior) requires 30-40+ participants per NN/g. -- The NNg article 'Why 5 Participants Are Okay in a Qualitative Study, but Not in a Quantitative One' states that in their quantitative UX classes they 'usually recommend more than 30' participants. The 30+ figure is directly sourced; the '40+' upper bound is a common extension but NNg's own language says '30+'. Confirmed at the 30 threshold; the '40+' qualifier is a reasonable extension but not precisely stated by NNg. (src: https://www.nngroup.com/articles/5-test-users-qual-quant/)
- [contested] The SUS score of 68 is the industry threshold for 'acceptable' usability; above 80 is rated 'good' (Sauro & Lewis, 2016). -- SUS=68 as the 'acceptable' threshold is confirmed: it represents the population median and Sauro & Lewis treat it as the floor for above-average. However, attributing 'good' to a score above 80 via Sauro & Lewis 2016 is inaccurate. On the Sauro-Lewis curved grading scale, 80 is an 'A-' (excellent), not 'good.' On the Bangor et al. (2009) adjective scale (a separate framework), 73-84 maps to 'Excellent' and 63-72 maps to 'Good.' The claim conflates two different frameworks and mislabels the Sauro-Lewis category for 80+. (src: https://uxpajournal.org/item-benchmarks-system-usability-scale-sus/)
- [confirmed] In a MeasuringU-documented case, over 90% of participants stated purchase intent in a usability session while actual trial-to-purchase conversion in the live product was no more than 10%. -- The MeasuringU article '9 Biases in Usability Testing' states: 'Over 90% of our users said they would [purchase], yet data from the company shows no more than 10% actually convert from trial-to-purchase in real life.' The case is anonymous with no methodology details or sample size, so it cannot be independently replicated, but the numbers as stated match the claim exactly. (src: https://measuringu.com/ut-bias/)
- [confirmed] Airbnb's Experiences prototype guerrilla test used 7 participants; 100% of users seeking a specific experience looked for a search bar that was absent. -- The freeCodeCamp/Medium article 'Guerrilla Usability Testing the New Experiences Feature on Airbnb's iOS App' confirms 7 participants were tested and states '100% of users looking for a specific experience in a new city intuitively looked for a search bar to type into.' The study was a community case study, not an official Airbnb publication, but the numbers match the claim precisely. (src: https://www.freecodecamp.org/news/airbnb-experiences-guerrilla-usability-test-on-ios-app-95c2b5186970/)
- [contested] Ash Maurya's Running Lean specifies a minimum of 10 solution interview participants before drawing conclusions. -- The O'Reilly preview of Chapter 8 does not surface a specific '10 minimum' rule for solution interviews. Secondary summaries of the book variously cite 10-15 face-to-face interviews or 8-12 interviews before identifying patterns, without specifying that '10' is an explicit minimum threshold for solution interviews alone. The exact figure cannot be independently verified from accessible text and may be a summarizer's paraphrase rather than a verbatim recommendation. (src: https://www.oreilly.com/library/view/running-lean-2nd/9781449321529/ch08.html)
- [confirmed] Dropbox's video prototype (non-clickable) drove waiting list growth from approximately 5,000 to 75,000 sign-ups overnight. -- Multiple independent sources including Drew Houston's own account confirm the waitlist went from approximately 5,000 to 75,000 overnight after the demo video was posted to Digg and Hacker News in late 2007/early 2008. The video showed a working-looking product that was not yet fully functional. The numbers are consistent across sources and attributed directly to Houston. (src: https://mmtm.io/articles/dropbox-go-to-market-story/)
- [confirmed] Three iterative rounds of 5 users each (15 total) are recommended by Nielsen over one 15-user study to catch more problems per total investment. -- Nielsen's 2000 NNg article explicitly states: 'Spend this budget on 3 studies with 5 users each' when discussing 15 total participants. The rationale is that iterating between rounds uncovers deeper problems that only become visible after earlier surface issues are fixed. This recommendation is quoted directly and corroborated across multiple secondary sources. (src: https://www.nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/)

**Citations**
- Nielsen (2000) - Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users (NN/g): https://www.nngroup.com/articles/why-you-only-need-to-test-with-5-users/
- Nielsen Norman Group - 5 Participants: Qualitative vs. Quantitative: https://www.nngroup.com/articles/5-test-users-qual-quant/
- Maurya (2012) - Running Lean Chapter 8: The Solution Interview (O'Reilly): https://www.oreilly.com/library/view/running-lean-2nd/9781449321529/ch08.html
- MeasuringU - 9 Biases in Usability Testing: https://measuringu.com/ut-bias/
- Dovetail - Ultimate Prototype Usability Testing Guide: https://dovetail.com/ux/prototype-usability-testing-guide/
- Maze - Prototype Testing Step-by-Step Guide: https://maze.co/guides/prototype-testing/
- FreeCodeCamp - Airbnb Experiences Guerrilla Usability Test on iOS App: https://www.freecodecamp.org/news/airbnb-experiences-guerrilla-usability-test-on-ios-app-95c2b5186970/
- Maze - 11 Types of UX Cognitive Biases (including social desirability): https://maze.co/guides/ux-cognitive-biases/types/
- Lyssna - Usability Metrics: https://www.lyssna.com/blog/usability-metrics/
- UXPin - 5 Methods for Testing Interactive Prototypes: https://www.uxpin.com/studio/blog/5-methods-for-testing-interactive-prototypes/

### 8. Wizard of Oz MVP

- **Category:** Solution & concept
- **Stage:** solution
- **Definition:** A Wizard of Oz MVP is a validation experiment in which users interact with what they believe is an autonomous, fully-functional product, but the backend is actually operated manually by a human "wizard" hidden from the user. Unlike the Concierge MVP, users are not told a person is fulfilling requests; they believe the system is real. The method tests whether a proposed solution design drives genuine behavioral commitment before any complex automation is engineered.
- **Validates:** Does our proposed solution design produce genuine user behavior (payment, return visit, referral) when users believe they are interacting with a real automated system?

**Protocol**
1. State a single falsifiable hypothesis about the solution: specify the exact behavior (purchase, repeat use, referral) that would constitute confirmation.
2. Identify which product elements are real, faked, or stubbed. Build a polished front-end interface that masks all manual operation; visual fidelity must be high enough that users perceive automation.
3. Design an operator console and playbook: define response tone, response templates, escalation rules, and a latency budget so the wizard's replies feel system-generated rather than human-typed.
4. Select and prepare the wizard: choose someone familiar with the domain and the playbook; run a pilot session internally before going live.
5. Recruit 10-30 users from the precise target segment (not friends, colleagues, or anyone who knows the wizard); run the experiment live for 1-3 weeks during actual usage hours.
6. Measure behavioral outcomes exclusively: payments made, return visits, referrals given. Track operator override rate (the fraction of wizard responses that diverged materially from any automation baseline) to gauge future engineering complexity.
7. Analyze the override log to identify which tasks required heavy human judgment versus rote execution; this map directly predicts automation difficulty.
8. Debrief participants after the test window closes to preserve trust; disclose the manual operation and explain the purpose.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Operator approval/override rate indicating automation viability: 90% wizard approval rate = off-the-shelf automation likely sufficient; 30% approval rate = most work is human judgment, significant engineering complexity ahead (src: Learning Loop – Wizard of Oz Experiment guide (learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz))
- Recommended participant sample size: 10-30 real users from target market (src: Learning Loop – Wizard of Oz Experiment guide (learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz))
- Recommended test duration: 1-3 weeks of live operation (src: Learning Loop – Wizard of Oz Experiment guide (learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz))
- Typical tooling cost (excluding operator labor): Approximately $100 in tooling (Retool, Airtable, LLM API calls) (src: Learning Loop – Wizard of Oz Experiment guide (learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz))
- General MVP experiment run duration (broader guideline): 2-6 weeks, or until enough data to make a decision (src: Rabit Solutions / parallel synthesis from multiple MVP practitioner sources)
- Evidence strength score (on Learning Loop's internal validation scale): 90 out of 100 (src: Learning Loop – Wizard of Oz Experiment guide (learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz))

**Origin and evidence:** The method was first formally documented by Don Norman and Allen Munro in 1973 for testing an airport computer-terminal travel assistant. Jeff Kelley coined the term "Wizard of Oz" in his 1983 Johns Hopkins dissertation on natural-language interfaces. Alberto Savoia at Google independently named an equivalent technique the "Mechanical Turk" as a core pretotyping pattern in his work codified in "The Right It" (2019). Eric Ries incorporated the concept into Lean Startup thinking without naming it separately; the startup-specific framing was popularized by Y Combinator (Jared Friedman) and practitioners such as the authors of The Real Startup Book (Kromatic). The evidence base rests primarily on named case studies (Zappos acquired by Amazon for ~$1.2B; Aardvark acquired by Google) rather than controlled comparative studies. No peer-reviewed RCT comparing Wizard of Oz to other MVP types exists in the public literature.

**Canonical sources:** Eric Ries – The Lean Startup (2011): Build-Measure-Learn loop; MVP as fastest path to validated learning; Alberto Savoia – The Right It (2019): 'Mechanical Turk' pretotyping technique; Jeff Kelley – 1983 Johns Hopkins dissertation (coined the term Wizard of Oz for human-in-the-loop prototyping); Don Norman and Allen Munro – 1973 Wizard of Oz airport-terminal prototype (first documented use); Jared Friedman (Y Combinator) – 'Ask YC: Upfront Technical Investments' blog post; Kromatic / The Real Startup Book – Wizard of Oz chapter (kromatic.com/real-startup-book); Nielsen Norman Group – 'The Wizard of Oz Method in UX' (nngroup.com/articles/wizard-of-oz)

**Failure modes**
- Human-attention false positive: users respond warmly to personal service quality, not the underlying solution's merit. High satisfaction scores without repeat visits or payment are the diagnostic signal.
- Operator drift: wizard responses become inconsistent across shifts or time, introducing variability that corrupts the behavioral dataset.
- Rubber-stamp bias: when LLMs or templates generate draft responses, wizards approve without critical review, causing the system to underestimate the true complexity of automation.
- Hawthorne effect: technologically sophisticated users suspect they are in a test or talking to a human, shifting their behavior toward what they think the tester wants to see.
- Low-fidelity interface break: if the UI latency or visual quality fails to sustain the illusion of automation, users shift into evaluation mode rather than natural use mode.
- Scalability confound: the manual operation is inherently more attentive and error-free than any at-scale automated system, so the experience validated may be unachievable in the real product.
- Scope creep during operation: wizards improvise beyond the intended product scope to satisfy users, producing demand signals for a product that will never be built.
- Ethical/trust damage: if users discover the deception without a proper debrief, it can permanently damage brand trust and generate negative word-of-mouth.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false positive mechanism is that human operators provide attentiveness, warmth, error correction, and judgment that no automated system will replicate at scale. Because the 'product' users evaluate is secretly powered by skilled human labor, measured satisfaction and even payment may reflect the quality of that labor rather than the viability of the proposed automation. The test appears to validate the solution when it has actually validated a premium concierge service. The diagnostic guard is pairing satisfaction metrics with behavioral commitment at scale (unsolicited return visits, organic referrals, payment without prompting) and cross-checking the operator override rate: a rate above 50% with no clear automation path means the validated experience cannot be engineered.

**Examples**
- Zappos: Founder Nick Swinmurn photographed shoes in local stores, listed them on a simple website, then bought and shipped the exact pair from the store each time an order arrived. Customers believed they were interacting with a real e-commerce inventory system. This validated that consumers would buy shoes online without any warehouse, inventory system, or logistics infrastructure being built. Zappos was later acquired by Amazon for approximately $1.2 billion.
- Aardvark: The Aardvark team wanted to test a social Q&A platform that algorithmically routed questions to the best-qualified human answerers. Rather than build the matching algorithm, they used an instant-messaging system and a team of human operators who manually read incoming questions and routed them to appropriate people. Users believed they were interacting with an automated system. The experiment validated the value proposition before any routing algorithm existed. Google later acquired Aardvark.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result shows: (1) users returning unprompted for a second or third session, (2) willingness to pay without heavy prompting, (3) organic referrals to other users, and (4) a wizard override rate below 30%, indicating most responses could be automated. All four together constitute high-confidence signal to invest in engineering the automated system.
- Weak signal: A weak result shows: high satisfaction scores paired with zero return visits, no payment, and no referrals; or a wizard override rate above 50% with no clear automation path visible in the override log. High satisfaction without behavioral commitment almost always means the experiment measured the quality of human service, not the viability of the product. In this case, pivot to problem-discovery methods (Concierge MVP or customer interviews) before re-designing the solution.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [contested] Wizard override rate of 90% approval = off-the-shelf automation likely sufficient (Learning Loop) -- The Learning Loop Wizard of Oz page (learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz) is live and does carry an evidence strength score of 90, but two independent fetches of the page found no mention of a 90% or 30% operator override/approval rate threshold or any automation-viability heuristic. The specific 90%-approval-means-automation-sufficient rule-of-thumb is not corroborated by any independently retrievable source and does not appear on the cited page itself. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz (fetched directly; claim absent from page))
- [contested] Wizard override rate of 30% approval = significant engineering complexity ahead; most work is human judgment (Learning Loop) -- Same finding as claim 1. Neither the 30% threshold nor the framing that low approval rates signal high engineering complexity appears anywhere on the Learning Loop page or in any other indexed source found. The specific numeric thresholds (30% / 90%) are unattributable to the cited source and cannot be corroborated independently. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz (fetched directly; claim absent from page))
- [contested] Recommended participant count: 10-30 real target-market users (Learning Loop) -- The Learning Loop Wizard of Oz page does not mention a 10-30 participant recommendation in any fetch. The 10-30 range does appear in a cognate context (Concierge MVP guidance at empat.tech and in card-sort UX research literature) but is not attributable to the Learning Loop source cited. The claim may be a reasonable practitioner rule-of-thumb, but it is not confirmed on the specific cited page. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz (claim absent); https://www.empat.tech/blog/concierge-mvp (10-30 range for concierge MVP, not Wizard of Oz))
- [contested] Recommended live operation window: 1-3 weeks (Learning Loop) -- No fetch of the Learning Loop page and no independent web search surfaced a '1-3 weeks' duration guideline for Wizard of Oz experiments. General practitioner sources describe ranges from 'a few days' to 'several months.' The specific 1-3 week window is not corroborated on the cited page or elsewhere. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz (claim absent from page); general MVP literature gives 'days to months')
- [contested] Estimated tooling cost excluding operator labor: approximately $100 (Retool, Airtable, LLM API) (Learning Loop) -- No fetch of the Learning Loop page and no independent search surfaced a $100 tooling-cost estimate for Wizard of Oz experiments. Airtable pricing for a small team and Retool entry tiers can fall near or above $100/month individually, making the aggregate claim plausible as a rough order-of-magnitude but it is not traceable to the cited source and was not corroborated by any independent reference. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz (claim absent from page); https://comparetiers.com/tools/airtable (Airtable pricing))
- [confirmed] Learning Loop evidence strength score for Wizard of Oz: 90/100 (Learning Loop internal scale) -- Two independent fetches of the Learning Loop Wizard of Oz page both returned 'Evidence strength: 90.' A Google search also surfaced third-party summaries citing the same score. This specific numeric claim is directly verifiable on the page. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz (confirmed by direct fetch and search))
- [confirmed] Zappos acquired by Amazon for approximately $1.2 billion -- The deal closed in late October/early November 2009. At announcement in July 2009 the stock-based deal was valued at ~$847M; by closing, Amazon's share price appreciation pushed the final value to approximately $1.2 billion (roughly 10 million Amazon shares at ~$117.40). Confirmed by TechCrunch's closing report and corroborated by the Las Vegas Sun ($847M announcement) and SEC filings. (src: https://techcrunch.com/2009/11/02/amazon-closes-zappos-deal-ends-up-paying-1-2-billion/)
- [confirmed] Alberto Savoia's cited new-product failure rate: at least 80%, and in some industries 90% or more -- The FourWeekMBA synthesis and Savoia's own published material (pretotyping.org PDF, The Right It) consistently state: 'at least 80% and in some industries at least 90% or more of new products and new businesses tend to fail.' The exact phrasing matches the claim. (src: https://fourweekmba.com/pretotyping-alberto-savoia/ and https://www.pretotyping.org/uploads/1/4/0/9/14099067/pretotype_it_10_year_anniversary_edition_pdf_version_1.0.pdf)
- [contested] General MVP experiment duration guideline: 2-6 weeks (practitioner synthesis) -- The 2-6 week range does appear in practitioner literature but specifically for Concierge MVPs and narrow single-hypothesis tests, not as a universal MVP experiment guideline. Tech MVP builds are typically cited at 6-12 weeks; no-code tools can compress to 2-6 weeks. The range is a reasonable rule-of-thumb for a specific MVP sub-type, not a broadly established benchmark. No single authoritative source uses '2-6 weeks' as a universal MVP experiment duration. (src: https://www.empat.tech/blog/concierge-mvp; https://modall.ca/blog/mvp-development-for-startups; general practitioner synthesis)
- [confirmed] Early adopters represent approximately 2-5% of potential buyers (YC / Kevin Hale Startup School) -- Kevin Hale's exact quote from YC Startup School Week 7 (2019): 'you kind of don't really get a lot of momentum and growth until you get past the first 2 to 5% of potential buyers of your market. These people in that 2 to 5%, they're called early adopters.' Confirmed directly on ycombinator.com. Note: Rogers' Diffusion of Innovation framework categorizes Early Adopters as 13.5% (with Innovators at 2.5%); Hale's 2-5% corresponds roughly to Innovators + early Early Adopters, a narrower slice than Rogers' full 'Early Adopter' band. (src: https://www.ycombinator.com/blog/startup-school-week-7-recap-kevin-hale-on-conversion-rates-and-pricing)

**Citations**
- Learning Loop – Wizard of Oz Experiment (primary protocol and benchmarks): https://learningloop.io/plays/wizard-of-oz
- Kromatic / The Real Startup Book – Wizard of Oz chapter: https://kromatic.com/real-startup-book/6-evaluative-product-experiment/wizard-of-oz/
- Kromatic – Concierge vs. Wizard of Oz Test (false positive mechanisms): https://kromatic.com/blog/concierge-vs-wizard-of-oz-test/
- Y Combinator – Ask YC: Upfront Technical Investments (Jared Friedman on Wizard of Oz): https://www.ycombinator.com/blog/ask-yc-upfront-technical-investments
- Nielsen Norman Group – The Wizard of Oz Method in UX (origin, Jeff Kelley 1983, Don Norman 1973): https://www.nngroup.com/articles/wizard-of-oz/
- FourWeekMBA – Pretotyping with Alberto Savoia (Mechanical Turk / failure rates): https://fourweekmba.com/pretotyping-alberto-savoia/
- Learning Loop – Concierge vs. Wizard of Oz (distinction and false positive bias): https://learningloop.io/blog/concierge-vs-wizard-of-oz
- Y Combinator – How to Build an MVP (Michael Seibel): https://www.ycombinator.com/library/Io-how-to-build-an-mvp

### 9. Concierge MVP

- **Category:** Solution & concept
- **Stage:** solution
- **Definition:** A Concierge MVP is a validation technique in which the founding team manually delivers the core service to a small cohort of real customers -- doing by hand exactly what software or automation would eventually do -- while customers know the service is human-operated. It is not a prototype and not a demo: actual value is delivered and, critically, payment is sought. The method is designed to generate validated learning about whether the proposed solution solves a real problem and whether customers will pay for it, before any scalable product is built.
- **Validates:** Does this solution deliver enough value that real customers will pay for it, engage repeatedly, and tolerate friction to get it? (Solution-demand fit, pre-automation.)

**Protocol**
1. Write a falsifiable hypothesis: name the target customer segment, the specific job-to-be-done, the proposed mechanism of delivery, and the minimum signal (e.g., 'At least 20% of trial users will pay $X/month after one manual session') that would constitute validation.
2. Recruit 10-30 users who match the problem profile -- from your own network, relevant forums, or direct outreach -- prioritising people who already have the problem acutely, not people who are merely curious. Do not recruit self-selected evangelists alone.
3. Charge from session one. Offer a discounted but non-zero price and require payment before service delivery. Free trials do not test willingness to pay; payment is the commitment threshold that separates real demand from polite interest.
4. Design a repeatable manual flow. Document every step you perform (intake, matching, delivery, follow-up) so you can measure time-per-task, cost-per-delivery, and later identify which steps to automate first. Use intake forms, templates, and shared docs -- not improvised one-offs.
5. Deliver the service with radical transparency. Tell customers the service is human-operated. Do not simulate automation. Observe where customers get confused, what they ask for that you did not anticipate, and what they ignore.
6. Track dual signals after each session: behavioral (did they return or re-engage within 30 days?) and economic (did they pay again, refer someone, or expand usage?). Log time-per-delivery and unit cost alongside satisfaction.
7. Run for 2-6 weeks or until you have served at least 10-20 paying customers and observed at least 2-3 complete usage cycles per customer.
8. Make an explicit build/pivot/stop decision by comparing observed conversion rate, repeat rate, and unit economics against your pre-stated hypothesis threshold. Automate only the steps that are now confirmed bottlenecks -- not everything at once.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Willingness-to-pay conversion rate (trial users who pay): >=15-25% of trial cohort (src: empat.tech Concierge MVP Guide (practitioner benchmark, not peer-reviewed))
- 30-day repeat use / retention: >=30% return within 30 days (src: empat.tech Concierge MVP Guide (practitioner benchmark))
- Recommended cohort size for initial run: 10-30 paying users (src: empat.tech Concierge MVP Guide)
- Setup and first-delivery cycle time: 1-2 weeks (vs 8-12 weeks for coded MVP) (src: bytestechnolab.com MVP types comparison)
- Compound weekly growth needed to reach 2M users from 100: 10% week-over-week for ~2 years (Paul Graham's '10% a week' framing) (src: Paul Graham, 'Do Things That Don't Scale', paulgraham.com/ds.html)
- Food on the Table -- A/B test activation improvement after design iteration post-concierge phase: 77% increase in new-user activation rate (src: Laura Klein case study, startuplessonslearned.com, 2011; reproduced by Eric Ries)
- Food on the Table -- design research time to produce the activation insight: ~2 days of user research, ~90 hours total designer time over 6 weeks (src: Laura Klein case study, startuplessonslearned.com, 2011)
- Airbnb -- manual concierge engagement duration that made the difference between success and failure: ~30 days of in-person user engagement in New York (src: Paul Graham, 'Do Things That Don't Scale', paulgraham.com/ds.html)

**Origin and evidence:** The Concierge MVP is directly attributed to Eric Ries and popularised through 'The Lean Startup' (Crown Business, 2011), which features Food on the Table (founded by Manuel Rosso, formerly VP Marketing at IMVU) as the canonical case study. Ries used the term to distinguish manual delivery (user-knows) from Wizard of Oz (user-unaware). Paul Graham independently codified the underlying principle in his 2013 essay 'Do Things That Don't Scale' (paulgraham.com/ds.html), naming it as one of the most important and counter-intuitive startup behaviours and citing Airbnb and Stripe (the 'Collison installation') as live examples. Ash Maurya's 'Running Lean' (O'Reilly, 3rd ed.) formalises it within the Lean Canvas as part of solution-validation sprints and the 'demo-sell-build' sequence. Evidence quality: practitioner-origin, not RCT. The benchmarks (15-25% conversion, 30% retention) are practitioner rules of thumb, not peer-reviewed thresholds. The Airbnb and Food on the Table outcomes are documented case studies with partial quantification.

**Canonical sources:** Eric Ries, 'The Lean Startup' (Crown Business, 2011) -- originating text and Food on the Table case; Paul Graham, 'Do Things That Don't Scale' (paulgraham.com/ds.html, July 2013) -- Airbnb and Stripe concierge examples, the 'Collison installation'; Ash Maurya, 'Running Lean' (O'Reilly, 3rd ed., 2022) -- 'demo-sell-build' framing, solution design sprints; Manuel Rosso, Food on the Table case study presentation at Startup Lessons Learned Conference (sllconf, 2011) -- first-person account of the concierge execution; Laura Klein, UX case study on Food on the Table, startuplessonslearned.com, January 2011

**Failure modes**
- Not charging: running a free concierge trial inflates apparent demand; payment is the commitment signal that separates real intent from politeness or FOMO.
- Likability bias: customers respond positively to the founder as a person, not to the product concept; enthusiasm tracks the relationship, not the value proposition.
- Selection bias: early cohorts self-select for patience and early-adopter motivation; they are not representative of the mainstream market the business must ultimately reach.
- White-glove inflation: the quality of human delivery exceeds what any automated product could achieve; customers later churn when automation is introduced because the product cannot replicate the human's judgment and responsiveness.
- No baseline metrics agreed in advance: teams declare success based on qualitative warmth ('they seemed excited') rather than pre-stated quantitative thresholds, making the experiment unfalsifiable.
- Scaling too early: teams begin automating before they have confirmed repeatability across at least two usage cycles per customer, encoding unvalidated assumptions into code.
- Ignoring unit economics: time-per-delivery and cost-per-customer are not tracked, so the team learns that customers want the solution but not whether the business can ever be profitable.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false-positive mechanism is the conflation of satisfaction with the human concierge for satisfaction with the product concept. A customer who returns and pays again may be responding to the founder's attentiveness, personalised service quality, and social obligation rather than to the core automated value proposition. This is structurally distinct from a Wizard of Oz MVP, where the false positive is about whether users accept automation -- in a Concierge MVP the false positive is that users accept a human-delivered service that the eventual product cannot replicate. A secondary mechanism is cohort contamination: recruiting from the founder's own network produces respondents with pre-existing goodwill, making churn estimates and willingness-to-pay signals systematically optimistic relative to a cold-acquisition cohort.

**Examples**
- Food on the Table (acquired by Albertsons / Foodie Always): Founder Manuel Rosso personally approached shoppers in grocery stores, enrolled them in a $10/month subscription, then manually compiled weekly meal plans and coupon-optimised shopping lists for each family. He and his chef co-founder walked store aisles each week to find the best values and drew up menus for one family at a time. They did not build a website or write code during this phase. The manual operation ran until demand volume made it operationally impossible to serve by hand; only then did they automate. This case is the canonical Concierge MVP example in 'The Lean Startup'.
- Airbnb: Founders Brian Chesky and Joe Gebbia personally photographed host listings, wrote listing copy, answered guest and host messages, and managed early bookings in New York -- acting as the platform's operations team. Paul Graham documented that approximately 30 days of this in-person engagement was the proximate cause of Airbnb's survival and early traction. The manual phase taught them pricing dynamics, trust requirements between strangers, and the outsized effect of photography quality on conversion -- all inputs that shaped the automated product.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is: (a) customers pay without heavy persuasion at the first session, (b) at least 20-30% return and pay again within 30 days without being prompted, (c) customers refer others unprompted, (d) qualitative feedback clusters around a consistent, specific job-to-be-done rather than scattered requests, and (e) the team can identify 2-3 repeatable manual steps that form a clear automation target. Convergence of payment, repeat use, and referral across a cohort of 15+ paying customers constitutes strong evidence of solution-demand fit and justifies moving to a coded prototype or Wizard of Oz phase.
- Weak signal: A weak result is: customers engage once but do not return; customers engage only when personally recruited by the founder and not through any reproducible channel; qualitative feedback is positive but diffuse ('I really like this idea') without evidence of payment or repeat use; the team cannot identify a repeatable delivery flow because every customer session requires bespoke improvisation; or the unit economics at manual delivery cost imply a price point the market has not validated. Single-session positive feedback with no repeat engagement is the most common false-positive pattern and should be treated as a null result, not a green light to build.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] 10-30 paying users is the recommended cohort size for a Concierge MVP run (empat.tech practitioner benchmark) -- The empat.tech Concierge MVP Guide explicitly states 'Aim for 10-30 users to start -- enough to learn, not too many to manage.' This is presented as practitioner guidance, not peer-reviewed research, and the page does not cite an external source for the figure. It is accurate as a description of what empat.tech recommends. (src: https://www.empat.tech/blog/concierge-mvp)
- [confirmed] >=15-25% of trial users converting to payment is the stated willingness-to-pay threshold (empat.tech practitioner benchmark) -- The empat.tech page states 'conversion or willingness-to-pay consistently meets or exceeds your target (for example, >15-25% of trial users convert)' as a signal for when to begin automating. However, this is presented as an illustrative example value ('for example'), not a cited research benchmark. It is accurate as a description of the empat.tech page's content, but the framing as an established benchmark overstates it. (src: https://www.empat.tech/blog/concierge-mvp)
- [contested] >=30% returning within 30 days is the stated repeat-use threshold (empat.tech practitioner benchmark) -- The empat.tech page does include a figure of '>=30% of users return within 30 days' as an illustrative threshold, so the attribution to the page is accurate. However, the figure is unsourced on the page and conflicts with broad industry data: average Day-30 app retention is approximately 7% (Adjust) and SaaS 30-day retention averages around 39% (Pendo). The 30% figure appears to be a rough practitioner rule-of-thumb with no cited empirical grounding, and whether it is a meaningful benchmark for a concierge MVP specifically (vs. a scaled product) is unresolved. (src: https://www.empat.tech/blog/concierge-mvp; https://www.pendo.io/pendo-blog/user-retention-rate-benchmarks/)
- [contested] 1-2 weeks is the stated setup-and-first-delivery cycle time for a Concierge/Wizard of Oz MVP, vs 8-12 weeks for a coded MVP (bytestechnolab comparison) -- The bytestechnolab article does not contain the specific '1-2 weeks vs 8-12 weeks' framing cited. The page states concierge tests take 'a few weeks' and coded single-feature products take 'three or four months.' The '8-12 weeks spent building the wrong layer' language in the article is used to describe failure patterns, not a typical coded MVP timeline. The 1-2 week figure appears in secondary aggregations citing bytestechnolab but is not directly present in the source article with that specificity. (src: https://www.bytestechnolab.com/uk/blog/types-of-mvp/)
- [confirmed] ~30 days of in-person concierge engagement by Airbnb founders in New York was cited by Paul Graham as 'the difference between success and failure' (paulgraham.com/ds.html) -- Verified verbatim in the primary source. The essay states: 'early on it was so fragile that about 30 days of going out and engaging in person with users made the difference between success and failure.' The attribution to paulgraham.com/ds.html ('Do Things That Don't Scale') is correct. (src: https://paulgraham.com/ds.html)
- [confirmed] Food on the Table achieved a 77% increase in new-user activation after iterative UX redesign post-concierge phase, measured over 6 weeks across several thousand users (Laura Klein / startuplessonslearned.com) -- Verified from the primary source. The startuplessonslearned.com post states 'a 77% increase in the number of new users making it all the way through activation' after 'running for about 6 weeks and a few thousand people.' All three figures (77%, 6 weeks, several thousand users) match the primary source exactly. (src: http://www.startuplessonslearned.com/2011/01/case-study-ux-design-and-food-on-table.html)
- [confirmed] Food on the Table UX research took approximately 2 days and ~90 total designer hours to produce the activation insight (Laura Klein / startuplessonslearned.com) -- The primary source states Klein's 'entire involvement with the project to do the research, design, and usability testing was just under 90 hours spread over about 6 weeks.' The approximately 2-day research phase is corroborated: one full day for UserTesting.com analysis and 'another day' for GoToMeeting sessions with current users. Both figures match the primary source. (src: http://www.startuplessonslearned.com/2011/01/case-study-ux-design-and-food-on-table.html)
- [confirmed] Paul Graham's compounding illustration: 10% weekly growth from 100 users reaches 14,000 users after one year and ~2 million after two years (paulgraham.com/ds.html) -- Verified verbatim from the primary source. The essay 'Do Things That Don't Scale' (paulgraham.com/ds.html) states: 'If you have 100 users, you need to get 10 more next week to grow 10% a week... After a year you'll have 14,000 users, and after 2 years you'll have 2 million.' All figures match exactly. (src: https://paulgraham.com/ds.html)
- [contested] U.S. startup failure rate rose 60% in one year (cited in empat.tech post, attributed to Harvard Business Review 2025 -- treat as approximate, not primary-verified) -- The statistic is real but the attribution chain is indirect. HBR's January 2025 article ('6 Lessons for Startups from a Museum Dedicated to Failure') does state 'U.S. startup failures have increased by 60% in the past year,' but that article cites the Financial Times as its source. The FT article in turn cites Carta data. The claim thus originates from Carta's platform data, is amplified by FT, and is then cited by HBR. Attributing it directly to HBR as a primary source overstates HBR's role. The 60% figure itself is approximately corroborated (Carta data shows a sharp rise in shutdowns), though exact percentage figures vary depending on the Carta dataset window used. (src: https://hbr.org/2025/01/6-lessons-for-startups-from-a-museum-dedicated-to-failure; https://www.hl.co.uk/news/start-up-failures-up-by-60-as-founders-face-hangover-from-boom-years)

**Citations**
- Eric Ries, The Lean Startup -- Lean Startup Co. overview: https://leanstartup.co/resources/articles/what-is-an-mvp/
- Paul Graham, 'Do Things That Don't Scale' (July 2013): https://paulgraham.com/ds.html
- Laura Klein / Eric Ries, Food on the Table UX case study, Startup Lessons Learned, January 2011: http://www.startuplessonslearned.com/2011/01/case-study-ux-design-and-food-on-table.html

### 10. Fake-door / smoke test landing page

- **Category:** Solution & concept
- **Stage:** demand
- **Definition:** A fake-door (smoke test) landing page presents a fully designed value proposition and a high-intent call-to-action -- sign up, pre-purchase, join waitlist -- for a product or feature that does not yet exist. When a visitor acts on the CTA, they reach a "coming soon" message or are told the product is unavailable, and their click is recorded as a behavioral signal of genuine intent. The technique was systematized by Alberto Savoia at Google under the term "pretotyping" and popularized in Eric Ries's Lean Startup as a smoke test MVP; it measures actual behavior rather than stated preference.
- **Validates:** Does sufficient demand exist -- expressed through real behavioral intent (click, signup, or simulated purchase) rather than survey self-report -- to justify building this product or feature?

**Protocol**
1. State the falsifiable hypothesis before building anything: specify the minimum conversion threshold (e.g., >=X% of visitors click 'Buy' within Y weeks) that would justify proceeding, and write it down as a kill criterion.
2. Build the smallest credible page: a headline that names the problem, a 2-4 sentence value proposition, optional 60-90s demo video, and a single high-intent CTA ('Get Early Access', 'Pre-order', 'Join Waitlist'). Do not describe the full feature set; describe the outcome the user gets.
3. Wire real tracking before launch: conversion pixel on the CTA click AND on the post-click page (coming-soon or pricing page). Track visit-to-CTA-click rate and, separately, visit-to-email-captured rate as distinct signals.
4. Drive targeted traffic from a channel that matches your actual buyer: paid search (Google Ads) or paid social (Facebook/LinkedIn) on keywords or audience segments that represent the real ICP, not your own network. Budget $50-300 in ad spend to reach a minimum of 200-500 visitors.
5. Add a friction step beyond the first click if willingness-to-pay is the hypothesis: a second page with a real pricing table and a 'Checkout' button (credit card not charged) generates a stronger behavioral signal than an email capture alone. Buffer's Joel Gascoigne calls the single-page email form 'missing the real validation'.
6. When a visitor hits the dead end, tell them honestly that the product is not yet available, collect their email for updates, and -- critically -- send a short personal follow-up email or book a 15-minute call. This converts the behavioral signal into qualitative learning about why they clicked.
7. Run until you hit your pre-specified sample size (a minimum of 200 unique visitors; 1,000+ recommended for reliable conversion rate estimation) or a calendar deadline, whichever comes first.
8. Evaluate against the kill criterion written in step 1. If you miss it, pivot the hypothesis or kill the idea. If you hit it, proceed to the next riskiest assumption.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Email capture rate from cold paid traffic, healthy range: 5-15% of unique visitors (src: LearningLoop.io / GLIDR Help Center -- widely cited practitioner benchmark; not from a controlled study)
- CTA click rate indicating purchase intent (cold traffic): 0.5-3.0% of unique visitors (src: LearningLoop.io / FasterCapital smoke test guides)
- Minimum visitor sample for reliable conversion rate signal: 200 visitors minimum; 1,000+ recommended (src: GLIDR Help Center, Landing Page Smoke Test article)
- Buffer (Joel Gascoigne, 2010): signups over 7-week smoke test landing page: 120 email signups (src: Joel Gascoigne, 'How to successfully validate your idea with a Landing Page MVP', Medium)
- Buffer: days to first paying customer after landing-page-validated launch: 3 days (src: Joel Gascoigne, Medium post cited above)
- Dropbox: waitlist before demo video: ~5,000 names (src: Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011), and Anthill Online writeup citing Ries)
- Dropbox: overnight signups after HackerNews demo video post: 75,000 signups overnight (src: Multiple secondhand citations of Eric Ries, The Lean Startup; Anthill Online 'How Dropbox followed the Lean Startup')
- Dropbox: registered users 15 months after smoke-test validation: 100,000 to 4,000,000 (40x growth) (src: Anthill Online citing Lean Startup case study)

**Origin and evidence:** Alberto Savoia coined the technique as the "Fake Door pretotype" around 2009 while Engineering Director at Google, crediting Jess Lee (then CEO of Polyvore) with naming it. Savoia published "Pretotype It!" (2011) and later "The Right It" (HarperCollins, 2019), where the Fake Door is one of seven pretotyping techniques. Eric Ries independently described the equivalent as a smoke test MVP in "The Lean Startup" (Crown Business, 2011), using the Dropbox demo video as the canonical case. The evidence base is practitioner-documented case studies and field experience, not controlled randomized experiments; there is no peer-reviewed RCT establishing conversion thresholds.

**Canonical sources:** Alberto Savoia, 'The Right It: Why So Many Ideas Fail and How to Make Sure Yours Succeed' (HarperCollins, 2019); Alberto Savoia, 'Pretotype It!' (self-published PDF, 2011) -- free at albertosavoia.com; Eric Ries, 'The Lean Startup' (Crown Business, 2011), chapter on MVP types including smoke tests; Joel Gascoigne, 'How to successfully validate your idea with a Landing Page MVP' (Medium, 2013); Ash Maurya, 'Running Lean' (O'Reilly, 2012) -- landing page as the first experiment in the Lean Canvas cycle

**Failure modes**
- Founder-network traffic bias: driving signups from personal Twitter, LinkedIn, or Slack communities where social obligation inflates conversion. The signal reflects the founder's network, not the market.
- Acquiescence bias without friction: a single email-capture CTA has almost no cost; adding a pricing page with a real checkout (credit card not charged) or a multi-step form significantly reduces false enthusiasm.
- Optimizing for volume, not learning: chasing a large signup count without following up with signups personally converts a behavioral signal into vanity metrics. Joel Gascoigne explicitly warns against this.
- Underpowered tests: drawing conclusions from fewer than 200 visitors where random variance dominates the conversion rate estimate.
- Mismatched traffic source: paid traffic on broad keywords attracts browsers, not buyers. The conversion rate from generic keyword traffic is not equivalent to conversion from the real ICP.
- Ethical and trust damage: leaving the fake door live for too long, or failing to disclose that the product does not exist, erodes trust with early adopters -- the highest-value segment.
- Survivor bias in follow-up: founders tend to call only the people who clicked 'yes' and ignore the majority who bounced, producing a falsely optimistic qualitative signal alongside the quantitative one.

**False-positive mechanism:** The core false positive mechanism is low-cost intent: clicking a button or entering an email requires almost no commitment, so it systematically overestimates willingness to pay versus willingness to use versus actual retention. The gap between 'intrigued enough to click' and 'willing to pay a real price, integrate it into a workflow, and return after day 1' is large and not captured by the landing page signal. A founder's charisma, the novelty effect of a new idea, or social-proof framing in ad copy can generate a conversion rate that exceeds the pre-set kill threshold without revealing genuine sustained demand -- exactly the false-positive PMF pattern where 'customer demand is very strong but the business model rests on shaky ground' (Sandy Kory, 2024).

**Examples**
- Dropbox (2008): Drew Houston posted a 3-minute demo video to Hacker News showing file sync across devices. The video linked to a landing page waitlist. The product did not exist. The waitlist went from ~5,000 to 75,000 overnight, confirming demand and guiding feature prioritization. Eric Ries cites this in 'The Lean Startup' as the archetypal smoke test MVP.
- Buffer (2010): Joel Gascoigne built a two-page site: page 1 explained the Twitter scheduling concept, page 2 showed a 'plans and pricing coming soon' message. He drove traffic via his personal Twitter account. Over 7 weeks he collected 120 email signups, then followed up personally with each one. Buffer launched and acquired its first paying customer within 3 days.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is a CTA conversion rate of 5%+ from cold, targeted paid traffic (i.e., not your personal network) where the CTA involved a meaningful friction step such as a pricing page click or checkout attempt, and where post-click qualitative follow-up confirms that users clicked because the problem description matched a real recurring pain they cannot solve with existing tools. The conversion signal is strengthened further if users reach out proactively, refer others, or offer to pre-pay.
- Weak signal: A weak or misleading result is a conversion rate that meets the threshold but comes primarily from: (a) the founder's personal network, (b) a low-friction single-email-field CTA on broad-match paid traffic, or (c) a very small absolute visitor count (under 200). It is also weak if follow-up interviews reveal that users clicked out of curiosity rather than because the problem is active and urgent. 120 signups over 7 weeks, as in Buffer's case, is a small absolute number but was sufficient for Joel Gascoigne because he followed up personally with every signup and confirmed real intent -- the raw count alone is not the signal.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [contested] 5-15% email capture rate from cold paid traffic is a healthy benchmark for smoke test landing pages (attributed to LearningLoop.io / GLIDR) -- The 5-15% range is a real practitioner benchmark, but it describes referral or semi-warm traffic, not cold paid traffic. Multiple landing page benchmark aggregators (Unbounce, Leadpages, Landerlab 2026) show cold paid social traffic converts at 0.5-1.5% and cold paid search at 2-5%. The LearningLoop.io fake-door page does not publish a 5-15% cold-traffic figure; it uses 5% as a hypothetical example only. GLIDR's landing page article does not state this range either. Applying '5-15%' to cold paid traffic overstates the expected rate by 3-10x for most channels. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing; https://landerlab.io/blog/landing-page-conversion-rate; https://leadpages.com/blog/landing-page-conversion-benchmarks-2026)
- [contested] 0.5-3.0% CTA click rate indicates purchase intent on cold traffic (attributed to LearningLoop.io / FasterCapital smoke test guides) -- The 0.5-3% range is broadly consistent with CRO industry data for cold traffic CTAs (display/social ads typically 0.5-1.5%; paid search higher-intent pages 2-3%). However, the LearningLoop.io fake-door page does not publish or endorse this specific range as a benchmark; it gives no explicit CTA click rate thresholds. Attribution to LearningLoop.io as a named source for this specific figure is unverifiable from the page content. The range itself is plausible but the sourcing claim is not confirmed. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing; https://foundrycro.com/blog/cta-button-conversion-rate-benchmarks-2026/)
- [refuted] Minimum 200 unique visitors needed for a reliable conversion rate estimate; 1,000+ recommended (attributed to GLIDR Help Center, Landing Page Smoke Test article) -- A direct fetch of the GLIDR Landing Page Smoke Test help article (help.glidr.io/en/articles/1648431) found no mention of '200 visitors minimum' or '1,000+ recommended' as thresholds. The GLIDR Fake Door article mentions 'on the order of 1,000+ views or 100+ clicks' but that is for fake-door button impressions, not unique visitors to a landing page. The specific '200 minimum / 1,000+ recommended' framing attributed to GLIDR does not appear in that article. (src: https://help.glidr.io/en/articles/1648431-landing-page-smoke-test; https://help.glidr.io/en/articles/1648423-fake-door-smoke-test)
- [confirmed] Buffer collected 120 signups over a 7-week smoke test landing page in 2010 (Joel Gascoigne, Medium) -- Joel Gascoigne's Medium article states verbatim: 'in a long 7 week period I only got 120 signups.' This is confirmed by the buffer.com first-person account and multiple secondary sources. The figure and duration are consistent across all sources checked. (src: https://medium.com/@joelgascoigne/how-to-successfully-validate-your-idea-with-a-landing-page-mvp-ef3c2d02dc51; https://buffer.com/resources/idea-to-paying-customers-in-7-weeks-how-we-did-it/)
- [contested] Buffer's first paying customer arrived 3 days after launch (Joel Gascoigne, Medium) -- Joel Gascoigne's Medium article says '1 started paying for Buffer 3 days after we launched,' confirming the 3-day claim. However, the official buffer.com post ('Idea to Paying Customers in 7 Weeks') says 'within 4 days of launching.' The discrepancy is minor (3 vs. 4 days) and likely reflects rounding across retellings. The named primary source (the Medium post) does say 3 days, but an equally authoritative Buffer-owned source says 4 days, so the claim is not cleanly confirmed. (src: https://medium.com/@joelgascoigne/how-to-successfully-validate-your-idea-with-a-landing-page-mvp-ef3c2d02dc51; https://buffer.com/resources/idea-to-paying-customers-in-7-weeks-how-we-did-it/)
- [contested] Dropbox had a waitlist of approximately 5,000 before the HackerNews demo video (cited from Eric Ries, The Lean Startup) -- The 5,000 pre-video waitlist figure is widely repeated across secondary sources (Shortform summary of The Lean Startup, YourStory, mmtm.io, multiple growth blogs) and is consistent with Drew Houston's general account. However, a direct fetch of Drew Houston's TechCrunch Founder Stories interview does not surface the specific '5,000' starting figure from Houston's own mouth. The figure traces to The Lean Startup book (Ries) and Houston's conference talks, but no independently verifiable primary source transcript confirms the exact number. It is plausible but the sourcing is secondhand. (src: https://techcrunch.com/2011/11/01/founder-storie-how-dropbox-got-its-first-10-million-users/amp/; https://www.shortform.com/blog/dropbox-mvp-explainer-video/)
- [confirmed] Dropbox went from ~5,000 to 75,000 signups overnight after the demo video (multiple secondhand citations of Ries; not independently verifiable) -- The 75,000 signups overnight figure is confirmed by Drew Houston directly in TechCrunch's Founder Stories interview: 'Within 24-hours Dropbox had 75,000 people signup for the wait-list. They were expecting 15,000, tops.' The 5,000 starting point (making the delta ~70,000) is widely cited but the exact pre-video count is from secondary sources. The 75,000 endpoint and the overnight nature of the jump are confirmed by a near-primary source. (src: https://techcrunch.com/2011/11/01/founder-storie-how-dropbox-got-its-first-10-million-users/amp/; https://www.shortform.com/blog/dropbox-mvp-explainer-video/)
- [confirmed] Dropbox grew from 100,000 to 4,000,000 registered users in 15 months post-validation (Anthill Online citing Lean Startup case study) -- Confirmed by the Anthill Online article ('in just over 15 months, Dropbox went from having 100,000 registered users to more than four million') and by the official Lean Startup case studies page (theleanstartup.com/casestudies), both attributing the figure to Drew Houston. The growth is attributed to the referral program, not the smoke test alone. The figures and timeframe are consistent across sources. (src: https://anthillonline.com/dropbox-followed-lean-startup-get-4-million-users-video/; https://theleanstartup.com/casestudies)

**Citations**
- Joel Gascoigne, 'How to successfully validate your idea with a Landing Page MVP' (Medium): https://medium.com/@joelgascoigne/how-to-successfully-validate-your-idea-with-a-landing-page-mvp-ef3c2d02dc51
- Alberto Savoia, Summary of Pretotyping Techniques PDF (albertosavoia.com): https://www.albertosavoia.com/uploads/1/4/0/9/14099067/summary_of_pretotyping_techniques.pdf
- Kromatic / The Real Startup Book: Fake Door / Smoke Test: https://kromatic.com/real-startup-book/fake-door-smoke-test
- PostHog Tutorial: How to run a fake door test: https://posthog.com/tutorials/fake-door-test
- Sandy Kory, 'False Positives Distort the Path to PMF' (Substack): https://sandykory.substack.com/p/false-positives-distort-the-path
- Anthill Online: How Dropbox followed the Lean Startup to get 4 million users: https://anthillonline.com/dropbox-followed-lean-startup-get-4-million-users-video/
- Edwin / Tradecraft, 'A Growth Marketer's guide to Smoke Tests' (Medium): https://medium.com/tradecraft-traction/a-growth-marketers-guide-to-smoke-tests-6ffa964a6181
- GLIDR Help Center: Fake Door Smoke Test: https://help.glidr.io/en/articles/1648423-fake-door-smoke-test
- LearningLoop.io: Fake Door Testing -- What It Is and How to Run One: https://learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing
- Mastering Pretotyping (EWOR blog, covering Savoia's methodology): https://www.ewor.com/blog/mastering-pretotyping-a-guide-to-getting-it-right
