### 11. Explainer-video MVP (the Dropbox demo-video pattern)

- **Category:** Solution & concept
- **Stage:** solution
- **Definition:** An explainer-video MVP is a short (typically 2-5 minute) recorded demonstration of a product concept or prototype that has not yet been fully built, released to a targeted audience with a measurable call to action (waitlist signup, pre-order, email capture). It tests whether the value proposition is understandable and attractive enough to generate demonstrated interest before significant engineering resources are committed. Eric Ries codified it in The Lean Startup (2011) as a smoke-test variant; Alberto Savoia labeled it the "YouTube Pretotype" in The Right It (2019), independently arriving at the same mechanism.
- **Validates:** Does a clearly communicated solution attract enough genuine pre-product demand from the right audience to justify building it?

**Protocol**
1. DEFINE the falsifiable hypothesis before recording: state what specific audience segment, what problem they have, and what threshold of response (e.g., N signups, M% click-through, P pre-orders) constitutes a positive result. Write this down before launch.
2. BUILD a working or simulated prototype sufficient to demonstrate the core value action on screen. The video must show the product doing something real or realistic -- a narrated slide deck is a weaker signal. Dropbox's first video showed actual file syncing on a prototype; it was not an animation.
3. RECORD a 2-5 minute screencast or narrated demo. Lead with the problem, show the solution in action, end with a single explicit call to action (join waitlist / pre-order / request access). Embed community-specific cultural references if targeting a niche technical audience to increase sharing.
4. SELECT the distribution channel based on where your target early adopters already congregate, not on general reach. Dropbox used Hacker News (technical founders, April 2007) then Digg and Reddit (early-tech consumers, 2008). Mismatch between video audience and intended buyer is a primary false-positive source.
5. ATTACH a friction-bearing call to action alongside the waitlist. A free email signup is the weakest signal; add a qualifying question, a deposit, or a paid pre-order to stratify curiosity from intent. If the business model is B2B, capture company name, role, and a brief problem description.
6. MEASURE within 48-72 hours: total views, CTA conversion rate (signups / views), share rate, and qualitative comments. Record the channel source for each signup. A high view count with a low conversion rate indicates appeal but not intent -- the video is not yet validating the solution.
7. FOLLOW UP with a sample of signups within one week via a one-question email or phone call using Mom Test principles (ask about past behavior and current workaround, not opinions about your future product). This separates the curious from the genuinely pained.
8. COMPARE results against your pre-stated threshold and against a plausible base rate for the distribution channel. Declare the result before you begin analysis, not after seeing numbers.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Waitlist growth from Dropbox second video (Digg/Reddit, 2008): 5,000 to 75,000 signups in under 24 hours (15x overnight) (src: First Round Review interview with Drew Houston; TechCrunch Founder Stories (2011))
- SaaS waitlist-to-active-user conversion (industry range): 25-50% if access granted within one month; falls below 20% if delayed beyond one month (src: 16vc.substack.com 'When Is Your Startup Ready to Raise Funding' (2025))
- Paid pre-order conversion from waitlist (cross-industry range): 5-25%, average approximately 20%; drops below 10% if wait exceeds a few weeks (src: 16vc.substack.com 'When Is Your Startup Ready to Raise Funding' (2025))
- Landing-page CTA benchmark as a weak-signal floor: Less than 10% conversion on a targeted-ad landing page suggests the problem is not urgent enough for the segment (src: Qubit Capital PMF framework article citing practitioner heuristic)
- 'No market need' as cause of startup failure: Cited in 34% of post-mortems (Failory, 80+ cases) and 42% (CB Insights, 111 cases) (src: Aggregated in 16vc.substack.com and Mag Startup PMF guides citing original CB Insights and Failory studies)

**Origin and evidence:** Drew Houston originated the pattern in practice in 2007-2008 for Dropbox. Eric Ries popularized it as a named MVP type in The Lean Startup (Crown Business, 2011), citing Dropbox explicitly. Alberto Savoia independently named the same technique the "YouTube Pretotype" in The Right It (HarperCollins, 2019), having developed pretotyping at Google circa 2010. The evidence base is a single canonical case (Dropbox) that is very well-documented via primary interviews (First Round Review, TechCrunch Founder Stories), plus Ries's theoretical framing and Savoia's independent taxonomy. There are no peer-reviewed controlled studies of the method's success rate. The Dropbox numbers are primary-source (Houston's own words in multiple interviews) and internally consistent across sources.

**Canonical sources:** Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (Crown Business, 2011) -- Chapter 6 'Test' covers video MVP / smoke test explicitly with Dropbox case; Alberto Savoia, The Right It (HarperCollins, 2019) -- names the 'YouTube Pretotype' as one of eight pretotyping techniques; Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (self-published, 2013) -- does not address video MVPs directly but provides the correct follow-up protocol to separate curiosity from real pain, which is the essential complement to the video test; First Round Review -- Drew Houston interview 'Drew Houston's Morph from Hacker to Hyper-Growth CEO' (primary source for Dropbox sequence and numbers); TechCrunch Founder Stories -- 'How Dropbox Got Its First 10 Million Users' (2011, Erick Schonfeld interview with Houston, primary source for second video and 75,000 number); Y Combinator Startup School -- Kevin Hale lectures on conversion rates and launch types (ycombinator.com/library)

**Failure modes**
- Measuring views or shares instead of CTA conversion: a video can go viral among people who will never buy, producing a vanity metric with no predictive value.
- Audience-solution mismatch: Dropbox's first video worked because Hacker News readers viscerally understood the 'write once, read anywhere' pain. The same video shown to a general audience would have underperformed. Teams often distribute on the easiest channel, not the right one.
- Validating the video, not the product: a well-produced, entertaining, or emotionally resonant video can attract signups from people who liked the video. This is distinct from people who need the solution.
- Survivorship bias in citing Dropbox: the case is taught because it worked spectacularly. The base rate of explainer-video MVPs generating this kind of signal is not documented. Teams anchor on the exceptional outcome.
- Waitlist decay: signups not contacted within days to weeks decay rapidly. A team that waits two months to launch may find that 80%+ of the list is cold, retroactively making the signal look weaker than it was.
- No cost signal: a free waitlist signup requires no commitment. Demonstrated willingness to pay, give access to a calendar, or share a job description is categorically stronger evidence than an email address.
- The video explains away objections the user would have found organically: a product demo removes friction that exists in reality, producing an optimistic view of adoption.

**False-positive mechanism:** The core false positive: a viewer finds the video entertaining, novel, or emotionally resonant and signs up out of curiosity or social proof (e.g., 'this is trending on Digg'), not because they have the problem and intend to pay to solve it. Because the call to action is free and low-friction, the cost of signing up is effectively zero, so the signup population is a superset of actual buyers. The Dropbox case partially avoids this because the audience (Digg/Reddit tech early adopters in 2008) had genuinely experienced the file-sync pain and because the product was shown working -- but even there, Houston had no paid conversion data at the video stage. The false positive is amplified whenever: the video is professionally produced (high production signals legitimacy, lowers skepticism), the distribution channel has a 'try new things' culture (Digg, Product Hunt), or the team interprets absolute signup volume rather than conversion rate as the success metric.

**Examples**
- Dropbox: Drew Houston posted a 3-minute screencast of a working prototype to Hacker News in April 2007 as part of his YC application, generating a waitlist and co-founder (Arash Ferdowsi noticed it). In 2008 he released a second video, seeded with Easter eggs for the Digg and Reddit communities, which grew the beta waitlist from 5,000 to 75,000 in under 24 hours. This was the second of two videos, not the first; the 75,000 number is frequently misattributed to a single original video.
- Crayon (now defunct SaaS): As documented in the pretotyping literature and Alberto Savoia's LinkedIn posts, multiple B2B SaaS teams have used short YouTube or Loom demos of a Keynote or Figma simulation as a YouTube Pretotype to test enterprise buyer interest before writing production code. Savoia has cited examples where a single video with a LinkedIn-targeted landing page generated enough inbound to greenlight or kill a product idea within two weeks. No specific signup numbers are publicly cited for these cases.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result has three simultaneous signals: (1) CTA conversion rate (signups / video views) is meaningfully above the channel baseline -- e.g., above 5-10% for a cold targeted-ad audience; (2) a follow-up qualitative check (Mom Test call or one-question email) on a sample of signups reveals respondents already have the problem and a current workaround they are paying for or spending significant time on; (3) organic sharing or referral happens unprompted from people who were not targeted -- i.e., the video spreads to adjacent communities without paid distribution. When all three align, the test provides credible evidence of solution-market resonance worth proceeding to a concierge or functional MVP. The Dropbox second video met criteria (1) and (3) clearly; criterion (2) was implicit in the audience's known pain.
- Weak signal: A weak result shows high view counts or shares but low CTA conversion (below 1-2%), or signups who do not respond to follow-up outreach, or signups who describe the problem as 'interesting' or 'nice to have' rather than active and painful in Mom Test follow-up. This pattern indicates the video communicated a concept that is comprehensible and appealing in the abstract but does not address an urgent, recurrent, budget-bearing problem. The correct response is not to iterate the video; it is to re-examine whether the target segment actually has the problem at the frequency and severity assumed, before spending further resources on either the video or the product.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Dropbox beta waitlist grew from 5,000 to 75,000 signups in under 24 hours following the second video posted to Digg and Reddit in 2008 (First Round Review, TechCrunch Founder Stories) -- Multiple authoritative sources confirm this. Wikipedia's Timeline of Dropbox pins the Digg video to March 2008 and documents the overnight 5,000-to-75,000 surge. The First Round Review primary interview with Drew Houston states 'Within a day, video #2 was at the top of Digg, spiking Houston's wait-list from 5,000 to 75,000.' TechCrunch Founder Stories corroborates the numbers and 24-hour window. One minor caveat: some sources say the video was posted to Digg alone, others add Reddit; the First Round Review interview names both Digg and Reddit. (src: First Round Review (review.firstround.com/drew-houstons-morph-from-hacker-to-hyper-growth-ceo/); Wikipedia Timeline of Dropbox (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Dropbox); TechCrunch Founder Stories (techcrunch.com/2011/11/01/founder-storie-how-dropbox-got-its-first-10-million-users/))
- [confirmed] The 75,000 spike came from the SECOND Dropbox video, not the first; the first video targeted Hacker News in April 2007 (First Round Review primary interview with Drew Houston) -- The First Round Review interview with Drew Houston explicitly identifies the waitlist spike as coming from 'video #2' posted to Digg and Reddit. The April 5, 2007 Hacker News post titled 'My YC app: Dropbox – Throw away your USB drive' is independently confirmed as the first video by multiple sources including the YC library and Shortform. Wikipedia places the Digg video in March 2008, consistent with it being a later second video. Several low-quality secondary sources confusingly attribute the spike to the first HN video, but the primary source (Drew Houston via First Round Review) is unambiguous. (src: First Round Review primary interview (review.firstround.com/drew-houstons-morph-from-hacker-to-hyper-growth-ceo/); YC Library (ycombinator.com/library/6S-on-starting-and-scaling-dropbox); Wikipedia Timeline of Dropbox (en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Dropbox))
- [contested] SaaS waitlist-to-active-user conversion is approximately 25-50% if access is granted within one month, falling below 20% with longer delays (16vc.substack.com, 2025) -- The 16vc.substack.com article exists and does state that waitlists 'convert at an average rate of 25% to 85% for free signups if users get access within a month—with a rapid fall below 20% if onboarding is delayed.' The lower bound of 25% matches, but the upper bound in the source is 85%, not 50% as claimed. The claim materially understates the cited range's upper end. Additionally, the 16vc article itself appears to be a Substack post of unclear methodological provenance rather than a peer-reviewed study, and other SaaS benchmark sources (Userpilot, ScaleMath) show wide variation, so the 85% ceiling is a practitioner estimate, not a controlled measurement. (src: 16vc.substack.com 'When Is Your Startup Ready to Raise Funding' (16vc.substack.com/p/when-is-your-startup-ready-to-raise); ScaleMath waitlist conversion benchmarks (scalemath.com/blog/what-is-a-good-waitlist-conversion-rate/))
- [confirmed] Paid pre-order conversion from a waitlist averages approximately 20%, ranging 5-25%, dropping below 10% if the wait exceeds a few weeks (16vc.substack.com, 2025) -- The 16vc.substack.com article states verbatim: 'Paid preorders convert at 5% to 25% (average ~20%), dropping below 10% if users must wait more than a few weeks.' The range, average, and time-decay threshold all match the claim exactly. Corroborating data from Lenny Rachitsky's newsletter (surveying founders directly) also cites a 5-25% range averaging ~20% for waitlist-to-paid conversions. The source is a Substack post rather than a large-sample empirical study, so treat the specific figures as practitioner heuristics, not audited benchmarks. (src: 16vc.substack.com (16vc.substack.com/p/when-is-your-startup-ready-to-raise); Lenny's Newsletter (lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-waitlist-conversion))
- [contested] Less than 10% landing-page CTA conversion on a targeted-ad audience is a practitioner heuristic indicating the problem is not urgent enough for the segment (Qubit Capital PMF framework) -- Qubit Capital does operate a PMF framework blog at qubit.capital/blog/assess-product-market-fit and qubit.capital/blog/prove-product-market-fit-user-metrics-software. However, neither article mentions a 10% landing-page CTA conversion threshold as a PMF signal. The articles discuss NPS, MRR growth, retention, and Sean Ellis test metrics, not landing page conversion floors. The specific '<10% CTA conversion = problem not urgent' heuristic could not be traced to any Qubit Capital publication. General landing page benchmarks from other sources (Landerlab, MADX, SaasHero) show average landing page conversion at 2-5%, making a 10% floor an unusually high threshold to call 'weak signal.' (src: Qubit Capital PMF article (qubit.capital/blog/assess-product-market-fit); Landerlab landing page benchmarks (landerlab.io/blog/landing-page-conversion-rate))
- [contested] 'No market need' is cited as a cause of failure in 34% of startup post-mortems (Failory, 80+ cases) and 42% (CB Insights, 111 cases) -- The CB Insights 42% figure is well-confirmed: their 2014-2021 study of 110+ startup post-mortems found 'no market need' as the top reason at 42%, updated to 43% in their 2024 study of 431 companies. However, the 34% figure attributed to Failory (80+ cases) cannot be corroborated. Failory's page on failed startups lists 42 no-market-need cases but states no percentage figure and specifies no study methodology or total sample size. No independent source surfaces a Failory post-mortem study yielding a 34% figure. The Failory attribution appears to lack a traceable primary source. (src: CB Insights startup failure analysis (preuve.ai/blog/why-startups-fail-market-fit citing CB Insights); Failory no-market-need page (failory.com/startups/no-market-need-failures))
- [refuted] Only 11% of startups that raised seed funding since 2020 successfully reached the next funding stage by mid-2025 (cited in 16vc.substack.com) -- The 16vc.substack.com article does not contain an 11% figure for seed-to-next-stage progression anywhere in its text. Crunchbase data, which is the standard authoritative source for this metric, shows the 2020 seed cohort had roughly 50% reach post-seed funding -- substantially higher. Even the weakest recent cohorts (2022-2024) show 16-24% progression rates, not 11%. For SaaS specifically, Crunchbase cites 12% for early 2022 cohorts within a two-year window, but this is a narrower metric (SaaS only, within two years) that is not equivalent to the broad claim. No source reviewed supports the specific 11% figure attributed to 16vc.substack.com. (src: Crunchbase seed cohort data (news.crunchbase.com/seed/data-bigger-deals-longer-seriesa-2026/); 16vc.substack.com article verified not to contain the 11% statistic (16vc.substack.com/p/when-is-your-startup-ready-to-raise))

---
### 12. Painted-door feature test

- **Category:** Solution & concept
- **Stage:** solution
- **Definition:** A painted-door test (also called a fake-door or smoke test) presents users with a realistic UI entry point -- a button, menu item, pricing tier, or ad -- for a feature or product that does not yet exist, then measures the click-through rate and downstream engagement (email capture, survey completion) when users hit the "door." The term is drawn from Alberto Savoia's pretotyping framework, where it is defined as testing "initial level of interest" by creating artifacts that suggest a product exists to see if people would engage with it, without building anything real. Unlike a survey or interview, it captures behavioral intent rather than stated preference.
- **Validates:** Does genuine behavioral demand exist for this feature or product idea -- before any engineering investment?

**Protocol**
1. Identify the riskiest demand assumption: name the specific feature or product variant and the user segment most likely to need it. Write a falsifiable XYZ hypothesis before touching anything (Savoia's format: 'At least X% of [segment] will click the [trigger] within [time window]').
2. Choose the test surface: in-product (button/nav item/pricing row) for existing users, or a standalone landing page with paid traffic for net-new audiences. In-product tests yield cleaner behavioral signal; landing pages add ad-creative confounds.
3. Build the door, not the room: add the UI element that would exist if the feature were real. No backend logic, no data model. One analytics event on click is the entire engineering requirement. Deploy behind a feature flag to the target segment only.
4. Build the honest disclosure: the click must surface an immediate, transparent message -- 'This feature is in development; join the early-access list' -- with an email-capture or 2-question survey. Never leave the user in a dead end. The disclosure page is also a recruitment instrument for follow-up interviews.
5. Pre-register your pass/fail threshold and minimum exposure window before launch. A commonly used practitioner heuristic is 5% CTR as the signal floor for a non-promoted in-product element, but the threshold must be calibrated to your product context and set before seeing data.
6. Run until you hit statistical significance or exhaust the exposure window. For two-variant painted-door A/B tests, the standard significance threshold is alpha = 0.05; required sample sizes for conversion rates around 5-8% typically run into the thousands (a two-variant smart-home product case documented ~6,800 participants for 14 days to distinguish 8.33% vs 5.50% conversion).
7. Segment results by user cohort before concluding. An aggregate 3% CTR masking 12% among power users and 1% among casual users is a strong signal for a premium feature, not a failed test.
8. Close the loop: email every person who clicked -- whether the test passes or fails -- with an update. This preserves trust, yields qualitative 'why did you click?' data, and seeds a beta recruitment list.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Weak demand signal: CTR threshold below which teams typically re-examine placement or kill idea: below 2% CTR (in-product, non-promoted element) (src: Practitioner consensus across learningloop.io, userintuition.ai, gethorizon.net -- no single primary study; treat as convention not empirical finding)
- Moderate demand signal: CTR range warranting segment investigation rather than binary go/no-go: 2-5% CTR (src: Practitioner consensus across multiple product management publications; no peer-reviewed source)
- Strong demand signal: CTR floor for greenlighting an MVP build (commonly cited practitioner heuristic): above 5% CTR (src: Practitioner convention; cited in gethorizon.net, learningloop.io, userintuition.ai -- not an empirically established threshold)
- Statistical significance threshold for two-variant painted-door A/B test: alpha = 0.05 (5% acceptable error rate) (src: gethorizon.net two-variant analysis guide, consistent with standard statistical practice)
- Sample size for distinguishing 8.33% vs 5.50% conversion rates in a two-variant painted-door test: approximately 6,800 participants over 14 days (src: gethorizon.net smart vacuum case study)
- Dropbox demo video: beta waitlist size before test: approximately 5,000 users (src: Multiple sources citing Ries, The Lean Startup (2011) and Drew Houston interviews)
- Dropbox demo video: beta waitlist size after overnight video release (painted-door / smoke test): 75,000 sign-ups overnight (src: Widely reported; yourstory.com, chameleon.io, learningloop.io -- originally from Drew Houston's public accounts and Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011))
- Real fake door test: Instagram ad CTR for highest-performing campaign variant (Dan Kim, 2023): 16.5% CTR on 'Get prizes' campaign variant (src: Dan Kim, Medium / Design Bootcamp, first-person case study)
- Real fake door test: downstream form conversion rate despite 16.5% CTR (Dan Kim): 2.47% (3 sign-ups from 121 form visitors) vs. pre-set goal of greater than 10% (src: Dan Kim, Medium / Design Bootcamp, first-person case study)
- Real fake door test: team-set pass threshold for sales conversion (recipe pack case): 5% conversion rate; actual result was 3.9% (3,700 clicks from 96,000 impressions) -- below threshold, idea parked (src: Reported in userintuition.ai reference guide)
- Startup failure rate attributed to no market need (context for why the test exists): approximately 35% of startups fail due to no market need (src: CB Insights failure analysis, cited in koji.so guide)
- Software features rarely or never used (Standish Group CHAOS Report): 64% of software features are rarely or never used (src: Standish Group CHAOS Report, cited in koji.so guide; Pendo later reported 80%)

**Origin and evidence:** The technique was named and systematized by Alberto Savoia (Google's first Engineering Director) in his pretotyping framework, first published as the free manifesto "Pretotype It" (2011) and expanded in "The Right It" (HarperCollins, 2019). Savoia named the "Fake Door" as one of eight pretotype types, defining it around measuring "Initial Level of Interest" (ILI) before any build investment. Eric Ries independently articulated the equivalent concept as a "smoke test" in "The Lean Startup" (2011), with Dropbox's demo video as the canonical example. The evidence base is almost entirely practitioner case studies and first-person accounts rather than peer-reviewed experimental literature; no randomized controlled trial establishes the 5% CTR threshold as empirically validated. The technique's face validity is strong -- it measures behavior rather than self-report -- but the specific thresholds in circulation are convention, not science.

**Canonical sources:** Alberto Savoia, 'Pretotype It' (free manifesto, 2011) and 'The Right It: Why So Many Ideas Fail and How to Make Sure Yours Succeed' (HarperCollins, 2019) -- originator of the 'Fake Door' pretotype name and ILI measurement framing; Eric Ries, 'The Lean Startup' (Crown Business, 2011) -- popularized the equivalent 'smoke test' concept; Dropbox demo video as primary example; Alberto Savoia, 'Summary of Pretotyping Techniques' cheat sheet at albertosavoia.com -- the McSpaghetti example and formal ILI definition; Ash Maurya, 'Running Lean' (O'Reilly, 2012) -- integrates smoke/fake-door tests into the Lean Canvas validation cycle

**Failure modes**
- Setting the threshold after seeing data: post-hoc threshold adjustment turns any result into apparent validation. The threshold must be locked before launch.
- Curiosity clicks vs. intent clicks: a prominent new element in a familiar UI generates novelty-driven clicks that are orthogonal to purchase or usage intent. This is especially severe when the door is visually prominent (bright button, top-of-nav placement) rather than contextually embedded.
- Wrong metric as primary: optimizing for CTR when downstream commitment (email capture, pre-payment, survey completion) is the real signal. Dan Kim's case: 16.5% CTR collapsed to 2.47% actual conversion, showing CTR and intent are separable.
- Insufficient follow-up: a click without a follow-up interview reveals nothing about the job-to-be-done. Teams that build based on CTR alone without qualitative follow-up risk building the wrong implementation of a real need.
- Trust destruction from deception: in B2B enterprise contexts or regulated industries (healthcare, finance), users who feel deceived by a missing feature can churn or escalate to legal and compliance. Particularly acute when the 'door' appears in a paying customer's production workflow.
- Small or biased exposure cohort: testing on early adopters or power users yields CTR that does not generalize to the broader user population. The signal is strong for a niche feature but misleading as a universal product decision.
- Short run duration leading to statistical underpower: terminating the test before adequate sample size inflates false-positive rates; p-values below 0.05 require thousands of exposures for typical in-product CTR ranges.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false-positive mechanism is novelty-driven or ambient curiosity clicks: any new, visually distinct UI element in a familiar interface will attract clicks from users exploring the UI rather than expressing demand for the underlying feature. A user clicking 'Export to PDF' may be satisfying curiosity about what a new button does, not expressing a genuine workflow need. This produces an inflated CTR that decays to near-zero actual usage once the feature ships. A secondary false-positive pathway is aspirational intent: users click because they can imagine the feature would be useful 'in theory' but would not actually change their workflow to use it. Both mechanisms are suppressed -- but not eliminated -- by requiring a higher-commitment downstream action (email capture, pre-payment, survey completion) as the primary metric rather than the raw click.

**Examples**
- Dropbox: Before building file-sync infrastructure, Drew Houston posted a 4-minute demo video on a landing page showing features not yet built. The beta waitlist jumped from approximately 5,000 to 75,000 sign-ups overnight. This is the canonical smoke-test / painted-door case in Eric Ries's 'The Lean Startup.' In a later variation, Dropbox placed a fake 'photo sharing' entry point in the homepage and monitored clicks to prioritize roadmap features.
- Groupon: Andrew Mason validated the group-buying concept in approximately one month by building nothing more than a WordPress blog (getyourgroupon.com) skinned to say 'Groupon.' Each day's deal was a blog post with a Flash widget from The Point. Coupon PDFs were generated in FileMaker and emailed via Apple Mail. At peak they were manually emailing 500 sushi coupons per day. No real e-commerce platform existed. Two years after this fake-door MVP, the business passed $100 million in sales.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is a CTR meaningfully above your pre-registered threshold (practitioner convention: above 5% for a non-promoted in-product element), sustained over a statistically adequate exposure window, concentrated in your target user segment, and followed by high downstream commitment rate (email capture or survey completion above 20% of clickers). Ideally, multiple qualitative follow-ups confirm the job-to-be-done matches your intended solution. Dropbox's waitlist jump from 5,000 to 75,000 overnight is the archetype: an order-of-magnitude overperformance against a pre-set target, combined with qualitative confirmation that the problem was real.
- Weak signal: A weak result is CTR below your pre-registered threshold, or high CTR that collapses at the downstream commitment step (Dan Kim's case: 16.5% CTR to 2.47% form conversion against a 10% goal). Before killing the idea, run three diagnostic checks: (1) was the element contextually visible or buried? (2) did the description accurately communicate the feature's value prop, or was it ambiguous? (3) was the exposure cohort actually the target segment? A weak result on a buried settings-page element is not the same as a weak result on a prominent homepage CTA. Reposition and rerun once before concluding demand is absent.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Dropbox beta waitlist grew from approximately 5,000 to 75,000 sign-ups overnight after the demo video fake-door test -- widely reported, sourced to Drew Houston accounts and Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011) -- Drew Houston's own words, quoted directly in a 2011 TechCrunch article written by Eric Ries: 'Our beta waiting list went from 5,000 people to 75,000 people literally overnight.' The figure is primary-source, not just secondary repetition. The 5,000 baseline and 75,000 endpoint are corroborated across multiple independent outlets tracing back to this same Houston quote. No authoritative source contradicts these numbers. (src: https://techcrunch.com/2011/10/19/dropbox-minimal-viable-product/ (Eric Ries, TechCrunch, 2011))
- [contested] 5% CTR is the commonly cited practitioner threshold for a 'strong signal' in an in-product painted-door test -- practitioner convention, not an empirically established figure -- The 5% threshold does appear across multiple practitioner guides (learningloop.io, userintuition.ai, gethorizon.net) and is therefore accurately described as a practitioner convention. However it has no peer-reviewed or empirical foundation. The claim's own caveat ('practitioner convention, not empirically established') is accurate and is the right characterisation. No independent authority challenges the convention itself, but no study validates it either. 'Contested' reflects the absence of an empirical basis rather than a factual error. (src: https://www.userintuition.ai/reference-guides/fake-door-testing-validate-demand-zero-code/ ; https://learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing)
- [contested] 2-5% CTR range is cited as 'moderate signal' warranting segment investigation -- practitioner convention -- The 2-5% range as 'moderate signal' is repeated consistently across practitioner sources (userintuition.ai, learningloop.io, gethorizon.net) and the claim correctly self-labels it as convention. However it is not empirically validated and the threshold boundaries are arbitrary. No primary study is cited by any of these sources. Correctly flagged as convention in the original claim. (src: https://www.userintuition.ai/reference-guides/fake-door-testing-validate-demand-zero-code/)
- [contested] Below 2% CTR is cited as a 'weak signal' -- practitioner convention -- Same status as the 2-5% moderate and above-5% strong tiers: the sub-2% 'weak signal' boundary appears in practitioner guides but has no empirical grounding. The claim correctly self-labels it as convention. Worth noting that userintuition.ai actually places the 'weak demand' floor at below 1%, not below 2%, showing that even practitioner sources do not fully agree on the boundary. (src: https://www.userintuition.ai/reference-guides/fake-door-testing-validate-demand-zero-code/)
- [confirmed] Dan Kim's Instagram fake-door test: 16.5% CTR on best-performing ad variant (Campaign C), 2.47% overall form conversion rate (3 sign-ups from 121 visitors), against a pre-set goal of greater than 10% conversion -- first-person case study, Medium/Design Bootcamp -- Direct fetch of the article confirms every figure exactly: Campaign C (Rewards) achieved 16.5% CTR; 3 sign-ups from 121 form visitors = 2.47% conversion; stated goal was greater than 10%. The article is a verifiable first-person account published November 2023 on Medium's Design Bootcamp. All three numerical claims are internally consistent and match the fetched content. (src: https://medium.com/design-bootcamp/my-fake-door-test-got-a-16-5-ctr-and-a-2-47-conversion-rate-heres-what-i-learned-f655a649c4e2)
- [contested] Recipe-pack fake-door test: 3,700 clicks from 96,000 impressions = 3.9% conversion, below team's 5% threshold -- reported in userintuition.ai guide -- The numbers (96,000 impressions, 3,700 clicks, 3.9%, 5% threshold) are confirmed in productspike.org, not userintuition.ai -- the claim's stated source is wrong. The productspike.org article by Mark Wheatley presents this as a personal consulting engagement with an unnamed 'world's largest kitchen appliance manufacturer'. It is a single anonymous practitioner account that cannot be independently corroborated. The arithmetic (3,700/96,000 = 3.85%, rounded to 3.9%) is correct. Verdict is contested because the source attribution is inaccurate and the underlying case study is unverifiable beyond one uncorroborated first-person account. (src: https://www.productspike.org/post/how-to-run-a-fake-door-test (actual source); userintuition.ai does not contain this case study)
- [confirmed] Two-variant painted-door A/B test distinguishing 8.33% vs 5.50% conversion required approximately 6,800 participants over 14 days at alpha = 0.05 -- gethorizon.net case study -- Direct fetch of the gethorizon.net article confirmed all four elements: 8.33% conversion (Landing Page A, 150 EUR), 5.50% conversion (Landing Page B, 180 EUR), sample size of 'around 6,800 people in total divided into the two landing pages', 14-day test period, and alpha = 0.05 explicitly stated. Every numeric claim matches the source. (src: https://www.gethorizon.net/blog/making-informed-product-decisions-a-comprehensive-guide-to-analyze-painted-door-tests-with-two-variants)
- [contested] CB Insights: approximately 35% of startups fail due to no market need -- cited in koji.so guide, originally from CB Insights failure analysis -- CB Insights has published multiple iterations of this analysis and the figure shifts across versions. The original and most widely cited CB Insights figure is 42% (from the ~101-startup post-mortem study, ~2014-2018). The 35% figure appears in a later updated version where methodology and sample changed and 'ran out of cash' overtook 'no market need' as the top reason. The 2024 update puts it at 43%. The 35% is technically sourced to a real CB Insights report but is not the canonical or most-cited figure, making the claim misleadingly precise. Koji.so links to CB Insights but the specific 35% figure requires the updated 2021 report rather than the primary study. (src: https://preuve.ai/blog/why-startups-fail-market-fit ; CB Insights original report (42%) vs 2021 update (35%))
- [contested] Standish Group CHAOS Report: 64% of software features are rarely or never used; Pendo later reported 80% -- cited in koji.so guide -- Both percentages are real but the CHAOS Report attribution is inaccurate. The 64% figure originated from Jim Johnson (Standish Group chairman) at the XP 2002 conference in Sardinia, based on a study of only four internal applications -- it was not in the CHAOS Report. Mike Cohn confirmed this directly with the Standish Group. The figure has been widely misattributed to the CHAOS Report for over 20 years. The Pendo 80% is correctly attributed: it comes from Pendo's 2019 Feature Adoption Report, based on aggregated anonymized usage data from their customer base. The 80% claim is confirmed; the CHAOS Report attribution for 64% is refuted. (src: https://www.mountaingoatsoftware.com/blog/are-64-of-features-really-rarely-or-never-used ; https://www.pendo.io/resources/the-2019-feature-adoption-report/)
- [confirmed] Groupon passed $100 million in sales approximately two years after its WordPress blog MVP launch -- multiple sources citing Andrew Mason interviews -- Groupon launched on a WordPress-skinned site in November 2008. Annual revenue was approximately $15 million in 2009 and $313 million in 2010. The $100 million cumulative or threshold mark was crossed during 2010, roughly 18-24 months after launch -- consistent with 'approximately two years'. The WordPress MVP origin is confirmed by multiple sources including the Mixergy interview. The claim is broadly accurate though the exact month of crossing $100M in cumulative sales is not pinpointed in available sources. (src: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groupon ; https://mixergy.com/interviews/andrew-mason-groupon-interview/)
- [confirmed] Groupon manually emailed 500 sushi coupon PDFs per day at peak of its fake-door MVP phase -- Mixergy interview, Andrew Mason -- Andrew Mason's Mixergy interview confirms the manual PDF email process: coupons were generated in FileMaker and emailed via Apple Mail, reaching 500 PDFs per day (specifically described as '500 sushi coupons in a day'). The Mixergy interview is the primary source and multiple secondary accounts (alexanderjarvis.com, cow-shed.com) corroborate the detail. The specific 'sushi' reference and 'Apple Mail' delivery mechanism are consistent across all accounts tracing back to Mason. (src: https://mixergy.com/interviews/andrew-mason-groupon-interview/)

### 13. Pre-sales / pre-orders with real money

- **Category:** Demand & willingness-to-pay
- **Stage:** demand
- **Definition:** Pre-sales (also called pre-orders or demand pre-validation) is the practice of soliciting and accepting real financial commitments from customers before a product is fully built, using those transactions as evidence of genuine willingness to pay rather than hypothetical interest. The technique is distinct from surveys, waitlists, or interviews: money is the signal, not stated intent. It encompasses landing-page smoke tests that accept payment, crowdfunding campaigns, signed letters of intent with deposits, paid pilot agreements, and the demo-sell-build sequence where a team sells a prototype before writing production code.
- **Validates:** Does a specific customer segment have sufficient pain and willingness to pay to support a viable business, before significant engineering investment is made?

**Protocol**
1. Crystallize the riskiest assumption: write a single falsifiable hypothesis of the form 'Customer segment X will pay $Y for outcome Z' before designing any test.
2. Build the minimum pitch surface: a focused landing page, a 3-4 minute demo video (Dropbox's model), or a direct outreach deck that describes the outcome, not the implementation. Do NOT build the product first.
3. Define a threshold in advance: specify the minimum number of commitments or revenue amount that constitutes validation (e.g., 'If we receive $1,000 in pre-orders within two weeks, we proceed'). Ash Maurya's Running Lean calls this a 'minimum success criterion'; Tom Morkes documents $500 and $1,000 pre-order gates used in practice.
4. Drive qualified traffic: reach the exact target segment via warm outreach, niche communities, or tightly targeted ads. Do not test on friends, family, or your email list if that audience differs from your real buyer; personal networks produce social-desirability bias and false positives.
5. Ask for real commitment: present pricing and ask for full pre-payment, a deposit, a signed letter of intent (B2B), or a paid pilot agreement. For B2B, an LOI with a defined dollar amount is the minimum; an actual purchase order or deposit is stronger. Rob Fitzpatrick in The Mom Test defines a valid commitment as giving up something with real cost: time, reputation, or money.
6. If payment is declined, run the Mom Test: ask why they would not commit, probe past behavior ('When did you last pay for something like this?'), and identify whether the objection is price, urgency, or existence of the problem. Absence of commitment is signal, not failure.
7. Fulfill or transparently refund: if the pre-sale threshold is not met, refund all deposits immediately. If met, deliver on the commitment or at minimum over-communicate the timeline. Reputation risk is real: crowdfunding campaigns that raise money and fail to ship (e.g., Coolest Cooler, Juicero) destroy trust permanently.
8. Analyze the result against your criterion: count only unrelated-buyer commitments (not friends or co-workers), check whether buyers came from your target segment, and note whether the commitment required direct founder selling or converted from cold traffic -- the latter is a stronger signal of scalability.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Tom Morkes pre-order gate for low-priced info product: $500 in pre-orders before proceeding to development (src: Tom Morkes, 'Lean Launch: How To Sell An Idea Before You Build It (a $41,000 Case Study)', tommorkes.com)
- Tom Morkes pre-order gate for higher-priced product (Insurgent Publishing): $1,000 in pre-orders before proceeding (src: Tom Morkes, 'Lean Launch: How To Sell An Idea Before You Build It (a $41,000 Case Study)', tommorkes.com)
- Demand validation commitment rate threshold (Lean Foundry 90-day framework): 80% or higher commitment rate from qualified early adopters (src: Ash Maurya / Lean Foundry, 'How to Validate Any Startup Idea in 90 Days', leanfoundry.com, Jul 31 2025)
- Problem validation interview sample (Lean Foundry 90-day framework): 10-15 customer interviews; at least 30% must show high problem intensity (src: Lean Foundry, 'How to Validate Any Startup Idea in 90 Days', leanfoundry.com, Jul 31 2025)
- Solution validation interest rate (Lean Foundry 90-day framework): At least 30% of fresh prospects show strong interest in the proposed solution (src: Lean Foundry, 'How to Validate Any Startup Idea in 90 Days', leanfoundry.com, Jul 31 2025)
- Dropbox pre-launch waitlist growth via demo video (smoke test benchmark): Waitlist grew from 5,000 to 75,000 signups in one day after video posted to Hacker News / Digg (no paid ads) (src: Drew Houston / Dropbox case study; documented in Eric Ries, The Lean Startup, and multiple post-mortems (thriveandgrow.com, shortform.com))
- Kickstarter overall project success rate: 39.11% of launched projects successfully funded (as of 2026 cumulative data) (src: SearchLogistics Kickstarter Stats & Facts 2026, searchlogistics.com)
- Kickstarter campaigns securing 20% of goal within 48 hours: 78% chance of reaching overall funding goal (src: SearchLogistics Kickstarter Stats & Facts 2026, searchlogistics.com)
- Kickstarter campaigns with video vs without: 50-54% success rate with video vs 30-39% without (src: SearchLogistics Kickstarter Stats & Facts 2026, searchlogistics.com)
- Kickstarter successful projects raising over $100,000: Only 4.2% of successfully funded Kickstarter projects raise over $100,000 (src: SearchLogistics Kickstarter Stats & Facts 2026, searchlogistics.com)
- B2B intent signal false positive rate: 52% of sales professionals report frequent false positives from third-party intent data (src: parallelhq.com / prospeo.io, 'What is Market Validation? The Complete 2026 Strategy Guide')

**Origin and evidence:** The technique was named and systematized in Eric Ries's The Lean Startup (2011), where a pre-order is defined as a canonical smoke test: 'A smoke test allows customers to pre-order a yet-to-be-built product to establish whether customers are interested in buying it.' The underlying principle -- that money is the only credible commitment signal -- appears explicitly in Rob Fitzpatrick's The Mom Test (2013): 'compliments are the fool's gold of customer learning: shiny, distracting, and worthless'; real validation requires giving up something of cost (money, time, or reputation). Ash Maurya's Running Lean (2012, 3rd ed. 2022) operationalizes it as a four-stage process culminating in obtaining 'pre-orders, signed letters of intent, or agreements to participate in beta testing' before building. Alberto Savoia's pretotyping framework (The Right It, 2019, originally Google internal, 2011) formalizes the mechanic as the 'Fake Door' pretotype: create a landing page asserting the product exists and measure how many people attempt to buy. The body of evidence is practitioner-documented case studies (Dropbox, Morkes) rather than peer-reviewed randomized trials; no RCT exists. Confidence in the directional claim ('money beats stated intent') is high given convergence across independent practitioners, but specific conversion-rate thresholds are heuristics, not statistically validated benchmarks.

**Canonical sources:** Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (Crown Business, 2011) -- smoke test / pre-order as MVP; Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) -- commitment hierarchy: money > time > reputation; compliments as false signal; Ash Maurya, Running Lean (O'Reilly, 2012; 3rd ed. 2022) -- demo-sell-build sequence; four-stage validation ending in pre-orders or LOIs; Alberto Savoia, The Right It (HarperOne, 2019) and pretotyping.org -- Fake Door pretotype; 'Market Engagement Score'; Tom Morkes, 'Lean Launch: How To Sell An Idea Before You Build It (a $41,000 Case Study)', tommorkes.com -- documented pre-order gates at $500 and $1,000; Y Combinator Startup Library, 'Order of Operations for Starting a Startup', ycombinator.com/library/61 -- pre-launch validation sequencing

**Failure modes**
- Setting the threshold after seeing results: founders who do not define a minimum success criterion in advance rationalize whatever they get as sufficient validation.
- Testing on the wrong audience: selling to personal networks, co-workers, or warm contacts who are motivated by social obligation rather than genuine product need. This is the single most common false positive source.
- Discounting refusals: when prospects decline to pre-order, founders frequently attribute it to price sensitivity or timing rather than lack of real demand, and proceed anyway.
- Crowdfunding as popularity contest: Kickstarter success driven by community novelty, niche enthusiasm (e.g., tabletop gaming), or viral shareability does not necessarily translate to sustained commercial demand at full retail price.
- LOI inflation in B2B: letters of intent are non-binding and frequently signed by champions who cannot actually authorize budget; the real buyer (economic decision-maker) has not committed.
- Hardware tinkerer distortion (identified by Sandy Kory): early hardware pre-orders disproportionately attract hobbyists and early adopters, not the mass market segment, producing an inflated demand signal.
- Founder-charisma substitution in enterprise: Sandy Kory documents that enterprise pre-sales of up to 10-20 customers and $1-2M in revenue can occur on founder relationships alone, masking the absence of a scalable sales motion.
- Refund-at-delivery collapse: customers who pre-order a future product and wait 12-24 months have high cancellation rates if expectations are not managed; revenue recognized at pre-sale is not necessarily retained revenue.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false positive mechanism is social obligation substituting for genuine demand: buyers who are friends, colleagues, or admirers of the founder pay to support the person, not the product. A secondary mechanism is novelty bias: early adopters pay to be first, not because the product solves a recurring, economically significant pain -- these customers do not represent the mainstream market. A third mechanism is Fitzpatrick's 'compliment laundering': when founders ASK 'would you buy this?' at the end of an interview, polite prospects say yes and may even pay a small deposit to avoid conflict, but they never become active users or repeat buyers. The result is a pre-sale number that clears the threshold while the actual addressable market remains unvalidated.

**Examples**
- Dropbox (Drew Houston, 2007-2008): Before the product was publicly available, Houston posted a 3-4 minute narrated demo video to Hacker News and Digg showing file syncing that did not yet exist at scale. The beta waitlist grew from 5,000 to 75,000 signups in a single day with no paid advertising. This is the canonical smoke test (non-monetary pre-registration) cited by Eric Ries in The Lean Startup as the legitimate MVP for a product where building a working prototype would have taken months.
- Tom Morkes / The Flight Formula (2014): Morkes pre-sold an in-person business incubator program at a $10,000 price point using only a sales page and a live webinar, generating $41,000 in gross revenue before any content or curriculum was built. He had previously used $500 and $1,000 in pre-order receipts as explicit go/no-go gates for lower-priced products, establishing documented thresholds for when pre-sales constitute sufficient demand validation.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is: (a) pre-sales from buyers who are NOT in the founder's personal network, reached through cold or near-cold channels, (b) who paid a price approximating the intended commercial price (not a 90%-discounted 'founding member' rate that no one would pay at launch), (c) with a commitment rate that met or exceeded the pre-defined minimum success criterion before results were inspected, and (d) where the buyer explicitly articulates a specific, recurring pain the product addresses. In Lean Foundry's framework, an 80%+ commitment rate from qualified early adopters on a defined offer meets this bar. Tom Morkes' $1,000+ pre-order gate (for a product with real overhead) is a documented minimum for lower-ticket items.
- Weak signal: A weak result is: pre-sales that required direct founder persuasion on every transaction, buyers who are in the founder's existing network or community, commitment rates below 30% of people pitched (indicating the pitch surface, not the product, may be the limiting factor), or pre-orders at a heavily discounted 'beta' price where buyers have explicitly stated they would not pay full commercial pricing. LOIs without deposit or budget authorization from the economic decision-maker (in B2B) are also weak: they validate the champion's enthusiasm, not the organization's willingness to spend.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Dropbox waitlist grew from 5,000 to 75,000 signups in one day after demo video posted to Hacker News/Digg (no paid ads) -- The 5,000 to 75,000 overnight figure is confirmed by Drew Houston's own quote published in TechCrunch (Oct 2011): 'Our beta waiting list went from 5,000 people to 75,000 people literally overnight.' One caveat: the 75k jump was driven by the *second* demo video (March 2008), posted primarily to Digg (and Reddit), not the first video posted to Hacker News. The claim conflates the two videos and omits Reddit, but the core numbers are accurate and traceable to a primary source. (src: https://techcrunch.com/2011/10/19/dropbox-minimal-viable-product/ (Eric Ries / Drew Houston quote, TechCrunch 2011))
- [confirmed] Tom Morkes used $500 in pre-orders as his go/no-go gate for a low-priced product -- The primary source article on tommorkes.com states: 'over $500 in preorders was enough for me to validate the idea' — referring to The Complete Guide to Pay What You Want Pricing. The claim accurately reflects the article. (src: https://tommorkes.com/lean-launch-how-to-sell-an-idea-before-you-build-it/)
- [confirmed] Tom Morkes used $1,000 in pre-orders as his go/no-go gate for Insurgent Publishing -- The tommorkes.com article states: '$1,000 in preorders = enough to validate the idea' for Insurgent Publishing (The Creative Entrepreneur journal). The claim accurately reflects the source. (src: https://tommorkes.com/lean-launch-how-to-sell-an-idea-before-you-build-it/)
- [contested] Tom Morkes generated $41,000 in pre-sales for The Flight Formula before any curriculum was built -- The $41,000 figure and the 'sell before you build' methodology are confirmed by the tommorkes.com article (it is the article's subtitle). However, the page does not explicitly confirm that *all* $41,000 came before *any* curriculum was built -- the article describes a lean launch approach where the event/curriculum outline existed and a sales page was created before the full program was built. The framing 'before any curriculum was built' may overstate the pre-build state. The number itself is real; the 'zero curriculum' qualifier is not definitively confirmed. (src: https://tommorkes.com/lean-launch-how-to-sell-an-idea-before-you-build-it/)
- [confirmed] Lean Foundry / Ash Maurya 90-day framework: 80% or higher commitment rate from qualified early adopters is the demand validation threshold -- The Lean Foundry newsletter article (Jul 31, 2025) states verbatim: 'An 80% or higher commitment rate from qualified early adopters proves you've validated demand.' The claim accurately quotes the source. (src: https://www.leanfoundry.com/lean-1-2-3/jul-31-2025)
- [confirmed] Lean Foundry framework: 10-15 customer interviews required; at least 30% must show high problem intensity to proceed to solution validation -- The Lean Foundry Jul 31, 2025 article states: 'Run 10-15 systematic customer interviews' and 'You need at least 30% of interviews showing high problem intensity — people actively frustrated and looking for solutions.' Both thresholds are explicitly stated in the cited source. The 10-15 interview number also appears in Ash Maurya's book Running Lean. (src: https://www.leanfoundry.com/lean-1-2-3/jul-31-2025)
- [confirmed] Lean Foundry framework: at least 30% of fresh prospects must show strong interest in proposed solution -- The Lean Foundry Jul 31, 2025 article states verbatim: 'You need at least 30% showing strong interest to prove problem-solution fit.' The claim accurately reflects the source. (src: https://www.leanfoundry.com/lean-1-2-3/jul-31-2025)
- [confirmed] Kickstarter overall project success rate: 39.11% (cumulative through 2026) -- SearchLogistics cites 39.11% as the cumulative success rate. Statista data (Jan 2025) puts it at ~41.98%, and Kickstarter's own stats page shows ~42.74% as of their latest data. The 39.11% figure is a real number from a named source (SearchLogistics 2026), but it sits at the lower end of the credible range. The claim is substantively accurate though the precise decimal figure varies by source and snapshot date. (src: https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/ ; https://www.statista.com/statistics/235405/kickstarter-project-funding-success-rate/)
- [contested] Kickstarter campaigns that secure 20% of funding goal within first 48 hours have a 78% chance of reaching their full goal -- The ~78-79% figure for projects that raise over 20% of their goal is corroborated by Kickstarter's own data and cited in multiple sources including The Crowd-sourceress. However, the specific '48 hours' qualifier is an addition not confirmed by Kickstarter's official statistics page, which says 'raised more than 20% of their goal' without a time window. The 78% success rate figure is real; the '48 hours' framing appears to be a common embellishment added by secondary commentators. (src: https://kickstarter.com/help/stats ; https://glowreports.com/en/articles/cracking-the-crowdfunding-code-what-109-million-in-kickstarter-data-reveals-about-building-winning-campaigns-in-2025-2)
- [confirmed] Kickstarter campaigns with video have 50-54% success rate vs 30-39% without video -- The range 50-54% with video vs 30-39% without is directly supported by Kickstarter's own blog data (which originally cited 54% vs 39%) and is corroborated by SearchLogistics 2026 and multiple independent analyses. Kickstarter's own blog post 'The Importance of Video' is the primary source for these figures. (src: https://www.kickstarter.com/blog/the-importance-of-video ; https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/)
- [confirmed] Only 4.2% of successfully funded Kickstarter projects raise over $100,000 -- SearchLogistics 2026 explicitly states 'just 4.2% of successful projects raise over $100k.' This is corroborated by Statista's distribution data showing 11,866 projects in the $100k-$999k range out of ~292,605 total successfully funded projects, which works out to approximately 4.1%. The figure is consistent across sources. (src: https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/ ; https://www.statista.com/statistics/235397/distribution-of-kickstarter-funds-raised-by-successful-projects)
- [contested] 52% of sales professionals report frequent false positives from B2B intent data -- The figure appears on fiftyfiveandfive.com (a B2B marketing agency) and has been widely recycled by prospeo.io and other content marketing sites. However, no traceable primary source -- no named survey, no research organization, no methodology -- is cited by any of these pages. The fiftyfiveandfive.com page presents it without attribution. This is a statistic that circulates without a verifiable origin, making it a candidate for a fabricated or unverifiable marketing claim rather than a grounded research finding. (src: https://fiftyfiveandfive.com/resources/zoominfo-intent/ (no primary source cited); https://prospeo.io/s/buyer-intent-signals)
- [contested] One documented case (Lean Foundry case study): 12 out of 15 architects (80%) committed to paying $5,000/month before a line of code was written -- The numbers (12/15 architects, $5,000/month, 80% rate, $180K pre-launch revenue) appear in the Lean Foundry Jul 31, 2025 newsletter article as an illustrative example. However, this is a Lean Foundry marketing newsletter, not an independently published case study. No company name, founder name, or corroborating external source is given. Independent search found no external verification of this specific case. It reads as an embedded marketing vignette used to illustrate the framework's potential, not a verifiably documented third-party case study. (src: https://www.leanfoundry.com/lean-1-2-3/jul-31-2025 (sole source; no independent corroboration found))
