### 14. Refundable deposits

- **Category:** Demand & willingness-to-pay
- **Stage:** demand
- **Definition:** A refundable deposit is a time-bounded financial commitment -- typically a small fraction of the anticipated purchase price -- collected from prospective customers before a product exists or ships, on the explicit promise that it will be returned on request. It is used as a behavioral demand signal: the act of transferring real money creates measurable "skin in the game" that verbal or survey-based interest cannot replicate. The method is distinct from a non-refundable preorder (stronger signal, higher friction) and from a free waitlist signup (weaker signal, near-zero friction).
- **Validates:** Does sufficient real-money demand exist at a stated price point, prior to committing to build or manufacture at scale?

**Protocol**
1. Define a specific, falsifiable hypothesis: state the target segment, price point, deposit amount, collection window, and the minimum number of deposits that would constitute a pass (e.g., 'At least 100 customers in our target segment will place a $500 refundable deposit at a $3,000 product price within 30 days').
2. Build the minimum collection surface: a landing page that communicates the product concept, the deposit amount, the refund terms, and the expected delivery timeline. Use a payment processor (Stripe, etc.) to collect deposits with explicit written disclosure that the deposit is refundable.
3. Drive a defined, trackable traffic source to the page -- paid search, direct outreach to a list, personal networks, or a crowdfunding platform. Log the source for each deposit to measure channel signal quality.
4. Do not contact depositors to ask their intent or qualify them further during the collection window; doing so introduces interviewer-effect bias. Let the deposit stand alone as the behavioral signal.
5. At the close of the window, compute: (a) total deposit count, (b) deposit count vs. the pre-stated pass threshold, (c) deposit amount as a fraction of the full price, and (d) cancellation/refund requests received during the window.
6. Interview a random sample of depositors post-window to understand purchase motivations and willingness to proceed -- this qualitative layer surfaces the 'why' behind the behavioral signal without contaminating it.
7. Evaluate against the threshold: if the pass number is reached, treat it as evidence of demand sufficient to justify the next capital commitment. If it fails, do not rationalize -- reframe the hypothesis or pivot before proceeding.
8. Honor all refund requests unconditionally and promptly; legal and reputational risk from withheld refundable deposits is real, and any friction in the refund process converts the deposit into an involuntary commitment that pollutes future data.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Tesla Model 3 refundable deposit reservations in the first week (April 2016): 325,000 reservations at $1,000 each, implying ~$14 billion in potential future sales (src: Reuters / Tesla SEC filing (Form 8-K, 2016); confirmed by investing.com)
- Tesla Model 3 total reservations at peak: 450,000 reservations (src: Green Car Reports, sourcing Second Measure data, June 2018)
- Tesla Model 3 deposit cancellation rate as of June 2018: ~23% of reservation holders had received refunds (~103,500 of 450,000) (src: Green Car Reports / Second Measure analysis, June 2018)
- Tesla Roadster original deposit amount (2006): $5,000 refundable deposit (src: Alberto Savoia, 'The Right It' (referenced in FourWeekMBA and Precoil podcast summaries))
- Tesla CyberTruck deposit amount: $100 refundable deposit (src: Alberto Savoia, 'The Right It' (referenced in FourWeekMBA and Precoil podcast summaries))
- Subsequent Roadster deposit amount: $250,000 deposit (src: Alberto Savoia, 'The Right It' (referenced in FourWeekMBA and Precoil podcast summaries))
- Pebble smartwatch Kickstarter: funds raised in first 28 hours (at $99-$115 preorder pledge level): Over $1 million raised (100x the $100,000 goal hit within 28 hours) (src: CNBC, MIT Technology Review, Silicon UK (2012 campaign data))
- Pebble smartwatch Kickstarter: total funds raised in 30-day campaign (2012): $10.3 million from ~85,000 backers against a $100,000 goal (src: HBS Digital Innovation submission; Silicon UK)
- Pebble Time Kickstarter: time to reach $1 million in funding: 49 minutes (src: HEXUS.net / CNN Money, March 2015)
- Reservation-funnel (small deposit, e.g. $1) to paid conversion rate uplift vs. raw email list: Email list baseline ~1-3%; deposit-backed reservation funnel converts at 35-40% to full purchase (src: LaunchBoom / Prelaunch.marketing crowdfunding agency data)
- Kickstarter follower-to-backer average conversion rate: ~30% (mean and median) (src: Prelaunch.marketing / KickstarterForum.org data)
- Presell conversion benchmark (B2B, high-signal case study): architects committing to $5,000/month before product was built: 12 of 15 qualified prospects (80%) committed; $180K in pre-launch committed revenue (src: LeanFoundry / Ash Maurya LEAN 1-2-3 newsletter, July 2025 issue)

**Origin and evidence:** The method was formalized and named within Alberto Savoia's pretotyping framework (developed at Google, c. 2010-2012, published in 'The Right It', 2019), where it is the canonical illustration of his 'Skin in the Game Caliper' -- the claim that any commitment requiring real money, time, or reputation outweighs any amount of verbal or survey-based data. Eric Ries's 'The Lean Startup' (2011) and Ash Maurya's 'Running Lean' (2012) treat financial pre-commitments (preorders, deposits, letters of intent) as the definitive willingness-to-pay test, though neither names the deposit method specifically. The Tesla deposit sequence (Roadster $5,000 -> Model 3 $1,000 -> CyberTruck $100 -> second Roadster $250,000) became the go-to practitioner illustration after Savoia adopted it in his lectures. Evidence strength is practitioner-strong and empirically supported by the Tesla and Pebble cases, but no controlled academic study isolates the deposit method's predictive validity for startup survival specifically.

**Canonical sources:** Alberto Savoia, 'The Right It: Why So Many Ideas Fail and How to Make Sure Yours Succeed' (HarperCollins, 2019) -- primary source for the Skin in the Game Caliper and YODA principle; Eric Ries, 'The Lean Startup' (Crown Business, 2011) -- financial precommitments as validated learning; Ash Maurya, 'Running Lean' (O'Reilly, 2012, 2nd ed.) -- presell and letter of intent as demand validation steps; Alberto Savoia's pretotyping lectures and website (pretotyping.org); LaunchBoom / Prelaunch.marketing -- deposit-funnel conversion benchmark data from ~2,000 crowdfunding campaigns

**Failure modes**
- Deposit amount is too low relative to the product price, making it effectively costless to commit and therefore no more predictive than a free signup (e.g., a $1 deposit for a $50,000 product).
- The deposit is perceived as non-refundable despite stated terms, which inflates commitment artificially and conflates sunk-cost psychology with genuine demand.
- The test population is drawn from personal networks or founder communities (friends, colleagues, LinkedIn followers), who are socially incentivized to commit and unrepresentative of the real buyer pool.
- The team sets no pre-stated falsification threshold, allowing them to rationalize any deposit count as 'validation' after the fact -- the most common form of confirmation bias in the method.
- Depositors defect en masse when delivery timelines slip or specs change -- the Tesla Model 3 case (23% refund rate) demonstrates that a deposit validates intent at the stated price/spec/timeline, not demand for the finished product as-shipped.
- The refundable nature means the deposit measures reversible intent, not purchase certainty; conversion from deposit to actual purchase is a separate (and usually lower) rate that must be measured.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false-positive mechanism is social-obligation capture: a prospect who knows and respects the founder places a refundable deposit because the cost of doing so (a few dollars, easily reclaimed) is lower than the social cost of declining. This produces a high deposit count that signals demand when it actually signals social goodwill. The refundable nature is the key amplifier -- because the financial downside of committing is zero, the barrier separating a polite 'yes' from a purchase-committed 'yes' is not cleared. The result is a deposit pool that appears to validate demand but dissolves when the product ships and conversion is required. A secondary mechanism is novelty bias: early depositors are attracted to the concept's novelty or the founder's energy, not the underlying product value, and would not re-purchase or refer others.

**Examples**
- Tesla (Model 3, 2016): Collected $1,000 fully refundable deposits for an unbuilt $35,000 electric vehicle. Received 325,000 reservations in the first week, implying ~$14 billion in potential sales and described by analysts as the largest consumer product preorder in history. By June 2018, 23% of depositors (roughly 103,500 people) had requested refunds, primarily due to production delays and price point divergence from the $35,000 promise. The deposit sequence across Tesla vehicles -- Roadster at $5,000, Model 3 at $1,000, CyberTruck at $100, second Roadster at $250,000 -- became a canonical illustration of how deposit size signals segment and commitment depth.
- Pebble (smartwatch, Kickstarter 2012): Offered $99-$115 preorder pledges (against a $150 planned retail price) on Kickstarter, with no deposit/no refund terms standard to Kickstarter campaigns. Raised over $1 million within 28 hours and $10.3 million in 30 days against a $100,000 goal, from approximately 85,000 backers -- demonstrating that crowdfunding preorders function as the non-refundable analogue of deposit validation. The campaign proved consumer demand for smartwatches years before Apple or Samsung entered the category, validating the product concept when institutional investors had refused to fund it.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is a deposit count that meets or exceeds the pre-stated falsification threshold, drawn from a representative (not network-captured) population, with a deposit-to-price ratio of at least 1-3% of the full purchase price (enough to create genuine friction), and minimal refund requests during the collection window. The B2B analogue -- where 10-12 out of 15 qualified prospects place a $500+ deposit or sign a letter of intent committing to a specific price -- is a strong signal: an 80% conversion rate on direct outreach to a qualified list is far above the general 2.9% B2B conversion baseline and suggests genuine willingness to pay at the stated price.
- Weak signal: A weak result is either a low raw count relative to the pre-stated threshold, OR a high count that cannot be attributed to the target segment (e.g., it came mostly from the founder's personal network). A 23%+ refund rate during or shortly after the collection window -- even if the absolute count was high -- signals that commitment was conditional on unstated assumptions (price, timeline, specific features) rather than unconditional demand. Any result from a collection window shorter than 7 days or from a sample smaller than 50 should be treated as directional only, not confirmatory.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Tesla Model 3 received 325,000 refundable $1,000 deposits in the first week of reservations (April 2016), implying ~$14 billion in potential future sales. -- Tesla's own Q1 2016 SEC Form 8-K (filed May 4, 2016) states 325,000+ first-week reservations at a $1,000 refundable deposit each, with Tesla itself characterizing this as "about $14 billion in implied future sales." Bloomberg, Green Car Reports, and IndustryWeek all corroborate the figure. The $14 billion is Tesla's own marketing arithmetic (325,000 x ~$43,000 average selling price), not an independent analyst figure, but it is a confirmed Tesla statement from SEC filings. (src: Tesla Motors Inc. Form 8-K Q1 2016 Shareholder Letter, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0001318605/000119312516577582/d158845dex991.htm; Bloomberg https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2016-04-07/tesla-says-model-3-pre-orders-surge-to-325-000-in-first-week)
- [confirmed] Tesla Model 3 total reservations reached 450,000 at peak. -- Tesla's Q1 2018 earnings disclosure stated net reservations "exceeded 450K" at end of Q1 2018. Elon Musk's Q2 2017 earnings call cited 518,000 gross / 455,000 net, with net staying above 450,000 into early 2018. The 450,000 figure as the disclosed peak net reservation count is accurate, though gross reservations peaked higher at 518,000. Green Car Reports confirmed this figure citing Tesla's own Q2 2017 call clarification. (src: Green Car Reports https://www.greencarreports.com/news/1111903_tesla-model-3-net-reservations-clarified-after-16-months-455000; Motley Fool https://www.fool.com/investing/2017/08/16/the-truth-about-tesla-incs-model-3-reservations.aspx)
- [contested] 23% of Tesla Model 3 reservation holders (~103,500 people) had requested refunds as of June 2018, primarily driven by production delays. -- Second Measure did report a 23% refund rate as of June 2018, corroborated by Green Car Reports, Electrek, TechSpot, and Autoblog. The production delay causation is consistent with reported data. However, the absolute "~103,500 people" figure is not from Second Measure directly -- it is a calculation applying 23% to 450,000 total reservations. Second Measure's dataset only tracked U.S. depositors and did not disclose its sample size or whether it represents the global pool. Tesla disputed the figures. The percentage itself is corroborated; the absolute number extrapolation is an inference. (src: Second Measure https://secondmeasure.com/datapoints/tesla-faces-accelerating-rate-of-model-3-refunds/; Green Car Reports https://www.greencarreports.com/news/1117043_study-shows-23-percent-cancelations-on-tesla-model-3-deposits; The Drive (Tesla denial) https://www.thedrive.com/news/21312/tesla-denies-report-claiming-that-23-percent-of-model-3-deposits-were-refunded)
- [contested] Tesla Roadster (original) required a $5,000 refundable deposit; Model 3 required $1,000; CyberTruck required $100; second Roadster required $250,000. -- Three of the four sub-claims are correct: Model 3 = $1,000 (confirmed), CyberTruck = $100 refundable (confirmed). The second-gen Roadster Founders Series = $250,000 full purchase price upfront (confirmed), though the standard reservation was only $5,000 + $45,000 wire = $50,000 total -- calling $250,000 'the deposit' conflates the Founders Series full price with a deposit. The original 2006 first-gen Roadster deposit is the most problematic: Tesla's own SEC filing (2011) describes the original Roadster deposit as "nonrefundable" and "varies by country," not a specific $5,000 refundable figure. The $5,000 figure found in SEC filings corresponds to the Model S reservation fee and to the initial credit card charge for the second-gen Roadster. No independent source corroborates a $5,000 refundable deposit for the original 2006 Roadster. (src: Tesla SEC filing 2011 (original Roadster nonrefundable deposit) https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1318605/000119312511308489/R10.htm; Electrek (Cybertruck $100) https://cyberbackpack.com/blogs/tesla-cybertruck-ev-blog/tesla-refunding-cybertruck; Electrek (Roadster 2 $250K founders) https://electrek.co/2021/12/20/tesla-removes-roadster-pricing-sold-out-250000-founders-series/)
- [confirmed] Pebble smartwatch raised over $1 million within 28 hours of its Kickstarter launch (April 2012) at a $99-$115 preorder pledge. -- Multiple independent sources including CNBC, HBS Digital Innovation, MIT Technology Review, and The Next Web confirm $1 million was raised within 28 hours of the April 11, 2012 launch. The $99 early-bird price (first 200 backers) and $115 standard pledge are confirmed by Silicon UK and other contemporaneous reports. Goal was $100,000, surpassed within 2 hours. The 28-hour/$1M milestone is well-documented. (src: CNBC https://www.cnbc.com/2012/05/11/pebble-smartwatch-tops-out-at-10-million-on-kickstarter.html; HBS Digital Innovation https://aiinstitute.hbs.edu/platform-digit/submission/pebble-smartwatch-the-serial-crowdfunded-superstar/; Silicon UK https://www.silicon.co.uk/mobility/tales-tech-history-pebble-smartwatch-220973)
- [contested] Pebble raised $10.3 million total in 30 days against a $100,000 goal, from approximately 85,000 backers. -- The $10.26-$10.3 million total and the $100,000 goal are confirmed across multiple sources (CNBC, HBS, The Week, TechCrunch). The campaign ran approximately 30 days (April 11 to May 18, 2012), which is accurate. However, the backer count of 85,000 is materially wrong: the verified backer figure is 68,929 per HBS and Kickstarter data. The 85,000 figure appears to refer to units ordered (some backers ordered multiple watches), not unique backers. One source (Silicon UK) does use the 85,000 figure but conflates it with buyers/units rather than backer accounts. (src: HBS Digital Innovation (68,929 backers) https://aiinstitute.hbs.edu/platform-digit/submission/pebble-smartwatch-the-serial-crowdfunded-superstar/; CNBC https://www.cnbc.com/2012/05/11/pebble-smartwatch-tops-out-at-10-million-on-kickstarter.html; Silicon UK (85,000 units figure) https://www.silicon.co.uk/mobility/tales-tech-history-pebble-smartwatch-220973)
- [confirmed] Pebble Time (2015) reached $1 million in funding in 49 minutes, the fastest campaign to do so at the time. -- Confirmed by CNN Money, Yahoo Tech, The Next Web, and Kickstarter's own spokesperson. The campaign launched February 24, 2015 and hit $1 million in 49 minutes, beating the previous record (Reaper Miniature Bones at 2 hours 42 minutes). The campaign ultimately raised $20.3 million from 78,463 backers. Multiple independent contemporaneous sources corroborate the 49-minute figure and the Kickstarter record claim. (src: CNN Money https://money.cnn.com/2015/02/24/technology/pebble-time-smartwatch; Yahoo Tech https://www.yahoo.com/tech/pebble-time-raises-1-million-in-49-minutes-111979398809.html; The Next Web https://thenextweb.com/plugged/2015/03/28/pebble-times-kickstarter-raised-more-than-20-million-smashed-records/)
- [contested] A deposit-backed reservation funnel converts email leads to purchasers at 35-40%, versus a raw email list baseline of 1-3%, per LaunchBoom/Prelaunch.marketing data from ~2,000 campaigns. -- The 35-40% conversion rate and 1-3% email baseline are directly stated by LaunchBoom and corroborated by Stonemaier Games referencing LaunchBoom data. LaunchBoom's own content cites this range. However, the specific claim of "~2,000 campaigns" as the dataset size was not found in any LaunchBoom or Prelaunch.marketing source reviewed -- this sample-size figure is unverified and may be an embellishment. The rate range itself (35-40%) is confirmed from LaunchBoom's own published material, with some sources citing ranges as wide as 20-54% depending on product category. (src: LaunchBoom https://www.launchboom.com/game-tips/why-the-1-dollar-reservation-helps/; LaunchBoom https://www.launchboom.com/crowdfunding-tips/kickstarter-crowdfunding/; Stonemaier Games https://stonemaiergames.com/prelaunch-promos-accessories-and-expansions-ks-lesson-281/)
- [confirmed] Kickstarter followers convert to backers at approximately 30% (mean and median), per Prelaunch.marketing data. -- Prelaunch.marketing (Prelaunch Club) explicitly states "The average conversion rate of Kickstarter Followers into Backers is roughly 30% with a launch day success" based on data from 43 live campaigns. Both mean and median are cited as ~30%. This is further corroborated by Kickstarter's own Comics Outreach Lead Sam Kusek stating "We see an average conversion rate of 31%." Creator communities broadly report 20-30% as the typical range. The sample (43 campaigns) is modest, which limits confidence, but the figure is consistently cited by independent parties. (src: Prelaunch.marketing https://prelaunch.marketing/blogs/academy/average-conversion-rates-for-kickstarter-followers; Kickstarter internal data (Sam Kusek quote) corroborated in same source)
- [confirmed] A B2B presell case study cited in LeanFoundry/LEAN 1-2-3 (July 2025) showed 12 of 15 prospects (80%) committing to $5,000/month before any product was built, yielding $180K in pre-launch committed revenue. -- The LeanFoundry LEAN 1-2-3 newsletter dated July 31, 2025 explicitly contains this case study: a founder named "Steve" who targeted architects at $5,000/month, obtained commitments from 12 of 15 qualified prospects (80%), yielding $180K in pre-launch committed revenue before writing any code. The same case study is repeated in the September 4, 2025 issue. Direct fetch of the July 31, 2025 newsletter confirmed all claimed metrics. The claim's attribution to "July 2025" is technically correct (July 31 is in July). This is a single anecdotal case study, not a statistically-validated benchmark, which the original claim does acknowledge. (src: LeanFoundry LEAN 1-2-3 newsletter July 31, 2025 https://www.leanfoundry.com/lean-1-2-3/jul-31-2025; corroborated in September 4, 2025 issue https://www.leanfoundry.com/lean-1-2-3/sep-4-2025)

### 15. Letters of Intent (LOIs) and Signed Paid Pilots

- **Category:** Demand & willingness-to-pay
- **Stage:** demand
- **Definition:** A Letter of Intent (LOI) is a short, typically non-binding written document in which a prospective customer declares their intention to purchase or use a product once defined conditions are met, including agreed-upon pricing terms; it is distinct from a contract. A signed paid pilot is a time-boxed, compensated production trial -- usually 60-90 days -- in which the customer pays a portion of the expected annual contract value to run the product in their real environment against pre-agreed success criteria. Together, these two instruments form a staged commitment ladder: the LOI tests stated buying intent before the product is complete, while the paid pilot tests real-world value delivery and converts stated intent into actual money-at-risk, constituting the strongest pre-full-contract demand signal available to a B2B startup.
- **Validates:** Do identifiable organizations, with budget authority and a named decision-maker, intend to pay for this solution -- and will they actually pay when given the opportunity?

**Protocol**
1. IDENTIFY ECONOMIC BUYERS, NOT USERS: Before soliciting any LOI or pilot, confirm you are engaging the person who controls the budget, not just an enthusiastic practitioner. If your pilot does not involve the economic buyer from day one, you are prioritizing making it work over making it sellable (per the 'who signs the check' discipline surfaced by Zero-to-Traction / Traction Lab). Time spent with users who lack authority is wasted validation.
2. DEFINE SUCCESS CRITERIA IN WRITING BEFORE STARTING: Align on 2-3 specific, measurable KPIs before the pilot begins -- e.g., 'reduce contract review time by 25% across 5 attorneys in 60 days.' A Forrester study found that enterprise pilots with predefined success criteria were 3.2x more likely to convert to paid contracts than open-ended evaluations. Make these criteria part of the signed pilot agreement, not a verbal side agreement.
3. CHARGE FOR THE PILOT -- NO FREE PILOTS: Per Jason Lemkin (SaaStr), if you do not charge, it is not a pilot -- it is an extended demo. The recommended structure is 10-30% of expected annual contract value (ACV) as a pilot fee, with 100% of that fee credited toward the first-year contract on conversion. Free pilots 'almost never' convert because no money is at risk on the customer side, feedback quality is 10-100x lower, and the team has no real buy-in to deploy (Lemkin, SaaStr). Example: Snowflake charged $25,000-$50,000 for 90-day enterprise pilots, all credited to year-one ACV.
4. GET THE LOI BEFORE THE PILOT IF THE PRODUCT IS PRE-BUILD: When the product is not yet built or is too early to pilot, get a signed LOI that includes: (a) the specific problem being solved, (b) the financial terms the customer would sign under (e.g., '$5/user/month'), (c) timeline for conversion to a real contract, and (d) a willingness to provide an investor reference call. Keep it to 1-2 pages -- longer documents require legal review and slow the signal. The LOI should map to a real sale process even though it is non-binding: walk the prospect through every step as if closing a real contract (per JumpStart Inc. framework for deep tech startups).
5. TARGET 4-6 SIGNED LOIs OR 2-3 PAID PILOTS FOR INITIAL SIGNAL: The Lean B2B community and accelerator programs (JumpStart, Alchemist) converge on 4-6 LOIs as the minimum to demonstrate non-anecdotal demand to investors. For paid pilots specifically, even 1-2 paying organizations generate qualitatively richer signal than dozens of verbal commitments, because the data is 'more honest and real when there is money attached' (Lemkin, SaaStr).
6. MANAGE STAKEHOLDER BREADTH INSIDE THE CUSTOMER: Identify the decision-maker, day-to-day lead, and technical evaluator on the customer side before the pilot starts. Engaging only the corporate innovation department -- which often lacks rollout authority -- is a documented failure mode (Alchemist Accelerator). Co-develop the pilot plan with procurement, IT, and an executive sponsor where possible to prevent 'pilot purgatory': a state where the pilot succeeds technically but stalls commercially because key stakeholders were never involved.
7. SET AN AUTOMATIC CONVERSION TRIGGER: Include a go/no-go clause tied to the predefined KPIs. A well-structured pilot contract should include automatic rollover into full-contract status once success metrics are met, or explicit opt-out rights if they are not, with a hard deadline. This closes the 'perpetual pilot' trap: the pattern where a startup becomes a consulting shop customizing for two large customers indefinitely, diverting all engineering resources and stalling scale (documented in SaaStr and startup accelerator post-mortems).
8. TREAT A 'NO' AS MORE VALUABLE THAN A SOFT YES: Per Rob Fitzpatrick (The Mom Test, Chapter 5), a rejection is better than a vague stall -- it provides clarity. If a prospect will not pay even a nominal pilot fee, that is decisive information. Do not renegotiate downward toward free to 'get in the door'; this sets an irreversible pricing precedent and, per Lean B2B (Garbugli / Brant Cooper), makes it very hard to charge the next customer.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Paid pilot to annual contract conversion rate (target, healthy SaaS): 70%+ once executing well; range 60%-90%+ (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr (https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2))
- Paid pilot to annual contract conversion -- high end (Adobe Sign / EchoSign): Well over 90% conversion, attributed to getting customers to full production during the pilot (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr (https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2))
- Paid pilot to annual contract conversion -- low end (high business-process-change products): ~60% (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr (https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2))
- Pilot fee structure (standard early-stage B2B): 10-30% of expected ACV, with 100% of pilot fee credited toward first-year contract on conversion (src: getmonetizely.com, How to Structure Enterprise Pilot Program Pricing (https://www.getmonetizely.com/articles/how-to-structure-enterprise-pilot-program-pricing-effective-proof-of-concept-strategies))
- Snowflake enterprise pilot fee example: $25,000-$50,000 for 90-day pilots, all credited to year-one contract (src: getmonetizely.com, How to Structure Enterprise Pilot Program Pricing (https://www.getmonetizely.com/articles/how-to-structure-enterprise-pilot-program-pricing-effective-proof-of-concept-strategies))
- Conversion rate lift from predefined success criteria vs. open-ended pilots: 3.2x more likely to convert to paid contract (Forrester, 2023) (src: getmonetizely.com citing Forrester 2023 (https://www.getmonetizely.com/articles/how-to-structure-enterprise-pilot-program-pricing-effective-proof-of-concept-strategies))
- Recommended minimum number of LOIs for B2B validation signal: 4-6 signed LOIs, each including pricing terms (src: JumpStart Inc. / Alchemist Accelerator framework; SaasFactor.co B2B validation guidance)
- B2B validation customer count alternative benchmark (YC context): 10 companies willing to commit to a pilot or LOI equivalent to 100 consumer landing-page signups for consumer validation (src: SaasFactor.co, How to Validate a Startup Idea Before Writing Code (https://www.saasfactor.co/blogs/how-to-validate-a-startup-idea-before-writing-code))
- Pilot duration (typical enterprise): 60-90 days (src: SaaStr, Alchemist Accelerator, getmonetizely.com (multiple sources converge on this range))
- Feedback quality improvement when money is attached vs. free: 10-100x more honest and real feedback (qualitative claim, not a controlled study) (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr (https://www.saastr.com/we-are-a-b2b-saas-startup-and-want-to-develop-our-product-in-pilots-with-customers-should-we-charge-for-the-pilots-and-how-much/))

**Origin and evidence:** The LOI-as-validation instrument was popularized through the B2B deep tech accelerator and startup-finance community, notably by organizations like JumpStart Inc. and Alchemist Accelerator, which codified it as a standard pre-revenue traction metric for investors evaluating pre-product deep tech companies. The 'commitment ladder' framing -- where financial commitment is the highest-signal tier above time and reputation commitments -- was articulated in Rob Fitzpatrick's 'The Mom Test' (2013, Chapter 5), which is the canonical practitioner source for distinguishing false-positive from real validation signals. The critique of LOIs as insufficient ('The LOI Fallacy') was published by Alice Bentinck (Entrepreneurs First, Medium) and independently by Etienne Garbugli and Brant Cooper in the Lean B2B tradition. Paid pilot conversion benchmarks originate from Jason Lemkin's SaaStr community, based on his operational experience at EchoSign (acquired by Adobe) and his work with SaaS founders. Evidence quality is practitioner-empirical and portfolio-observation-based rather than peer-reviewed academic. No published academic study with controlled design was found for the core benchmarks.

**Canonical sources:** Rob Fitzpatrick, 'The Mom Test' (2013) -- Chapter 5: Commitment and Advancement; the commitment-currency framework (time, reputation, money) and the critique of compliments-as-false-positives; Jason Lemkin, SaaStr -- 'Dear SaaStr: Should We Charge for Pilots and How Much?' and 'What is The Typical Conversion from Paid Pilot to Annual Contract in B2B SaaS?' -- primary operational benchmarks on pilot pricing and conversion; Etienne Garbugli, 'Lean B2B: Build Products Businesses Want' (2014, 2nd ed. 2022) -- B2B customer development sequencing including pilot customers; Alice Bentinck (Entrepreneurs First), 'The Letter of Intent Fallacy' (Medium) -- canonical critique of LOIs as the sole validation instrument for deep tech startups; JumpStart Inc., 'Non-Binding Letters of Intent from Potential Customers' (2016 PDF) -- structured practitioner template for deep tech startup LOIs; Alchemist Accelerator, 'Using Pilots, POCs, and LOIs to Build Trust and Scale' -- B2B enterprise sequencing of POC -> pilot -> LOI; Brant Cooper / MIT Entrepreneurship Center (via Ken Morse, cited in Lean B2B community) -- 'LOIs can't be taken to the bank'; false-validation warning; Y Combinator Startup Library / SaasFactor summary -- benchmark of 10 B2B LOIs/pilot commitments as equivalent demand signal to 100 consumer signups

**Failure modes**
- LOI treated as the terminal validation artifact: Teams collect signatures and stop doing customer development, never achieving genuine understanding of customer operations, workflows, or willingness to actually deploy. Alice Bentinck (Entrepreneurs First) documents this as the primary failure mode for deep tech startups -- 'more often than not we see potential customers renege on their LOI to the team's surprise and dismay.'
- Free pilots that mimic paid pilots: Running free pilots as a shortcut to gain customer access produces near-zero conversion. Jason Lemkin (SaaStr) states free pilots 'almost never' convert because without money at risk, deployment is deprioritized by the customer, feedback is sanitized, and the startup has no leverage to demand real engagement or reach the economic buyer.
- Perpetual pilot trap: Startups customize endlessly for 1-2 early pilot customers, diverting all engineering capacity and effectively becoming consulting shops. The pilot never converts because success criteria were not predefined, there is no contractual conversion trigger, and the customer has no time pressure. Documented by Siddharth Shah (Substack #100) and SaaStr.
- Engaging the wrong stakeholder: Running pilots with enthusiastic users (not economic buyers) produces adoption data but no purchasing signal. The economic buyer -- the person who controls the budget -- is often not in the room. This is the single most common B2B validation error flagged by the Traction Lab / Zero-to-Traction community.
- Over-eager sales execution: Sales reps close pilot deals that cannot actually deploy, producing a distorted (high) pilot start rate and a distorted (low) conversion rate. This is distinct from product failure -- it is a sales filtering failure (Lemkin, SaaStr).
- LOI with no pricing terms: An LOI that expresses general enthusiasm without specifying dollar amounts, seat counts, or implementation fees is functionally a polite endorsement. It does not test price sensitivity and is nearly worthless as a willingness-to-pay signal. YC explicitly notes that the more specific the LOI (mentioning price points), the stronger the signal.
- Scope creep converting a pilot into a custom build: When the customer asks for detailed roadmaps, broad feature sets, and finished-product quality, the pilot has transformed into a full engagement. This drains resources without yielding generalizable validation evidence (Alchemist Accelerator warning).

**False-positive mechanism:** The central false-positive mechanism is social courtesy and organizational inertia: a manager in a potential customer organization signs an LOI because it costs them nothing (non-binding, no budget commitment, no legal review needed), it makes the meeting end pleasantly, and it deflects a pushy founder. Rob Fitzpatrick (The Mom Test) identifies this as the 'deadly inaccuracy' -- 'I would definitely buy that' sounds like validation but reflects optimism about future behavior, not current intent. The LOI amplifies this because it converts a verbal compliment into a piece of paper that looks like a commitment but still involves no money at risk, no procurement process, and no internal champion who has bet their reputation on the purchase. A related second mechanism is the 'innovation department' false positive: corporate innovation labs sign LOIs and invite pilots routinely as part of their mandate to explore emerging technology, without any authority or intention to roll the product out to production. The startup interprets the signed agreement as organizational demand; in reality it reflects one team's exploratory charter. A third mechanism specific to paid pilots is 'pilot purgatory' as a false signal of traction: a customer paying a nominal pilot fee ($5,000-$10,000) against a $200,000 ACV deal is not a validated buyer -- they are a hedged evaluator. If the startup counts active paid pilots as 'demand' without tracking conversion intent, it may raise a funding round on a pipeline that is structurally unlikely to close.

**Examples**
- EchoSign (acquired by Adobe as Adobe Sign): Jason Lemkin ran pilot programs at EchoSign and documented that conversion from paid pilot to annual contract was 'well over 90%' when customers reached full production deployment during the pilot period. This benchmark -- cited repeatedly in SaaStr writing -- became the gold standard for B2B SaaS pilot conversion, attributed to fast time-to-value: customers who were productive on day one of the pilot converted at the highest rates.
- Snowflake (early enterprise expansion strategy): Snowflake's enterprise go-to-market used a structured paid pilot model charging $25,000-$50,000 for 90-day pilots, with the entire pilot fee credited toward the first year's contract on conversion. This 'credit on conversion' structure is widely cited as a model for removing the customer's financial objection to committing while still ensuring real money is at risk, which dramatically improves deployment seriousness and feedback quality.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is: (1) 4-6 signed LOIs that each include specific pricing terms and a reference-call commitment from a named decision-maker with budget authority, OR (2) 1-3 organizations actively paying a pilot fee of at least 10% of expected ACV, with a defined 60-90 day timeline and pre-agreed measurable success criteria, and with the economic buyer visibly engaged in the pilot review process. In the paid pilot case, a conversion rate of 70%+ from completed pilots to signed annual contracts is the operational benchmark (Lemkin, SaaStr) indicating the product delivers sufficient value to justify purchase. A secondary strong signal is the quality of pushback: customers who negotiate price terms aggressively and ask detailed deployment questions during an LOI process are exhibiting buying behavior -- they are treating it like a real purchase even though it is non-binding.
- Weak signal: A weak result is: (1) LOIs signed by innovation lab managers, VP-level explorers, or individual practitioners with no purchasing authority, especially if obtained without discussing actual pricing; (2) free pilots (zero money exchanged) that a customer agreed to 'just to try it out'; (3) pilot conversion rates below 60%, especially if the low conversion is attributed to deployment difficulty rather than product-value failure -- this signals an integration/onboarding problem, not lack of demand; (4) a pipeline of pilots that have been running longer than 90 days without a conversion decision, indicating perpetual pilot dynamics; or (5) fewer than 4 LOIs, or LOIs from companies in different verticals or use cases, which do not demonstrate repeatable demand from a coherent buyer segment. A single enthusiastic LOI from one large-brand company is a vanity signal -- it may reflect that company's procurement process for exploring all relevant vendors, not genuine intent to purchase from this startup specifically.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Paid pilot to annual contract conversion target: 70%+ (Lemkin, SaaStr) -- The SaaStr article at the cited URL directly states "70%+ once you are doing it right" as the headline benchmark. The source is real and the claim is accurately quoted. (src: https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2)
- [confirmed] Paid pilot to annual contract conversion range: 60%-90%+ (Lemkin, SaaStr) -- The cited SaaStr article states the range is "roughly 60% to over 90%" across companies Lemkin works with. The range is accurately represented. (src: https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2)
- [confirmed] Adobe Sign / EchoSign paid pilot conversion: well over 90% (Lemkin, SaaStr) -- The SaaStr article states "it was well over 90% at EchoSign" and attributes this to getting customers fully into production during the pilot. Lemkin was the EchoSign co-founder, lending direct credibility. The specific number is an anecdote, not an audited figure, but the source quote is accurate. (src: https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2)
- [confirmed] Low-end paid pilot conversion (high business-process-change products): ~60% (Lemkin, SaaStr) -- The SaaStr article directly identifies a startup with 60% conversion due to complex business-process-change deployment requirements. The claim is accurately quoted from the cited source. (src: https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2)
- [contested] Recommended minimum LOI count for B2B validation: 4-6 signed LOIs with pricing terms (JumpStart Inc. / Alchemist Accelerator / SaasFactor) -- The "4-6" figure traces to a JumpStart Inc. PDF template document -- the PDF was retrieved and confirmed to contain the "4-6 signed letters" language. However, no independent evidence ties this specific number to Alchemist Accelerator's formal requirements, and SaasFactor's article uses different thresholds ("10 companies" or "20 interviews"). The specific requirement for pricing terms in the LOI was not corroborated across all three cited sources. The claim conflates three sources that do not uniformly agree. (src: https://www.jumpstartinc.org/wp-content/uploads/2016/09/Non-Binding-Letters-of-Intent-9616.pdf)
- [contested] Pilot fee structure: 10-30% of expected ACV, 100% credited to year-one contract (getmonetizely.com) -- getmonetizely.com states this verbatim, but getmonetizely.com appears to be a content marketing site with no cited primary research. No independent authoritative source (Gartner, Forrester, SaaStr, or a primary case study) corroborates the specific 10-30% figure as a standard range. The 100% credit structure is a recognized practice but the precise percentage band is unverified. This reads as a synthesized rule of thumb from a single non-primary source. (src: https://www.getmonetizely.com/articles/how-to-structure-enterprise-pilot-program-pricing-effective-proof-of-concept-strategies)
- [contested] Snowflake enterprise pilot fee: $25,000-$50,000 for 90-day pilots (getmonetizely.com) -- The $25,000-$50,000 / 90-day figure appears only in getmonetizely.com and is not corroborated by any official Snowflake pricing page, press release, or independent source. Snowflake's $25,000 minimum does appear in multiple independent pricing guides as a capacity commitment contract threshold, but that is a usage commitment, not a structured pilot fee. The specific framing as a 90-day pilot with 100% credit is unverified. (src: https://www.getmonetizely.com/articles/how-to-structure-enterprise-pilot-program-pricing-effective-proof-of-concept-strategies)
- [refuted] Pilots with predefined success criteria: 3.2x more likely to convert vs. open-ended evaluations (Forrester 2023, cited in getmonetizely.com) -- Extensive search found no Forrester 2023 report, study, or publication containing this 3.2x statistic. The figure appears only on getmonetizely.com, which is a content marketing site. Searches on Forrester's own domain and via general web search returned zero corroborating sources for this specific stat. This is a strong indicator of a fabricated or misattributed citation, a common pattern in AI-generated content. Interestingly, SaasFactor.co uses the same 3.2x figure but attributes it to Stripe Atlas (for a different claim), suggesting the number may be recycled across low-quality sources. (src: Web search: no primary Forrester source found; only getmonetizely.com (https://www.getmonetizely.com/articles/how-to-structure-enterprise-pilot-program-pricing-effective-proof-of-concept-strategies))
- [contested] B2B validation equivalence: 10 LOI/pilot commitments = approximately 100 consumer landing-page signups in signal weight (SaasFactor, referencing YC framing) -- SaasFactor.co does state "instead of 100 landing page signups, you might need 10 companies willing to commit to a pilot or LOI" -- the claim is accurately reflected in the source. However, no YC attribution for this specific framing was found in any YC-published content, blog post, or indexed partner talk. It appears to be SaasFactor's own synthesis or a widely circulated informal heuristic, not a verified YC doctrine. The equivalence is directionally reasonable but the YC attribution is unverified. (src: https://www.saasfactor.co/blogs/how-to-validate-a-startup-idea-before-writing-code (source confirmed via search result snippet; direct fetch returned 403))
- [confirmed] Typical pilot duration: 60-90 days (SaaStr, Alchemist Accelerator, getmonetizely.com -- multiple sources) -- Multiple independent sources corroborate 60-90 days as the standard enterprise pilot window. SaaStr references a 60-day opt-out structure as a mature pilot alternative. getmonetizely.com and B2B SaaS practitioner content describe 90-day structured pilots as the dominant framework. The 60-90 day range is broadly consistent across practitioner literature even if Alchemist Accelerator's specific endorsement was not independently verified. (src: https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2; https://www.b2b-news.uk/how-to-get-procurement-to-approve-your-saas-pilot-with-a-90-day-roi-playbook-cfos-cant-refuse)
- [confirmed] Feedback quality with money attached vs. free: 10-100x more honest (qualitative claim, Lemkin, SaaStr -- not a controlled study) -- The SaaStr article at the second cited URL states verbatim: "The feedback is 10-100x more honest and real when there is money attached to that usage." The claim is accurately sourced and the parenthetical caveat that it is qualitative, not a controlled study, is appropriate and honest. No independent empirical study corroborates the specific multiplier, but the source attribution is accurate. (src: https://www.saastr.com/we-are-a-b2b-saas-startup-and-want-to-develop-our-product-in-pilots-with-customers-should-we-charge-for-the-pilots-and-how-much/)
- [confirmed] Average B2B purchase involves 6-10 stakeholders and 3-18 month sales cycles (general enterprise sales research, cited in getmonetizely.com synthesis) -- The 6-10 stakeholder figure is directly attributable to Gartner's B2B Buying Journey research and is corroborated by multiple independent sources (LinkedIn citing Gartner, Madison Logic, Growth Method). The 3-18 month sales cycle range is supported by multiple independent benchmarks: mid-market B2B is 3-6 months, enterprise is 9-18 months, making the combined range accurate as a span. Forrester's 2024 data and Gartner both support this picture. The claim is broadly accurate though the upper bound of 18 months applies primarily to large enterprise and regulated sectors. (src: Gartner B2B Buying Journey (2023), corroborated at https://growthmethod.com/gartner-b2b-buying-journey/ and https://instantly.ai/blog/decision-maker-benchmarks-enterprise-buying-committee-size/)

**Citations**
- Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test Chapter 5: Commitment and Advancement (dev.to summary): https://dev.to/egepakten/the-mom-test-chapter-5-commitment-and-advancement-1k27

---
### 16. Fake checkout / payment-intent capture

- **Category:** Demand & willingness-to-pay
- **Stage:** demand
- **Definition:** A fake checkout (also called a smoke test, fake door, painted door, or dry-wallet test) is a market experiment in which a team presents a real-looking purchase flow -- landing page, pricing page, or "Buy Now" button -- for a product or feature that does not yet exist. When a user reaches the payment step, they encounter a "not ready yet" message, a waitlist, or a letter-of-intent form rather than an actual charge. The primary signal is behavioral click-through and checkout-attempt rate, not self-reported opinion, making it a stronger demand signal than surveys or interviews.
- **Validates:** Does a defined target audience exhibit sufficient willingness-to-pay -- operationalized as initiating a real purchase flow -- to justify building the product?

**Protocol**
1. State a falsifiable hypothesis with a pre-committed numeric kill criterion BEFORE building anything -- e.g., 'If fewer than 5% of landing page visitors click through to the pricing page and attempt checkout within 14 days, we kill the project.' Writing this down before launch is mandatory; post-hoc threshold-setting is the primary source of false positives.
2. Build the minimal credible surface: a landing page with a headline, three to four benefit bullets, a pricing section, and a single CTA ('Buy Now' / 'Get Early Access'). Use a real custom domain and credible visual design; implausible-looking pages deflate conversion and produce false negatives. Do NOT collect actual payment credentials unless you intend to charge.
3. Add an analytics layer (event tracking on every CTA click, page scroll depth, time on page) and, optionally, a short two-question post-click survey asking why they clicked and what they expected to pay. This separates genuine purchase intent from curiosity clicks.
4. Drive a minimum of 1,000 targeted visitors before drawing conclusions. Use paid search (high-intent keywords), paid social, or a direct outreach email to a list that matches your target persona. Cold, untargeted traffic invalidates the result.
5. At the checkout-attempt point, show an honest resolution page: explain the product is not yet available, offer an email sign-up or waitlist, and if ethical and legal constraints permit, offer a pre-order at the stated price with a no-questions-asked refund guarantee. Capturing emails is table stakes; capturing actual payment intent (even with a refund option) is the gold-standard variant.
6. Measure the two-step funnel: (a) landing page visit to CTA click rate, and (b) CTA click to checkout-attempt or email-capture rate. Interpret BOTH together -- a high click rate plus near-zero checkout-attempt rate signals messaging that outperforms the underlying product desire.
7. Debrief within 48 hours of hitting sample size. Compare results against the pre-committed kill criterion. If below threshold: kill or pivot. If above: conduct five to ten follow-up discovery calls with users who attempted checkout to understand the exact job-to-be-done before spending on development.
8. If running a B2B variant, replace the checkout flow with a Letter of Intent form or a pilot-agreement page requiring an authorized signature. Signed LOIs with a dollar figure attached are the B2B equivalent of a payment capture.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Landing page visit-to-CTA-click conversion rate (minimum viable signal): 0.5% is cited as the lower threshold; below 0.5% the idea is not viable or needs iteration (src: Bundl Venture Club, 'Smoke Testing: 4 Steps to Validate Purchase Intent' (bundl.com/articles/techniques-validating-purchase-intention-in-4-easy-steps-the-smoke-test))
- Established e-commerce checkout conversion rate (reference baseline for comparison): 2-3% of site visitors complete a purchase on mature e-commerce sites (src: Bundl Venture Club, same article)
- Fake door success threshold (example used in practitioner guidance): 5% of targeted users click the fake feature prompt; 20% of those who click sign up for updates (src: Learning Loop, 'Fake Door Testing: What It Is and How to Run One' (learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing))
- Minimum sample size before drawing conclusions: On the order of 1,000+ views or 100+ clicks (src: The Real Startup Book by Tristan Kromer, as cited in Learning Loop fake door testing guide)
- Buffer paid-plan upgrade conversion (post-launch outcome of the smoke test): Around 4% of active users upgraded to paid plans after launch (src: Joel Gascoigne, 'Idea to Paying Customers in 7 Weeks: How We Did It,' buffer.com/resources/idea-to-paying-customers-in-7-weeks-how-we-did-it/)
- Dropbox waitlist from video smoke test: More than 70,000 people joined the waitlist overnight after the explainer video (src: Learning Loop, fake door testing guide, citing the Dropbox case)
- Tesla Roadster pre-order commitment level: $5,000 deposit required to secure a build date, used as a high-commitment payment-intent capture before production (src: Learning Loop, fake door testing guide)
- Minimum target audience size for a reliable smoke test: 1.5 to 5 million addressable reach recommended; minimum 250,000 (src: Horizon, 'Smoke Tests in Market Research: The Complete Guide' (gethorizon.net/guides/smoke-tests-in-market-research-the-complete-guide))
- Test duration minimum: At least one week before analysis (src: Horizon, 'Smoke Tests in Market Research: The Complete Guide')

**Origin and evidence:** The technique was formalized in two overlapping lineages. Eric Ries codified the 'smoke test' as an MVP variant in The Lean Startup (2011), explicitly defining it as 'little more than an advertisement' used to measure whether customers are interested before a product is built. Alberto Savoia coined the overlapping 'pretotyping' and 'fake door' framing at Google circa 2011 (Pretotype It!, later expanded into The Right It, 2019), grounding it in the Law of Market Failure: most new ideas fail in the market even if competently executed. Ash Maurya's Running Lean (2012) embedded fake-buy-button smoke tests inside the Lean Canvas customer-discovery cycle. The Buffer case (Joel Gascoigne, 2010-2011) is the most-cited practitioner origin story for the specific two-page fake-checkout implementation. Evidence strength: the underlying principle (behavioral signals outperform stated intentions) has strong support from behavioral economics (see Kahneman; the 'say-do gap' literature), but there are no randomized controlled trials specifically comparing fake-checkout to other validation methods on predictive validity for startup success.

**Canonical sources:** Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011) -- Chapter 6 ('Test') defines the smoke test MVP variant; Alberto Savoia, The Right It (2019) and Pretotype It! (2011) -- coins 'fake door' and 'Law of Market Failure'; Ash Maurya, Running Lean (2012, 2nd ed.) -- embeds fake buy buttons in problem/solution fit stage; Joel Gascoigne, 'Idea to Paying Customers in 7 Weeks: How We Did It,' Buffer Blog (2011) -- canonical practitioner case study; Tristan Kromer, The Real Startup Book -- quantifies sample size guidance (1,000+ views / 100+ clicks); Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) -- provides the theoretical foil: explains why verbal signals ('I would buy it') are unreliable, reinforcing why behavioral checkout attempts are required

**Failure modes**
- Curiosity clicks inflate CTR: compelling copy or prominent placement drives clicks from users who have no genuine purchase intent; click rate looks strong but no one would ever pay
- No pre-committed kill criterion: founder sets the threshold after seeing the data, always finding a way to call the result 'encouraging'; confirmation bias invalidates the experiment
- Untargeted traffic: cold social media traffic or friends-and-family visits do not represent the target customer; a 10% CTR from the wrong audience is worthless
- Undersized sample: fewer than 1,000 views produces results dominated by random variance; a team may kill a good idea or proceed with a bad one based on statistical noise
- Misleading resolution page: users who hit 'not ready yet' and feel deceived leave negative word-of-mouth; trust damage disproportionately affects early-stage B2C products where brand reputation is still forming
- Single-price testing: showing one price point reveals whether users click at that price, but not whether they would pay more or less; the team may leave margin on the table or anchor incorrectly
- The test validates message-market fit, not product-market fit: a great landing page for a bad product passes the smoke test; the checkout-attempt rate measures the persuasiveness of the frame, not the value of the solution

**False-positive mechanism:** The central false-positive mechanism is curiosity-click inflation: a well-written headline and a prominent 'Buy Now' button can drive checkout attempts from users who are intrigued by the marketing copy but would never complete an actual purchase once a real product exists and they confront real switching costs. Because the test intercepts users before a genuine transaction clears, there is no financial commitment to separate genuine buyers from casual browsers. A team with strong copywriting skills and a mediocre underlying idea will systematically pass this test, because the signal being measured is 'did the landing page convert' rather than 'will people pay repeatedly and tell others.' Secondary mechanism: social desirability among early audiences (tech communities, personal networks) produces inflated signals for ideas that sound innovative or socially valuable even when the actual job-to-be-done is weak.

**Examples**
- Buffer (2010): Joel Gascoigne built a two-page landing page: Page 1 described the idea; Page 2, added in a second iteration, showed three pricing tiers ($0, $5, $20 per month) before the 'not ready yet' message. A small number of users clicked on paid plans. Gascoigne treated this as sufficient signal to begin building. The product launched seven weeks later, achieved its first paying customer within four days of launch, and eventually converted approximately 4% of active users to paid plans. This is the canonical real-world example of the two-step fake-checkout protocol.
- Dropbox (2007): Drew Houston recorded a three-minute explainer video demonstrating a product that did not yet exist and posted it to Hacker News. The video drove more than 70,000 email signups overnight. While this is a video-landing-page variant rather than a checkout-page variant, it is universally cited alongside the fake checkout family because it operationalizes the same principle: measure behavioral commitment (email capture) to a non-existent product before writing production code.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is a checkout-attempt rate that exceeds the pre-committed threshold (e.g., above 5% of targeted visitors initiate checkout) from a sample of at least 1,000 targeted visitors -- meaning visitors recruited from channels that match the intended customer persona, not friends, colleagues, or tech-curiosity audiences. Strength is further confirmed when: (a) post-click survey responses cite a specific pain point the product resolves rather than generic interest, (b) a meaningful fraction of checkout-attempters leave an email and respond to a follow-up call, and (c) the result holds across at least two separate traffic sources. At this point the team has de-risked the demand hypothesis and can justify building a narrow MVP to test solution fit.
- Weak signal: A weak result is a checkout-attempt rate below the pre-committed threshold, OR a rate that is above threshold but accompanied by: (a) visitors sourced from untargeted or biased channels, (b) high click rate but near-zero post-click engagement (suggesting curiosity-driven clicks), (c) post-click survey responses that are vague or cite features the team has not described. A weak result means either the demand does not exist, the target segment is wrong, the price point is too high, or the messaging fails to communicate the value. The team should not build; they should iterate the hypothesis (different segment, different price, different framing) and rerun before committing resources.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Bundl: below 0.5% conversion rate on the fake checkout CTA signals the idea is not viable -- The Bundl article states verbatim: 'Anything lower than 0,5% means the idea isn’t viable or still needs work' while '0,5% or higher is a green light to take your idea to the next level.' This is confirmed directly from the primary source at bundl.com. (src: https://www.bundl.com/articles/techniques-validating-purchase-intention-in-4-easy-steps-the-smoke-test)
- [confirmed] Bundl: established e-commerce sites achieve 2-3% checkout conversion rates (reference baseline) -- The Bundl article states 'larger e-commerce stores consistently hit 2 to 3% conversion rates.' This is corroborated independently by multiple 2026 industry benchmarks (IRP Commerce, Dynamic Yield, Triple Whale, Shopify CRO data) placing the average e-commerce conversion rate in the 2-3% range, with significant variation by vertical. (src: https://www.bundl.com/articles/techniques-validating-purchase-intention-in-4-easy-steps-the-smoke-test; https://blendcommerce.com/blogs/shopify/ecommerce-conversion-rate-benchmarks-2026)
- [contested] Learning Loop / The Real Startup Book: accumulate 1,000+ views or 100+ clicks before drawing conclusions -- The Learning Loop article paraphrases The Real Startup Book as '1,000+ views or 100+ clicks.' However, the primary source (The Real Startup Book, anteo.gitbooks.io) states 'at least 1000 visits or 10,000 views' - a materially different threshold. The '100+ clicks' figure does not appear in the primary source. Learning Loop appears to have restated the guidance imprecisely. (src: https://anteo.gitbooks.io/the-real-startup-book/content/fake_door_smoke_test/description.html; https://learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing)
- [confirmed] Learning Loop example success criterion: 5% of targeted users click the fake feature; 20% of those clickers sign up for updates -- Confirmed verbatim from the Learning Loop article: 'If this feature idea is valuable, at least 5% of targeted users will click the fake feature prompt, and 20% of those who click will sign up for updates.' This is presented as an example hypothesis template, not a universal benchmark. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing)
- [confirmed] Buffer: approximately 4% of active users upgraded to paid plans post-launch -- Confirmed from Joel Gascoigne's original Buffer blog post: 'we are generating revenue through our paid monthly plans at a conversion rate of around 4% of people upgrading.' Note: this figure applied at very early scale (~500 users). Buffer's own later reporting shows the rate fell to ~1.5-2% as the user base grew to hundreds of thousands. (src: https://buffer.com/resources/idea-to-paying-customers-in-7-weeks-how-we-did-it/)
- [contested] Dropbox: 70,000+ email signups overnight from the explainer video landing page -- The Learning Loop article says 'more than 70,000 people joined the waitlist overnight,' which is technically not false (75,000 > 70,000), but it is imprecise. Primary sources - including TechCrunch's direct interview with Drew Houston and his talk at Startup Lessons Learned 2010 - consistently cite 75,000 signups overnight (up from 5,000). The '70,000' figure appears only in secondary retellings. The stated source (Learning Loop) cites 70,000+, but the underlying primary record is 75,000. (src: https://techcrunch.com/2011/11/01/founder-storie-how-dropbox-got-its-first-10-million-users/; https://yourstory.com/2026/02/dropbox-fake-demo-video-75k-signups-mvp)
- [contested] Tesla Roadster: $5,000 deposit per reservation as payment-intent capture before production -- The $5,000 figure appears in the Learning Loop article and matches the second-generation Roadster's initial credit card payment - but that is only the first installment of a $50,000 total deposit (a $45,000 wire transfer follows within 10 days). The original first-generation Roadster (2006-2012) used deposits of up to $50,000 total, with a separate European option of €3,000. Framing $5,000 as the deposit amount understates the actual financial commitment and conflates which Roadster generation is being discussed. (src: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tesla_Roadster_(first_generation); https://www.autoevolution.com/news/you-can-now-place-a-reservation-for-the-tesla-roadster-if-you-can-spare-50k-indefinitely-186207.html)
- [confirmed] Horizon: recommended target audience size is 1.5 to 5 million; minimum 250,000 for reliable results -- Confirmed verbatim from the Horizon guide: 'Aim for a target audience size between 1.5-5 million, at least 250,000.' This is Horizon's own platform-specific guidance for setting up paid ad-based smoke tests (e.g., Facebook Ads targeting), not a universal statistical rule. (src: https://www.gethorizon.net/guides/smoke-tests-in-market-research-the-complete-guide)
- [confirmed] Horizon: run the test for a minimum of one week before analysis -- Confirmed verbatim from the Horizon guide: 'You should aim to let the tests run for at least a week to allow for sufficient data collection.' The article also notes 'In as little as one week, you can have behavioural insight on a product.' (src: https://www.gethorizon.net/guides/smoke-tests-in-market-research-the-complete-guide)
- [confirmed] Buffer: first paying customer arrived within 4 days of product launch; product launched 7 weeks after the smoke test -- Both sub-claims confirmed from Gascoigne's primary blog post. 'I had my first paying customer within 4 days of launching the rough around the edges product.' The 7-week figure refers to the time spent building version one after the smoke test validated demand - Gascoigne describes building 'the first version in evenings and weekends over a period of 7 weeks' following the two-page landing page validation. (src: https://buffer.com/resources/idea-to-paying-customers-in-7-weeks-how-we-did-it/)

**Citations**
- Joel Gascoigne, 'Idea to Paying Customers in 7 Weeks: How We Did It,' Buffer Blog: https://buffer.com/resources/idea-to-paying-customers-in-7-weeks-how-we-did-it/
- Learning Loop, 'Fake Door Testing: What It Is and How to Run One': https://learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing
- Learning Loop, 'Dry Wallet Test': https://learningloop.io/plays/dry-wallet
- Bundl Venture Club, 'Smoke Testing: 4 Steps to Validate Purchase Intent': https://www.bundl.com/articles/techniques-validating-purchase-intention-in-4-easy-steps-the-smoke-test
- Horizon, 'Smoke Tests in Market Research: The Complete Guide': https://www.gethorizon.net/guides/smoke-tests-in-market-research-the-complete-guide
- Alberto Savoia, 'Summary of Pretotyping Techniques' (albertosavoia.com PDF): https://www.albertosavoia.com/uploads/1/4/0/9/14099067/summary_of_pretotyping_techniques.pdf
- Kromatic / The Real Startup Book, 'Fake Door Smoke Test': https://kromatic.com/real-startup-book/fake-door-smoke-test
- Optimizely, 'What is a Painted Door Test?': https://www.optimizely.com/optimization-glossary/painted-door-test/
- Y Combinator Library, 'How to Get and Test Ideas': https://www.ycombinator.com/library/7x-how-to-get-and-test-ideas
