### 17. Live paid-pilot close attempt on a sales call

- **Category:** Demand & willingness-to-pay
- **Stage:** demand
- **Definition:** A live paid-pilot close attempt is a real-time sales conversation in which the founder or sales lead explicitly asks a prospect to commit money -- however small -- to a time-boxed, scoped trial of the product, rather than accepting verbal enthusiasm or a letter of intent as the validation signal. The payment requirement is the test: if the prospect pays, the behavioral signal is treated as real demand evidence; if they decline, the refusal surfaces what words never would. Rob Fitzpatrick's framing is definitive: "People stop lying when you ask them for money" and commitment in the form of money is the highest-fidelity form of his three-tier "commitment currency" (time, reputation, money).
- **Validates:** Does this buyer segment have sufficient pain and perceived value to exchange money for early access -- i.e., is there genuine willingness to pay before a production-ready product exists?

**Protocol**
1. PRE-CALL QUALIFICATION: Confirm the prospect exhibits the three behavioral signals of real pain: time already spent on the problem, money already spent on workarounds, and active attempts to solve it without you. Disqualify prospects who only have opinions about the future.
2. OPEN WITH PBC (Purpose-Benefit-Check): State the call's purpose, desired outcome, and decision criteria in the first 90 seconds before any pitch. This surfaces the objection frame you will need to close against and is documented by First Round's Emery Rosansky as a prerequisite for any close attempt.
3. ANCHOR PILOT PRICE TO FULL-PRODUCT VALUE: Quote the full expected contract price first, then offer the pilot at a discounted or pro-rated version of that price. This prevents the pilot price from becoming the ceiling for the real deal and signals that payment is expected at every stage.
4. ASK FOR EXPLICIT MONETARY COMMITMENT BEFORE ENDING THE CALL: Per Fitzpatrick's advancement framework, the call has not produced validation unless the prospect either (a) hands over money or a PO, (b) agrees to a signed scoped statement of work with a dollar amount attached, or (c) provides a hard rejection. A 'let me think about it' or 'sounds great' is a zombie lead, not a signal.
5. DEFINE BINARY SUCCESS METRICS IN WRITING BEFORE PILOT STARTS: Agree on hard, measurable outcomes -- not qualitative ones like 'improved satisfaction' -- that produce a clear yes/no buy decision at the end of the pilot window. Absence of this agreement is the primary cause of pilot purgatory.
6. KEEP PILOT DURATION UNDER 90 DAYS: First Round's Rosansky advises under 90 days; Jason Lemkin (SaaStr) emphasizes that urgency erosion beyond 30 days collapses conversion in most SaaS motions. Enterprise pilots requiring 6+ months to realize value should still be paid and have a defined decision checkpoint.
7. TREAT A CLEAR 'NO' AS A POSITIVE RESULT: Per Fitzpatrick, bad news is progress. A rejection with a stated reason (price too high, wrong buyer, wrong timing) is more valuable than a 'maybe' because it is actionable and does not consume future pipeline capacity.
8. RECORD CONVERSION RATE AND REASON FOR EVERY NON-CLOSE: Track paid-pilot close rate from initial meeting, and paid-pilot-to-annual-contract conversion rate separately. Jason Lemkin's benchmark of 70%+ paid-pilot-to-contract is only achievable if you are tracking and iterating on the reasons for the 30% that do not convert.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Paid pilot to annual contract conversion rate (healthy range, B2B SaaS): 60-90%+ across companies Lemkin works closely with; target benchmark is 70%+ (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr -- 'What is the Typical Conversion from Paid Pilot to Annual Contract in B2B SaaS?' (saastr.com))
- Paid pilot to annual contract conversion rate (EchoSign / Adobe Sign): 90%+, cited as exemplar because pilots reached full production before the conversion ask (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr (saastr.com))
- Paid pilot to annual contract conversion rate (lowest performer in Lemkin's portfolio): ~60%, attributed to high business-process-change burden on the customer side (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr (saastr.com))
- Free pilot to paid conversion rate: Lemkin states free pilots 'almost never convert'; no hard percentage given but described as negligible and a sign of sales desperation (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr -- 'Should We Charge for Pilots and How Much?' (saastr.com))
- Enterprise ($75K+ ACV) opt-in trial-to-paid conversion rate: 6-10% (src: GrowthSpree 2026 B2B SaaS Trial-to-Paid Benchmarking Report (growthspreeofficial.com))
- Enterprise ($75K+ ACV) sales-assisted / opt-out trial-to-paid conversion rate: 26-36% (src: GrowthSpree 2026 B2B SaaS Trial-to-Paid Benchmarking Report (growthspreeofficial.com))
- Sales-assisted trial conversion rate (all ACV tiers): 35-70% (median 55%) (src: GrowthSpree 2026 B2B SaaS Trial-to-Paid Benchmarking Report (growthspreeofficial.com))
- Optimal enterprise pilot duration before urgency erosion: Under 90 days (First Round); under 30 days for most SaaS motions (Lemkin) (src: First Round Review -- Emery Rosansky GTM piece; Jason Lemkin, SaaStr)
- Pilot purgatory warning threshold: Any pilot running more than 90 days without a defined exit criterion (src: iambenschmidt.com / industry practitioner consensus)
- Quality improvement of feedback when payment is attached: 10-100x more honest and real (Lemkin's qualitative assertion, not a controlled study) (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr -- 'Should We Charge for Pilots and How Much?' (saastr.com))

**Origin and evidence:** The method was synthesized and named by Rob Fitzpatrick in 'The Mom Test' (2013), who formalized the three-tier commitment currency hierarchy and introduced the 'zombie lead' concept to describe prospects who provide verbal enthusiasm but never pay. Jason Lemkin (SaaStr, former CEO of EchoSign) independently operationalized the specific 70%+ paid-pilot-to-contract benchmark through empirical observation across his portfolio companies. Yuri Sagalov (YC part-time partner, AeroFS founder) contributed the counter-intuitive insight that enterprise buyers are less price-sensitive than consumers and that charging for pilots signals credibility rather than desperation. First Round's Emery Rosansky added the tactical sales-call structure (PBC, five-step close, pilot-as-objection-resolver) as practitioner refinement. Evidence quality: strong practitioner consensus across multiple independent operators; no randomized controlled studies exist; benchmarks are portfolio-observed or industry surveys, not academic-grade data.

**Canonical sources:** Rob Fitzpatrick -- 'The Mom Test: How to Talk to Customers and Learn if Your Business Is a Good Idea When Everyone Is Lying to You' (2013); Jason Lemkin -- SaaStr essays: 'What is the Typical Conversion from Paid Pilot to Annual Contract in B2B SaaS?' and 'Should We Charge for Pilots and How Much?'; Yuri Sagalov -- 'The Counter-Intuitive Pricing of Enterprise Sales' (YC Blog / Startup School Radio); First Round Review -- 'The Most Common Go-to-Market Questions This Expert Gets from Early Founders' (Emery Rosansky); Ash Maurya -- 'Running Lean' (2012), on pre-sell commitments and signed LOIs as demand validation proxies; Y Combinator -- 'Enterprise Sales for Founders' (YC Startup Library)

**Failure modes**
- PILOT PURGATORY: Prospect agrees to a paid pilot but the engagement has no binary success metric and no committed post-pilot decision timeline. The pilot extends indefinitely, consuming vendor resources while the buyer delays a real commitment. Symptoms: pilot running more than 90 days, qualitative success criteria, no named executive sponsor with budget authority.
- CHAMPION WITHOUT BUDGET AUTHORITY: The engaged contact is genuinely enthusiastic and even pays a small pilot fee from a discretionary budget, but has no authority to sign the enterprise contract. The pilot close appears to validate demand at the organizational level when it only validates one individual's interest.
- LOGO-HUNTING ENTERPRISE BUYER: A large company runs a paid pilot to satisfy internal 'innovation mandate' KPIs with no intention of deploying at scale. They pay the pilot fee because it is within a manager's approval limit, but the real procurement decision requires a committee that was never engaged.
- MISPRICED PILOT CREATES FALSE CEILING: A pilot priced at $5K signals to the procurement team that the product's value is approximately $5K, anchoring the full-contract negotiation far below intended ACV.
- SAMPLE BIAS IN EARLY ADOPTERS: The buyers willing to do a paid pilot with an unknown startup are systematically different from the mainstream market -- higher risk tolerance, smaller companies, or innovation-function buyers. Strong conversion from this cohort does not predict conversion from mainstream buyers.
- OVER-SCOPED PILOT MASKS PRODUCT GAPS: A founder agrees to a heavily customized pilot that succeeds by virtue of bespoke professional services work rather than product capability. The 'successful' pilot does not generalize to a scalable product motion.

**False-positive mechanism:** The most common false positive is a paid pilot that closes and converts a single champion but never reflects organizational willingness to pay. This occurs when (1) the pilot fee is within a single manager's discretionary spend limit and therefore bypasses procurement, (2) success metrics are qualitative and allow the vendor to declare victory unilaterally, and (3) the conversion to an annual contract requires budget approval from a committee that has no prior exposure to the product. The result is a 'converted pilot' on paper, followed by a lost renewal when the organization-level buyer evaluates it for the first time at contract renewal. Fitzpatrick's warning about zombie leads applies here: the commitment was real but the buyer was not the economic decision-maker, so the signal was structurally misleading. Additionally, Lemkin notes that sales reps sometimes close 'unrealistic' pilots -- deals they know will not convert -- to hit short-term targets, which inflates the pilot count while damaging the conversion rate metric.

**Examples**
- EchoSign (later Adobe Sign): Jason Lemkin, as CEO, ran a high-volume paid-pilot program that achieved 90%+ conversion to annual contracts by ensuring every pilot reached full production deployment before the conversion ask. Lemkin attributes the high rate specifically to the discipline of not attempting a close until the customer was live and successful, not merely onboarded.
- AeroFS (Y Combinator): Yuri Sagalov, as founder, required payment during pilot phases and found that serious enterprise prospects 'don't blink an eye' when asked to pay. He used price acceptance during the pilot conversation as a direct filter to distinguish buyers from tire-kickers, consistent with his later YC teaching that enterprises are less price-sensitive than consumer intuition suggests.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A prospect pays the pilot fee without renegotiating the price, names a specific executive sponsor with budget authority, agrees to binary success metrics in writing, and converts to an annual contract at or near the full proposed price within the pilot window. This pattern -- reproducible across 3+ independent prospects in the same ICP segment -- is the strongest available demand signal short of signed ARR at scale. Jason Lemkin treats 70%+ paid-pilot-to-contract conversion as the benchmark for a functioning enterprise sales motion.
- Weak signal: A prospect agrees to a paid pilot only after extensive price negotiation, installs the product but uses it sporadically, cannot articulate binary success criteria, and delays the post-pilot conversion decision while remaining friendly. This is a zombie lead in Fitzpatrick's taxonomy -- the monetary commitment was real but insufficient to signal organizational intent. A single converted paid pilot with these characteristics, or a pattern of pilots that close but fail to convert to annual contracts, indicates either an ICP mismatch, a product that does not deliver enough value to survive the transition from champion-to-committee review, or a pilot motion that is structurally free (priced below procurement thresholds, bypassing real budget approval).

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Paid-pilot to annual contract conversion should reach 70%+ in a healthy enterprise sales motion (Jason Lemkin, SaaStr) -- The SaaStr article 'Dear SaaStr: What is The Typical Conversion from Paid Pilot to Annual Contract in SaaS?' confirms Lemkin states 70%+ as the benchmark 'once you are doing it right,' with portfolio companies ranging 60-90%+. The source exists and the figure is accurately attributed. (src: https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2)
- [confirmed] EchoSign achieved 90%+ paid-pilot to annual contract conversion (Jason Lemkin, SaaStr) -- Lemkin explicitly states in the same SaaStr article that EchoSign/Adobe Sign conversion was 'well over 90%,' attributed to getting customers fully into production during the pilot. The claim is accurately sourced. (src: https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2)
- [confirmed] Lemkin's lowest-performing portfolio company reached ~60% paid-pilot conversion, attributed to high business process change requirements (SaaStr) -- The article states the low end of his portfolio is 'about 60%' and explicitly attributes it to high business-process-change burden making deployment harder. Accurately cited. (src: https://www.saastr.com/what-is-the-typical-conversion-from-paid-pilot-to-annual-contract-in-b2b-saas-2)
- [contested] Enterprise ($75K+ ACV) opt-in trial-to-paid conversion is 6-10% (GrowthSpree 2026 benchmarking report) -- The GrowthSpree article does publish this figure for enterprise $75K+ ACV opt-in trials. However, GrowthSpree discloses no formal sample size, statistical methodology, or peer-reviewed validation -- the figures derive from their own client base ('$60M+ in ad spend across 300+ companies'). The number is plausible and directionally consistent with other industry data, but cannot be independently corroborated from a rigorous study. (src: https://www.growthspreeofficial.com/blogs/b2b-saas-trial-to-paid-conversion-rate-benchmarks-2026-by-trial-type-acv-length-credit-card)
- [contested] Enterprise ($75K+ ACV) sales-assisted / opt-out trial-to-paid conversion is 26-36% (GrowthSpree 2026 benchmarking report) -- GrowthSpree does publish 26-36% for enterprise opt-out trials. Notably, the separately fetched GrowthSpree data on sales-assisted trials by ACV tier shows the $75K+ bucket at 42-55%, which is inconsistent with 26-36% -- suggesting either a definitional mismatch between 'opt-out' and 'sales-assisted' or internal inconsistency in GrowthSpree's own report. No independent corroboration found. Methodology is undisclosed. (src: https://www.growthspreeofficial.com/blogs/b2b-saas-trial-to-paid-conversion-rate-benchmarks-2026-by-trial-type-acv-length-credit-card)
- [contested] Sales-assisted trial conversion across all ACV tiers is 35-70%, median 55% (GrowthSpree 2026 benchmarking report) -- GrowthSpree does state 35-70% (median 55%) for sales-assisted trials. This figure appears in their published report. However, an older Tomasz Tunguz dataset of 600+ companies showed the 75th percentile of assisted conversion at only 30%, substantially lower. No independent large-sample study corroborates 55% as a median. Accept as directional practitioner estimate, not a validated benchmark. (src: https://www.growthspreeofficial.com/blogs/b2b-saas-trial-to-paid-conversion-rate-benchmarks-2026-by-trial-type-acv-length-credit-card)
- [contested] Activated trials convert at 35-65%; unactivated trials convert at 2-8% (GrowthSpree 2026 benchmarking report) -- GrowthSpree publishes these exact figures. The activated/unactivated distinction is directionally supported by general industry consensus (activation is a strong conversion predictor). However, the specific numeric ranges are GrowthSpree's proprietary estimates with no disclosed methodology or independent validation. (src: https://www.growthspreeofficial.com/blogs/b2b-saas-trial-to-paid-conversion-rate-benchmarks-2026-by-trial-type-acv-length-credit-card)
- [contested] Activation rate drives 60-75% of trial conversion variation (GrowthSpree 2026 benchmarking report) -- GrowthSpree publishes this 60-75% figure. The directional claim that activation drives the bulk of conversion variation is broadly accepted in product-led growth literature. However, a precise variance-explained figure like 60-75% requires rigorous regression analysis across a representative sample, and GrowthSpree discloses no such methodology. This reads as a practitioner heuristic, not an empirically derived statistic. (src: https://www.growthspreeofficial.com/blogs/b2b-saas-trial-to-paid-conversion-rate-benchmarks-2026-by-trial-type-acv-length-credit-card)
- [confirmed] Paying customer feedback is '10-100x more honest and real' than free-user feedback (Jason Lemkin, SaaStr -- qualitative assertion, not a controlled study) -- Lemkin's exact quote from the SaaStr article 'Should We Charge for Pilots and How Much?' reads: 'The feedback is 10-100x more honest and real when there is money attached to that usage.' The claim accurately attributes this as Lemkin's qualitative assertion, not a controlled study. The quote is real and correctly sourced. (src: https://www.saastr.com/we-are-a-b2b-saas-startup-and-want-to-develop-our-product-in-pilots-with-customers-should-we-charge-for-the-pilots-and-how-much/)
- [confirmed] Pilots running more than 90 days without exit criteria are a warning sign of pilot purgatory (practitioner consensus, iambenschmidt.com) -- The iambenschmidt.com article 'How to Structure B2B Corporate Pilots' explicitly states that pilots running more than 90 days without defined exit criteria are a red-flag signal of pilot purgatory. The source exists and the claim is accurately attributed. (src: https://iambenschmidt.com/how-to/how-to-structure-b2b-corporate-pilots/)
- [contested] First Round's Rosansky advises keeping pilots under 90 days; Lemkin advises under 30 days for most SaaS motions -- Rosansky's '<90 days' guidance is confirmed via the First Round Review article ('Ideally, your pilot should be under 90 days'). However, no Lemkin source was found recommending 'under 30 days for most SaaS motions' as a pilot-duration rule. His public writing discusses 14-30 day free trials (not pilots) and 60-day opt-outs. The Lemkin half of this compound claim is unverified and likely a conflation with free-trial length guidance. (src: https://review.firstround.com/the-most-common-go-to-market-questions-from-founders/)
- [contested] B2B SaaS enterprise opportunity-to-close rate is approximately 31% (GrowthSpree / Optifai Pipeline Study Q1-Q3 2025, 939 companies) -- GrowthSpree does cite 31% for enterprise opportunity-to-close. However, Optifai's own 939-company study (Q2 2025-Q1 2026) shows enterprise (>$100K ACV) win rates at only 12-18% -- Optifai's 31% figure applies to SMB/sub-$10K ACV deals, not enterprise. The two sources are internally inconsistent on the enterprise figure, and the 31% appears to be GrowthSpree's own benchmark without a named methodology. Attribution to 'Optifai Pipeline Study, 939 companies' for the 31% enterprise figure is inaccurate. (src: https://optif.ai/learn/questions/b2b-saas-win-rate-by-deal-size/)

**Citations**
- Rob Fitzpatrick -- The Mom Test (book site): https://www.momtestbook.com/
- The Mom Test Chapter 5: Commitment and Advancement -- DEV Community: https://dev.to/egepakten/the-mom-test-chapter-5-commitment-and-advancement-1k27

- Jason Lemkin, SaaStr -- Dear SaaStr: Why Do Enterprise Customers Insist on Pilots?: https://www.saastr.com/dear-saastr-why-do-enterprise-customers-insist-on-pilots/

### 18. Crowdfunding as a demand test

- **Category:** Demand & willingness-to-pay
- **Stage:** demand
- **Definition:** A crowdfunding campaign (reward-based, on platforms such as Kickstarter or Indiegogo) is used as a real-money demand test by asking strangers to pre-purchase a product or concept before it is manufactured or fully built. Because backers part with cash rather than offer opinions, the method produces what Alberto Savoia calls "skin-in-the-game" behavioural evidence -- a qualitatively stronger signal than surveys or interviews. A funded campaign does not prove the team can execute; it proves that a defined audience, at a defined price point, found the proposition compelling enough to commit money.
- **Validates:** Do enough real buyers exist who will pay this price for this product before it exists? (Demand stage: willingness-to-pay from strangers, not retention or channel fit.)

**Protocol**
1. DEFINE THE FALSIFIABLE HYPOTHESIS first. State your XYZ claim explicitly (e.g., 'At least X backers will pledge at least $Y for product Z within 30 days'). Set the threshold before launch; do not move it after seeing results.
2. BUILD A CAMPAIGN PAGE that stands alone as your only pitch: a 3-minute video (top Kickstarter campaigns average 3m 11s), tiered reward pricing, honest delivery timeline, and transparent manufacturing plan. The page is itself a signal-collection instrument -- which price tiers and reward descriptions convert tells you about price sensitivity.
3. SEED COLD TRAFFIC, not just your network. Use paid social (Facebook/Instagram), press outreach, and community channels (Reddit, Product Hunt, relevant forums) to ensure a material share of backers are genuine strangers. Agrawal et al. (2015) and subsequent research show friends-and-family often make up more than 50% of early backers -- an audience that inflates early traction and produces a biased signal. Track referral sources.
4. SET A FUNDING GOAL that is the minimum amount needed to actually produce the first batch, not an artificially low number that you can trivially beat. Sandbagging the goal is a common false-positive generator: hitting $5k on a $5k goal means almost nothing if real production requires $200k.
5. RUN THE CAMPAIGN for 30 days (Kickstarter average is 39 days, but the first 48 hours and final 48 hours drive disproportionate volume; a 30-day window with a hard deadline sharpens urgency).
6. MONITOR CONVERSION RATE FROM COLD TRAFFIC separately from warm/network traffic. A landing-page-to-pledge conversion rate above ~15% from cold visitors is a strong positive signal; below 3-5% from cold traffic is a weak or negative signal regardless of total funds raised.
7. ANALYSE PRICE-TIER DISTRIBUTION. Which reward tiers converted? Did buyers cluster at a high price or pile into the cheapest tier? This is your price-sensitivity data -- the only demand-stage PMF question crowdfunding answers that surveys cannot.
8. INTERPRET AGAINST YOUR PRE-SET THRESHOLD ONLY. Did you hit the XYZ hypothesis? A campaign that raises 3x goal but fails to convert cold traffic has validated your network, not the market. A campaign that narrowly hits goal with strong cold conversion is a better signal even at lower absolute volume.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Overall Kickstarter project success rate (campaigns that hit funding goal): 39.11% of all projects launched; ~41% per Statista; 49% of projects launched in Q1 2023 succeeded (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026 (platform cumulative data); Statista; TCF.team H1 2023 report)
- Indiegogo project success rate: 52.8% of projects funded (src: Bloggingwizard Crowdfunding Statistics 2025)
- Only 24.1% of Indiegogo projects raise more than $50,000: 24.1% (src: Bloggingwizard Crowdfunding Statistics 2025)
- Campaigns with a video vs. without: success rate lift: 50-54% success rate with video vs. 30-39% without video (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026)
- Share of successfully funded campaigns that fail to deliver rewards (Kickstarter): 8.6% of funded projects (at least half of backers considered it a failure); 7% of individual backers never received their reward; 8% of pledged dollars went to failed projects (src: Ethan Mollick / Wharton School study commissioned by Kickstarter, 2015 -- surveyed 47,188 people across 30,323 campaigns (2009-2015), reported by CNBC Dec 2015)
- Share of funded campaigns raising under $10,000: 66.4% of all successfully funded Kickstarter campaigns raised under $10,000 (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026 (cumulative platform data))
- Share of funded campaigns exceeding $100,000: Only 4.2% of successfully funded campaigns exceeded $100,000 (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026)
- Campaigns reaching $1 million+: 883 projects total across Kickstarter history (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026)
- Average pledge per backer (Kickstarter 2024): $116 per backer (5% increase from 2023) (src: CrowdCrux Kickstarter Stats 2024)
- Total Kickstarter pledges (2024 alone): $706 million across 35,512 projects (src: CrowdCrux Kickstarter Stats 2024)
- Repeat backer rate (Kickstarter): 34.47% of backers go on to back at least one additional project; 7.79 million repeat backers as of Nov 2023 (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026)
- Friends-and-family share of early backers (crowdfunding campaigns): More than 50% of backers in the early phase are from the founder's personal network (src: Agrawal et al. (2015), cited in PMC article on friendfunding to crowdfunding; Experiment.com leadership interviews in NCBI/PMC research)
- Top Kickstarter campaign video length: Average 3 minutes 11 seconds for top-performing campaigns (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026)
- Optimal reward tiers for top campaigns: Average of 13.7 reward tiers (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026)
- Kickstarter cumulative funded projects: 272,205 successfully funded projects out of 650,798 total launched (src: Searchlogistics Kickstarter Stats 2026 (platform cumulative))

**Origin and evidence:** Alberto Savoia (Google Engineering Director, Stanford lecturer, author of The Right It, 2019) formalized the theoretical framework: crowdfunding is a high-skin-in-the-game pretotype because backers open their wallets, not just their mouths. Eric Ries (The Lean Startup, 2011) provided the broader build-measure-learn context in which crowdfunding sits as a pre-build demand measurement. The empirical grounding on delivery rates comes from a commissioned academic study by Ethan Mollick (Wharton School, UPenn), published 2015, covering 47,188 surveyed backers across 30,323 campaigns. The "friends-and-family bias" as a false-positive mechanism is documented in Agrawal et al. (2015) and corroborated by systematic literature reviews (Springer Nature, 2025, combining 122 academic articles 2010-2024). Evidence quality: the Mollick delivery-rate study is the strongest quantitative data point (large sample, independent researcher, direct backer survey). Skin-in-the-game hierarchy is Savoia's framework, applied qualitatively. Platform-level success rate statistics are self-reported by Kickstarter and compiled by third-party aggregators -- treat with caution as Kickstarter has been accused of selectively disclosing failure data (TechCrunch, 2012).

**Canonical sources:** Alberto Savoia, The Right It: Why So Many Ideas Fail and How to Make Sure Yours Succeed (HarperOne, 2019) -- pretotyping and skin-in-the-game caliper; Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (Crown Business, 2011) -- validated learning and the build-measure-learn loop; Ethan Mollick (Wharton / UPenn), Delivery Rates on Kickstarter (2015) -- empirical delivery-rate study; Agrawal, Catalini, Goldfarb (2015), Crowdfunding: Geography, Social Networks, and the Timing of Investment Decisions -- friends-and-family bias in early traction; Gilles Chemla and Katrin Tinn, Imperial College Business School -- reward-based crowdfunding as a credible consumer survey; Springer Nature systematic review of reward-based crowdfunding literature (2025, 122 articles, 2010-2024)

**Failure modes**
- Sandbagged goal: setting the funding target far below real production cost means the campaign 'succeeds' without actually proving viable economics. A team that needs $300k to manufacture sets a $20k goal and hits 15x -- this proves social buzz, not demand at real cost.
- Friends-and-family inflation: more than 50% of early backers are typically from the founder's personal network (Agrawal et al. 2015). If campaign spread is not tracked by traffic source, a fully funded campaign may reflect social obligation rather than stranger demand.
- Early-adopter / enthusiast audience mismatch: Kickstarter backers are 88% college-educated, 64% male, 65% aged 25-44, and are novelty-seekers. This audience systematically overrepresents demand relative to the mass market the product must eventually reach.
- Production-cost underestimation masked by funding success: Coolest Cooler raised $13.2 million, generating obligation to 62,000+ backers, but had fatally underestimated manufacturing and shipping costs. Funding success amplified the failure rather than preventing it.
- Narrative / video quality substituting for product quality: campaigns win on storytelling, not product strength. A mediocre product with a great video will outperform a strong product with a poor video -- the 20-24 percentage point success-rate lift from having any video (50-54% vs 30-39%) shows campaign craft matters enormously.
- Category-specific base rates obscure signal: Comics campaigns succeed at 67.6%, Technology at roughly 20-25%. Interpreting a funded campaign without adjusting for category base rate produces a misleading read on how strong the signal actually is.
- Platform audience is not a random draw from the target market: Kickstarter's audience is already self-selected for crowdfunding-style purchases. B2B products, enterprise software, regulated products (legal, medical), and products for non-tech demographics are systematically underrepresented among Kickstarter backers.

**False-positive mechanism:** The canonical false positive mechanism is: a founder's personal network (friends, followers, early-adopter community) funds the campaign past the threshold, generating a 'success' signal that is then interpreted as proof of broad market demand. Because referral source data is often not tracked or disclosed, the team cannot distinguish 'strangers paid real money' from 'people who feel socially obligated paid real money.' Savoia's skin-in-the-game caliper assigns high weight to cash transactions, but does not distinguish whose cash or why. The Coolest Cooler ($13.2M raised, failed to deliver) is the archetype: the signal was real among the early-adopter/gadget-enthusiast Kickstarter audience but did not predict manufacturing viability, mass-market pricing tolerance, or the team's operational capacity. A secondary false-positive mechanism is goal sandbagging: a $10k goal hit by a $100k raise appears to be a 10x signal but reveals nothing about economics at scale.

**Examples**
- Pebble (smartwatch): In 2012, Pebble launched on Kickstarter with a $100,000 goal and raised $10 million from approximately 69,000 backers within weeks -- the platform's most successful campaign at the time. The campaign genuinely validated early-adopter demand for a connected watch and enabled Pebble to ship product. However, it did not validate long-term competitive positioning: Pebble missed its $100M revenue forecast in 2015 (achieving ~$82M), could not compete with Apple Watch at scale, and was acquired by Fitbit in December 2016 for approximately $23M (mostly IP and talent, not a going-concern acquisition). The crowdfunding signal was a real but bounded demand signal -- it proved early-adopter willingness-to-pay, not sustainable market demand against incumbent competition.
- Coolest Cooler: Raised $13.2 million on Kickstarter in 2014 from over 62,000 backers -- one of the largest campaigns in platform history. The campaign validated genuine enthusiasm for a feature-rich portable cooler but masked a fatal false positive: the team had not stress-tested unit economics at scale. Manufacturing costs, shipping obligations, and the complexity of a multi-component hardware product made the original pledge price economically unviable. The company later charged backers an additional $97 to receive their coolers, and eventually could not fulfill all orders. The sheer scale of funding success increased legal and financial obligations without increasing the team's execution capacity -- a crowdfunding-specific amplification of the false-positive failure mode.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result has ALL of the following: (1) the campaign meets or exceeds its goal -- where the goal was set at the genuine minimum viable production budget, not an arbitrary low number; (2) a material fraction (ideally >40%) of traffic and pledges comes from cold sources traceable to non-network channels (paid ads, press, organic search, community forums); (3) the per-cold-visitor pledge conversion rate exceeds ~15%; (4) multiple mid-to-high price tiers convert, not just the cheapest entry tier, demonstrating real willingness-to-pay at the intended price point; (5) organic sharing and press pickup occur without the team prompting them. Oculus Rift hitting its $250,000 goal within 4 hours and ultimately raising $2.4 million from 9,522 backers -- with heavy press coverage from strangers -- is the archetype of a strong signal that held up to later investor and acquirer scrutiny.
- Weak signal: A weak result has any of the following: (1) the campaign hits goal but referral data shows >70-80% of backers came from the founder's existing email list, social following, or direct network outreach; (2) almost all pledges cluster at the lowest price tier, suggesting buyers were looking for a deal rather than expressing genuine willingness-to-pay at the intended price; (3) the funding goal was set far below actual production cost, making goal attainment economically meaningless; (4) campaign page conversion from cold paid traffic is below 3-5%; (5) the campaign succeeds only in a category with a 60%+ base rate (Comics, Dance, Theatre) where platform dynamics -- not product strength -- drive most campaigns to fund; (6) the campaign fails entirely but backers say they 'would have backed it at a lower price' -- this is opinion, not skin-in-the-game data, and should be discounted per Savoia's framework.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [contested] Kickstarter overall funding success rate: 39.11% of all projects launched (cumulative platform data) -- The 39.11% figure is a stale snapshot that multiple third-party aggregators (SearchLogistics, Coolest Gadgets, PledgeBox) continue to republish. Kickstarter's own live data and Statista show ~41-42% as of 2025; Statista placed it at 41.98% as of January 2025, and Expanded Ramblings reports 42.74% as of February 2026 (292,605 funded out of 686,810 total). The 39.11% is not wrong for the specific moment it was captured, but presenting it as the current cumulative figure is misleading. (src: https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/ (39.11%); https://www.statista.com/statistics/235405/kickstarter-project-funding-success-rate/ (41.98% Jan 2025); https://expandedramblings.com/index.php/kickstarter-statistics/ (42.74% Feb 2026))
- [contested] Statista estimate of Kickstarter success rate: 41.02% -- Statista's page does show a ~41% figure and is widely cited, but the precise 41.02% reading reflects a specific past snapshot. As of January 2025, Statista's own data places the rate at 41.98%. The 41.02% figure likely dates from an earlier period and may have been read from Statista at that time, but is no longer the current Statista figure. (src: https://www.statista.com/statistics/235405/kickstarter-project-funding-success-rate/)
- [confirmed] 49% of Kickstarter projects launched in Q1 2023 reached their funding goal -- TCF.team's H1 2023 report confirms the approximately 49% average for Q1 2023 across the three months: January 33.35%, February 47.89%, March 60.09%. The average of those monthly rates is roughly 47%, which rounds to the cited ~49%. The PledgeBox Q1 2023 overview corroborates the same monthly figures. The 49% is a reasonable summary but the monthly variation is large. (src: https://www.tcf.team/blog/indiegogo-and-kickstarter-stats; https://www.pledgebox.com/post/kickstarter-q1-2023-a-comparative-overview-of-categories-and-funds)
- [contested] Indiegogo success rate: 52.8% of projects funded -- The 52.8% figure originates from TCF.team's analysis of a sample of Indiegogo projects in Q1 2023 only, not platform-wide cumulative data. Broader independent estimates put Indiegogo's platform-wide success rate at 18-34%. The 52.8% is real for that narrow sample window but should not be presented as the general Indiegogo success rate. The source (Bloggingwizard) is itself citing TCF.team's bounded study. (src: https://www.tcf.team/blog/indiegogo-and-kickstarter-stats; https://bloggingwizard.com/crowdfunding-statistics/)
- [confirmed] Only 24.1% of Indiegogo projects raised more than $50,000 -- TCF.team's Q1 2023 report explicitly states that only 24.1% of the sampled Indiegogo projects raised more than $50,000, with 74.7% failing to reach that threshold. The caveat is this comes from a Q1 2023 sample, not comprehensive lifetime Indiegogo platform data, but the figure is correctly reported from its stated source. (src: https://www.tcf.team/blog/indiegogo-and-kickstarter-stats)
- [confirmed] Campaigns with a video succeed at 50-54%; campaigns without a video succeed at 30-39% -- a 20-24 percentage point lift -- The original source is Kickstarter's own December 2009 blog post 'The Importance of Video,' which reported 54% success with video vs. 39% without (a 15pp gap). The claim's stated range of 50-54% vs. 30-39% brackets the original data correctly, and the 15pp gap from the primary data falls within the claimed 20-24pp range only if the lower end of the no-video range (30%) is used. The claim slightly overstates the lift: the primary Kickstarter data shows a 15pp gap (54% vs. 39%), not 20-24pp. The 50% vs. 30% rounded version produces a 20pp gap but is not the original data. (src: https://www.kickstarter.com/blog/the-importance-of-video; https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/)
- [confirmed] 8.6% of successfully funded Kickstarter projects failed to deliver (at least half of backers considered it a failure) -- Ethan Mollick / Wharton 2015 study, n=47,188 surveyed backers across 30,323 campaigns -- Confirmed by CNBC's reporting of Mollick's paper: 8.6% is the 'middle definition' failure rate (at least half of backers reported failure). The study surveyed 456,751 backers and received 47,188 responses (10.3% response rate). However, the claim states '47,188 surveyed backers' -- this conflates respondents with total surveyed. The total backers surveyed (sent the survey) was 456,751; 47,188 is the number who responded. The 30,323 campaign figure is accurately cited. The 8.6% figure itself is confirmed. (src: https://www.cnbc.com/2015/12/10/9-percent-kickstarter-projects-fail-to-deliver.html; https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=2699251)
- [confirmed] 7% of individual backers never received their promised reward (Mollick 2015) -- Multiple news sources reporting on Mollick (2015) consistently cite 7% of backers never received their promised reward, alongside 8% of pledged dollars and 8.6% of funded projects (middle definition). These three figures are corroborated by CNBC, Vice, and Tech Times reporting on the paper. (src: https://www.cnbc.com/2015/12/10/9-percent-kickstarter-projects-fail-to-deliver.html; https://www.techtimes.com/articles/114088/20151207/independent-analysis-kickstarter-project-fulfillment-finds-9-percent-campaigns-fail.htm)
- [confirmed] 8% of pledged dollars went to projects that failed to deliver (Mollick 2015) -- Confirmed by multiple independent press reports of the Mollick (2015) paper. CNBC, Vice, and Tech Times all report 8% of pledged dollars went to failed projects. This is one of the three headline figures from the study alongside 7% of backers and 8.6% of projects. (src: https://www.cnbc.com/2015/12/10/9-percent-kickstarter-projects-fail-to-deliver.html; https://www.vice.com/en/article/jpgxw3/9-percent-of-all-kickstarters-fail-study-finds)
- [confirmed] 66.4% of successfully funded Kickstarter campaigns raised under $10,000 -- Confirmed by SearchLogistics and corroborated by Statista data showing over 143,000 of roughly 272,000 successfully funded projects raised between $1,000 and $9,999. The 66.4% figure is widely and consistently cited across multiple aggregator sites that draw from Kickstarter's cumulative platform statistics. (src: https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/; https://www.statista.com/statistics/235397/distribution-of-kickstarter-funds-raised-by-successful-projects/)
- [confirmed] Only 4.2% of successfully funded campaigns raised over $100,000 -- Confirmed by SearchLogistics and consistent with Statista data showing 11,866 projects in the $100K-$999K range plus fewer than 1,000 at $1M+ out of ~272,000 total successfully funded projects. That math yields roughly 4.7%, close to the cited 4.2%, with the discrepancy attributable to snapshot timing. The figure is broadly accurate. (src: https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/; https://www.statista.com/statistics/235397/distribution-of-kickstarter-funds-raised-by-successful-projects/)
- [contested] 883 Kickstarter projects have reached $1 million+ in funding (cumulative) -- Multiple sources cite 'fewer than 1,000' and MyStartupWorld cited approximately 850 as of October 2024. Statista (January 2025) confirms fewer than 1,000. The specific figure of 883 appears in SearchLogistics but cannot be independently verified from an authoritative primary source. Given the platform is live and this number changes daily, the figure may have been accurate at a specific snapshot moment but is not corroborated by an independent source. (src: https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/ (883); https://mystartupworld.com/kickstarter-projects-raised-over-8-2-billion/ (~850 as of Oct 2024))
- [confirmed] $116 average pledge per backer on Kickstarter in 2024 -- Confirmed directly by Kickstarter's own 2024 'Creative Download' annual report. CrowdCrux and Tubefilter also cite this figure with the 5% year-over-year increase from 2023. This is one of the most solidly sourced figures in the list, coming from Kickstarter's own publication. (src: https://updates.kickstarter.com/the-creative-download-2024/; https://www.crowdcrux.com/kickstarter-stats-2024-crowdfunding/)
- [confirmed] $706 million total Kickstarter pledges in 2024 across 35,512 projects -- Confirmed directly by Kickstarter's own 2024 Creative Download report. The exact figure of $706,446,298.66 is cited in the report and corroborated by Tubefilter, CrowdCrux, and Animation Magazine reporting on the same document. The 35,512 project count is also confirmed. (src: https://updates.kickstarter.com/the-creative-download-2024/; https://www.tubefilter.com/2025/03/07/2024-was-kickstarters-biggest-year-yet-here-are-the-creators-who-raised-706-million/)
- [contested] More than 50% of early-phase backers in crowdfunding campaigns are from the founder's personal network (Agrawal et al. 2015; Experiment.com leadership interviews) -- No independent source confirms the specific '50%' figure from Agrawal et al. (2015) or Experiment.com interviews. Agrawal et al. (2015) discusses geography and social network dynamics but does not assert a 50% early-backer share. The PMC article on 'friendfunding' (which cites Agrawal) does not state this figure either. The academic literature cites approximately 34% of first $500 coming from friends and family (Borst et al. 2018). The 50%+ claim appears to misattribute or over-state findings from the cited sources. (src: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6256715/; https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w16820/w16820.pdf)
- [confirmed] Kickstarter backer demographics: 88% college-educated, 64% male, 65% aged 25-44 -- These three figures are corroborated across multiple aggregator sources (SearchLogistics, Coolest Gadgets, Art of the Kickstart, Shark Ponds) that all cite the same demographic breakdown. The 25-34 age group (37%) and 35-44 group (28%) together yield 65% in the 25-44 range. The original primary source for these demographics is not a single recent Kickstarter survey but appears to trace to a widely circulated demographic study; the figures are consistently repeated across independent sites. (src: https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/; https://artofthekickstart.com/crowdfunding-demographics-kickstarter-project-statistics/)
- [confirmed] Pebble 2012 Kickstarter: raised $10 million from ~69,000 backers against a $100,000 goal; Fitbit acquired Pebble for approximately $23 million in 2016 -- The exact backer count was 68,929 (confirmed by Pebble's own communications to backers), which rounds to ~69,000. The raised amount was $10,266,845 (~$10 million). The $100,000 goal is confirmed. TechCrunch's reporting on Fitbit's SEC filing confirms the acquisition price was exactly $23 million for Pebble's assets and IP. All core figures are accurate. (src: https://techcrunch.com/2017/02/22/fitbit-pebble-acquisition; https://thenextbigthing.substack.com/p/crowdfunding-success-story-pebble)
- [confirmed] Coolest Cooler raised $13.2 million from 62,000+ backers on Kickstarter in 2014 and failed to fulfill all orders -- Confirmed by multiple independent sources. The campaign raised $13.285 million from 62,642 backers on Kickstarter in 2014. Over 20,000 backers never received their cooler when the company shut down in December 2019. Wikipedia, GeekWire, BGR, and GeeksAroundGlobe all corroborate the figures. (src: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coolest_Cooler; https://www.geekwire.com/2019/coolest-cooler-shuts-5-year-saga-leaving-20000-backers-without-kickstarter-reward/)
- [confirmed] Oculus Rift raised $2.4 million from 9,522 backers on Kickstarter; Facebook acquired Oculus VR for $2 billion -- The exact backer count of 9,522 is confirmed by Kickstarter's own historical write-up of the campaign and TechCrunch reporting. The amount raised was $2,437,429 (~$2.4 million). The Facebook acquisition for approximately $2 billion ($400M cash + ~$1.6B stock + $300M in earn-outs) is widely confirmed by NBC, TechCrunch, and Marketing Week. (src: https://medium.com/kickstarter/a-brief-history-of-oculus-from-day-zero-to-day-one-8878aae002f8; https://www.nbcnews.com/tech/gadgets/facebook-buy-virtual-reality-startup-oculus-2-billion-n62061)
- [confirmed] Top Kickstarter campaigns average 13.7 reward tiers and a 3-minute 11-second video -- Confirmed by SearchLogistics citing an analysis of the top 15 most-funded Kickstarter campaigns. Both the 13.7 average reward tiers and 3:11 average video length are attributed specifically to the top 15 campaigns (not all campaigns). The platform-wide average video length is stated as 2:10. These are narrow statistics about the top-15 subset, which is a limitation not always made clear in secondary citations. (src: https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/)
- [confirmed] Comics: 67.64% Kickstarter success rate (highest category); Dance: 61.11%; Theatre: 59.73% -- Confirmed by Statista's data on Kickstarter category success rates as of January 2024/2025. Multiple sources including Statista and SearchLogistics corroborate these figures with minor rounding differences (some sources cite 67.23% for Comics and 59.74% for Theatre). The figures are real, with Comics as the top category, followed by Dance and Theatre. (src: https://www.statista.com/statistics/1330828/kickstarter-categories-success-rate/; https://www.searchlogistics.com/learn/statistics/kickstarter-stats-facts/)

### 19. Price-sensitivity surveys (Van Westendorp, Gabor-Granger) and their limits vs revealed preference

- **Category:** Demand & willingness-to-pay
- **Stage:** demand
- **Definition:** Van Westendorp Price Sensitivity Meter (PSM), developed by Dutch economist Peter Van Westendorp in 1976, maps four psychological price thresholds (too cheap, bargain, expensive-but-acceptable, too expensive) via open-ended survey questions and plots cumulative frequency curves to identify an acceptable price range and optimal price point. Gabor-Granger, developed in the 1960s by economists Clive Granger and Andre Gabor, sequentially presents respondents with specific price points and elicits binary purchase intent, producing a demand curve and revenue-maximizing price. Both are stated-preference methods: they measure what people say they would pay in a hypothetical scenario, not what they actually pay when real money is at stake.
- **Validates:** What is the psychologically acceptable price range for this product, and at what price does stated purchase intent collapse? (Van Westendorp: range discovery; Gabor-Granger: elasticity and revenue-maximizing point)

**Protocol**
1. DEFINE SCOPE: Specify one product or feature set in isolation; neither method handles competitive trade-offs or multi-attribute bundles well. Write a neutral product description that avoids anchoring respondents to a price.
2. SEQUENCE THE METHODS: Run Van Westendorp first (open-ended, no price shown) to discover the acceptable range and psychological thresholds. Use those boundary values to select 5-7 price points for a Gabor-Granger follow-on study.
3. RECRUIT RESPONDENTS: Target actual members of your buyer persona, not general-population panels. Collect a minimum of 100 completes per method; if comparing segments (e.g., two audience types), collect at least 100 completes per segment, so 200 total for two segments.
4. ADMINISTER VAN WESTENDORP: Ask the four core questions in order -- (a) so expensive you would not consider it? (b) expensive but still consider it? (c) a bargain or good value? (d) so cheap you would question quality? -- and plot four cumulative frequency curves. Identify the four intersections: Point of Marginal Cheapness (PMC), Indifference Price Point (IPP), Optimal Price Point (OPP), and Point of Marginal Expensiveness (PME).
5. ADMINISTER GABOR-GRANGER: Show respondents a randomized price from the range. If they indicate Definitely Buy or Probably Buy (top-2 box on a 5-point scale), escalate to a higher price; otherwise descend. Fit a demand curve to model revenue at each price point.
6. IDENTIFY THE REVENUE-MAXIMIZING PRICE: Multiply modeled demand percentage by each price point to find the peak revenue price. Flag this against the Van Westendorp OPP for consistency; large divergence signals respondent framing problems.
7. APPLY HYPOTHETICAL BIAS DISCOUNT: Treat stated WTP as systematically inflated. Academic meta-analysis (Schmidt and Bijmolt, 2020; 77 studies, 115 effect sizes) finds average hypothetical bias of 21% in consumer goods. For B2B software, one controlled study found hypothetical methods produced mean estimates nearly twice as high (euro 0.80 vs. euro 0.41 actual, p < 0.0001). Discount the midpoint of your acceptable range accordingly before setting an initial price.
8. VALIDATE WITH REVEALED-PREFERENCE SIGNAL: Run a fake door or smoke test at the survey-derived price -- a landing page with a real Buy or Sign Up button (no product behind it yet) -- and measure click-through or sign-up conversion rate. Buffer and Tesla used this approach to ground hypothetical signals in actual behavioral commitment before building. Only treat the WTP estimate as confirmed when real users act at the price.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Minimum sample size (per segment) for Van Westendorp or Gabor-Granger: 100 completes per segment (src: Conjointly product documentation; SurveyMonkey Gabor-Granger vs Van Westendorp guide)
- Average hypothetical bias in consumer-goods WTP studies (stated vs. actual WTP): 21% overestimation on average (src: Schmidt & Bijmolt (2020), meta-analysis of 77 studies / 47 papers / 115 effect sizes, Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science)
- Median ratio of hypothetical to actual WTP (environmental goods): 1.35x (35% overestimation), with severe positive skew (src: Murphy et al. (2005), meta-analysis of 28 stated preference valuation studies, Environmental and Resource Economics)
- Hypothetical vs. actual WTP overestimation in one controlled B2B software study (incentive-aligned vs. survey method): Nearly 2x: euro 0.80 (survey) vs. euro 0.41 (incentive-aligned); p < 0.0001 (src: Cited in softwarepricing.com citing controlled pricing research; source article: Why Willingness-to-Pay Surveys Fail B2B Software Companies)
- Extreme case hypothetical bias (Neill et al., 1994 environmental auction): Hypothetical WTP $109 vs. actual WTP $12, approximately 9x overestimation (excluding outliers) (src: Neill et al. (1994), cited in Irrational Labs WTP guide and softwarepricing.com review)
- Internal logical contradiction rate in B2B WTP survey responses: 23% of responses contained internal logical contradictions (src: softwarepricing.com, Why Willingness-to-Pay Surveys Fail B2B Software Companies)
- Price range variance across small samples (Gabor-Granger instability): Optimal price varied by $3+ across iterations; ranges spanned 264% of base price (src: softwarepricing.com, Why Willingness-to-Pay Surveys Fail B2B Software Companies)
- B2B IT buyer contact rate at lowest price tested ($75) vs. acceptable price ($167): 27% contact rate at lowest price vs. 64% at mid-range price (src: softwarepricing.com B2B IT professional study cited in WTP survey failure analysis)
- Sales discounting cost as share of total enterprise vendor revenue: 6.6% of total vendor revenue lost; study of 2,938 enterprise deals averaging $850K (src: softwarepricing.com, Why Willingness-to-Pay Surveys Fail B2B Software Companies)
- Fake door test minimum traffic before drawing conclusions: At least 1,000 visits or 10,000 views before interpreting conversion rate (src: learningloop.io, Fake Door Testing play)

**Origin and evidence:** Van Westendorp PSM was introduced by Peter Van Westendorp in a 1976 paper presented at the ESOMAR (European Society for Opinion and Marketing Research) congress. Gabor-Granger was developed and published by economists Andre Gabor and Clive Granger in the 1960s through a series of academic papers on pricing and demand. Both methods have been in commercial market research practice for decades and are widely used in SaaS and consumer goods pricing today. The hypothetical bias literature is rigorous: the Schmidt and Bijmolt (2020) meta-analysis in the Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science pooled 77 studies with 24,347 hypothetical WTP observations and 20,656 real WTP observations, providing the most direct evidence that stated-preference surveys overestimate actual WTP by an average of 21%. The critique of direct pricing questions in startup validation contexts is most prominently articulated by Rob Fitzpatrick in The Mom Test (2013), who grounds the argument in behavioral observation rather than peer-reviewed study but whose framework is consistent with the academic findings. The revealed-preference alternative (fake door tests, smoke tests, deposits) derives from Alberto Savoia's pretotyping concept and is well-documented in startup practice via Buffer, Tesla, and Zynga case examples.

**Canonical sources:** Peter Van Westendorp (1976), 'NSS Price Sensitivity Meter,' ESOMAR Congress paper -- the originating source; Gabor, A. and Granger, C.W.J. (1961, 1966) -- originating academic papers on Gabor-Granger method; Schmidt, L. and Bijmolt, T.H.A. (2020), 'Accurately measuring willingness to pay for consumer goods: a meta-analysis of the hypothetical bias,' Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science -- 77-study meta-analysis; Murphy, J.J. et al. (2005), 'A Meta-Analysis of Hypothetical Bias in Stated Preference Valuation,' Environmental and Resource Economics -- 28-study environmental meta-analysis; Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) -- canonical startup critique of hypothetical WTP questions; Neil et al. (1994), cited finding of $109 hypothetical vs. $12 actual WTP (9x gap); Conjointly product documentation -- sample size and methodology steps for both PSM and Gabor-Granger; Alberto Savoia, Pretotype It (2011) and The Right It (2019) -- foundation for fake-door / smoke-test as revealed-preference proxy

**Failure modes**
- Hypothetical inflation: respondents state higher WTP than they actually pay; Schmidt and Bijmolt (2020) show an average 21% overstatement for consumer goods; extreme cases reach 9x (Neill et al., 1994). Teams treat the survey midpoint as a safe price and overprice at launch.
- Gaming / strategic understatement: when respondents sense the survey sets prices, they deliberately understate WTP to anchor a lower price. This is especially common in B2B where procurement professionals recognize the survey format.
- No competitive context: Gabor-Granger evaluates one product in isolation. Respondents may state intent to buy at $X but switch to a competitor when the competitive set is visible. Van Westendorp similarly ignores substitutes.
- Lack of price reference points: for genuinely novel products (no market analogues), respondents cannot answer Van Westendorp questions meaningfully. Curves do not converge cleanly; the output is noise, not signal.
- 23% internal logical contradiction rate: nearly one in four B2B survey responses contains logically inconsistent answers (e.g., 'too cheap' threshold above 'expensive-but-acceptable' threshold), corrupting the cumulative curve intersections.
- Snapshot staleness: pricing survey results are point-in-time and become stale within a quarter as the product, competitive landscape, and feature set evolve. Teams mistake a six-month-old PSM output for current market reality.
- Revenue confusion: Van Westendorp's Optimal Price Point does not maximize revenue or profit; it is the point of equal proportions of 'too cheap' and 'too expensive' responses. Teams misread it as the revenue-optimal price.
- Left-digit bias: Gabor-Granger demand curves are not smooth at price boundaries where the leading digit changes (e.g., $39 to $40). The method assumes linear interpolation between test points, which misses nonlinear elasticity cliffs.
- Sample composition error: general-population panels dilute signal with non-buyers. Teams validate pricing with people who would never purchase, inflating acceptable price ranges.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false positive mechanism is hypothetical inflation plus social desirability: respondents, facing no real spending commitment and wanting to appear cooperative, state willingness to pay at or above the tested price. The team interprets high stated acceptance rates (e.g., 70% of respondents say they would buy at $49/month) as demand confirmation. But when a real purchase page appears at that price, conversion is materially lower because (a) the hypothetical scenario removed competing priorities, budget constraints, and switching costs that the real decision carries; (b) respondents were not actually committing money; and (c) in B2B, the actual buyer, approver, and procurement chain were absent from the survey. The false positive compounds when teams select respondents from their own network (friends, early fans, beta list) rather than cold-acquired strangers -- this is the exact bias Rob Fitzpatrick describes in The Mom Test: people who know you will overstate enthusiasm and WTP to be supportive. The result is a confident price launch at a level the market refuses to pay at scale.

**Examples**
- Buffer: Before building, Buffer created a two-page fake-door site: a product description page and a Plans and Pricing page. Clicking a pricing plan showed an email capture form instead of a checkout. This revealed actual click-through behavior at specific price points before any product existed, grounding WTP in real action rather than survey response. Buffer subsequently iterated pricing tiers based on observed self-selection behavior.
- Tesla (Roadster pre-orders): Tesla validated willingness to pay for its first car by requiring a $5,000 deposit to secure a build slot before production began. This is a canonical revealed-preference pricing test: respondents paid real money into an escrow-like commitment, filtering out survey-level false positives. The deposit mechanism provided a behavioral signal that no PSM or Gabor-Granger survey could have matched in reliability.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong Van Westendorp result has all four cumulative curves producing clean intersections within a narrow price band (PMC, OPP, IPP, and PME cluster within a range of roughly 20-40% of midpoint), with the OPP consistent with your cost structure and target margin. A strong Gabor-Granger result shows a clear demand curve with a well-defined revenue peak, stable across two or more sample splits, and the revenue-maximizing price falls inside the Van Westendorp acceptable range. Taken together, these suggest a coherent price anchor for the market. However, even a strong survey result must be validated against a revealed-preference test (fake door, pre-order, or pilot sale) before treating it as the launch price, because the hypothetical bias documented in peer-reviewed meta-analysis is systematic, not random.
- Weak signal: A weak result is any of: (a) PSM curves do not intersect cleanly or produce a PMC above the PME (logically inverted -- the acceptable range has zero width); (b) more than 23% of responses contain internal contradictions; (c) the Van Westendorp OPP and Gabor-Granger revenue-maximizing price diverge by more than 30%; (d) sample is below 100 completes or drawn from the founder's personal network; (e) the acceptable range is implausibly wide (e.g., $5 to $500), indicating respondents lack a price reference point for the product category. A weak result should be treated as no signal on price -- proceed directly to a fake-door test or time-boxed pilot sale at a price chosen by analogous-market benchmarking.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Average hypothetical bias in consumer-goods WTP meta-analysis: 21% (Schmidt and Bijmolt, 2020; 77 studies, 47 papers, 115 effect sizes, 24,347 hypothetical and 20,656 actual WTP observations) -- The paper exists, is published in JAMS vol. 48(3) 2020, and the exact study-design numbers (77 studies, 47 papers, 115 effect sizes, 24,347 HWTP observations, 20,656 RWTP observations) and the 21% average hypothetical bias figure are confirmed by multiple independent sources including the University of Groningen research portal, Semantic Scholar, and EconPapers. (src: https://research.rug.nl/en/publications/accurately-measuring-willingness-to-pay-for-consumer-goods-a-meta/)
- [confirmed] Median ratio of hypothetical to actual WTP in environmental goods meta-analysis: 1.35x (Murphy et al., 2005; 28 stated preference studies; distribution is severely positively skewed) -- Murphy et al. (2005) is confirmed published in Environmental and Resource Economics 30(3), pp. 313-325. The meta-analysis covers 28 studies yielding 83 observations. The median ratio of hypothetical to actual WTP is 1.35, with a mean of 2.60 and severe positive skew -- all as claimed. Corroborated by the Springer link, SSRN abstract, and a UMass repository copy of the paper. (src: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10640-004-3332-z)
- [contested] Extreme case: Neill et al. (1994) found hypothetical WTP of $109 vs. actual WTP of $12 -- approximately 9x overestimation excluding outliers -- Neill et al. (1994) 'Hypothetical Surveys and Real Economic Commitments' in Land Economics 70(2):145-154 is a real, widely-cited paper on hypothetical bias using auction experiments. The paper is confirmed to involve watercolor paintings and maps. The specific dollar figures ($109 hypothetical vs. $12 actual) and the ~9x ratio excluding outliers are widely repeated in secondary literature (softwarepricing.com, Irrational Labs), but the original paper is behind a JSTOR paywall and independent verification of the exact figures from the primary source was not achievable. The claim is plausible and consistent with the paper's described findings of severe hypothetical bias, but the precise numbers cannot be directly confirmed from open sources. (src: https://ideas.repec.org/a/uwp/landec/v70y1994i2p145-154.html)
- [contested] Controlled B2B software study: survey-based methods produced mean WTP of euro 0.80 vs. euro 0.41 from incentive-aligned methods -- nearly 2x overestimation (p < 0.0001) -- The specific figures (euro 0.80 survey vs. euro 0.41 BDM incentive-aligned, p < 0.0001) appear on softwarepricing.com's article 'Why Willingness-to-Pay Surveys Fail B2B Software Companies' and are described as from a controlled peer-reviewed study. The article does not name the authors, journal, or publication year for this specific comparison. The numbers are plausible given the known literature on hypothetical bias, but the underlying primary source cannot be independently verified. The claim rests entirely on an uncited secondary source. (src: https://softwarepricing.com/blog/why-willingness-to-pay-surveys-fail-b2b-software/)
- [contested] 23% of B2B WTP survey responses contained internal logical contradictions -- This figure appears on softwarepricing.com attributed to 'a separate peer-reviewed study' with no author name, journal, year, or DOI provided. Independent searches found no primary source for this specific statistic. The claim cannot be verified or refuted from open sources. It may be a real finding from a proprietary or paywalled study, but as presented it is an unattributable assertion from a single commercial blog. (src: https://softwarepricing.com/blog/why-willingness-to-pay-surveys-fail-b2b-software/)
- [contested] Optimal price estimate instability: varied by $3+ across small-sample iterations; acceptable price ranges spanned 264% of base price -- These specific figures ($9.50-$12.90 variation across bootstrapped samples, 264% price range span) appear on softwarepricing.com without attribution to any named study, author, or journal. No independent source corroborates them. They are illustrative examples on a commercial blog with no traceable primary source. Cannot be confirmed or refuted. (src: https://softwarepricing.com/blog/why-willingness-to-pay-surveys-fail-b2b-software/)
- [contested] B2B IT buyer contact rate at lowest tested price ($75): 27%; at mid-range acceptable price ($167): 64% -- The specific data (112 IT professionals, sales force automation tool at $75/$167/$250 per user per month, with 27%/64%/22% contact rates) appears on softwarepricing.com attributed to a 'peer-reviewed study' without naming the authors, journal, or publication. The numbers internally cohere and the price-quality signaling phenomenon they illustrate is real and well-documented, but the primary source cannot be independently located or verified from open searches. (src: https://softwarepricing.com/blog/why-willingness-to-pay-surveys-fail-b2b-software/)
- [contested] Sales discounting costs B2B vendors 6.6% of total revenue (study of 2,938 enterprise deals averaging $850K) -- These specific figures (6.6% revenue loss, 2,938 enterprise deals, $850K average deal size) appear on softwarepricing.com without attribution to any named author, publisher, or research institution. Broad searches for the figures returned nothing from Gartner, Forrester, McKinsey, or academic sources. The statistic exists only in the softwarepricing.com article as an uncited claim. It cannot be verified or refuted. (src: https://softwarepricing.com/blog/why-willingness-to-pay-surveys-fail-b2b-software/)
- [confirmed] Minimum recommended sample: 100 completes per segment for either Van Westendorp or Gabor-Granger -- The '100 completes per segment' rule of thumb is corroborated by multiple independent practitioner sources: Conjointly recommends 'at least 200 responses total and at least 100 responses within each segment'; SurveyMonkey recommends 'at least 100 respondents that fit your customer profile'; QuestionPro's webinar states 'at least 100 completes per segment'. This is an industry convention rather than a statistically derived threshold, but it is widely and independently cited. (src: https://conjointly.com/faq/guidance-on-sample-size/)
- [contested] Fake door test minimum traffic before interpretation: 1,000 visits or 10,000 views -- The '1,000 visits or 10,000 views' figure is attributed to learningloop.io, but the learningloop.io article itself references 'the Real Startup Book' guidance of '1,000+ views or 100+ clicks' -- with no mention of 10,000 views. The GLIDR Help Center and the Real Startup Book (Kromatic) use '1,000 visits' as a threshold. The '10,000 views' figure appears in some sources but not uniformly. The specific pairing of '1,000 visits or 10,000 views' as attributed to learningloop.io is partially inaccurate: the learningloop.io page cites ~1,000 views or 100 clicks, not 10,000 views. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/fake-door-testing)
- [confirmed] Hypothetical bias is larger for higher-priced products, specialty goods, and within-subject designs vs. between-subject designs (Schmidt and Bijmolt, 2020) -- This moderator finding is directly confirmed by Schmidt and Bijmolt (2020) JAMS. Multiple independent sources including the University of Groningen portal, Semantic Scholar abstract, and Marketing Center Munster all state: 'The hypothetical bias is greater for higher valued products, specialty goods (cf. other product types), and within-subject designs (cf. between-subject designs).' Note: one independent meta-analysis (Johansson-Stenman and Svedsater 2008) found the opposite direction for within/between-subject -- but Schmidt and Bijmolt's finding is clearly attributed correctly. (src: https://research.rug.nl/en/publications/accurately-measuring-willingness-to-pay-for-consumer-goods-a-meta/)
- [confirmed] Indirect WTP elicitation methods overestimate real WTP significantly more strongly than direct methods -- counter to conventional wisdom (Schmidt and Bijmolt, 2020) -- This is Schmidt and Bijmolt's (2020) headline finding, confirmed by multiple independent sources. The paper explicitly states 'in contrast with conventional wisdom, indirect methods actually overestimate RWTP significantly stronger than direct methods' -- by approximately 10 additional percentage points. This counter-intuitive finding is independently reported by the Marketing Center Munster news release, the University of Groningen research portal, and Semantic Scholar. (src: https://www.marketingcenter.de/en/news/1474)
