### 20. Sean Ellis product-market-fit survey (the 40% very-disappointed benchmark)

- **Category:** Usage, retention & PMF
- **Stage:** retention
- **Definition:** The Sean Ellis PMF survey asks active users a single core question -- "How would you feel if you could no longer use [product]?" -- with response options of Very disappointed, Somewhat disappointed, Not disappointed, and N/A. The 40% benchmark holds that if at least 40% of respondents answer "Very disappointed," the product has likely achieved product-market fit; companies consistently below 40% lack sufficient must-have attachment to support scalable growth. Ellis derived the threshold from benchmarking approximately 100 early-stage startups and observing a consistent pattern: above 40%, companies grew with relative ease and strong word-of-mouth; below 40%, growth required constant costly effort and faced recurring churn.
- **Validates:** Answers the question: "Is this product a must-have for a sufficient portion of active users, or merely a nice-to-have?" It does not validate problem existence or solution concept; it validates whether the current implemented product has earned genuine emotional dependency in users who have activated and used it.

**Protocol**
1. Define the eligible respondent pool: active users from the past 30 days who have completed key onboarding actions and have at least 2-3 weeks of usage. Exclude new signups (under 2 weeks), non-activated users, and churned customers.
2. Send the 4-question survey: (a) 'How would you feel if you could no longer use [product]?' [Very disappointed / Somewhat disappointed / Not disappointed / N/A]; (b) 'What type of people do you think would most benefit from [product]?'; (c) 'What is the main benefit you receive from [product]?'; (d) 'How can we improve [product] for you?'
3. Collect a minimum of 40 responses before drawing conclusions -- 30 is the floor for any signal, 100+ yields margin of error under ±10 percentage points at 95% confidence. At n=50 with a 40% estimate the margin of error is approximately ±13%; at n=1,000 it drops to ±3%.
4. Calculate the raw PMF score: (count of 'Very disappointed' responses) / (total valid responses) x 100.
5. Segment the 'Very disappointed' cohort: extract their answers to questions (b) and (c) to build a profile of the High-Expectation Customer (HXC) -- the person for whom the product is already a must-have.
6. Focus iteration on the 'Somewhat disappointed' users where the core stated benefit resonates but something holds them back. Ignore 'Not disappointed' users for roadmap decisions -- their preferences dilute signal.
7. Build the roadmap with a roughly 50/50 split: half of capacity deepening what 'Very disappointed' users already love; half removing the specific friction points that prevent 'Somewhat disappointed' users from crossing over.
8. Re-survey the same eligible population on a quarterly cadence, track the score as a primary OKR metric, and rebuild the roadmap each cycle. Treat the score as a directional signal to improve, not a one-time gate.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- PMF threshold (must-have attachment): >=40% 'Very disappointed' responses (src: Sean Ellis, startup-marketing.com 'The Startup Pyramid' (2009); benchmarked across ~100 startups)
- Gray zone -- growth possible but costly: 25-40% 'Very disappointed' (src: FitSignal analysis of Ellis framework; Ellis's own guidance on the 25-40 band)
- Fundamental PMF absent, churn exceeds acquisition: <25% 'Very disappointed' (src: FitSignal 'The Sean Ellis 40% Test: The Ultimate Guide')
- Minimum responses for directional signal: ~40 responses (src: Lenny Rachitsky, 'How to Know If You've Got Product-Market Fit', Lenny's Newsletter)
- Minimum responses for statistical validity (floor): 30 responses (src: FitSignal PMF benchmark guide)
- Margin of error at n=50 with 40% estimate: approximately ±13% (range 27-53%) (src: MeasuringU, 'What Is the Product-Market Fit (PMF) Item?')
- Margin of error at n=1,000 with 40% estimate: approximately ±3% (range 37-43%) (src: MeasuringU, 'What Is the Product-Market Fit (PMF) Item?')
- Superhuman initial PMF score (Summer 2017): 22% 'Very disappointed' (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit' (2018))
- Superhuman PMF score after segmentation (no product change): 33% (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit' (2018))
- Superhuman final PMF score (achieved within 3 quarters): 58% 'Very disappointed' (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit' (2018))
- Slack PMF score (Hiten Shah open survey, 2015): ~51% 'Very disappointed' (731 users surveyed) (src: Hiten Shah public Slack PMF survey, 2015; cited in multiple PMF survey guides)
- Slack margin of error (n=731, 95% confidence): approximately ±3.5% (src: MeasuringU analysis of the Slack survey)

**Origin and evidence:** Sean Ellis originated the survey question and the 40% threshold in a 2009 blog post ('The Startup Pyramid') on startup-marketing.com, drawing on his experience taking approximately 100 startups through early growth at companies including Dropbox, LogMeIn, and Eventbrite. The evidence base is practitioner-empirical, not peer-reviewed: Ellis observed that companies above 40% consistently showed strong word-of-mouth and scalable growth, while those below consistently struggled, but no peer-reviewed study has independently validated this threshold. Rahul Vohra's 2018 First Round Review article ('How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit') significantly extended the methodology by adding segmentation (the HXC concept), the 50/50 roadmap split, and a repeatable quarterly cadence -- the Superhuman case is the closest to a documented before-after validation, moving from 22% to 58% alongside a scaling business. MeasuringU (Jeff Sauro and colleagues) published the only known systematic empirical critique, finding no peer-reviewed papers validating the benchmark on Google Scholar and flagging the statistical imprecision at small sample sizes. The methodology is widely adopted (Y Combinator, a16z-backed startups, Reforge curriculum) but the 40% figure itself remains an expert heuristic, not a statistically derived threshold.

**Canonical sources:** Sean Ellis, 'The Startup Pyramid' (2009), startup-marketing.com -- original source of the question and the 40% benchmark; Sean Ellis and Morgan Brown, 'Hacking Growth' (2017, Crown Business) -- book-length treatment of the methodology; Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit', First Round Review (2018) -- the definitive extension: segmentation, HXC, 50/50 roadmap; MeasuringU (Jeff Sauro), 'What Is the Product-Market Fit (PMF) Item?' -- the primary statistical and empirical critique; Lenny Rachitsky, 'How to Know If You've Got Product-Market Fit', Lenny's Newsletter -- practitioner synthesis with complementary retention-based benchmarks

**Failure modes**
- Selection bias / sampling only power users: surveying only the most active or happiest users inflates the score. Buffer reportedly saw ~78% 'very disappointed' from a 200-user power-user sample of a new feature, a result that did not reflect broader user behavior or retention.
- Confusing problem/solution fit with product/market fit: a product can score above 40% because users desperately want A solution to the problem, not because the current product actually solves it well. Kromatic's documented case: a founder's startup exceeded 40% while the product 'simply didn't work that well' and the market was too small -- they had problem desire, not product fit.
- No peer-reviewed validation of the 40% threshold: MeasuringU found zero peer-reviewed papers validating the benchmark. The number is a practitioner heuristic grounded in Ellis's ~100-startup sample, not a statistically derived or independently replicated finding.
- Statistical noise at small sample sizes: at n=50 the margin of error is approximately ±13 percentage points, meaning a true score of 40% could read anywhere from 27% to 53%. A team near the threshold cannot distinguish signal from noise without 100+ responses.
- PMF as a continuous phenomenon, not a discrete state: retention data in large-scale analyses shows continuous incremental improvement, not a phase transition at 40%. Treating the threshold as a binary pass/fail ignores that a product at 38% may need very different interventions than one at 12%.
- Category and geography non-universality: the 40% benchmark was derived primarily from US SaaS companies. Documented exception -- Zoom reportedly scored around 30% 'very disappointed' during the pandemic despite obvious category dominance, illustrating that commodity network-effect products may show lower survey scores than their growth trajectories suggest.
- PMF score decay after product changes: a score above 40% is not permanent. Poor releases, competitive moves, or market shifts erode it. Teams that survey once and stop monitoring lose the signal.
- The 'not disappointed' users can contaminate if not excluded: including genuinely non-activated users or churned users who happened to receive the survey deflates scores, creating false negatives. Conversely including only power users inflates them (see selection bias above).

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false-positive mechanism is desire-for-a-solution being mistaken for satisfaction-with-the-product. When users are surveyed early, their 'Very disappointed' response often reflects the intensity of their underlying problem (high problem pain) rather than the quality of the current product. Because the question is hypothetical and future-oriented ('how would you feel if...'), customers project their desire for a category solution onto the specific product, especially if it is the only option they have tried. This inflates scores even when the product fails at retention, has poor activation, or does not work reliably. The asymmetry of the threshold compounds this: below 40% is a reliable negative signal (lacking PMF is fairly clear), but above 40% is a weak positive (it is a necessary but not sufficient condition). Teams that hit 41% and shift to scaling before confirming retention curves and churn data are acting on a survey artifact, not genuine product-market fit.

**Examples**
- Superhuman: In Summer 2017 Superhuman's PMF score was 22%. Rahul Vohra applied a 4-step engine: surveyed users with the 4-question Ellis battery, segmented to identify the HXC profile ('Nicole' -- a busy professional processing 100-200 emails daily), split the roadmap 50/50 between deepening speed/shortcuts (what fans loved) and adding mobile/integrations (what held fence-sitters back), and re-surveyed quarterly. The score moved to 33% after segmentation alone (no product change), then to 47%, 56%, and finally 58% -- all within roughly 3 quarters.
- Slack (Hiten Shah survey, 2015): Hiten Shah ran an independent open PMF survey of 731 Slack users and reported approximately 51% answered 'Very disappointed'. At the time Slack had approximately 500,000 paying users. The result was widely cited as validation that Slack had achieved strong PMF before its hyper-growth phase; the n=731 sample also provided a statistically meaningful margin of error of approximately ±3.5% at 95% confidence, making it one of the cleaner real-world data points in the public record.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A score of 40% or above -- measured on a representative sample of active, adequately activated users (not power-user-only cohorts) with n >= 100 -- is a strong signal that a core must-have product exists for an identifiable customer segment. It should be accompanied by: retention curves that flatten at a non-zero asymptote, low voluntary churn in the 'Very disappointed' cohort, and word-of-mouth as a meaningful acquisition channel. Under these conditions the score supports shifting investment from product iteration toward growth and distribution. The 'Somewhat disappointed' segment simultaneously reveals the highest-leverage roadmap bets for expanding the must-have audience.
- Weak signal: A score below 40% -- especially below 25% -- is a reliable signal to stop investing in growth and continue iterating on the product. The score alone does not tell you what to fix; qualitative follow-up ('What is the main benefit?' and 'How can we improve?') from the 'Somewhat disappointed' cohort is the diagnostic tool. A score in the 25-40% range indicates partial fit: a real must-have exists for a subset, but it is too small or too narrow to support scalable growth without either broadening the product or tightening the target segment. Scores above 40% on samples below 40 respondents, or from obviously biased populations (power users, survey incentive recipients), should be treated as directional only and re-run on a larger, less selected sample before any growth investment decision is made.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] 40% 'Very disappointed' is the PMF threshold, derived from benchmarking approximately 100 startups (Sean Ellis, 2009) -- Multiple independent sources corroborate this. Ellis's Seedcamp London presentation (September 22, 2009) and Berkeley-Columbia MBA presentation (October 16, 2009) both pre-date or confirm 2009 as the origin. The ~100 startup benchmark figure is repeated across Ellis's own substack and widely cited practitioner sources. The claim is accurate as stated, though MeasuringU notes no peer-reviewed academic paper validates the threshold itself. (src: https://measuringu.com/product-market-fit-item/ ; https://www.slideshare.net/slideshow/startup-customer-development-seedcamp-london/2056395)
- [confirmed] 25-40% band: growth possible but requires constant effort and high marketing spend, churn is recurring (Ellis framework / FitSignal) -- The FitSignal article directly states: 'Companies could grow but it required constant effort. Marketing spend was high. Churn was a recurring problem.' This language closely tracks Ellis's own framework descriptions. The characterization is internally consistent with Ellis's stated observations, though it remains a practitioner heuristic without controlled empirical validation. (src: https://www.fitsignal.com/blog/sean-ellis-40-percent-test)
- [confirmed] Below 25%: fundamental PMF absent, churn exceeds acquisition (FitSignal) -- The FitSignal article explicitly states that below 25%, 'Churn exceeded acquisition. Product-market fit was clearly absent.' This matches the attributed source. The claim is accurate as a description of what FitSignal says; it is a practitioner assertion rather than a peer-reviewed finding. (src: https://www.fitsignal.com/blog/sean-ellis-40-percent-test)
- [contested] Minimum ~40 responses for directional signal (Lenny Rachitsky) -- The claim appears widely in PMF guides but the direct attribution to Lenny Rachitsky is weak. Fetching Lenny's own newsletter article on PMF found no mention of a specific minimum sample size. The 40-response figure is more directly traceable to Hiten Shah (who ran the Slack survey) and is echoed in tools like Buffer case studies, not clearly sourced to Rachitsky. The number itself is a reasonable rule of thumb but the specific attribution is unverified. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-to-know-if-youve-got-productmarket (no specific figure found); https://hitenism.com/slack-product-market-fit-survey/)
- [confirmed] Minimum 30 responses as absolute floor for any statistical validity (FitSignal) -- The FitSignal article states explicitly: 'Aim for 30+ responses minimum. Fewer than that and statistical noise will dominate your results.' This matches the claimed source. However, MeasuringU's analysis shows that even 30 responses yields a very wide confidence interval (wider than ±13% for a 40% estimate at n=50), making the '30 as a validity floor' a practitioner convention rather than a statistical guarantee. (src: https://www.fitsignal.com/blog/sean-ellis-40-percent-test ; https://measuringu.com/product-market-fit-item/)
- [confirmed] At n=50 with a 40% true score, margin of error is approximately ±13 percentage points (range 27-53%) at 95% confidence (MeasuringU) -- The MeasuringU article states verbatim: 'the margin of error for 95% confidence is ±13%, making the plausible range from about 27 to 53%.' This is mathematically correct for a binomial proportion confidence interval at p=0.40, n=50. The figures check out independently. (src: https://measuringu.com/product-market-fit-item/)
- [confirmed] At n=1,000 with a 40% true score, margin of error drops to approximately ±3 percentage points (range 37-43%) at 95% confidence (MeasuringU) -- The MeasuringU article states explicitly: 'If the sample size is 1,000, then the margin of error is ±3% (plausible range from 37 to 43%).' This is mathematically consistent with the standard formula for a binomial proportion at p=0.40, n=1000 (±1.96 * sqrt(0.4*0.6/1000) ≈ ±0.030). (src: https://measuringu.com/product-market-fit-item/)
- [confirmed] Superhuman initial PMF score: 22% (Summer 2017, First Round Review) -- The First Round Review article authored by Rahul Vohra is available and directly states the initial PMF score was 22% in summer 2017. Multiple independent sources (SaaS Club podcast, digidai.github.io, zonkafeedback) corroborate the specific figures and date. The First Round Review URL is confirmed. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [confirmed] Superhuman score after segmentation with no product change: 33% (First Round Review) -- The First Round Review article explicitly states the score rose from 22% to 33% after segmentation and identifying the high-expectation customer profile, before any product changes. This is corroborated by the SaaS Club podcast transcript and multiple secondary sources. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [confirmed] Superhuman final score within approximately 3 quarters: 58% (First Round Review) -- The First Round Review article states the score reached 58% 'within just three quarters of work to improve the product.' The article also provides the quarterly progression: 22% → 33% (re-segmentation) → 47% (Q1) → 56% (Q2) → 58% (Q3), so '3 quarters' is accurate for the product-improvement phase after segmentation. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [confirmed] Slack PMF score (Hiten Shah, 731 users, 2015): approximately 51% 'Very disappointed' -- Hiten Shah's original blog post at hitenism.com confirms 731 responses were collected in April 2015, with 51% (approximately 373 respondents) answering 'very disappointed.' The date, user count, and percentage all match. Note: MeasuringU cites '750 Slack users' rather than 731, a minor discrepancy likely due to rounding or different counting methodology, but the ~51% figure is consistent across all sources. (src: https://hitenism.com/slack-product-market-fit-survey/ ; https://measuringu.com/product-market-fit-item/)
- [confirmed] Slack survey margin of error at n=731: approximately ±3.5% at 95% confidence (MeasuringU) -- MeasuringU explicitly cites 'approximately 3.5% margin of error with 95% confidence' for the Slack survey. This is mathematically consistent: ±1.96 * sqrt(0.51*0.49/731) ≈ ±0.036, so ±3.6% rounding to ±3.5% is reasonable. The figure checks out independently. (src: https://measuringu.com/product-market-fit-item/)
- [contested] Buffer power-user subsample: approximately 78% 'Very disappointed' in a 200-user skewed sample (cited as false-positive case) -- The 78% figure and the 200-user framing are repeated across multiple PMF guides (learningloop.io, surveysparrow.com), and the conceptual false-positive warning is well-documented. However, no primary source (original Buffer blog post or official Buffer case study) was found to verify the exact 78% figure and 200-user sample size. All citations trace back to secondary practitioner guides, making the specific numbers unverifiable from primary sources. The general lesson is sound; the precise figures are unconfirmed. (src: https://learningloop.io/plays/product-market-fit-survey (secondary source only; no primary Buffer source found))
- [contested] Zoom reportedly scored approximately 30% during pandemic despite category dominance (Lenny Rachitsky) -- The ~30% PMF figure for Zoom is confirmed by an independent source (nira.com conducted a survey of 1,000 people in April 2020 and found exactly 30% 'very disappointed'), but the specific attribution to Lenny Rachitsky is not corroborated. Fetching Lenny's PMF newsletter article found no mention of Zoom or a 30% score. The factual claim about Zoom's score appears accurate; the attribution to Rachitsky as the source appears to be incorrect or unverifiable. (src: https://nira.com/zoom-product-market-fit/ (confirms ~30% score); Rachitsky attribution not corroborated at https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-to-know-if-youve-got-productmarket)
- [confirmed] No peer-reviewed papers validating the 40% benchmark found on Google Scholar (MeasuringU empirical audit) -- The MeasuringU article explicitly states: 'Using Google Scholar, we could not find any peer-reviewed papers describing research with the PMF item or the Sean Ellis test.' This is a direct factual claim about their literature search, and no contradicting peer-reviewed paper has been surfaced by any independent search. The claim accurately describes MeasuringU's finding. (src: https://measuringu.com/product-market-fit-item/)

---
### 21. Retention cohort curves (flattening / smile curve)

- **Category:** Usage, retention & PMF
- **Stage:** retention
- **Definition:** A retention cohort curve plots the percentage of users from a given acquisition cohort who remain active over successive time periods (days, weeks, or months). "Flattening" is the inflection at which the curve stops declining and holds a stable horizontal level, indicating that a durable core of users has found lasting value. The "smile curve" (distinct term) refers to a histogram of monthly days-active distribution that forms a U- or smile-shape, signaling a bimodal population of low-engagement and high-engagement users; used alongside cohort flattening, it identifies who the power-user core is.
- **Validates:** Does a sufficient fraction of users derive recurring, habitual value from the product -- i.e., is there real product-market fit with a definable audience, not just initial curiosity?

**Protocol**
1. Define the active event: choose the single action that represents genuine value delivery (not login, but the core value action -- e.g., a document saved, a message sent, a search completed). Specify cadence that matches natural product frequency: daily for social/messaging, weekly for SaaS tools, monthly or per-event for low-frequency products.
2. Instrument and extract cohort data: group all users by their first-use week or month. For each cohort, record the fraction still active at each subsequent period (D1, D7, D30, M3, M6, M12). Minimum cohort size: ~500 users per cohort for statistical reliability; roll up adjacent weeks for early-stage products below that threshold.
3. Plot the curve for each cohort and visually inspect the shape: look for the inflection point where the curve stops declining and holds. A curve still dropping steeply at month 3 with no sign of stabilization signals no PMF. Three consecutive near-flat data points are the minimum signal; a single flat-looking point is noise.
4. Segment the retained population: split the flattened tail by acquisition source, user role, company size, geography, use-case, and any other available dimension. The goal is to identify who flattened vs. who continued to churn -- this tells you who your actual market is, not just whether PMF exists.
5. Benchmark the plateau level against your category: compare the level at which the curve flattens to published benchmarks for your product type (see benchmarks section). A curve that flattens at 2% for a B2B SaaS is not PMF; one that flattens at 40% for consumer social is strong PMF.
6. Plot improvement across successive cohorts: if the January cohort flatlines at month 4 and the March cohort flatlines at month 2, with a higher plateau level, that is a positive trend confirming that product changes are improving PMF for the same target segment.
7. Pair with the power-user curve (smile check): build a histogram of days-active-per-month for your most recent cohort at the M3 or M6 mark. A smile shape (bimodal: many low-use + many high-use users) confirms the cohort flattening is driven by a genuine power-user core, not an artifact.
8. Confirm against qualitative signal: survey the retained users vs. churned users on why they stayed or left. The retained segment should articulate a specific repeated job-to-be-done. This rules out the false positive of a small-n cohort with survivorship bias explaining the flat tail.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Consumer social - 6-month user retention (GOOD / GREAT): ~25% GOOD, ~45% GREAT (src: Lenny Rachitsky + Casey Accidental, 'What is good retention' (survey of 20 growth practitioners from Slack, Pinterest, Uber, etc.) -- lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-retention-issue-29)
- Consumer transactional - 6-month user retention (GOOD / GREAT): ~30% GOOD, ~50% GREAT (src: Lenny Rachitsky + Casey Accidental, 'What is good retention')
- Consumer SaaS - 6-month user retention (GOOD / GREAT): ~40% GOOD, ~70% GREAT (src: Lenny Rachitsky + Casey Accidental, 'What is good retention')
- SMB / Mid-Market SaaS - 6-month user retention (GOOD / GREAT): ~60% GOOD, ~80% GREAT (src: Lenny Rachitsky + Casey Accidental, 'What is good retention')
- Enterprise SaaS - 6-month user retention (GOOD / GREAT): ~75% GOOD, ~90% GREAT (src: Lenny Rachitsky + Casey Accidental, 'What is good retention')
- D1 / D7 / D30 retention (consumer, Andrew Chen a16z baseline): D1 >= 60%, D7 >= 30%, D30 >= 15% (src: Andrew Chen (a16z), cited across Indie Hackers summary of his magic metrics post (x.com/andrewchen/status/1184170125525577728) and Browsee.io breakdown)
- 50% D30 retention (exceptional consumer product signal): 50% D30 -- Chen sees this 'once every couple of years' (src: Andrew Chen, 'Lessons learned from staring at thousands of retention curves' (andrewchen.substack.com))
- Actives / registered users > 25% (validates TAM alongside flattening): >25% (src: Andrew Chen, 10 magic metrics for PMF (x.com/andrewchen/status/1184170125525577728))
- Consumer subscription annual retention signal: >65% annual paid-user retention (src: Andrew Chen, 10 magic metrics for PMF)
- B2B SaaS floor for curve plateau (rule of thumb): 70% or higher monthly retention (src: Brian Balfour / Reforge, cited in 'How to Evaluate Product/Market Fit by Analyzing Retention Cohorts' (leanb2bbook.com))
- Consumer product floor for curve plateau (rule of thumb): 25% or higher monthly retention (src: Brian Balfour / Reforge, cited in leanb2bbook.com)
- Eight-week retention -- media/finance elite threshold: >25% at 8 weeks is elite for media/finance (src: Industry benchmark cited in Andrew Chen ecosystem summaries (Browsee.io))
- Eight-week retention -- SaaS/e-commerce elite threshold: >35% at 8 weeks is elite for SaaS/e-commerce (src: Industry benchmark cited in Andrew Chen ecosystem summaries (Browsee.io))
- Bottom-Up SaaS Net Revenue Retention (GOOD / GREAT): ~100% GOOD, ~120% GREAT (src: Lenny Rachitsky, tweet/thread (x.com/lennysan/status/1278352679786303488))
- Enterprise SaaS Net Revenue Retention (GOOD / GREAT): ~110% GOOD, ~130% GREAT (src: Lenny Rachitsky, tweet/thread (x.com/lennysan/status/1278352679786303488))
- Curve must flatten by month 6 or no PMF in that cohort: Month 6 deadline for stabilization (src: Widely cited in PMF literature synthesized from Balfour / Reforge framework (pmtoolkit.ai, buildmvpfast.com))
- Minimum cohort size for statistical reliability: ~500 users per cohort (src: Cohort retention analysis best-practice literature (count.co, mcpanalytics.ai))

**Origin and evidence:** The cohort retention curve as a PMF signal was systematized by Brian Balfour (Founder/CEO of Reforge, former VP Growth at HubSpot) in his widely cited essay 'The Never Ending Road To Product Market Fit' (brianbalfour.com, c. 2017). Andrew Chen (a16z General Partner) independently popularized the D1/D7/D30 benchmarks and the "power user curve smile" framing in a 2019 Twitter thread and subsequent a16z essay. Lenny Rachitsky conducted the most rigorous quantitative benchmark study (surveying 20 growth practitioners from Slack, Pinterest, Uber, etc.) published in his newsletter circa 2021. The evidence base is practitioner-empirical: large-scale pattern recognition across hundreds of consumer and SaaS products, not controlled experiments. No RCT or peer-reviewed study establishes causal thresholds. The benchmarks are observational and category-dependent.

**Canonical sources:** Brian Balfour, 'The Never Ending Road To Product Market Fit' (brianbalfour.com/essays/product-market-fit); Andrew Chen, 'Lessons learned from staring at thousands of retention curves' (andrewchen.substack.com); Andrew Chen, '10 magic metrics indicating a consumer tech startup probably has PMF' (Twitter/X, Oct 2019, x.com/andrewchen/status/1184170125525577728); Andrew Chen / a16z, 'The Power User Curve: The Best Way to Understand Your Most Engaged Users' (a16z.com); Lenny Rachitsky + Casey Accidental, 'What Is Good Retention' (lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-retention-issue-29); Reforge / Brian Balfour, 'Understand Your Most-Engaged Users with the Power User Curve' (reforge.com/blog)

**Failure modes**
- Conflating the power-user smile curve (a histogram of monthly engagement frequency) with the cohort retention flattening curve (percentage active over time): these are complementary tools, not the same metric. Treating either alone as sufficient is a common error.
- Seasonal products and low-frequency products generate artificial flattening: a tax product or an annual-contract SaaS may show a plateau driven by contract lock-in or seasonal re-engagement, not genuine habit. The curve must be interpreted against the natural cadence of the job-to-be-done.
- Small cohort sizes inflate apparent stability: fewer than ~500 users per cohort produces wide confidence intervals at each checkpoint. A flat-looking tail from 80 users may be pure noise.
- Reading a single near-flat data point as the floor: three consecutive near-flat checkpoints is the minimum credible signal; one flat point is statistical noise.
- Survivorship bias in the retained tail: if the churned users left because of a bad onboarding, a pricing barrier, or a platform bug -- not because the product lacks value -- the retained tail looks like PMF but actually reflects a selection artifact.
- Cohort contamination from resurrection: users who churned and reactivated inflate later-period retention counts and can produce a false flattening signal.
- Using login or session as the active event instead of the core value action: a product where users log in to check a notification but never perform the value action shows a flat curve that masks zero value delivery.
- Early cohorts outperform later cohorts systematically: first adopters are enthusiasts who self-select. If the curve flattens for the first two cohorts but not for subsequent, scaled cohorts, PMF exists only for a niche, not the broad market.

**False-positive mechanism:** The most dangerous false positive arises when a flat retention tail is produced by structural lock-in rather than genuine value: annual contracts that prevent cancellation, sunk-cost inertia (especially in B2B where the buyer and user are different), or a small survivor cohort of power users who were going to be retained regardless of the broader product experience. In consumer products, seasonal patterns (holiday cohorts, back-to-school cohorts) routinely produce a flat stretch that disappears in the next seasonal trough. In B2B SaaS, user-level retention and dollar-level (net revenue) retention diverge sharply: a product can show flat user retention while dollar retention decays (seat consolidation) or vice versa (expansion revenue masking user churn). Teams that measure only one dimension routinely misread their PMF status. Additionally, products with high viral coefficients can sustain acquisition fast enough that a perpetually decaying cohort curve looks flat in aggregate -- the incoming new users mask the ongoing decay of older cohorts in blended dashboards.

**Examples**
- Snapchat (c. 2012): Balfour cites Snapchat as the canonical 'trifecta' case: approximately 200K downloads, 100K daily active users, and those 100K DAUs sending an average of 10 pictures per user per day. The retention curve flattened because users adopted a daily photo-sharing habit; the power-user smile was visible in the daily-active concentration. This satisfied all three of Balfour's PMF checkpoints simultaneously.
- Facebook (consumer social benchmark): Andrew Chen and Lenny Rachitsky both cite Facebook as the reference point for right-leaning power-user smile curves, with 60%+ of MAUs returning daily (60-70% 6-month user retention), setting the upper bound for consumer social benchmarks. Facebook's retention curve famously flattened at high absolute levels, which a16z and Reforge use as the category ceiling against which other social products are measured.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: The retention curve flattens before month 6 and holds a plateau at or above the category benchmark (e.g., 40%+ at 6 months for consumer SaaS, 60%+ for SMB SaaS) across at least two or three successive cohorts, with the plateau level improving cohort-over-cohort. Segmentation reveals a coherent, definable user segment in the retained tail, not a random residual. The power-user smile curve shows a bimodal distribution with a meaningful right-side concentration. Qualitative interviews with retained users yield consistent, specific, unprompted articulation of the repeated job-to-be-done. This combination -- quantitative plateau at benchmark level, cross-cohort improvement trend, identifiable segment, and qualitative convergence -- is the strongest available non-survey PMF signal.
- Weak signal: The curve declines continuously past month 3 with no visible inflection across every cohort regardless of acquisition timing, or it flattens far below the category benchmark (e.g., at 3-5% for a B2B tool, or 5-8% for a consumer social app). The retained tail is too small to characterize a segment. Successive cohorts show no improvement in plateau level despite product changes. Segmentation of the retained tail produces no coherent pattern -- the survivors look like noise rather than a defined audience. Qualitatively, retained users give vague or inconsistent reasons for staying. A weak result means the product is not yet solving a problem with enough force or frequency to create habitual use in any definable population; acquisition spending in this state fills a leaking bucket.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Consumer social 6-month retention: ~25% GOOD, ~45% GREAT (Rachitsky + Accidental) -- Direct fetch of lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-retention-issue-29 returns exactly '~25% is GOOD, ~45% is GREAT' for Consumer Social. Multiple secondary sources (twtext.com thread unroll, productstrategy.co) corroborate. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-retention-issue-29)
- [confirmed] Consumer transactional 6-month retention: ~30% GOOD, ~50% GREAT (Rachitsky + Accidental) -- Direct fetch of the Lenny newsletter returns exactly '~30% is GOOD, ~50% is GREAT' for Consumer Transactional. Corroborated by multiple secondary sources. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-retention-issue-29)
- [confirmed] Consumer SaaS 6-month retention: ~40% GOOD, ~70% GREAT (Rachitsky + Accidental) -- Direct fetch of the Lenny newsletter returns exactly '~40% is GOOD, ~70% is GREAT' for Consumer SaaS. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-retention-issue-29)
- [confirmed] SMB/Mid-Market SaaS 6-month retention: ~60% GOOD, ~80% GREAT (Rachitsky + Accidental) -- Direct fetch of the Lenny newsletter returns exactly '~60% is GOOD, ~80% is GREAT' for SMB/Mid-Market SaaS. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-retention-issue-29)
- [contested] Enterprise SaaS 6-month retention: ~75% GOOD, ~90% GREAT (Rachitsky + Accidental) -- The primary source (direct fetch of Lenny's newsletter) states '~70% is GOOD, ~90% is GREAT' for Enterprise SaaS — not ~75%. The ~75% figure appears only in a secondary paraphrase (e.g. davidjaxon.wordpress.com). The GREAT threshold of ~90% is correct but the GOOD threshold is overstated by ~5 points. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-good-retention-issue-29)
- [confirmed] Andrew Chen D1/D7/D30 baseline: 60% / 30% / 15% -- Verified in Andrew Chen's tweet thread (x.com/andrewchen/status/1184170125525577728) listing '60/30/15' as a D1/D7/D30 magic metric, and corroborated by Goodreads notes from The Cold Start Problem which quote the same figures as Chen's a16z evaluation baseline. The claim accurately reflects the tweet. (src: https://x.com/andrewchen/status/1184170125525577728)
- [confirmed] Andrew Chen: >65% annual retention for consumer subscription products -- The same tweet thread lists 'For subscription, > 65% annual retention' as a magic metric. Corroborated by multiple secondary sources (Browsee.io, Indie Hackers post, Mailbrew archive). (src: https://x.com/andrewchen/status/1184170125525577728)
- [contested] Brian Balfour / Reforge rule of thumb: consumer product plateau >= 25% monthly, B2B SaaS plateau >= 70% monthly -- The thresholds circulate widely attributed to Balfour/Reforge, but the direct fetch of the most-cited Balfour source (leanb2bbook.com/blog/retention-cohorts-product-market-fit) contains no numerical benchmarks — only Balfour's qualitative quote about flattening curves. The 25%/70% figures appear in secondary synthesis posts (Glasp, various substack articles) without traceable primary Reforge publication. Cannot confirm they originate from Balfour rather than being community interpolation. (src: https://leanb2bbook.com/blog/retention-cohorts-product-market-fit/)
- [confirmed] Eight-week retention >25% is elite for media/finance products; >35% is elite for SaaS/e-commerce -- Direct fetch of Mixpanel's benchmark article confirms: 'over 25 percent is considered elite' for media/finance and 'over 35 percent retention is considered elite' for SaaS/e-commerce. Source is Mixpanel's 2019 Product Benchmarks Report (1.3B users). The claim incorrectly attributed these to 'Andrew Chen ecosystem summaries (Browsee.io)' — the primary source is Mixpanel, not Chen — but the numbers themselves are accurate. (src: https://mixpanel.com/blog/whats-a-good-retention-rate/)
- [confirmed] Bottom-Up SaaS Net Revenue Retention: ~100% GOOD, ~120% GREAT (Rachitsky) -- The tweet at x.com/lennysan/status/1278352679786303488 lists exactly 'Bottom-Up SaaS: 100% GOOD, 120% GREAT'. Corroborated by multiple independent SaaS benchmark sources (Bessemer, SaaStr, Kissmetrics). (src: https://x.com/lennysan/status/1278352679786303488)
- [confirmed] Enterprise SaaS Net Revenue Retention: ~110% GOOD, ~130% GREAT (Rachitsky) -- The tweet at x.com/lennysan/status/1278352679786303488 lists exactly 'Enterprise SaaS: 110% GOOD, 130% GREAT'. Corroborated by industry benchmarks from Bessemer, SaaStr, and m3ter.com. (src: https://x.com/lennysan/status/1278352679786303488)
- [contested] Retention curve that has not flattened by month 6 = no PMF in that cohort (widely cited synthesis of Balfour framework) -- The month-6 threshold appears on pmtoolkit.ai as 'If your curve does not flatten by month 6, you do not have PMF in that cohort' but the page itself provides no citation for that specific deadline — it is the author's interpretive rule. Balfour's own sourced quote says only 'if it flattens off at some point' without specifying month 6. The claim is widely repeated but the specific month-6 cutoff is not traceable to a primary Balfour/Reforge publication. (src: https://pmtoolkit.ai/learn/strategy/product-market-fit-guide)
- [contested] Minimum ~500 users per cohort for statistical reliability at each retention checkpoint -- The most commonly cited minimum in primary best-practice literature (Amplitude, Mixpanel, Personizely) is 100 users per cohort, not 500. The figure 500 appears as an illustrative worked example in tutorials, not as a stated statistical threshold. No authoritative source (count.co, mcpanalytics.ai, or otherwise) was found establishing 500 as the canonical minimum. (src: https://amplitude.com/explore/analytics/cohort-retention-analysis)
- [confirmed] Snapchat example: ~200K downloads, 100K DAUs, ~10 pictures/user/day at the time of the cited PMF signal (Balfour) -- Brian Balfour's essay 'The Never Ending Road To Product Market Fit' (brianbalfour.com/essays/product-market-fit) and PostHog's summary of it both confirm: ~200K downloads, 100K DAUs (50% DAU/install ratio), 1M pictures/day (10 per DAU). The figures in the claim match the Balfour trifecta case study exactly. (src: https://brianbalfour.com/essays/product-market-fit)
- [confirmed] Facebook: 60%+ of MAUs return daily (Andrew Chen, a16z power user curve essay) -- Direct fetch of andrewchen.com/power-user-curve confirms the exact quote: 'Facebook would have a very right-leaning smile, with 60%+ of its MAUs coming back daily.' This is a DAU/MAU ratio framing, not a classic retention curve, but the claim accurately represents what the essay says. (src: https://andrewchen.com/power-user-curve/)

### 22. Activation Rate and Time-to-Value

- **Category:** Usage, retention & PMF
- **Stage:** retention
- **Definition:** Activation rate is the percentage of new users who reach a predefined activation milestone -- the earliest in-product action that is empirically predictive of long-term retention. Time-to-value (TTV) is the elapsed time between a user's first session and the moment they experience that core value, operationalized as the aha moment or first meaningful outcome. Together, the two metrics answer whether a product delivers its core value proposition quickly enough and reliably enough to the users it acquires -- a necessary (though not sufficient) precondition for product-market fit.
- **Validates:** Does the product deliver its core value to new users fast enough, and to enough of them, that a retention-sustaining habit can form? The activation rate measures breadth (how many); TTV measures speed (how fast). Together they diagnose whether an onboarding funnel converts acquisition into durable engagement.

**Protocol**
1. Define the aha moment empirically, not aspirationally: pull two cohorts from product analytics -- users retained at 90 days and users who churned before day 7 -- then compare every action taken in the first 7 days. The action (or combination) that most sharply separates the two cohorts is your candidate activation event. Validate that it reflects genuine value, not a setup step (e.g., 'first automated workflow triggered' beats 'email confirmed').
2. Define the Setup Moment and Habit Moment around the aha moment (Reforge framework): the Setup Moment is the minimum prerequisite to reach the aha moment; the Habit Moment is the repeated action frequency that predicts long-run engagement (e.g., Pinterest: setup = 5 topics chosen on day 1; aha = first pin within 7 days; habit = pinned on 4 of first 28 days). Both need a qualitative definition and a quantitative threshold.
3. Instrument TTV: log a timestamp at signup and at the activation event. Compute the distribution of TTV across all new users. Segment by acquisition channel, plan tier, and user role to surface heterogeneity.
4. Compute activation rate by cohort week: activated users / users who completed signup. Track as a weekly time series so regressions are visible immediately rather than buried in rolling averages.
5. Run cohort retention curves split by 'activated vs. not activated within N days'. A retention gap of 10-15 percentage points at 30 days between activated and non-activated users is a reasonable validation signal that the activation event is correctly defined (Userpilot 2024 guidance).
6. A/B test onboarding interventions aimed at reducing TTV: empty-state removal (pre-populate with sample data), progressive disclosure (defer non-essential setup), role-based flows, and checklist-driven guidance. Correcto example: activation rate moved from 17.4% to 53.5% after onboarding rework; 30-day retention reached 64.3% (Amplitude, 2025 Product Benchmark Report).
7. Cross-validate the activation definition against the Sean Ellis PMF survey (ask retained and churned segments 'How disappointed would you feel if you could no longer use this product?'). A segment with greater than or equal to 40% 'very disappointed' should correlate strongly with users who hit your activation event within the target TTV window. If they do not, your activation event definition is misaligned with the actual source of value.
8. Set a weekly review cadence on activation rate and TTV p50/p90. For B2B: target 90%+ activation within 30 days for SMB, 30-90 days for mid-market, and value evidence within 90 days for enterprise (Jason Lemkin / SaaStr). For PLG SaaS: target aha moment within 24-48 hours for the median user.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Median activation rate across all product types: 25% (src: Lenny Rachitsky, 'What Is a Good Activation Rate?' (lennysnewsletter.com, 2022), survey of 500+ products)
- Average activation rate across all product types: 34% (src: Lenny Rachitsky, 'What Is a Good Activation Rate?' (lennysnewsletter.com, 2022), survey of 500+ products)
- Median activation rate, SaaS only: 30% (src: Lenny Rachitsky, 'What Is a Good Activation Rate?' (lennysnewsletter.com, 2022))
- Average activation rate, SaaS only: 36% (src: Lenny Rachitsky, 'What Is a Good Activation Rate?' (lennysnewsletter.com, 2022))
- Median first-week activation rate, SaaS (2019 Mixpanel): 17% (src: Mixpanel Product Benchmarks Report, 2019)
- 90th-percentile first-week activation rate, SaaS (2019 Mixpanel): 65% (src: Mixpanel Product Benchmarks Report, 2019)
- AI & ML product category activation rate: 54.8% (src: Userpilot Benchmark Report 2024/2025, sample of 547 SaaS companies)
- FinTech & Insurance product category activation rate: 5% (src: Userpilot Benchmark Report 2024/2025, sample of 547 SaaS companies)
- B2B SaaS average activation rate: 37.5% (src: Multiple practitioner aggregations citing Mixpanel and Userpilot data, 2023-2024)
- Median TTV across SaaS (Userpilot 2024): 1 day 12 hours 23 minutes (src: Userpilot Time-to-Value Benchmark Report 2024/2025, 547 SaaS companies)
- Sean Ellis PMF threshold ('very disappointed'): 40% or more of surveyed users (src: Sean Ellis, original 2010 blog post; validated across 100+ startups)
- YC benchmark: free consumer app DAU/monthly retention for PMF: Greater than 40% (src: Y Combinator / Gustaf Alstromer, YC Startup School)
- YC benchmark: consumer subscription paid monthly retention for PMF: Greater than 50% (src: Y Combinator, analysis of 3,000+ portfolio companies)
- Activation target for SMB B2B SaaS: 90% within 30 days (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr (Klaviyo cited as achieving this at $600M ARR))
- Activation target for mid-market B2B SaaS: 90% within 30-90 days (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr)
- Activation target for enterprise B2B SaaS: Value evidence within 90 days; full activation within 3-6 months (src: Jason Lemkin, SaaStr)
- Proportion of products with strong early activation that also show strong 3-month retention: 69% (src: Amplitude, 2025 Product Benchmark Report, 2,600+ companies)
- Three-month retention gap: top performers vs. median: 18.5% vs. 3.8% (src: Amplitude, 2025 Product Benchmark Report)
- Users who churn within 2 weeks if they have not experienced value: More than 98% (src: Amplitude, 2025 Product Benchmark Report)
- Correcto case: activation rate improvement after onboarding rework: From 17.4% to 53.5%; 30-day retention reached 64.3% (src: Amplitude, 2025 Product Benchmark Report)
- Superhuman initial PMF survey score ('very disappointed'): 22% (src: Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product Market Fit', First Round Review, 2018)
- Superhuman PMF score after segmentation and product iteration: 58% (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review, 2018)
- Slack PMF survey score ('very disappointed'): 51% of 731 surveyed users (src: Hiten Shah open survey, 2015, cited in First Round Review ecosystem)
- Week-one retention rate, cross-industry average (2023 Mixpanel): 28% (down from 50% in 2022) (src: Mixpanel 2024 Benchmarks Report, 7,700+ companies, 11.7 trillion events)
- Top-10% growers vs. average: time spent on product: Nearly 3x more (src: Mixpanel 2024 Benchmarks Report)

**Origin and evidence:** The activation rate concept was operationalized in growth practice through Sean Ellis's 2010 PMF survey methodology (net: measure the fraction of users who would be 'very disappointed' without the product, threshold 40%). The aha moment framing was popularized by growth practitioners at Facebook (Chamath Palihapitiya: '7 friends in 10 days' as the activation signal) and Twitter ('follow 30 people' within the first session). Reforge (Brian Balfour, Casey Winters, Bangaly Kaba) formalized the Setup-Aha-Habit three-moment structure in their Retention and Engagement program (approx. 2017-2018). Rahul Vohra's 2018 First Round Review essay on Superhuman made PMF-survey-driven activation iteration widely known. Lenny Rachitsky's 2022 newsletter survey (500+ products) is the most comprehensive public benchmark dataset on activation rates. Evidence quality is practitioner-strong but academic-weak: the benchmarks come from product analytics platforms (Mixpanel, Amplitude, Userpilot) using real product data, but none are peer-reviewed or RCT-controlled. The correlational claim (activation predicts retention) is robust across multiple large-sample datasets but does not establish causation at the individual product level.

**Canonical sources:** Sean Ellis, original PMF survey blog post (2010) and Hacking Growth (2017, with Morgan Brown); Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product Market Fit', First Round Review (2018); Lenny Rachitsky, 'What Is a Good Activation Rate?', lennysnewsletter.com (2022), 500+ product survey; Reforge Retention and Engagement program, Brian Balfour and team (2017-present): Setup-Aha-Habit framework; Mixpanel Product Benchmarks Reports (2019, 2024); Amplitude 2025 Product Benchmark Report, 2,600+ companies; Userpilot Time-to-Value Benchmark Report 2024/2025, 547 SaaS companies; Jason Lemkin, SaaStr: '90% activation in shortest practical time' heuristic; Y Combinator / Gustaf Alstromer, Startup School retention and PMF lectures; Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011): validated learning and cohort analysis as the primary instrumentation method for activation measurement

**Failure modes**
- Choosing a vanity activation event: picking an event that is easy to hit (e.g., 'viewed the dashboard') rather than one that actually predicts retention leads to high measured activation rates that coexist with terrible month-2 churn. SocialCam (YC example): 16 million downloads, activation of the initial event, near-zero 10-day retention.
- Confusing setup steps with value delivery: requiring users to complete account configuration before experiencing any outcome inflates measured TTV and deflates activation rate -- but the root problem is that the 'activation event' is really a setup friction point, not a value moment.
- Optimizing TTV in isolation: compressing time-to-first-action by removing necessary setup (e.g., skipping team member invites in a collaboration tool) can lift the activation rate metric while lowering downstream retention by delivering a degraded first experience.
- Single-metric tunnel vision: a product can show a 60%+ activation rate in the first week while still lacking PMF if the activated users do not form a habit. Activation is necessary but not sufficient; it must be paired with the habit moment and a flat (non-zero) long-run retention curve.
- Heterogeneous cohort averaging: averaging activation rates across acquisition channels, user roles, or plan tiers hides the segment that actually has PMF. Superhuman's initial 22% score masked a sub-segment well above 40%; averaging suppressed the signal.
- Survivorship bias in benchmark sourcing: activation rate benchmarks from Mixpanel and Userpilot are drawn from companies that use these analytics tools, which skews toward more sophisticated, growth-oriented teams. Naive adoption of 30% as a 'median' target may not apply to an early-stage product with different distribution.

**False-positive mechanism:** The most dangerous false positive occurs when a product achieves a high activation rate driven by a motivated early-adopter cohort (friends, beta users, people who found the product through a highly targeted channel) whose behavior does not generalize to the broader market. These users activate quickly because they already understand the value proposition and are predisposed to succeed; the activation rate measures their conversion, not the product's ability to convert a representative user. A team that sees 50%+ activation from this cohort and declares PMF validation will be shocked when the rate collapses as they broaden acquisition. The false positive is compounded if the activation event is a low-friction proxy action (e.g., 'created a project') rather than an action that requires genuine product comprehension. Corrective discipline: always run cohort analysis stratified by acquisition source, and compare activation rates of organic/direct users (who have self-selected intent) against paid or cold-channel users (who have not). A robust activation signal holds across both strata.

**Examples**
- Superhuman: In 2017, Rahul Vohra ran Sean Ellis's PMF survey and found only 22% of users would be 'very disappointed' without Superhuman -- well below the 40% threshold. Rather than treating this as binary failure, he segmented respondents by persona and identified the high-value sub-segment (fast-moving professionals who sent high email volume). By optimizing onboarding and product specifically for that segment's activation path (keyboard-first flow, zero setup friction), Superhuman raised their PMF score from 22% to 33% and eventually to 58%. Time-to-first-value was explicitly compressed: new users were onboarded one-on-one in 30-minute sessions to ensure every user reached the aha moment before being left to self-serve.
- Pinterest: Pinterest, as documented in Reforge's Retention and Engagement curriculum, operationalized the three-moment framework: Setup Moment = user selects 5 interest topics on day 1 (ensuring relevant feed content); Aha Moment = user pins something within first 7 days (first experience of core value); Habit Moment = user pins on 4 of first 28 days (habit formation). Each moment had a binary quantitative definition allowing cohort analysis and A/B testing of interventions at each stage independently, rather than optimizing a single monolithic 'activation rate'.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: Activation rate at or above the 60th percentile for the product category (approximately 36%+ for SaaS per Lenny Rachitsky's survey), paired with a TTV p50 under 48 hours for PLG products (or within the SMB-appropriate 30-day window for B2B), and a retention curve that shows a 10-15+ percentage point gap between activated and non-activated cohorts at day 30, is a strong signal that the product delivers its core value proposition reliably and fast enough to support retention-driven growth. If the Sean Ellis survey score of the activated sub-segment also clears 40%, the signal becomes cross-validated. These conditions together suggest the onboarding funnel is not the primary growth constraint.
- Weak signal: Activation rate below the 20-25% range (below the cross-product median), or a TTV that is measured in days for a self-serve product where competitors deliver value in minutes, signals that either the activation event is mis-defined (too hard to reach, or not actually predictive of retention), or the onboarding funnel has a structural conversion failure. A flat activation rate that does not respond to onboarding interventions -- especially if the Sean Ellis score from the 'activated' cohort is still below 40% -- is evidence that the problem is not onboarding mechanics but product-market fit itself: the product does not yet deliver a value moment that enough users find worth returning for.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Lenny Rachitsky survey of 500+ products: median activation rate 25%, average 34% -- Directly published at lennysnewsletter.com on October 25, 2022, in 'What Is a Good Activation Rate?' The survey was conducted with growth advisor Yuriy Timen. The 500+ respondent count, 25% median, and 34% average across all product types are stated verbatim. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-a-good-activation-rate)
- [confirmed] Lenny Rachitsky: SaaS-only median activation rate 30%, SaaS-only average 36% -- The same October 2022 Lenny Rachitsky newsletter article explicitly breaks out SaaS-only figures: median 30% and average 36% after removing marketplaces, e-commerce, and DTC products. Figures match exactly. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/what-is-a-good-activation-rate)
- [confirmed] Mixpanel 2019: median first-week SaaS activation rate 17%, 90th percentile 65% -- Confirmed by Mixpanel's own October 2019 blog post announcing the 2019 Product Benchmarks Report, based on 1.2 billion users across 638 products. The 17% SaaS median and 65% 90th-percentile figures are stated in the report and corroborated by Digital Authority Partners' summary. (src: https://mixpanel.com/blog/2019/10/21/2019-product-benchmarks-report-mixpanel/)
- [contested] Userpilot 2024 (547 SaaS companies): median TTV = 1 day 12 hours 23 minutes -- The 'median TTV = 1 day 12 hours 23 minutes' figure is confirmed by Userpilot's Time-to-Value benchmark page, and the 547-company headline is real. However, the TTV metric specifically was derived from a 62-company subset that used Userpilot's New User Activation dashboard, not all 547 companies. Attributing this figure to '547 SaaS companies' is a material overstatement of the sample that supports the TTV number. (src: https://userpilot.com/blog/time-to-value-benchmark-report-2024/)
- [contested] Userpilot 2024: AI & ML activation rate 54.8%, FinTech & Insurance 5% -- The specific figures (AI & ML 54.8%, FinTech 5%) are confirmed by Userpilot's User Activation Rate Benchmark Report 2024 page. However, the activation rate data derives from only 62 B2B companies (not the headline 547), and vertical breakdowns within those 62 companies represent very small sub-samples. The category-level figures carry high uncertainty from sample size. (src: https://userpilot.com/blog/user-activation-rate-benchmark-report-2024/)
- [confirmed] Sean Ellis 40% threshold: validated across 100+ startups -- The 40% threshold is confirmed as originating from Sean Ellis's May 2010 blog post on startup-marketing.com, derived from studying 'nearly 100 startups.' The figure is widely corroborated across secondary sources and Ellis's own subsequent writing. The 'nearly 100' figure is consistently cited; the claim of 'validated across 100+ startups' is a minor rounding of the original 'nearly 100' description. (src: https://www.startup-marketing.com/2010/01/)
- [confirmed] Superhuman initial PMF score: 22%; after iteration: 58% -- Confirmed by Rahul Vohra's First Round Review article and Superhuman's own blog. The 22% starting score and 58% eventual score are stated explicitly. The article also clarifies the path (22% -> 33% after segmentation, then 47%, 56%, then 58% after sustained product iteration). (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [confirmed] Slack PMF score (Hiten Shah, 2015, 731 users): 51% 'very disappointed' -- Confirmed by Hiten Shah's original August 2015 post at hitenism.com. The survey used Typeform, received 731 responses, and 370 respondents (50.6%, reported as 51%) said they would be 'very disappointed' without Slack. All stated details match. (src: https://hitenism.com/slack-product-market-fit-survey/)
- [confirmed] Amplitude 2025 (2,600+ companies): 69% of products with strong early activation also had strong 3-month retention -- Confirmed by Amplitude's blog post 'Time to Value: The Key to Driving User Retention,' which directly cites the 2025 Product Benchmark Report covering 2,600+ companies (data September 2023 to September 2024). The 69% figure is stated verbatim. (src: https://amplitude.com/blog/time-to-value-drives-user-retention)
- [confirmed] Amplitude 2025: 3-month retention top performers 18.5% vs. median 3.8% -- Confirmed by Amplitude's 2025 Product Benchmark Report blog coverage. Top performers (90th percentile) retain 18.5% of users at three months vs. 3.8% for median products. The figures are stated explicitly in Amplitude's own published content. (src: https://amplitude.com/blog/time-to-value-drives-user-retention)
- [confirmed] Amplitude 2025: more than 98% of users churn within 2 weeks if they have not experienced value -- Confirmed verbatim by Amplitude's blog post citing the 2025 Product Benchmark Report: 'More than 98% of users churn within two weeks if they haven't experienced value.' This appears to be derived from the drop-off pattern across the 2,600+ company dataset. (src: https://amplitude.com/blog/time-to-value-drives-user-retention)

The Klaviyo customer count and ARR are independently corroborated by SaaStr's own coverage of Klaviyo's IPO. This is Lemkin's qualitative observation from working with startups, not a controlled study. (src: https://www.saastr.com/the-silent-lurking-churn-activation-rates-less-than-90)

Alstromer's published slides and YC library content show retention curve shapes as PMF indicators but do not cite percentage cutoffs. The >40% and >50% figures circulate widely in the startup ecosystem and are consistent with Alstromer's general framing, but the specific attribution to YC and the '3,000+ portfolio companies' provenance could not be verified from any primary source.
