### 32. Champion vs Economic-Buyer Mapping

- **Category:** Organizational / B2B
- **Stage:** org-buyer
- **Definition:** Champion vs economic-buyer mapping is the explicit, continuous process by which a B2B founder or salesperson identifies and distinguishes two structurally different stakeholder types inside a prospect organization: the champion (an internal advocate with influence but typically without final budget authority) and the economic buyer (the individual with unilateral authority to approve or kill a purchase). The method treats these as separate hypotheses to be validated, not assumed — because a champion who cannot access the economic buyer is insufficient to close a deal, and an economic buyer engaged without a champion lacks internal adoption momentum. The mapping is used as a validation instrument: if a founder cannot name and reach the economic buyer, the signal of organizational demand is considered unreliable.
- **Validates:** Validates whether a perceived organizational demand signal is real and actionable — specifically: does anyone with budget authority actually experience the problem acutely enough to approve spend, and does the champion have sufficient access and credibility to navigate the buying committee to a decision? The core question is: "Is the enthusiasm I am observing coming from the person or persons who can commit organizational resources, or only from a well-meaning user who cannot?"

**Protocol**
1. Before or at the first discovery conversation, map the organization's decision-making unit on paper: hypothesize who the end users, champion, economic buyer, technical evaluators, and veto-holders (e.g., IT, legal, procurement) are likely to be — then treat every hypothesis as unconfirmed until you have a name.
2. In every early interview, ask Rob Fitzpatrick's required B2B probe: 'Where does the money come from?' and 'Who else would be involved in a decision like this?' Record exact names, titles, and roles — not generalities. Never leave an interview without asking 'Who else should I talk to?'
3. Test whether your current contact is a champion or merely a coach: ask them to introduce you to the economic buyer via a specific calendar invite (not 'I will forward something'). A real champion can and will make this introduction; a coach will stall. If after two attempts no introduction materializes, treat the deal as unqualified.
4. Identify the economic buyer by title and confirm budget authority directly. Bill Aulet recommends asking four questions plainly: 'What would be needed for a product trial?', 'Who else must participate in deciding?', 'What could derail this?', and 'Who controls the budget?' For companies under 25 employees the EB is typically the founder or CFO; for 25–500+ employees budget authority disperses across a committee of 3–13+ stakeholders.
5. Validate the champion's actual internal standing: ask them to articulate your differentiation in their own words (can they pitch you back accurately?), ask them to co-create a mutual success plan with their own metrics, and observe whether they proactively share bad news. A genuine champion does all three; a coach does none.
6. Map coverage gaps: build a live stakeholder matrix listing every known role (champion, economic buyer, technical evaluator, procurement, legal, veto-power), the name occupying each role, your current engagement level with each, and what each cares about. Treat any unfilled cell as an open risk — not a safe assumption.
7. Run a parallel interview with the economic buyer directly, without the champion present. Confirm that the economic buyer can articulate the pain in quantified business terms (not just product terms) and can describe what a successful outcome looks like for the organization. If the EB cannot quantify the pain, the deal lacks the business case needed to survive internal review.
8. Repeat the map update at every significant deal stage (demo, pilot, proposal). Champion relationships erode, EBs change, and new veto-holders surface. Treat the map as a living document, not a one-time artifact.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Average B2B buying committee size (Gartner 2023): 8–13 stakeholders per enterprise purchase; grew from 5.4 in 2015 (CEB/Gartner Challenger Customer) to 6.8 in 2017 (HBR/CEB) to 8–13 by 2023 (src: Gartner B2B Buying Journey research 2023, cited in Attainment Labs and multiple synthesis sources; original 5.4 figure from CEB The Challenger Customer (2015))
- Deals lost without economic buyer access (sales manager post-mortem): 6 out of 7 lost deals had no meeting with the economic buyer; 5 out of 6 won deals had EB access (src: Prospeo 2026 B2B Economic Buyer Playbook, citing a widely-referenced sales manager post-mortem anecdote)
- Closing probability drop when EB raises ROI concerns post-presentation: 79% drop in close probability when the economic buyer raises ROI concerns after the solution has already been presented (src: Prospeo 2026 B2B Economic Buyer Playbook)
- Deal stall rate in B2B buying process: 86% of B2B deals stall during the buying process (src: Prospeo 2026 B2B Decision-Making guide)
- Minimum interview volume for B2B signal (Lean B2B): At least 5 businesses in the same segment sharing the same pain, with 2–3 stakeholders interviewed per company for mid-market/enterprise accounts (src: Etienne Garbugli, Lean B2B (2nd ed.), as cited in leanb2bbook.com customer discovery guide)
- Minimum company interview volume for strong B2B signal (Forum Ventures): 30 companies minimum to achieve a reliable signal for B2B validation (src: Forum Ventures B2B Idea Validation Playbook (forumvc.com))
- Cross-departmental involvement in enterprise purchases (Forrester 2025): 89% of enterprise purchases involve stakeholders from two or more departments; Forrester 2025 Buyers Journey Survey (n=16,000+ buyers/sellers, 19 countries) puts average at 13 internal + 9 external stakeholders (src: Forrester Buyers Journey Survey 2025, cited in Influ2 and Prospeo synthesis sources)
- Budget approval as top B2B deal bottleneck (Influ2 2026): 34% of B2B deals cite budget approval as the primary bottleneck; 22% cite internal alignment (src: Influ2 2026 buying committee survey, cited in Influ2 Academy)

**Origin and evidence:** The champion/economic buyer distinction was operationalized as a formal sales qualification element inside PTC (Parametric Technology Corporation) in 1996, created by Dick Dunkel and formalized alongside John McMahon and Jack Napoli as the MEDDIC framework (Metrics, Economic Buyer, Decision Criteria, Decision Process, Identify Pain, Champion). PTC used the framework to scale from near-bankruptcy to over $1 billion in revenue during the 1990s. The framework was subsequently commercialized and popularized by John McMahon in enterprise software and SaaS. The customer-discovery application of the same distinction — validating with the right organizational buyer before building — was independently codified by Rob Fitzpatrick (The Mom Test, 2013) who made 'Where does the money come from?' a required B2B probe, and by Etienne Garbugli (Lean B2B, 2012/2022) who formalized the decision-making unit as an interview target. Bill Aulet's Disciplined Entrepreneurship (MIT, 2013) added the three-role taxonomy (End User, Champion, Primary Economic Buyer) that is now widely taught. The evidence base is practitioner-derived and field-validated, not from controlled academic studies; it rests on decades of enterprise sales post-mortems, interview methodology refinement, and observed deal-flow patterns rather than randomized experiments.

**Canonical sources:** Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) — B2B discovery probes for navigating organizational buyers; Etienne Garbugli, Lean B2B: Build Products Businesses Want (2012, 2nd ed. 2022) — decision-making unit framework for B2B founders; Bill Aulet, Disciplined Entrepreneurship (MIT, 2013) — End User / Champion / Primary Economic Buyer taxonomy and four direct validation questions; Dick Dunkel / John McMahon / Jack Napoli, MEDDIC framework (PTC, 1996) — original formalization of Economic Buyer and Champion as distinct qualification fields; John McMahon, The Qualified Sales Leader (2021) — champion testing and economic buyer access as deal-qualification disciplines; Gartner, B2B Buying Journey research (2023) — buying committee size and complexity data; a16z / Peter Levine, All Things Sales: 16 Mini-Lessons for Startup Founders (2018) and A Framework for Finding a Design Partner — user vs. buyer distinction for startups; CEB / Brent Adamson & Matthew Dixon, The Challenger Customer (2015) — original 5.4 stakeholder average and mobilizer/champion roles in enterprise deals

**Failure modes**
- Treating a coach as a champion: the most common failure. A coach shares information and is enthusiastic but will not sell internally when you are not in the room and has no personal win tied to your success. Deals built on coaches collapse at the final approval stage.
- Assuming the economic buyer is whoever has the highest title you have met. Title does not equal budget authority; in many enterprises a VP of Engineering controls evaluation but a CFO or COO controls spend.
- Single-threading: building the entire deal relationship through one champion who then departs, goes quiet, or loses internal credibility, leaving no other mapped stakeholders.
- Mapping once and never updating: the buying committee shifts throughout a deal. New veto-holders (IT security, legal, procurement) enter late; an unmapped champion replacement can kill a deal that looked closed.
- Conflating organizational enthusiasm with economic buyer commitment: multiple enthusiastic end users create a false signal of demand if none of them have surfaced to the budget holder.
- Over-rotating to the economic buyer too early without a champion: cold-approaching the CFO before the champion has built internal credibility can get you blacklisted from the account.
- Accepting 'I'll forward something internally' as champion behavior: this is the canonical false-positive tell. A champion books a meeting; a coach forwards an email.

**False-positive mechanism:** The canonical false positive occurs when a founder conducts numerous enthusiastic customer discovery conversations with end users or department-level champions who validate the problem intensely but have no budget authority and no reliable path to the economic buyer. The conversations produce strong qualitative signal — detailed pain articulation, feature requests, willingness to run a pilot — all of which appear indistinguishable from genuine organizational demand. The false positive is revealed only at the conversion stage, when the economic buyer is engaged for the first time and either has no knowledge of the project, frames the problem entirely differently (in ROI and procurement terms, not workflow terms), or declines because no business case was built for them during the discovery phase. In MEDDIC terms, this is the deal where 'Identify Pain' and 'Champion' appear green but 'Economic Buyer' was never actually accessed, only assumed. The falseness is structural: champion enthusiasm is a leading indicator of user adoption potential, not of organizational willingness to pay — and these are different purchasing decisions in enterprises.

**Examples**
- PTC (Parametric Technology Corporation): PTC's sales leadership (Dick Dunkel, John McMahon, Jack Napoli) codified the MEDDIC qualification framework internally in 1996 precisely because reps were winning technical champions inside customer accounts but losing deals at the economic buyer stage. By requiring reps to explicitly name and access the economic buyer as a qualification gate — not an afterthought — PTC drove revenue from near-bankruptcy to over $1 billion. The champion/economic buyer mapping became the single most documented case of this validation discipline applied at organizational scale in enterprise software.
- Lumos (a16z-backed, raised $30M+): Lumos, an internal app store for companies, used the a16z design partner framework that explicitly requires identifying both the internal champion (the employee who will drive adoption and provide feedback) and the buyer (the person who can approve and implement the software). The framework warns that CEOs who get excited are neither buyers nor users and cannot force adoption — Lumos specifically sought champions at the operational level with direct connection to budget-holding IT or procurement leads, avoiding the false-positive trap of executive enthusiasm without implementation authority.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result looks like: (1) the champion can name the economic buyer by name and title, describes having run a purchase through them before, and books a concrete meeting between you and the EB within two weeks; (2) the economic buyer, when interviewed independently, articulates the same pain in quantified business terms without being prompted by your framing; (3) the economic buyer asks unprompted about pricing, timeline, or procurement process — signals of active evaluation rather than passive interest; (4) the champion co-creates a mutual success plan with their own internal metrics. This configuration indicates genuine organizational demand: the pain is real at the user level, the champion has standing, and the economic buyer has already begun mentally allocating resources.
- Weak signal: A weak or false result looks like: (1) you can name a champion but the champion has never introduced you to or named the economic buyer after being asked directly more than once; (2) the most senior person you have met uses language like 'this is interesting' or 'let me see what I can do internally' but cannot describe the internal approval process; (3) the economic buyer, when eventually reached, has not heard of your company or frames the problem differently from the champion; (4) the champion responds to requests for the EB introduction with email forwards rather than calendar invitations; (5) no one in the organization can quantify the cost of the problem in business terms. These are indicators that you have validated user pain but not organizational willingness or capacity to buy — a category error that wastes multiple months of sales cycle.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [contested] Average B2B buying committee grew from 5.4 stakeholders (CEB/Gartner, 2015) to 6.8 (HBR/CEB, 2017) to 8–13 (Gartner, 2023) -- The 5.4 figure is confirmed from CEB's 'The Challenger Customer' (2015). The 6.8 figure is confirmed from CEB research published via HBR 'The New Sales Imperative' (2017), though the data was collected in 2016, not 2017. The 8–13 figure attributed to 'Gartner 2023' is cited only through secondary synthesis sources (Attainment Labs, Prospeo, Instantly.ai) and not traceable to a named Gartner primary report. One secondary timeline cites 8.2 for Gartner 2023; the 'up to 13' range appears to conflate Forrester 2025 data. The 2023 Gartner anchor specifically is unverified at the primary level. (src: CEB 'The Challenger Customer' (2015); HBR 'The New Sales Imperative' (2017) citing CEB/Brent Adamson; https://www.attainmentlabs.com/blog/b2b-buying-committees-doubled (secondary))
- [contested] One sales manager post-mortem: 6 of 7 lost deals had no economic buyer meeting; 5 of 6 won deals had economic buyer access (Prospeo 2026, citing field anecdote) -- The anecdote is real and traceable to a first-person article by Rizan Flenner at iseeit.com, reproduced by Prospeo. However, it is an unattributed personal anecdote with no named company, no peer review, no methodology, and a sample of only 13 deals total. It is a single sales manager's internal post-mortem, not a study. Presenting it as a data benchmark is misleading; it is illustrative at best. (src: https://now.iseeit.com/hitting-an-80-close-rate-economic-buyer/ (original anecdote, author Rizan Flenner, unnamed company); https://prospeo.io/s/economic-buyer (reproduces it))
- [contested] When the economic buyer raises ROI concerns after the solution is presented, the probability of closing drops 79% (Prospeo 2026) -- The 79% figure traces back to Ebsta's 'B2B Sales Benchmarks 2024' PDF report, not an original Prospeo claim. Prospeo cites FlowState Sales, which cites Ebsta. The Ebsta PDF could not be read to verify methodology or sample size. The figure appears to originate from Ebsta's CRM-data analysis, which is a proprietary dataset of unknown scope and composition. The claim cannot be confirmed or refuted without access to the Ebsta report's methodology section. (src: https://flowstatesales.com/resource-hub/economic-buyer/ (cites Ebsta B2B Sales Benchmarks 2024 PDF); https://prospeo.io/s/economic-buyer (secondary reproduction))
- [confirmed] 86% of B2B deals stall during the buying process (Prospeo 2026) -- This figure is confirmed from Forrester's 'The State of Business Buying, 2024' report, announced December 4, 2024, based on a survey of 16,000+ global business buyers. Prospeo attributes it to Forrester, which is accurate. The report year is 2024, not a 2026 Prospeo original, but the underlying source is real and authoritative. (src: Forrester 'The State of Business Buying, 2024' (announced Dec 4, 2024); https://investor.forrester.com/news-releases/news-release-details/forrester-master-b2b-buying-mayhem-providers-must-prioritize/)
- [contested] Lean B2B (Garbugli) minimum threshold: 5 businesses in the same segment sharing the same pain, with 2–3 stakeholders per mid-market/enterprise company -- The '5 businesses in the same segment' threshold is confirmed verbatim from leanb2bbook.com's customer discovery guide. However, the '2–3 stakeholders per mid-market/enterprise company' clause was NOT found on the Lean B2B site; it appears instead in the Forum Ventures playbook. The claim conflates two separate sources into one attributed to Garbugli. The 5-business minimum is Garbugli's; the 2–3 stakeholder-per-company specification is Forum Ventures'. (src: https://leanb2bbook.com/blog/customer-discovery-startup-guide/ (confirms 5-business minimum); https://www.forumvc.com/playbooks/forum-ventures-playbook-idea-validation (source of 2–3 stakeholders per enterprise company, not Garbugli))
- [confirmed] Forum Ventures benchmark: 30 companies minimum for a reliable B2B signal -- The Forum Ventures 'Idea Validation and Customer Discovery: A B2B Founder Playbook' explicitly states to aim for at least 30 companies as a baseline for achieving signal in B2B customer discovery. The playbook is publicly accessible (gated by email form) at forumvc.com. The claim accurately represents the source. (src: https://www.forumvc.com/playbooks/forum-ventures-playbook-idea-validation)
- [contested] Forrester 2025 Buyers Journey Survey (n=16,000+, 19 countries): average B2B purchase involves 13 internal + 9 external stakeholders; 89% of enterprise purchases cross departmental lines -- The 13 internal + 9 external figure is confirmed from Forrester's 2025 Buyers' Journey Survey as cited in 'The State of Business Buying, 2026' report. However, the sample size appears to be approximately 18,000 respondents, not 16,000+ (the 16,000 figure applies to the 2024 survey). The country count of 19 could not be independently verified. The 89% cross-departmental figure (the Forrester 2026 report says 73% of purchases involve 3+ departments, not 89%) appears inflated or sourced from a different dataset. The core stakeholder counts are real; the ancillary details (n=16,000, 19 countries, 89%) are unverified or contradicted. (src: https://www.forrester.com/press-newsroom/forrester-2026-the-state-of-business-buying/ (confirms 13 internal + 9 external from 2025 survey); https://www.digitalcommerce360.com/2026/01/22/forrester-b2b-buying-ai-2026/ (73% figure, contradicting 89% claim))
- [contested] 34% of B2B deals cite budget approval as primary bottleneck; 22% cite internal alignment (Influ2 2026 survey) -- The 34% and 22% figures appear on Influ2's Academy page and are attributed to an Influ2 2026 survey. However, the disclosed sample size is only 50 enterprise and mid-market buyers, which is far too small to generate reliable population-level statistics. No methodology, confidence intervals, or sampling framework are disclosed. These percentages may be real findings from Influ2's own small survey, but the sample size makes them unsuitable as authoritative benchmarks. Presenting them without the n=50 caveat is misleading. (src: https://www.influ2.com/academy/buying-committees (published March 20, 2026; n=50 disclosed for at least one finding, no full methodology))
- [confirmed] MEDDIC created at PTC in 1996; PTC grew to over $1 billion in revenue using this qualification discipline -- Multiple independent sources confirm MEDDIC was created at PTC in 1996 by Dick Dunkel, in collaboration with Jack Napoli under SVP John McMahon. PTC's growth from ~$300 million to over $1 billion in revenue over approximately four years following MEDDIC adoption is consistently reported across sales methodology sources. The 1996 date and $1 billion milestone are treated as established facts in the MEDDIC literature, corroborated by the MEDDICC.com official resource, iseeit.com interview with Jack Napoli, and FlowState Sales' interview with Dick Dunkel. (src: https://meddicc.com/resources/who-created-meddic; https://now.iseeit.com/meddic-sales-process-founder-jack-napoli/; https://flowstatesales.com/resource-hub/the-history-of-meddic-interview-with-dick-dunkel/)

**Citations**
- Prospeo: Economic Buyer 2026 B2B Sales Playbook: https://prospeo.io/s/economic-buyer
- MEDDICC.com: MEDDPICC Sales Methodology and Process (origin at PTC 1996): https://meddicc.com/meddpicc-sales-methodology-and-process
- Forum Ventures: B2B Idea Validation Playbook: https://www.forumvc.com/playbooks/forum-ventures-playbook-idea-validation
- leanb2bbook.com: Customer Discovery Startup Guide (Garbugli, Lean B2B): https://leanb2bbook.com/blog/customer-discovery-startup-guide/
- a16z: A Framework for Finding a Design Partner: https://a16z.com/a-framework-for-finding-a-design-partner/
- a16z: All Things Sales — 16 Mini-Lessons for Startup Founders (Peter Levine): https://a16z.com/all-things-sales-16-mini-lessons-for-startup-founders/
- Disciplined Entrepreneurship (MIT/Aulet): The Decision-Making Unit: https://www.d-eship.com/articles/the-decision-making-unit-the-who-is-who-of-your-b2b-sales-process/
- Influ2: B2B Buying Committees — How to Target Decision Makers (2026 data): https://www.influ2.com/academy/buying-committees
- Attainment Labs: B2B Buying Committees Have Doubled — 8–13 Stakeholders: https://www.attainmentlabs.com/blog/b2b-buying-committees-doubled

### 33. Procurement / Security Review as a Deal-Survival Test

- **Category:** Organizational / B2B
- **Stage:** org-buyer
- **Definition:** A validation method in which a startup deliberately surfaces its product to an enterprise buyer's procurement, security, legal, and compliance gatekeepers before or early in the sales cycle, treating whether the deal survives this institutional veto as the definitive signal of org-buyer fit. Unlike user-level demand validation, it tests whether the organizational buying structure will permit purchase, not merely whether individuals want the product. A deal that advances through procurement and security review with no fatal objection constitutes positive validation; a deal that dies there, regardless of user enthusiasm, signals a product or positioning gap with organizational gatekeepers.
- **Validates:** Can this product clear the institutional veto layer (procurement, security, legal, IT) required to close an enterprise deal, independent of end-user demand?

**Protocol**
1. Identify a live prospect with a genuine budget and a named economic buyer, not a friendly contact. The deal must be real enough that a 'no' from procurement would be a material loss.
2. Map the full buying committee before the security review arrives. Use the champion to surface who owns security review, legal sign-off, procurement negotiation, and IT approval. Gartner 2024 data puts the average committee at 6.8 stakeholders; complex SaaS deals regularly involve 10-25.
3. Send a proactive vendor security package to the prospect's security and procurement contacts before they ask. Include: any SOC 2 or ISO 27001 documentation, a completed standard vendor security questionnaire (e.g., CAIQ or a VSQ template), data processing agreement, subprocessor list, and uptime SLA. Do not wait for the prospect to initiate this.
4. Request a structured security review call with the prospect's CISO, IT lead, or procurement officer. Ask them to walk through their standard vendor assessment criteria live. Treat this call as a discovery interview in the Rob Fitzpatrick sense: surface the actual blocking criteria, not the polite objections.
5. Track every objection raised during procurement and legal review. Categorize each as: (a) fixable in weeks, (b) requires months of investment (SOC 2 audit, architectural change), or (c) structurally incompatible with the current product. Category (c) objections are the validation signal.
6. Measure deal survival rate across the procurement phase across at least 3-5 active enterprise prospects. A single deal dying in security review is noise; a pattern across multiple prospects pointing to the same gap is signal.
7. After each procurement outcome (win or loss), conduct a retrospective with the champion to identify which gatekeeper killed or nearly killed the deal and on what specific criterion. This is the equivalent of a post-interview debrief in problem-discovery validation.
8. Use the aggregate pattern to decide: (a) invest in compliance infrastructure (SOC 2, SSO, data residency) if the gap is consistent and fixable; (b) reposition the product or target a different buyer segment if the gap is architectural or segment-specific.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Share of B2B purchases that stall at some point in the buying process: 86% (src: Forrester State of Business Buying, 2024)
- Average number of stakeholders in a B2B buying group (2024): 6.8 (up from 5.4 in 2020) (src: Gartner, 2024, cited by Iterators and Intentsify)
- Share of organizations that experienced a third-party-involved breach or cyberattack in the prior 12 months: 47% (src: Ponemon Institute / Imprivata Global Research, February 2025)
- Share of all 2024 data breaches that were third-party-related: 35.5% (src: SecurityScorecard 2025 Global Third-Party Breach Report (analysis of 1,000 breaches))
- Share of companies experiencing a third-party breach or incident in 2023: 61% (src: Prevalent Inc. 2024 Third-Party Risk Management Study)
- Typical enterprise SaaS sales cycle for >$100K ACV deals: 90-180+ days (src: Optifai B2B Sales Cycle Length Benchmarks, 939 companies)
- Share of 12% of SaaS deals that collapse specifically in the proposal/negotiation stage (which includes procurement): 12% (src: Development Corporate analysis of SaaS deal loss data, 2025)
- YC Demo Day minimum revenue target (ARR) cited as context for vendor risk concern: $800K ARR in 12 weeks (src: Licens.io citing Zack Korman, Embroidery co-founder, YouTube interview; no primary YC document found)

**Origin and evidence:** No single founding text treats procurement review explicitly as a startup validation instrument. The practice is implicit in the enterprise sales literature and was popularized operationally by YC's enterprise sales canon (notably the 'Enterprise Sales for Hackers' essay, which advises founders to 'avoid IT until required' as a tactical hedge, implicitly acknowledging the procurement veto as a deal-survival gate). The framing of procurement as a validation test rather than a sales obstacle is an applied synthesis from practitioner writing (Ciphrix, Iterators, SecurityBoulevard, 2025), grounded in Gartner/Forrester buying-committee research and third-party breach data from SecurityScorecard and Ponemon Institute. Evidence strength is moderate: the underlying buying-committee and breach statistics are from primary research firms; the specific claim that '73% of enterprise sales for startups fail during vendor assessment' circulates widely in practitioner content but no primary study has been located to verify it - treat as unconfirmed industry lore.

**Canonical sources:** Y Combinator, 'Enterprise Sales for Hackers' (blog, ycombinator.com); Y Combinator, 'Enterprise Sales for Founders' (YC Startup Library, ycombinator.com/library); Gartner, B2B Buying Group research (2024), via multiple secondary sources; Forrester, State of Business Buying 2024 (Forrester Research); SecurityScorecard, 2025 Global Third-Party Breach Report (businesswire.com, March 2025); Ponemon Institute / Imprivata, Third-Party Risk Research, February 2025; Prevalent Inc., 2024 Third-Party Risk Management Study; Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) — not specific to procurement, but frames the principle that polite enthusiasm from users does not constitute organizational commitment to purchase

**Failure modes**
- Running the test too late: engaging procurement only after months of relationship-building means the deal failure is costly and slow to generate learning; the test should be front-loaded.
- Testing with the wrong prospect tier: SMB or mid-market buyers may not have a formal procurement or security review layer, making a pass meaningless for enterprise validation.
- Conflating user enthusiasm with org-buyer approval: a champion's verbal 'yes' and an end-user's excitement are not procurement approval; deals die in exactly this gap.
- Mistaking a slow review for a pass: a deal still 'under review' after 8+ weeks has often informally failed but the prospect is too polite to say so.
- Survivorship bias in the pipeline: if only the friendliest prospects are pushed to procurement, the test population is pre-selected to pass, yielding false confidence.
- Confusing a one-off exception with repeatability: a large enterprise may grant a procurement exception for a pilot but refuse to expand; one passed review does not predict scalable motion.
- Treating remediable objections as validation failures: if the only blocker is 'get SOC 2,' that is a fixable investment decision, not evidence the product lacks org-buyer fit.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false positive is a deal that passes procurement under a pilot or exception carve-out that would not apply at scale or at other accounts. Enterprise procurement teams sometimes grant informal waivers for promising pilot vendors ('we'll accept a SOC 2 readiness letter for now') without those waivers representing repeatable policy. A startup that interprets a waiver-aided pilot approval as product-market validation at the org-buyer layer will invest in sales capacity before the compliance infrastructure that broader deployment requires, then discover the exception does not generalize when the second or third enterprise account applies standard policy. A secondary false positive occurs when the champion actively shields the procurement process from the startup, filtering objections before they surface; the deal appears to pass review cleanly when in fact the champion is absorbing institutional resistance that will resurface at renewal or expansion.

**Examples**
- Mercor (YC-backed): A YC-backed recruiting marketplace suffered a breach exposing 4 terabytes of data affecting 40,000+ contractors and triggering five class action lawsuits within one week, with downstream exposure to OpenAI, PayPal, Stripe, Amazon, and Microsoft. This case is now cited by enterprise procurement teams as a concrete reason to apply heightened vendor risk scrutiny to accelerator-stage companies, illustrating how the absence of a procurement-survival test before scaling enterprise sales creates compounding institutional and legal risk.
- Delve (YC-backed): Delve, a YC-backed compliance startup, was found to have fabricated 494 SOC 2 reports while raising $32 million at a reported $300 million valuation. Enterprise buyers who had relied on Delve's compliance attestations as a procurement shortcut discovered that the artifact they used to clear vendor review was fraudulent. The case is a documented example of the false-positive mechanism: a fabricated compliance artifact can allow a startup to pass procurement review without the underlying security posture the review is designed to verify.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A deal advances through a prospect's full standard vendor security questionnaire, legal data processing agreement review, and procurement negotiation with no fatal objections and no exception carve-outs -- and this pattern repeats across at least three independent enterprise accounts in the same target segment. This constitutes strong evidence that the product, its architecture, and its compliance posture are fit for org-buyer approval in that segment, and that sales motion can be built without a compliance remediation detour.
- Weak signal: A single deal passes procurement with significant champion intervention, an informal SOC 2 waiver, or after the startup made reactive architecture changes mid-review. Or: the deals tested are mid-market accounts without formal security review processes. Either scenario yields weak signal -- the test has not been run against the actual institutional gatekeeping machinery that governs enterprise purchasing, and the result cannot be generalized to the broader target segment.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] 86% of B2B purchases stall at some point in the buying process (Forrester State of Business Buying, 2024) -- Directly confirmed by Forrester's official press release dated December 4, 2024, titled 'Forrester: To Master B2B Buying Mayhem, Providers Must Prioritize Buyers' Needs,' based on a survey of more than 16,000 global business buyers. The 86% figure is stated verbatim in the release and mirrored on BusinessWire and Forrester's own investor newsroom. (src: https://www.forrester.com/press-newsroom/forrester-the-state-of-business-buying-2024)
- [contested] Average B2B buying group involves 6.8 stakeholders in 2024, up from 5.4 in 2020 (Gartner 2024) -- The '6.8 stakeholders' figure originates from Harvard Business Review's 'The New Sales Imperative' (2017), not a Gartner 2024 report. The '5.4' baseline traces to CEB/Gartner's 'The Challenger Customer' circa 2015, not 2020. No Gartner 2024 report citing '6.8' was located. By 2024, independent sources (6sense, Gartner 2023, Forrester) all report figures of 8-13 stakeholders, meaning the 6.8 number is both misattributed and outdated. The claimed year of origin (2024 for 6.8; 2020 for 5.4) is wrong for both data points. (src: https://www.attainmentlabs.com/blog/b2b-buying-committees-doubled)
- [confirmed] 47% of organizations experienced a third-party-involved breach or cyberattack in the prior 12 months (Ponemon Institute / Imprivata, February 2025) -- Confirmed by Imprivata's official press release (February 13, 2025) and GlobeNewswire, reporting results of a Ponemon Institute survey of 1,942 IT security practitioners in the US, UK, Germany, and Australia. The 47% figure is stated explicitly as the global rate, with the UK rate higher at 51%. (src: https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2025/02/13/3025931/0/en/Imprivata-Study-Finds-Nearly-Half-of-Organizations-Suffered-a-Third-Party-Security-Incident-in-Past-Year.html)
- [confirmed] 35.5% of all 2024 data breaches were third-party-related (SecurityScorecard 2025 Global Third-Party Breach Report, analysis of 1,000 breaches) -- Confirmed by SecurityScorecard's own press release (March 26, 2025) and the full PDF report available on their site. The report explicitly states 35.5% of all 2024 breaches involved third-party infrastructure, up 6.5 percentage points from 2023, based on analysis of 1,000 breaches by the STRIKE Threat Intelligence Unit. Multiple independent outlets (Dark Reading, CYBERSOL, Yahoo Finance) corroborate the figure. (src: https://securityscorecard.com/company/press/securityscorecard-2025-global-third-party-breach-report-reveals-surge-in-vendor-driven-attacks/)
- [confirmed] 61% of companies experienced a third-party breach or cybersecurity incident in 2023 (Prevalent Inc. 2024 Third-Party Risk Management Study) -- Confirmed by Prevalent Inc.'s BusinessWire press release (May 8, 2024) and the study itself, hosted on prevalent.net and mitratech.com. The survey covered heads of information security, risk management, procurement, and IT executives whose supply chains collectively represent half a million vendors. The 61% figure is stated directly, representing a 49% year-over-year increase. (src: https://www.businesswire.com/news/home/20240508397249/en/Third-Party-Data-Breaches-Rose-49-in-2023-Reaching-Record-Level-New-Prevalent-Study-Finds)
- [contested] Enterprise SaaS sales cycle for >$100K ACV deals averages 90-180+ days (Optifai, 939-company benchmark dataset) -- The Optifai page does exist and does cite 90-180+ days for >$100K ACV, with the dataset labeled 'Optifai Pipeline Study (2026, N=939 B2B SaaS companies)'. However, the page discloses no methodology, sampling approach, or how the 939 companies were selected or whether the data is proprietary or aggregated. The source is a vendor blog with no independent verification. The range itself (90-180+ days) is consistent with other B2B SaaS benchmarks, but the specific sourcing is opaque. (src: https://optif.ai/learn/questions/sales-cycle-length-benchmark/)
- [contested] 12% of SaaS deals collapse specifically in the proposal and negotiation stage, which includes procurement review (Development Corporate, 2025) -- The Development Corporate article exists and does state 12% of SaaS deals are lost at the proposal/negotiation stage. However, a direct fetch of the page reveals no underlying primary dataset, named study, or disclosed methodology. The figure traces internally to another Development Corporate blog post from August 2025. No external primary source (Gartner, SiriusDecisions, etc.) is cited. The claim is plausible but unverifiable as stated. (src: https://developmentcorporate.com/saas/saas-deals-proposal-negotiation-losses/)
- [contested] YC Demo Day minimum revenue target cited as $800K ARR in 12 weeks (Licens.io citing Zack Korman; no primary YC document verified) -- The Licens.io article attributes the $800K ARR figure to a 'Y Combinator partner's post' as discussed in a video by Zack Korman (Embroidery co-founder). No direct URL, author name, or timestamp for the original YC partner post is provided. A separate search found Garry Tan posts referencing $1M+ median ARR and $23K MRR examples, but not an explicit $800K minimum bar. The claim is secondhand via a commentary video, not traceable to a primary YC document. The caveat in the original claim ('no primary YC document verified') is accurate. (src: https://licens.io/blog/yc-incentive-structure-vendor-risk/)
- [contested] '73% of enterprise sales for startups fail during vendor assessment' -- circulates widely in practitioner content (Iterators, HST, others) but NO primary study has been located; treat as unconfirmed industry claim -- Confirmed unverifiable. The statistic appears in the Iterators blog (April 2026) with the phrase 'Research shows' but no citation. No academic paper, Gartner report, Forrester report, IDC study, or named primary dataset was found containing this figure. The claim's own characterization as 'no primary study has been located' is accurate. The Whistic 2022 Vendor Security Report offers related (but different) data: 45% of deals pushed back and 34% lost due to slow security review response, which does not sum to 73%. (src: https://www.iteratorshq.com/blog/enterprise-sales-for-startups-security-legal-and-procurement-requirements/)
- [confirmed] Mercor breach: 4 terabytes of data stolen, 40,000+ contractors affected, 5 class action lawsuits in one week (Licens.io, documented case) -- All three sub-claims are corroborated. The 4TB figure (comprising ~939 GB source code, 211 GB user database, ~3 TB video recordings) is confirmed by TechCrunch, Staffing Industry Analysts, and the Licens.io article. The 40,000+ contractors figure appears in the class action filings and is corroborated by byteiota.com, claimdepot.com, and strikegraph.com. Five lawsuits in one week (April 1-7, 2026) are individually named in the Licens.io article (Gill, Deboni, Esson, a fourth multi-defendant suit, and a Texas federal filing). The 'five lawsuits in one week' claim is specifically confirmed by the licens.io article headline and body. (src: https://licens.io/blog/mercor-litellm-class-action-lawsuits/)
- [confirmed] Delve: 494 fabricated SOC 2 reports, $32 million raised (Licens.io, documented case) -- Both figures are corroborated across multiple independent sources. The 494 reports figure comes from a leaked Google Spreadsheet analyzed by the anonymous DeepDelver group; 493 of 494 (99.8%) showed identical boilerplate. The $32 million Series A (led by Insight Partners at a $300M valuation) is confirmed by TechCrunch, byteiota.com, captaincompliance.com, rafter.so, and the licens.io article. Minor nuance: the body of some articles specifies that 493 of the 494 reports were problematic while the headline rounds to '494 fake reports,' but the discrepancy is immaterial to the claim. (src: https://byteiota.com/delve-compliance-fraud-32m-startup-faked-494-soc-2-audits/)

### 34. The Budget Test (Put It in Next Quarter's Budget)

- **Category:** Organizational / B2B
- **Stage:** org-buyer
- **Definition:** The budget test is a B2B customer-discovery technique in which a founder or salesperson asks a prospect to take a concrete organizational action -- specifically, to add the proposed solution as a named line item in an upcoming budget cycle or procurement plan -- rather than simply expressing enthusiasm. The act of voluntarily initiating internal budget approval is treated as a commitment signal that separates genuine pain with organizational urgency from polite interest. It surfaces whether the prospect controls or can access a budget owner, what the approval chain looks like, and whether there is a forcing function (deadline, compliance, cost pressure) that makes the problem urgent enough to displace existing spending.
- **Validates:** Does this organization consider this problem urgent enough to allocate real money in the next planning cycle, and does our contact have the authority and internal will to make that happen?

**Protocol**
1. Qualify problem severity first: confirm the prospect has a live, costly problem before raising the budget question. Use past-behavior questions (The Mom Test style): 'What have you already paid for or tried to solve this?' 'How much is this costing you per quarter?'
2. Ask where the money comes from: 'If you decided to solve this today, whose budget would it come from?' Map the economic buyer, department, and any shared-budget complexity before proceeding.
3. Propose the forward action directly: 'Would you be willing to put this as a line item in your Q[N+1] budget so we can move forward once it opens?' Observe the response -- hesitation, deflection, or immediate redirection reveal organizational friction.
4. Test against competing priorities: 'What else is competing for that budget right now?' If the prospect cannot name competitors for the same funds, they likely have not run the internal conversation yet.
5. Probe for a champion: 'Would you need to loop in Finance, IT, or your manager to get this approved?' Map who else must say yes. A prospect who refuses to name stakeholders is not a real buyer.
6. Ask for a non-financial advance commitment if budget is genuinely locked: an introduction to the budget owner, an LOI (letter of intent), or a written pilot scope with agreed evaluation criteria (time commitment + stakeholder involvement).
7. Set a concrete checkpoint: 'When does Q[N+1] budget planning start? Can we schedule a call the week before so I can give you the vendor-summary your finance team will need?' Convert budget-cycle intent into a calendared action.
8. Disqualify cleanly if no path exists: 'It sounds like budget is truly frozen until [date]. Does it make sense to reconnect then, or is there another path to fund this?' A prospect who cannot answer this question has not internalized the problem as urgent.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- (no hard-numbered benchmark sourced)

**Origin and evidence:** The budget test as a discrete named method is not attributed to a single originator; it is a practitioner synthesis drawing on three documented streams. First, Steve Blank's Customer Development framework (The Four Steps to the Epiphany, 2005) defined the 'earlyvangelist' as an enterprise customer who specifically 'has or can acquire budget to solve the problem' -- budget possession is one of five required qualifying criteria, making budget-status a formal screen, not an afterthought. Second, Rob Fitzpatrick's The Mom Test (2013) codified 'where does the money come from?' as a required B2B discovery question and classified financial commitments (letters of intent, pre-orders, deposits) as the strongest signal tier above time and reputation commitments. Third, Etienne Garbugli's Lean B2B (2014, 2nd ed. 2022) explicitly addressed the 'committed budget' objection ('the company would love to buy, but unfortunately budgets are all committed for the year') and built a Budget Displacement Grid scoring problems by existing vs. to-be-created budget availability. The method has no peer-reviewed RCT evidence; its authority rests on practitioner consensus from these canonical frameworks and documented case studies (Zip, Ramp, Gusto founders all ran 30-100+ prospect conversations before building, with budget-qualification as part of discovery). Evidence strength: practitioner-strong, academically weak.

**Canonical sources:** Steve Blank, The Four Steps to the Epiphany (2005) -- earlyvangelist criteria including 'has budget to solve the problem'; Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) -- commitment hierarchy (financial > reputation > time) and 'where does the money come from?' as a required B2B question; Etienne Garbugli, Lean B2B: Build Products Businesses Want (2014, 2nd ed. 2022) -- Budget Displacement Grid, committed-budget scoring, and 'how B2B startups deal with committed budgets'; IBM BANT framework (1960s, widely documented) -- Budget as the first qualifying dimension in enterprise sales; Lenny Rachitsky, 'How to validate your B2B startup idea' (Lenny's Newsletter) -- 30 customer interviews as median pre-build threshold; Zip founders (75 interviews), Ramp founders (100+ conversations) as documented cases

**Failure modes**
- The prospect agrees to budget the solution but the organization never actually initiates the process -- a 'soft yes' that costs no social capital and therefore signals nothing.
- Budget-cycle language is used as a polite deferral ('come back next quarter') that founders mistake for genuine intent; Garbugli explicitly names this as a classic false excuse.
- The contact who agrees to budget it has no real authority; they are an influencer or end-user, not the economic buyer, so their commitment cannot survive the actual approval process.
- Free or heavily discounted pilots are agreed to as a substitute for budget commitment, creating the illusion of organizational buy-in without price validation; Garbugli notes that free pilots are often never activated because 'budget size dictates attention'.
- The team validates only that one champion wants to budget it, missing the 6-10 person buying committee (security, legal, finance, IT) whose objections will kill the deal later.
- Founders interpret 'we would budget for this' as proof of product-market fit when it is only proof of problem-market fit -- whether the specific solution is the one they will pay for remains unvalidated.

**False-positive mechanism:** The core false-positive mechanism is social-commitment cost asymmetry: saying 'I'll put it in the budget' costs the prospect almost nothing -- no money changes hands, no approval is triggered, no internal meeting is required -- while the founder walks away with strong conviction. The prospect's incentive is to avoid conflict and maintain optionality; budget-cycle language is the lowest-friction way to defer without refusing. This is a variant of the same dynamic Rob Fitzpatrick describes across all commitment signals: verbal commitments without skin in the game ('I would definitely buy that,' 'let me know when it launches') are reliably false positives. The budget-test version is particularly insidious in B2B because the organizational framing makes the commitment sound structural and binding when it is in fact entirely discretionary until the prospect actually calls a Finance meeting or creates a PO line.

**Examples**
- Zip (YC-backed procurement startup): Founders spoke with 75 potential users -- CFOs, heads of procurement, and heads of finance -- over two to three weeks before committing to the idea, accumulating approximately 110 pages of notes. A central focus of those conversations was understanding where procurement budgets lived, who controlled them, and whether a new vendor could enter an existing budget line. The budget-mapping conversations directly shaped the product's positioning toward existing spend rather than requiring new budget creation.
- Ramp (corporate card and spend management): Founders talked to 100+ finance teams before building, specifically probing current spending patterns, existing budget ownership in the finance function, and whether teams would displace existing tools (existing committed budgets) rather than create a new category. This 'budget displacement' approach -- targeting problems with money already flowing to inferior solutions -- is the canonical application of the budget-test logic.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is when the prospect (a) names the specific budget owner and department without being prompted, (b) proactively offers to schedule a meeting with Finance or their manager before being asked, (c) asks for vendor-qualification materials (security questionnaire, SOC 2, pricing sheet) needed to begin the approval process, or (d) provides a letter of intent or agrees to a paid pilot with a named stakeholder from Finance or IT involved. An even stronger signal is when a prospect pulls from discretionary or unplanned budget to pay immediately rather than waiting for the next cycle -- Garbugli notes that when a problem truly matters, companies find money outside the normal cycle.
- Weak signal: A weak result is any variant of 'I'll bring it up in the next planning cycle' without a named budget owner, a date, or a next meeting scheduled. Budget-cycle deferral without a calendar checkpoint is statistically indistinguishable from a polite no. Similarly weak: the prospect has enthusiasm but cannot name who controls the relevant budget, or says 'we don't really have a budget for this yet' without volunteering to create one -- Garbugli's Budget Displacement Grid codes problems that require new budget creation as significantly higher friction than those displacing existing spend.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [contested] Steve Blank's earlyvangelist framework specifies 'has the budget to solve the problem' as one of five required qualifying criteria -- all five must be present for a prospect to count as a validated early customer (The Four Steps to the Epiphany, 2005) -- The five earlyvangelist criteria are well-documented across multiple secondary sources and trace back to The Four Steps to the Epiphany. The fifth criterion is budget-related, but the exact wording varies: one secondary source (HandWiki, citing Salim Virani) reads 'has or can acquire a budget,' not strictly 'has the budget.' More importantly, IBM's own BANT guidance and some Blank summaries note that meeting three of four (or some subset) can suffice; the 'all five must be present' requirement is asserted by many paraphrases but is not definitively stated in indexed secondary sources. The book itself was not directly accessible for confirmation of the strict all-or-nothing rule. (src: https://handwiki.org/wiki/Social:Earlyvangelist; https://salimvirani.com/earlyvangelist/; https://scottburleson.substack.com/p/book-summary-the-four-steps-to-the)
- [contested] Lenny Rachitsky documents a median of 30 customer conversations before committing to build as the threshold across multiple successful B2B founders (Stytch ~30, Gusto ~30, Amplitude ~30) -- The median-of-30 figure and the Stytch and Gusto examples at approximately 30 conversations are confirmed directly from Lenny's Newsletter article. However, the Amplitude example is mischaracterized: Spenser Skates said he talked to 30 companies but explicitly reflected that this was insufficient and he 'should have aimed higher, like 50 companies in a month.' Grouping Amplitude as a positive ~30-conversation validation example misrepresents the source -- Skates used his 30-conversation experience as a cautionary note, not a success benchmark. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-to-validate-your-b2b-startup)
- [confirmed] Zip founders conducted 75 interviews over 2-3 weeks accumulating ~110 pages of notes before committing (sourced from Lenny's Newsletter) -- The Lenny's Newsletter article was fetched directly and contains a quote attributable to Zip's founders: 'We did a lot of interviews with CFOs, heads of procurement, heads of finance (I think the exact number is around 75)' with '110 pages of notes or something in those two or three-ish weeks.' The figures match the claim. The source attribution is accurate. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-to-validate-your-b2b-startup)
- [confirmed] Ramp founders talked to 100+ finance teams before building (sourced from Lenny's Newsletter) -- The Lenny's Newsletter article was fetched directly and contains the quote: 'Before we shipped a single card, we talked to over 100 finance and founder teams.' The claim and source attribution are accurate, though it is 'finance and founder teams,' not finance teams only. (src: https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-to-validate-your-b2b-startup)
- [refuted] Etienne Garbugli (Lean B2B) states that an 80% or higher commitment rate from qualified early adopters proves validated demand -- though this figure refers to the broader committed-beta criterion, not the budget test specifically -- No indexed content from Lean B2B or from Garbugli himself surfaces this 80% figure. The leanfoundry.com page that contains this exact 80% language attributes it to Ash Maurya's 90-day framework, not Garbugli. A web fetch of that page confirmed the claim originates from Ash Maurya's newsletter guidance, not from Lean B2B. The attribution to Garbugli appears to be a misattribution. (src: https://www.leanfoundry.com/newsletter/jul-31-2025 (Ash Maurya, not Garbugli); no corroborating Garbugli/Lean B2B source found)
- [refuted] IBM's BANT framework (1960s) places Budget as the first of four qualification dimensions, a sequence replicated across modern frameworks including MEDDIC and CHAMP -- IBM BANT originating in the 1960s with Budget as the first criterion is confirmed. However, the claim that the Budget-first sequence was 'replicated' in MEDDIC and CHAMP is factually wrong. MEDDIC starts with Metrics (not Budget) and was developed at PTC in the 1990s. CHAMP deliberately inverts the BANT order, starting with Challenges precisely to avoid leading with budget. Both frameworks are widely described as explicit corrections to BANT's budget-first ordering, not replications of it. (src: https://meddicc.com/resources/meddicc-versus-other-qualification-frameworks-like-bant; https://spotio.com/blog/b2b-sales-qualification-frameworks/; https://salesmotion.io/blog/lead-qualification-framework-guide)

**Citations**
- Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test -- momtestbook.com: https://www.momtestbook.com/
- Etienne Garbugli, How B2B Startups Deal with Committed Budgets (Goodreads/Lean B2B blog): https://www.goodreads.com/author_blog_posts/7560352-how-b2b-startups-deal-with-committed-budgets
- Etienne Garbugli, How to Score Customer Problems by Budget Availability (leanb2bbook.com): https://leanb2bbook.com/blog/how-to-score-customer-problems-by-budget-availability/

### 35. The Mom Test: Past Behavior Over Future Intent Interviewing Discipline

- **Category:** Cross-cutting methodology
- **Stage:** problem
- **Definition:** The Mom Test is a customer interviewing discipline, codified by Rob Fitzpatrick, that structures every question around a prospect's actual past behavior rather than their stated future intentions or opinions about a hypothetical product. The core insight is that people are socially wired to be polite and optimistic, so any question that exposes your idea or invites a forward-looking judgment will produce encouraging but unreliable answers -- what Fitzpatrick calls "the world's most deadly inaccuracy: I would definitely buy that." Valid learning comes only from specific, verifiable facts the customer has already lived: what they did, how much time or money they spent, what workarounds they built, and what commitments (time, reputation, money) they are willing to make today.
- **Validates:** Answers the question: does a real, painful, frequently-occurring problem exist for a defined customer segment, and is evidence of that problem visible in how they already spend time or money? It directly invalidates the most dangerous assumption in early-stage startups -- that enthusiasm for an idea is a proxy for willingness to pay or change behavior.

**Protocol**
1. Define the hypothesis and customer segment in writing before any conversation. Specify exactly what past behavior or current workflow you need to observe -- not what you want them to say about your idea.
2. Open by asking about the customer's life and workflow, never by pitching or revealing your solution. Use openers such as: 'Walk me through the last time this came up' or 'How are you handling X today?'
3. Ask exclusively about the past and the specific. Good probes: 'When did this last happen?' / 'How much time did that take?' / 'What did you try before that?' / 'How much did that cost you?' / 'Why did that not work?' Bad probes: 'Would you ever...?' / 'Do you think you might...?' / 'What would you want it to do?'
4. Listen for and record only three categories of valid signal: (a) facts about current behavior including tools used, money spent, time lost, and workarounds built; (b) emotional intensity -- frustration, resignation, elaborate hacks signal real pain; (c) concrete commitments the customer offers unprompted (introductions, letters of intent, advance payment, time for a follow-up).
5. Treat these as noise and explicitly exclude from your record: compliments ('This is such a great idea'), generic claims ('I always have this problem'), future-tense promises ('I would definitely use that'), and hypothetical maybes ('I might switch if...'). Fitzpatrick labels these three categories -- compliments, fluff, and wishlists -- as the three types of bad data.
6. After each interview write a one-page summary of only the facts and commitments. Tally: how often the problem occurred, what the customer spent to address it, and whether they volunteered any commitment. Discard compliments from the tally.
7. Continue until thematic saturation: when three consecutive interviews surface no new facts, behaviors, or unmet needs. Cindy Alvarez (Lean Customer Development) places the first pattern-recognition threshold at approximately 10 interviews; Rob Fitzpatrick notes that contradictory feedback across 10 interviews is a signal to narrow the customer segment rather than average the data.
8. Advance only when you have observed concrete commitments or behavioral evidence from multiple respondents. A strong outcome requires facts, commitments, or advancement -- never compliments alone -- to justify proceeding to solution design or building.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Minimum interviews before drawing conclusions about a customer segment's core problem: 20-30 structured discovery interviews per Rob Fitzpatrick; 5-20 before first build (src: Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013), as cited by Koji.so Mom Test summary 2026)
- Interview count at which patterns begin to emerge (thematic saturation onset): 5-8 interviews to see first patterns; 10 interviews for meaningful pattern recognition (src: Cindy Alvarez, Lean Customer Development (O'Reilly, 2014), via Summaries.com and Bookey PDF)
- Steve Blank's recommended conversations across discovery and validation phases: 50-100 conversations; Blank's Stanford course requires 50+ stakeholder interviews (src: Steve Blank, The Four Steps to the Epiphany (2005) and The Startup Owner's Manual (2012), corroborated by yourstory.com and kromatic.com)
- Signal to stop interviewing: Stop when three consecutive interviews produce no new surprises (qualitative saturation) (src: Cindy Alvarez, Lean Customer Development, via Summaries.com)
- Sean Ellis PMF threshold (adjacent demand-stage benchmark using behavioral survey): >=40% of active users say they would be 'very disappointed' without the product, derived from benchmarking nearly 100 startups (src: Sean Ellis, surveyed ~100 startups; published via First Round Review / Rahul Vohra 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit' (2019))
- Superhuman baseline PMF score before segmentation: 22% 'very disappointed' on Sean Ellis survey across full user base (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review, 2019 (review.firstround.com))
- Superhuman PMF score improvement over one year using targeted discovery: 33% to 58% 'very disappointed' in one year after segmentation and iteration (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review, 2019 (review.firstround.com))
- Developer time saved per hour of customer development interviews: 5-10x to 20x hours of wasted development time avoided per interview hour (src: Cindy Alvarez, Lean Customer Development, via Summaries.com)
- Narrow-segment signal: when to narrow rather than average: If 10 customers give contradictory feedback, the customer segment is too broad (src: Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test, via mtlynch.io book report)

**Origin and evidence:** Rob Fitzpatrick originated and popularized this specific framing in The Mom Test (self-published 2013, widely adopted by 2014). The underlying behavioral economics principle -- that stated intentions are poor predictors of actual behavior -- is rooted in decades of social psychology and is consistent with work by Daniel Kahneman (planning fallacy, optimism bias). Fitzpatrick's contribution was operationalizing the principle into a practitioner-facing interview protocol. The methodology was built on top of Steve Blank's Customer Development framework (The Four Steps to the Epiphany, 2005) and Eric Ries' Lean Startup (2011), which established 'get out of the building' and validated learning as canonical startup practice. The evidence base is practitioner-empirical rather than randomized-controlled: Fitzpatrick drew on his own experience founding multiple startups and advising hundreds of founders, and the framework has been widely adopted by Y Combinator, HBS, and Lean Startup practitioners. No peer-reviewed RCT validates the specific interview count thresholds; those figures are expert heuristics.

**Canonical sources:** Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test: How to Talk to Customers and Learn If Your Business Is a Good Idea When Everyone Is Lying to You (2013); Steve Blank, The Four Steps to the Epiphany (2005) and The Startup Owner's Manual with Bob Dorf (2012); Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011); Cindy Alvarez, Lean Customer Development: Building Products Your Customers Will Buy (O'Reilly, 2014); Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit', First Round Review (2019); Sean Ellis, GoPMF / early blog posts establishing the 40% 'very disappointed' benchmark; Julia Austin, HBS Startup Guide on customer interviewing techniques (startupguide.hbs.edu)

**Failure modes**
- Conducting interviews after pitching the idea: once the founder reveals their concept, the respondent shifts into encouragement mode, making all subsequent answers unreliable regardless of question quality.
- Selecting biased interview subjects: friends, family, warm introductions who feel obligated to support the founder; or existing paying customers who represent survivorship bias and will not surface the problem's non-prevalence.
- Mistaking enthusiasm for evidence: high-energy responses such as 'I love this' or 'This would be so useful' feel like validation but cost the respondent nothing and predict nothing. Fitzpatrick calls these 'the fool's gold of customer learning.'
- Asking hypothetical or future-tense questions: 'Would you use this?', 'Would you pay for X?', 'Could you see yourself switching?' all invite optimistic fiction rather than behavioral fact.
- Generic probes that produce generic answers: 'Do you usually have this problem?' generates 'I always do' -- an unverifiable and unreliable generalization with no diagnostic value.
- Confirmation bias in synthesis: founders subconsciously weight the responses that validate their hypothesis. Without a written, commitment-tallied record from each interview, pattern-matching drifts toward what the founder wanted to hear.
- Interviewing too narrow a slice and over-generalizing: speaking only to enthusiasts within a broader segment produces a false signal that the whole segment has the problem at the same intensity.
- Stopping too early or too late: stopping at 3-5 interviews risks finding patterns in noise; continuing past saturation without narrowing the segment wastes time and can cause a founder to average out contradictions rather than diagnose segment heterogeneity.

**False-positive mechanism:** The primary false positive mechanism is social desirability bias activated by idea exposure. When a founder reveals or implies their solution during an interview, respondents suppress honest skepticism to avoid hurting feelings, and instead offer compliments ('Wow, this is really needed'), hypothetical affirmations ('I would totally use this'), and feature wishlists -- all of which are cheap to provide and carry no commitment cost. The founder then exits the interview having heard mostly positive signals, records them as validation, and advances to building. The mechanism is self-reinforcing: the more the founder believes in the idea, the more they unconsciously frame questions to confirm it, and the more respondents pick up on that and comply. A secondary false positive mechanism is self-report optimism bias: even when respondents are trying to be honest, they describe an idealized version of their future self ('I would definitely track my spending more carefully with a good app') rather than their actual past self, who did not. The test's core discipline -- asking only about documented past behavior -- is designed specifically to break this mechanism, because past behavior cannot be revised in the telling the way future intent can.

**Examples**
- Airbnb (early customer discovery): The Airbnb founders conducted in-person, life-focused conversations with early hosts, asking about their actual experiences renting space and what they had already tried, rather than pitching the Airbnb concept. This approach -- talking about users' lives rather than the product, and seeking specific past behaviors -- surfaced genuine friction points in the existing short-term rental workflow and helped them understand what hosts had already paid for and tolerated. Their discovery method is frequently cited by Y Combinator as a canonical example of Mom Test-aligned interviewing.
- Superhuman (PMF measurement using behavioral survey): Rahul Vohra applied the Sean Ellis 'very disappointed' survey -- a behavioral-intent measure -- to approximately 100-200 active users (defined as those who had used the product at least twice in the prior two weeks, i.e., demonstrated past behavior). The initial score was 22%, well below the 40% threshold. Rather than averaging all users, Vohra segmented by role to identify the High-Expectation Customer, found the score jumped to the high 30s within that cohort, and then drove the full-base score from 33% to 58% over one year by focusing product iteration exclusively on that segment. This is a downstream application of past-behavior grounding: the survey eligibility criterion (recency of actual use) ensured responses reflected real experience, not hypothetical enthusiasm.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result looks like: the respondent spontaneously describes a specific past incident with named dates, dollar amounts, or tools ('Last Tuesday I spent four hours re-building that report because X does not export correctly'); they have already spent money or significant time on a workaround; they describe having searched for solutions and found them wanting; and they volunteer a concrete commitment unprompted -- an introduction, a pre-order, or a scheduled follow-up with decision-making authority. Across 10-15 interviews with a well-defined segment, if 60-80% of respondents independently surface the same behavioral pattern without being led, that is strong evidence the problem is real, frequent, and motivating enough for customers to have already taken action on it.
- Weak signal: A weak result looks like: the interview is dominated by compliments and hypothetical affirmations with no behavioral specifics; respondents struggle to recall a recent instance of the problem or say they would handle it 'eventually'; no one has sought, built, or paid for a workaround; no commitments are offered; and accounts across interviews are inconsistent in problem frequency or severity. If after 10-15 interviews in a clearly-bounded segment fewer than a third of respondents can describe a concrete past instance and none have taken prior action, the problem likely does not create enough pain to drive purchase behavior, or the segment is wrong. Contradictory feedback across 10 interviews, per Fitzpatrick, is a reliable signal that the target segment is too heterogeneous and needs to be narrowed before further interviews are meaningful.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [contested] Rob Fitzpatrick recommends 20-30 structured discovery interviews before drawing conclusions, and 5-20 before first build -- No independent source corroborates the specific figures of 20-30 or 5-20. What Fitzpatrick actually writes is a qualitative signal: 'keep meeting with customers until you stop learning new things,' with 'sometimes three to five meetings are enough' offered as an illustrative minimum. The 20-30 and 5-20 numbers appear to be secondary-source elaborations not traceable to a direct Fitzpatrick quote in The Mom Test (2013). (src: https://mtlynch.io/book-reports/the-mom-test/ ; https://www.koji.so/blog/mom-test-customer-interviews-2026)
- [confirmed] Cindy Alvarez states that for each hour of customer development interviews, businesses save 5-10 to 20 hours of wasted development time -- Multiple independent summaries of Lean Customer Development (O'Reilly, 2014) cite this figure. The Summaries.com summary and leanb2bbook.com both reproduce the claim as '5 to 10 to 20 hours of wasted development time saved per interview hour.' The exact range in the book is slightly broader than the '5-10' variant in the claim, but the core assertion is corroborated. (src: https://summaries.com/blog/lean-customer-development ; https://leanb2bbook.com/blog/customer-development-interviews/)
- [contested] Cindy Alvarez places the first pattern-recognition threshold at approximately 10 interviews, with initial patterns visible after 5-8 -- The ~10 interview threshold for pattern recognition is corroborated across multiple summaries of Lean Customer Development. However, no source independently confirms the 5-8 sub-threshold for 'initial patterns.' That specific number does not appear in any indexed summary or direct Alvarez quote; it may be paraphrased from Nielsen's usability research or from a non-indexed passage. Only the ~10 figure is firmly attributable. (src: https://summaries.com/blog/lean-customer-development ; https://cdn.bookey.app/files/pdf/book/en/lean-customer-development.pdf)
- [contested] Steve Blank's Stanford course requires 50+ stakeholder interviews; Blank recommends 50-100 conversations across discovery and validation phases -- The 50+ stakeholder interview requirement for Stanford's Lean LaunchPad / Hacking for Defense courses is corroborated by multiple sources referencing team interview counts in the hundreds across 8-team cohorts, and secondary sources citing The Startup Owner's Manual (2012) as recommending at least 50 potential customers in discovery and 60 in validation. However, the specific '50-100 conversations across discovery and validation phases' framing was not found as a direct Blank quote in either book, and the '100' upper bound lacks a traceable primary source citation. (src: https://innovationobserver.substack.com/p/principles-of-a-stakeholder-discovery ; https://learn.marsdd.com/article/blanks-customer-discovery-method-part-4-the-customer-development-model-in-value-proposition/)
- [confirmed] Sean Ellis derived the 40% 'very disappointed' PMF threshold by benchmarking nearly 100 startups -- Confirmed by Ellis's own published statement: 'I defined it after comparing results across nearly 100 startups.' Multiple independent sources reproduce this quote from his 2010 blog post. Ellis himself acknowledged the threshold is 'a bit arbitrary' but grounded it in this empirical benchmarking exercise. (src: https://www.fitsignal.com/blog/sean-ellis-40-percent-test ; https://surveysparrow.com/blog/product-market-fit-survey/)
- [confirmed] Superhuman's initial 'very disappointed' score was 22% across all users, below the 40% threshold -- Directly confirmed by the First Round Review article authored by Rahul Vohra (2019): 'When we started this journey in the summer of 2017, our product-market fit score was 22%.' This is unambiguous and consistently reproduced across multiple independent summaries of the article. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [contested] Superhuman moved their PMF score from 33% to 58% in one year through segmentation and targeted discovery -- The 33% to 58% trajectory is confirmed by the First Round Review article. However, the timeframe is materially mischaracterized: the article states the 33%-to-58% improvement occurred 'within just three quarters,' not 'one year.' The full journey from 22% to 58% also took roughly three quarters. Calling it 'one year' overstates the timeframe. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/ ; https://saasclub.io/podcast/rahul-vohra-superhuman-342/)
- [contested] Slack showed 51% 'very disappointed' responses in a 2015 open survey of 731 users conducted by Hiten Shah, when the product had approximately 500,000 paying users -- The 51% figure, the 731 respondents, and the 2015 date are all confirmed by Hiten Shah's own blog post (published August 17, 2015). However, the '500,000 paying users' figure is inaccurate. At the time of the survey (April 2015), Slack had approximately 750,000 DAU and roughly 200,000 paid seats. The 500,000 DAU milestone was reached in February 2015 when paid seats were ~135,000. No point in 2015 had both 500,000 users and that being paying users; the 'approximately 500,000 paying' figure conflates DAU with paying subscribers. (src: https://hitenism.com/slack-product-market-fit-survey/ ; https://techcrunch.com/2016/10/20/slunk/)
- [confirmed] Fitzpatrick's heuristic: if 10 customers give contradictory feedback, the customer segment is too broad -- Directly corroborated by a verbatim passage from The Mom Test reproduced in reader notes: 'If you've run more than 10 conversations and are still getting results that are all over the map, then it's possible that your customer segment is too vague.' Multiple independent summaries reproduce this heuristic consistently. (src: https://mtlynch.io/book-reports/the-mom-test/ ; https://www.goodreads.com/work/quotes/26235307)
- [contested] Thematic saturation heuristic: stop interviewing when three consecutive interviews produce no new surprises -- Alvarez's actual documented stopping signal is qualitative: 'Stop interviewing when you stop hearing things that surprise you.' The specific operationalization of 'three consecutive interviews' was not found in any indexed primary Alvarez source or book summary. The 'three consecutive interviews with no new codes' formulation does appear in qualitative research methodology literature (e.g., Thirdside, 2024) and is a general saturation heuristic, but cannot be traced to Alvarez or The Mom Test as claimed. It is likely a secondary-source elaboration rather than a direct Alvarez attribution. (src: https://summaries.com/blog/lean-customer-development ; https://thirdside.com/science-of-insight-emergence/)

**Citations**
