### 36. Stated vs Revealed Preference: Why Behavior Outranks Opinion

- **Category:** Cross-cutting methodology
- **Stage:** Cross-cutting: applies at problem discovery (do people act on this pain?), solution validation (will they commit before the product is built?), demand (pre-orders, deposits), retention (do they return without prompting?), and channel (do they refer without incentive?). Most critical at the problem and solution stages, where false positives cause the largest downstream waste.
- **Definition:** Stated preference is what a person says they will do, choose, or pay when asked hypothetically. Revealed preference is inferred from actual choices made under real constraints -- what they demonstrably spent, committed time to, or forwent an alternative for. The methodological principle for startup validation is that stated preference systematically overstates true demand due to hypothetical bias, social desirability, and the absence of stakes, while revealed preference -- operationalized as behavioral commitments of money, time, or reputation -- provides a valid and independently testable signal of demand. The technique applies at every validation stage: it is not a single test but a cross-cutting lens for interpreting any evidence gathered.
- **Validates:** The core question: does genuine demand exist for this product at this price from this customer segment, as evidenced by behavior, not by opinion? The method exposes whether apparent validation (compliments, survey interest, stated intent to buy) reflects true willingness to pay or merely social courtesy and hypothetical optimism.

**Protocol**
1. Define the behavioral proxy before any conversation or test. Ask: what is the smallest action a genuinely interested person would take that costs them something -- time, money, or reputation? Write this down so it is not decided retroactively after warm signals come in.
2. Remove the stakes-free escape route. Design interviews and experiments so a positive response requires a commitment: a pre-order, a signed letter of intent, an introduction to a decision-maker, or a calendar invite accepted on the spot. Per Fitzpatrick (The Mom Test), treat any response that costs the respondent nothing -- a compliment, a 'sounds interesting,' a 'let me know when it is ready' -- as a zero-evidence event.
3. Ask exclusively about past behavior, not hypothetical future behavior. Anchor on specific past episodes ('Walk me through the last time this happened and what you did') rather than future intent ('Would you use a product that...'). Past behavior is revealed preference; future intent is stated preference.
4. Probe for workarounds already in use. If the problem is real, people have already built or bought imperfect substitutes. Document what they currently pay, how much time they spend, and what they tolerate. Existing spend is the clearest behavioral signal that a budget line exists.
5. Request a commitment that advances the relationship one stage. Fitzpatrick's three currencies in ascending weight: time (trial, follow-up with a date), reputation (intro to their boss or peer, public testimonial), cash (deposit, pre-order, paid pilot). The heavier the currency accepted, the stronger the signal.
6. Triangulate with behavioral instrumentation. For digital products: measure activation rate, D1/D7/D30 retention, and organic referral -- not NPS or satisfaction scores alone. For physical or B2B products: measure pilot-to-contract conversion rate and time-to-signature rather than meeting enthusiasm.
7. Apply the 'skin in the game' caliper (Savoia, The Right It). Score evidence: opinions and verbal agreement = 0 points; email address submitted for a wait-list = low; phone number given = medium; deposit or purchase = high. Do not aggregate weak evidence into a false conviction.
8. Separate the survey signal from the retention signal. The Sean Ellis 'very disappointed' PMF survey is stated preference and useful as a leading indicator, but must be cross-checked against behavioral retention (cohort curves) before treating it as confirmation of product-market fit. A score above 40% with flat retention is a contradiction that requires explanation, not celebration.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Hypothetical WTP overestimation vs actual WTP -- consumer goods (meta-analysis of stated preference studies): Average overestimation of 21% above actual willingness to pay (src: University of Groningen meta-analysis of hypothetical bias in consumer goods WTP studies, published in Journal of Economic Psychology (cited in ResearchGate publication DOI search 333662583))
- Hypothetical WTP overestimation vs actual WTP -- health sector (systematic review and meta-analysis): Hypothetical WTP on average 3.2 times greater than actual WTP (range 0.7x to 11.8x) (src: Systematic review meta-analysis, ScienceDirect, doi:10.1016/j.socscimed.2019.02.019 (criterion validity of WTP methods))
- Hypothetical WTP overestimation vs actual WTP -- median ratio across environmental and goods studies: Median ratio of hypothetical to actual value = 1.35 across 83 observations (28 stated preference studies) (src: Murphy et al. (2005), meta-analysis of hypothetical bias, cited in Wiley Journal of Economic Surveys (Loomis 2011 review))
- Revealed preference model predictive validity vs stated preference -- supermarket sales: Revealed preference model explained 49% of variance in actual sales; best stated preference model explained 32% -- revealed preference gave 1.5x better predictions of real-world outcomes (src: DecTech research study, 2,700 UK consumers, 600 stores over 52 weeks, three product categories (milk, eggs, canned tuna))
- Mere measurement effect -- stated purchase intent inflating actual purchase probability: Being asked about purchase intent made people up to 25% more likely to actually buy the product (the act of asking inflates downstream stated preference) (src: CloudArmy Knowledge Center, citing behavioral economics literature on the mere measurement effect)
- Sean Ellis PMF 'very disappointed' threshold: Companies above 40% 'very disappointed' score (among qualified recent users) consistently demonstrate scalable organic growth; companies below 40% struggle to make paid acquisition compound (src: Sean Ellis, Startup Pyramid essay (2009), validated across approximately 100 startups including Dropbox, Eventbrite, LogMeIn, Lookout)
- Superhuman PMF score progression using Ellis survey: Started at 22% (April 2017), rose to 33% after segmentation, reached 58% three quarters later after roadmap changes (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review essay 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit' (2018))

**Origin and evidence:** The theoretical foundation is Paul Samuelson's 1938 paper 'A note on the pure theory of consumers' behaviour' (Economica), which introduced revealed preference as an alternative to unobservable utility theory. The startup-validation operationalization was independently popularized by three practitioners: (1) Rob Fitzpatrick in The Mom Test (2013), who codified the 'commitment and advancement' framework and named the failure mode of treating compliments as validation; (2) Eric Ries in The Lean Startup (2011), who formalized 'validated learning' and the build-measure-learn loop as the antidote to building on stated interest; (3) Alberto Savoia in The Right It (2019), who introduced the 'skin in the game caliper' scoring evidence by behavioral cost. The Sean Ellis PMF benchmark (40% threshold, 2009) adds a quantitative stated-preference proxy, explicitly intended as a leading indicator, not a behavioral confirmation. The evidence base is practitioner-strong and theory-grounded but lacks large-scale randomized controlled studies comparing teams that applied behavioral validation vs. those who did not. The meta-analytic evidence on hypothetical bias (Loomis 2011; Murphy et al. 2005; University of Groningen consumer goods study) comes from academic environmental economics and consumer research, not directly from startup outcomes, but is directionally consistent with practitioner accounts.

**Canonical sources:** Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) -- Chapter 5 'Commitment and Advancement' is the definitive startup-practice treatment; Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (2011) -- 'validated learning,' build-measure-learn loop, and the value hypothesis; Alberto Savoia, The Right It (2019) -- skin-in-the-game caliper, YODA (Your Own DAta), pretotyping techniques; Paul Samuelson, 'A note on the pure theory of consumers' behaviour,' Economica (1938) -- theoretical origin of revealed preference; Sean Ellis, Startup Pyramid blog post (2009) -- origin of the 40% 'very disappointed' PMF benchmark; Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product Market Fit,' First Round Review (2018) -- canonical case study of operationalizing the Ellis benchmark; Ash Maurya, Running Lean (2012) -- customer interviews anchored in past behavior and the Lean Canvas problem validation; Y Combinator / Paul Graham, 'Do Things That Don't Scale' (2013) -- behavioral traction over stated interest as the YC philosophy

**Failure modes**
- Treating the absence of rejection as confirmation. People are socially motivated to avoid conflict; a customer who does not say 'no' is not saying 'yes.' Founders routinely mistake polite interest for validated demand.
- Using future-tense questions. 'Would you use this?' and 'Would you pay for this?' are invitations to hypothetical optimism, not behavioral evidence. They measure stated preference by construction and inherit its full bias.
- Conflating the Ellis PMF survey score with behavioral retention. A score above 40% is a stated-preference leading indicator; it is not equivalent to flat or rising retention cohorts. A case reported by Sean Ellis himself showed 55% 'very disappointed' score co-existing with low retention -- a direct contradiction of the naive inference.
- Anchoring on the wrong population. The Ellis survey requires respondents who have used the product at least twice in the past two weeks and reached the core value. Surveying all sign-ups or one-time users systematically understates the score and misidentifies the target segment.
- Accepting commitment proxies that carry no real cost. An email address for a wait-list is minimal behavioral signal; it does not distinguish genuine demand from idle curiosity. Savoia's caliper scores opinion at 0 and an email at low -- teams that treat wait-list sign-ups as demand validation are operating on near-stated-preference data.
- Zombie leads. Fitzpatrick's term for prospects who remain warm indefinitely but never advance. Because they never say 'no,' they consume calendar and pipeline attention while providing false positive signals of market interest.
- Selection bias in survey samples. Survey respondents skew toward more engaged users; response rates below roughly 10% of the target population likely overstate the 'very disappointed' percentage, making the stated-preference score systematically optimistic relative to the full user base.

**False-positive mechanism:** The canonical false positive is social desirability bias compounded by absence of stakes. When a founder pitches their idea and asks 'would you use this?', the respondent faces a social incentive to agree (not to disappoint someone who is visibly excited) and zero cost for doing so -- no money changes hands, no time is committed, no reputation is pledged. Academic meta-analyses confirm this mechanism quantitatively: hypothetical willingness-to-pay overestimates actual willingness-to-pay by a median of 1.35x (Murphy et al. 2005) to an average of 3.2x (health sector systematic review) depending on domain and elicitation method. In startup practice, this surfaces as a full pipeline of 'interested' leads who never convert, a wait-list that does not activate, or a beta with high sign-up but near-zero day-7 retention. The mere measurement effect adds a second layer: the act of asking purchase intent questions makes respondents up to 25% more likely to subsequently state they intend to buy (CloudArmy / behavioral economics literature), inflating follow-up stated-preference signals collected in the same research session.

**Examples**
- Superhuman: In April 2017, Rahul Vohra ran Sean Ellis's 'very disappointed' PMF survey across early users and received a 22% score -- well below the 40% threshold. Rather than treating the score as binary failure, he segmented the 'very disappointed' cohort to identify who they were (founders, managers, executives, business development), narrowed the addressable segment to those personas, and used roadmap prioritization (half on doubling down what the 22% loved, half on removing blockers for fence-sitters) to drive the score to 33% after segmentation and 58% three quarters later. The case demonstrates that even stated-preference instruments are more informative when segmented by user archetype rather than read as aggregate averages.
- Dropbox (as reported by Sean Ellis): Dropbox was one of the roughly 100 early-stage companies across which Sean Ellis validated the 40% 'very disappointed' threshold before publishing his Startup Pyramid benchmark in 2009. Ellis ran growth operations at Dropbox early in its history, observing that companies with strong organic traction consistently exceeded the 40% threshold -- companies below it struggled to make paid acquisition work. This grounded the benchmark in behavioral outcomes (growth rates, referral loops) rather than solely in survey data.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong result is convergent: (a) customers describe vivid, specific, recent past episodes of pain and existing workarounds with budget already being spent; (b) a meaningful fraction of those interviewed accept a commitment that costs them something real (deposit, signed pilot, introduction to their CFO, calendar block for a trial) in the same session or within 48 hours without follow-up prompting; (c) early product or MVP retention shows a cohort that returns on its own initiative (day-7 or day-30 retention well above baseline for the category); and (d) any stated-preference survey (Ellis) scores above 40% among users who have reached core value. When all four converge, the signal is robust. If behavioral commitments are present but the Ellis score is low, prioritize the behavioral data. If the Ellis score is high but retention is flat, treat the stated preference as a leading indicator requiring behavioral confirmation before scaling spend.
- Weak signal: A weak result is one where positive signals are exclusively verbal: respondents say the problem is important, they express enthusiasm for the idea, they request to be notified when the product launches, but no commitment of money, time, or reputation materializes in the meeting or shortly after. Fitzpatrick's diagnostic: if every meeting ends with compliments and no defined next step, you have zombie leads, not demand validation. Similarly, a PMF survey score below 40%, or a score above 40% that co-exists with poor retention cohorts, indicates that the stated preference and behavioral preference are diverging -- the product has some verbal appeal but does not yet deliver durable value. A wait-list of email addresses without activation, or a beta with high sign-up but sub-10% day-7 retention, are canonical weak-result signatures.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Hypothetical willingness-to-pay overestimates actual WTP by an average of 21% in consumer goods settings (University of Groningen meta-analysis) -- Schmidt & Bijmolt (2020) from the University of Groningen confirm exactly 21% average hypothetical bias across 77 studies (115 effect sizes, 24,347 observations). One detail to flag: the paper was published in the Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science, not the Journal of Economic Psychology as implied by the ResearchGate DOI citation. The 21% figure and the University of Groningen attribution are accurate. (src: https://research.rug.nl/en/publications/accurately-measuring-willingness-to-pay-for-consumer-goods-a-meta/)
- [confirmed] Hypothetical WTP is on average 3.2 times greater than actual WTP in health sector studies, range 0.7x to 11.8x (ScienceDirect systematic review) -- The DOI 10.1016/j.socscimed.2019.02.019 resolves to a real 2019 Social Science & Medicine article ('The criterion validity of willingness to pay methods: A systematic review and meta-analysis of the evidence'). It reports hypothetical WTP 3.2 times greater than actual WTP with range 0.7-11.8x. Note: the same paper also reports a separate effect size metric (ratio of means: 1.79, 95% CI 1.56-2.04), so the 3.2x figure refers to percent-difference summaries. The claim is directionally accurate but the paper notes high heterogeneity and mixed evidence. (src: https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0277953619302151)
- [confirmed] Median ratio of hypothetical to actual WTP = 1.35 across 83 observations from 28 stated preference studies (Murphy et al. 2005, cited in Loomis 2011 Journal of Economic Surveys) -- Murphy et al. (2005), published in Environmental and Resource Economics (30: 313-325), confirms exactly 83 observations from 28 stated preference studies with a median ratio of 1.35. Loomis (2011) in the Journal of Economic Surveys cites this work. The source journal for Murphy's paper is Environmental and Resource Economics, not the Journal of Economic Surveys (which is Loomis's review journal). All numerical claims are accurate. (src: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10640-004-3332-z)
- [confirmed] Revealed preference model explained 49% of variance in actual supermarket sales vs 32% for the best stated preference model, a 1.5x predictive advantage (DecTech study, 2,700 UK consumers, 600 stores, 52 weeks) -- The DecTech article explicitly confirms all specific figures: 2,700 UK consumers surveyed online, 52 weeks of sales validated across 600 stores, three product categories (milk, eggs, canned tuna), revealed preference model at 49% variance explained vs stated preference best at 32%, characterized as 1.5x better predictions. All details check out against the primary source. (src: https://www.dectech.co.uk/news-insights/buzz/how-to-ask-why-stated-versus-revealed-preference-research/)
- [contested] Mere measurement effect: being asked about purchase intent made people up to 25% more likely to state intent to buy the product (behavioral economics literature, cited by CloudArmy) -- The 25% figure originates from Greenwald, Carnot, Beach & Young (1987) and refers to a 25% increase in VOTING behavior (not purchase intent) when people were asked about voting intentions 48 hours before an election. Purchase-intent studies by Morwitz et al. (1993) found a different figure: over 35% increase in car purchases. The claim conflates two distinct studies and misattributes the voting statistic to purchase behavior. The effect is real but the 25% number as applied to purchase intent is inaccurate. (src: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mere-measurement_effect)
- [confirmed] Sean Ellis PMF benchmark: companies where 40% or more of qualified recent users say 'very disappointed' consistently demonstrate scalable organic growth; validated across approximately 100 startups (Ellis, Startup Pyramid 2009) -- The 40% threshold is well-documented and traces to Ellis's 2009 'Startup Pyramid' blog post (now hosted on Substack, originally on startup-marketing.com, referenced on Venture Hacks as of November 2009). The 'approximately 100 startups' figure is widely cited in secondary sources as 'nearly a hundred startups.' The claim is accurate; the only caveat is that the ~100 figure is Ellis's own informal benchmark count from advisory work, not a formal peer-reviewed study. (src: https://venturehacks.com/sean-ellis)
- [contested] Superhuman PMF score: 22% baseline (April 2017) --> 33% after segmentation --> 58% after three quarters of roadmap changes (Vohra, First Round Review 2018) -- The scores (22%, 33%, 58%) and the three-quarter timeframe are confirmed by the First Round Review article and Superhuman's own blog. However, the article states the baseline was measured in 'summer 2017,' not 'April 2017.' The intermediate score after segmentation is 33% (not 32%), which matches. The year of publication is 2018. The core progression is accurate but the 'April 2017' date is not supported by the primary source. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [confirmed] First Round Review / Vohra: directionally correct PMF survey results emerge around 40 respondents; Superhuman surveyed 100-200 early users per survey cycle -- The First Round Review article and the Superhuman Coda document both explicitly state that Superhuman had 'between 100 and 200 users to poll' and that 'you start to get directionally correct results around 40 respondents.' Both figures match the primary source exactly. (src: https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [confirmed] Savoia's skin-in-the-game caliper: opinion = 0 validation points; email address on a wait-list = low; cash deposit or completed purchase = high (The Right It, 2019) -- The Right It by Alberto Savoia (HarperCollins, 2019) is a real book and the skin-in-the-game caliper framework is real. Multiple secondary sources confirm the ordinal ranking: opinions carry near-zero value, email sign-ups carry low value, cash deposits and completed purchases carry high value. The exact numerical scale ('0 validation points') is a characterization of the framework rather than a direct quote, but the framework's hierarchy matches what is described. No evidence refutes this. (src: https://nbry.wordpress.com/2021/05/16/make-sure-your-innovation-succeed-with-the-right-it-by-alberto-savoia/)
- [confirmed] Paul Samuelson originated revealed preference theory in 1938 (Economica); it predates its startup-practice application by roughly 75 years -- Samuelson's 'A Note on the Pure Theory of Consumer's Behaviour' was published in Economica, Vol. 5(17), pp. 61-71, February 1938 (DOI: 10.2307/2548836). This is confirmed by Semantic Scholar, Britannica, and multiple academic databases. The '~75 years' gap to startup application is an approximation: if startup-practice adoption is dated to circa 2009-2013, the gap is 71-75 years, making 'roughly 75 years' reasonable. (src: https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/A-Note-on-the-Pure-Theory-of-Consumer's-Behaviour-Samuelson/1de2464829944076ef547f7a4f8b9aaa8dc871a0)

- Rahul Vohra, How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product-Market Fit, First Round Review (2018): https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/

---
### 37. Pre-registering thresholds and kill criteria before a test

- **Category:** Cross-cutting methodology
- **Stage:** Cross-cutting: applies at problem, solution, demand, retention, and channel stages. Most critical at the problem and demand stages, where motivated reasoning is most dangerous because no sunk cost exists yet to counterbalance optimism bias.
- **Definition:** Pre-registering thresholds and kill criteria means committing in writing, before any data is collected, to the exact numeric success threshold, failure threshold, sample size, and time-box that will determine whether an experiment passes or kills the underlying assumption. It transforms a vague assumption into a falsifiable hypothesis by eliminating all post-hoc flexibility: if the result does not meet the pre-stated pass criterion, the assumption is treated as invalidated regardless of how promising the data may feel. The practice is adapted from scientific pre-registration, which was developed to combat HARKing (Hypothesizing After Results are Known), p-hacking, and motivated reasoning in clinical and behavioral research.
- **Validates:** Validates whether any single riskiest assumption (the Riskiest Assumption Test, or RAT) in a business model is true enough to justify the next resource commitment -- applicable at any stage where an assumption is being tested rather than a decision already made on intuition.

**Protocol**
1. Identify the single riskiest assumption (RAT) in the current business model -- the one that kills the business if wrong. Write it as an explicit assumption statement, not a goal.
2. Convert the assumption into a falsifiable XYZ hypothesis: 'At least X% of Y will do Z.' Specify the behavior (Z) as an observable action (sign-up, payment, repeat use), not an attitude (interest, excitement).
3. Pre-commit to a Minimum Success Criterion (MSC) before any data collection begins. Three methods for calibrating the threshold: (a) Early Adopter baseline -- set MSC above 70% when recruiting pre-screened high-need users; (b) Industry Benchmark -- use published conversion norms, e.g. 3-8% for B2B SaaS free-to-paid; (c) Back-of-envelope viability -- calculate the minimum rate at which the unit economics become viable.
4. Define the kill criterion explicitly: if results fall below MSC after the pre-stated sample size or time-box, the assumption is invalidated and the team must pivot or stop -- not rerun the experiment with tweaked framing.
5. Set the sample size and time-box before launching the experiment. Individual experiments should be time-boxed to 2 weeks or less at the experiment level; 90-day cycles govern clusters of experiments; a 3-year Minimum Success Criteria anchors the cascade (Ash Maurya's 3-level MSC framework).
6. Run the experiment without altering the hypothesis, success threshold, or sample-size target mid-run. Any deviation from the pre-registered plan must be logged as a protocol deviation and treated as exploratory, not confirmatory.
7. At the time-box close, compare results against the pre-registered MSC and issue a written decision: Persevere (result above MSC), Pivot (result below MSC but learning points to adjacent hypothesis worth testing), or Kill (result below MSC with no credible adjacent hypothesis).
8. Document the decision memo: verdict, the evidence used, what specific data would reverse the verdict, and the next smallest reversible step.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- Minimum success threshold for early-adopter-screened interview samples: Greater than 70% (i.e., 7 of 10 interviewees must support the assumption) (src: Tendayi Viki, 'Lean Experiments: Three Methods For Setting Minimum Success Criteria,' The Lean Product Lifecycle on Medium)
- B2B SaaS free-to-paid conversion industry baseline for threshold calibration: 3%-8% (src: Tendayi Viki, 'Lean Experiments,' The Lean Product Lifecycle on Medium, citing industry benchmarks)
- PMF survey threshold (Sean Ellis Test): minimum share of active users who say they would be 'very disappointed' without the product, required to confirm product-market fit before scaling: 40% or more (src: Sean Ellis, benchmarked across approximately 100 startups; documented at fitsignal.com and koji.so)
- PMF survey: score below which a major pivot is indicated: Below 25% 'very disappointed' (src: Sean Ellis test scoring tiers, documented at fitsignal.com and koji.so)
- Minimum sample size for Sean Ellis PMF survey to be statistically reliable: 40 responses minimum; 100+ for confident decisions (src: Sean Ellis methodology documentation at pmtoolkit.ai and koji.so)
- Share of large NHLBI clinical trials pre-2000 (pre-registration era) that reported positive primary outcomes: 57% (17 of 30 studies) (src: Kaplan & Irvin (2015), 'Likelihood of Null Effects of Large NHLBI Clinical Trials Has Increased over Time,' PLOS ONE 10, e0132382)
- Share of large NHLBI clinical trials post-2000 (post pre-registration requirement) that reported positive primary outcomes: 8% (2 of 25 studies) (src: Kaplan & Irvin (2015), PLOS ONE 10, e0132382 -- the drop from 57% to 8% is the core empirical evidence that pre-registration suppresses false positives)
- Superhuman PMF score before focused hypothesis-driven iteration: 22% 'very disappointed' (src: Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit,' First Round Review)
- Superhuman PMF score after 18-month hypothesis-driven rebuild targeting core segment: 58% 'very disappointed' (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review)

**Origin and evidence:** The practice originates from two parallel traditions that converged in startup methodology. (1) Scientific pre-registration: formalized in clinical research after FDA mandated ClinicalTrials.gov registration in 2000; popularized across behavioral sciences by the Center for Open Science and Brian Nosek's 'preregistration revolution' (PNAS, 2018). The Kaplan and Irvin (2015) PLOS ONE study provided the key empirical demonstration that pre-registration dramatically reduces false-positive rates in large trials. (2) Lean Startup / hypothesis-driven product development: Eric Ries popularized the Build-Measure-Learn loop in 'The Lean Startup' (2011) and introduced the 'pivot or persevere' decision framework. Ash Maurya operationalized pre-committed thresholds through the Minimum Success Criteria (MSC) cascade in 'Running Lean' (2012) and 'Scaling Lean' (2016). Alberto Savoia's XYZ Hypothesis framework ('The Right It,' 2019) provided the syntax ('At least X% of Y will do Z') for quantifying hypotheses. Tendayi Viki added the three calibration methods for setting MSC thresholds. Evidence strength: strong in clinical science (RCT-level evidence that pre-registration reduces false positives); practitioner-level in startup contexts (no controlled study comparing startups that pre-registered vs. did not, but the causal mechanism -- motivated reasoning -- is well-documented in cognitive psychology).

**Canonical sources:** Eric Ries, 'The Lean Startup' (Crown Business, 2011) -- pivot or persevere framework, build-measure-learn loop, actionable vs. vanity metrics; Ash Maurya, 'Running Lean' (O'Reilly, 2nd ed. 2012) and 'Scaling Lean' (Portfolio/Penguin, 2016) -- Minimum Success Criteria cascade, 3-level MSC framework; Alberto Savoia, 'The Right It' (HarperOne, 2019) -- XYZ Hypothesis syntax, pretotyping, YODA (Your Own DAta); Tendayi Viki, 'Lean Experiments: Three Methods For Setting Minimum Success Criteria' (Medium / The Lean Product Lifecycle) -- three calibration methods for MSCs; Sean Ellis, benchmark of approximately 100 startups establishing the 40% PMF threshold; also Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit,' First Round Review; Kaplan and Irvin (2015), 'Likelihood of Null Effects of Large NHLBI Clinical Trials Has Increased over Time,' PLOS ONE 10(8):e0132382 -- empirical evidence for pre-registration effect; Nosek et al. (2018), 'The Preregistration Revolution,' PNAS 115(11):2600-2606 -- theoretical and empirical case for pre-registration

**Failure modes**
- Threshold set after data collection has begun (or after seeing early results), rendering the pre-registration meaningless -- the most common real-world failure mode.
- Threshold set implausibly low to make pass nearly certain (threshold gerrymandering), providing false confidence while avoiding a real kill decision.
- Multiple outcomes tracked but only the one that passed reported as the primary metric (outcome switching), equivalent to HARKing in academic research.
- Sample size not pre-committed: experiment extended until the desired result appears (optional stopping), which inflates false-positive rate substantially in frequentist analysis.
- Kill criterion defined but not enforced: team continues despite failing the pre-registered test by reframing the experiment as 'exploratory' or 'learning-stage' after the fact.
- Qualitative escape hatch: 'the numbers are bad but the conversations felt positive' used to override a quantitative kill -- mixing evidence types post-hoc.
- Single-threshold thinking: pre-registering a pass threshold but failing to pre-register a kill threshold leaves an ambiguous zone where motivated reasoning continues unchecked.

**False-positive mechanism:** The central false-positive mechanism is motivated reasoning combined with post-hoc threshold adjustment. A team that has emotional or financial investment in an hypothesis will unconsciously (or consciously) lower the success threshold after seeing results that fall below the original criterion, reframe which metric is 'really' primary, extend the time-box when results are trending upward, or exclude data points as outliers. Because startup experiments typically lack statistical power (small n, short duration), any of these degrees of freedom can push a failing experiment into apparent success. The clinical-trial analog is stark: Kaplan and Irvin found that 57% of large NHLBI trials pre-pre-registration reported positive results, vs. 8% post-pre-registration -- a difference best explained by the elimination of researcher discretion over which outcomes to report. In startup contexts, the failure mode compounds because there is no external registry or peer review: the only check on goalpost-moving is the founding team's own discipline.

**Examples**
- Superhuman: Rahul Vohra pre-committed to the Sean Ellis 40% 'very disappointed' benchmark as the explicit go/no-go threshold for scaling growth investment. Starting at 22%, the team ran 18 months of focused hypothesis-driven iteration targeting a specific user segment (mobile professionals), re-running the PMF survey each cycle as the confirmatory metric. Scaling did not begin until the score crossed 58%, well above the pre-stated 40% threshold. The threshold was public and non-negotiable, preventing premature scaling.
- Ash Maurya's own LeanStack validation cycles (documented in Scaling Lean): Maurya operationalized the 3-level MSC cascade in his own product work: a 3-year founder-level minimum success criterion was decomposed into 90-day cycle targets, which were further decomposed into individual experiment-level hypotheses with 2-week time-boxes and pre-committed pass/fail thresholds. Each 90-day cycle ended with an evidence-based Persevere/Pivot/Pause decision against the pre-stated MSC, not against the team's intuitions. This process is documented as the core method in Running Lean (2nd ed.) and Scaling Lean.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: Result meets or exceeds the pre-registered MSC within the pre-stated time-box and sample size, without any protocol deviation. A strong result is credible precisely because the threshold was non-negotiable before data collection: no degrees of freedom were available for motivated adjustment. The decision to persevere or proceed to the next stage is justified; the team should document the evidence and the specific assumption that was validated (not over-generalize to the entire business model).
- Weak signal: Result falls below the pre-registered MSC, or meets the MSC only after extending the time-box, reducing the threshold, or switching the primary metric. A weak result where the team continues anyway is the canonical false-positive: the experiment has failed by its own pre-stated standard, and continuation is driven by motivated reasoning rather than evidence. The correct response is a documented pivot decision -- change one specific Lean Canvas block -- or a kill, not a rerun of the same experiment with softer criteria.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Tendayi Viki: early-adopter interview MSC should exceed 70% (7 of 10) to confirm an assumption with pre-screened high-need users -- Viki's article explicitly states: 'If you are running an experiment on a group you have defined as early adopters, your minimum success/fail criteria should be higher than 70% (i.e. 7 out of 10 support your assumptions).' The text and the threshold are verbatim in the primary source. (src: Tendayi Viki, 'Lean Experiments: Three Methods For Setting Minimum Success Criteria,' Medium / tendayiviki.com (January 2018) — https://medium.com/the-lean-product-lifecycle/lean-experiments-b5280c9d1820)
- [confirmed] Tendayi Viki: B2B SaaS free-to-paid conversion industry benchmark is 3%-8%, usable as an MSC baseline for public-channel demand tests -- The same Viki article states 'typical conversion rates for a B2B software as a service company range from 3%–8%' and frames this as the anchor for MSC when early-adopter screening is not feasible. Independent SaaS benchmark sources (CrazyEgg, Pulseahead, GrowthSpree 2026) place the freemium-to-paid median at 2–8%, broadly consistent with the cited range. (src: Tendayi Viki, 'Lean Experiments,' Medium — https://medium.com/the-lean-product-lifecycle/lean-experiments-b5280c9d1820; corroborated by https://www.pulseahead.com/blog/trial-to-paid-conversion-benchmarks-in-saas)
- [confirmed] Sean Ellis: 40% or more 'very disappointed' on PMF survey is the threshold for confirmed product-market fit, derived from benchmarking approximately 100 startups -- Multiple independent sources (fitsignal.com, koji.so, pmtoolkit.ai, First Round Review) consistently attribute the 40% threshold to Sean Ellis after benchmarking 'nearly 100 startups.' The derivation story (growth at Dropbox, LogMeIn, Eventbrite) is consistent across all sources. (src: Sean Ellis methodology; documented at https://www.fitsignal.com/blog/sean-ellis-40-percent-test and https://www.koji.so/docs/sean-ellis-test-product-market-fit)
- [confirmed] Sean Ellis: below 25% 'very disappointed' signals a major pivot is required -- The three-tier scoring (below 25% = major pivot, 25–40% = iterate, 40%+ = scale) is consistently documented across secondary sources that cite Ellis. However, this precise tri-tier breakdown does not appear to be sourced in Ellis's own earliest writing — it is a widely cited interpretation of his framework. No source actively contradicts it, but the 25% lower bound is a derivative convention, not a statement Ellis is documented as having made in a primary publication. (src: https://www.koji.so/docs/sean-ellis-test-product-market-fit; https://www.kissmetrics.io/glossary/product-market-fit-score)
- [contested] Sean Ellis: minimum 40 survey responses required for statistical validity; 100+ recommended for confident decisions -- The '40 minimum / 100+ recommended' split is widely repeated across secondary documentation sites (koji.so, pmtoolkit.ai, mapster.io). However, one source attributes Ellis himself to recommending ~30 representative users based on trial and error, and no peer-reviewed statistical basis for '40' specifically has been identified. The 40 figure may be a folk-convention that echoes the 40% threshold number rather than a formal sample-size calculation. The 100+ recommendation is broadly sensible but also unverified as an Ellis primary statement. (src: https://www.koji.so/docs/sean-ellis-test-product-market-fit; countervailing note at https://fullstackresearcher.substack.com/p/productmarket-fit-surveys-for-data)
- [confirmed] Kaplan and Irvin (2015): 57% of large NHLBI clinical trials published before 2000 (pre-registration era) reported positive primary outcomes -- The primary PLOS ONE paper (DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0132382) reports exactly '17 of 30 studies (57%) published prior to 2000 showed a significant benefit of intervention on the primary outcome.' The exact numbers, percentages, chi-square (12.2, df=1) and p-value (0.0005) are verified directly from the paper. (src: Kaplan RM, Irvin VL (2015), PLOS ONE 10(8): e0132382 — https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0132382)
- [confirmed] Kaplan and Irvin (2015): only 8% of large NHLBI clinical trials published after 2000 (post pre-registration requirement) reported positive primary outcomes -- The paper reports exactly '2 among the 25 (8%) trials published after 2000.' Both the numerator/denominator and the percentage are verified in the primary PLOS ONE source. The causal attribution to pre-registration is the paper's interpretation, not a mathematical fact, and the authors themselves note confounders (improving baseline cardiovascular care). (src: Kaplan RM, Irvin VL (2015), PLOS ONE 10(8): e0132382 — https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0132382)
- [confirmed] Slack PMF score: approximately 51% 'very disappointed' (reported in PMF methodology documentation) -- Hiten Shah's August 2015 survey of 731 Slack users found exactly 370 respondents (51%) said they would be 'very disappointed' without Slack. The original write-up is publicly available at hitenism.com and is consistently cited in PMF literature. The 51% figure matches the primary source. (src: Hiten Shah, '731 Slack Users Reveal Why It's So Addictive,' August 2015 — https://hitenism.com/slack-product-market-fit-survey/)
- [contested] Superhuman PMF score: rose from 22% to 58% 'very disappointed' after 18 months of hypothesis-driven iteration targeting a specific user segment -- The starting score of 22% and the endpoint of 58% are confirmed in Rahul Vohra's First Round Review article. However, the canonical primary source says 'within just three quarters of our work to improve the product, the score nearly doubled to 58%' — roughly 9 months, not 18 months. The '18 months' figure circulates widely in secondary summaries and recaps but does not appear in the primary article's direct claim about the 22%-to-58% arc. The numbers are right; the timeframe is overstated. (src: Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit,' First Round Review — https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/)
- [confirmed] Ash Maurya: individual experiments should be time-boxed to 2 weeks or less; 90-day cycles govern clusters of experiments; 3-year horizon anchors the MSC cascade -- Maurya's Lean Foundry newsletter (November 2025) and Running Lean 3rd Edition both explicitly describe a three-tier MSC structure: Founder level (3-year goal), 90-day cycle, and Experiment (2 weeks or less). The 2-week sprint as the atomic experiment unit and the 90-day cycle as the cluster cadence are documented in Running Lean ch. 7 and the Scaling Lean book. All three levels are confirmed from primary Maurya sources. (src: Ash Maurya, Running Lean 3rd Edition (O'Reilly, 2022), ch. 7; Lean Foundry newsletter Nov 2025 — https://www.leanfoundry.com/lean-1-2-3/nov-20-2025; https://www.oreilly.com/library/view/running-lean-3rd/9781098108762/ch07.html)

- Nature News: 'Registered Clinical Trials Make Positive Findings Vanish': https://www.nature.com/news/registered-clinical-trials-make-positive-findings-vanish-1.18181

- PNAS (2018), 'The Preregistration Revolution' (Nosek et al.): https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.1708274114
- Sean Ellis Test: The 40% Rule for Product-Market Fit (Complete 2026 Guide), koji.so: https://www.koji.so/docs/sean-ellis-test-product-market-fit
- FitSignal: The Sean Ellis 40% Test -- Ultimate Guide: https://www.fitsignal.com/blog/sean-ellis-40-percent-test
- LeanPivot.ai: Pivot or Persevere -- Lean Startup Guide: https://leanpivot.ai/lean-startup-guide/pivot-persevere/

### 38. Riskiest Assumption Test (RAT) and Proper Sequencing

- **Category:** Cross-cutting methodology
- **Stage:** problem
- **Definition:** The Riskiest Assumption Test (RAT) is a pre-build validation discipline that forces founders to explicitly surface all assumptions underlying their business model, identify the single assumption whose falsity would kill the venture (the "riskiest"), and design the smallest possible experiment -- not a product -- to test it before committing development resources. Pioneered in practice by Eric Ries's "leap of faith" sequencing in The Lean Startup (2011) and named as RAT by Rik Higham in a 2016 HackerNoon essay, the method inverts the MVP's "build-measure-learn" cycle into a "learn-test" cycle: remove existential uncertainty first, then build. Proper sequencing demands the existential (desirability) assumption be invalidated or confirmed before secondary (viability, then feasibility) assumptions are tested.
- **Validates:** Does the single most dangerous unproven belief underlying this business model hold -- and are we testing assumptions in the order that would kill the business fastest if wrong?

**Protocol**
1. ENUMERATE: Write down every assumption embedded in your business model (a Lean Canvas typically contains 15-30 hidden assumptions). Capture all categories: value proposition, customer segment, problem existence, willingness to pay, acquisition cost, and execution feasibility.
2. TRIAGE: Plot each assumption on a 2x2 matrix: Y-axis = Impact if wrong (minor pivot vs. business death), X-axis = Uncertainty (proven vs. pure guess). Assumptions in the top-right quadrant (high impact, high uncertainty) are your RATs. Narrow to the 3-5 that could individually kill the venture.
3. SEQUENCE: Order RATs by the desirability-viability-feasibility hierarchy (Ash Maurya). Test desirability first ('do customers have this problem and want this solution?'), then viability ('will they pay, and can you acquire them profitably?'), then feasibility ('can you build it?'). Never test feasibility before desirability is confirmed.
4. DESIGN THE MINIMUM TEST: For each RAT, ask: what is the cheapest, fastest experiment that would falsify this assumption? Methods in ascending cost: customer interviews focused on past behavior (Mom Test protocol), landing page with email capture or waitlist, pre-order / concierge, smoke test (buy button with no product behind it), Wizard of Oz prototype. Do NOT build a functional product to test a desirability assumption.
5. STATE THE FALSIFICATION CRITERION: Before running the test, write down a specific, measurable threshold that would count as a failure. Example: 'If fewer than 15% of landing-page visitors click sign-up, the demand assumption fails.' Thresholds set after results are rationalization, not validation.
6. RUN THE TEST: Execute the experiment on a small, representative sample of the target customer segment. For interview-based tests: 5-10 deep interviews are sufficient to surface major pattern breaks (Rob Fitzpatrick). For quantitative tests: aim for a minimum of 30 qualified respondents before drawing conclusions.
7. EVALUATE AND DECIDE: Compare results against the pre-set falsification criterion. Three outcomes: (a) assumption confirmed -- move to the next RAT; (b) assumption refuted -- pivot the relevant element of the business model and re-test; (c) ambiguous -- tighten the test or expand sample before proceeding.
8. REPEAT UNTIL EXISTENTIAL RISK IS ELIMINATED: Only after the top-tier desirability RAT is confirmed should you begin building. Continue RAT cycles on secondary assumptions in parallel with early development, not after launch.

**Benchmarks and thresholds**
- PMF signal threshold (Sean Ellis survey): share of qualified users answering 'very disappointed' if product disappeared: >=40% = strong PMF signal; <40% = insufficient (src: Sean Ellis, benchmarked across ~100 startups (circa 2010); cited in The Real Startup Book / Kromatic and First Round Review Superhuman case study)
- Superhuman PMF score at launch (Rahul Vohra, summer 2017): 22% 'very disappointed' -- well below 40% threshold (src: First Round Review: 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit' (Rahul Vohra))
- Superhuman PMF score after segmentation and roadmap optimization: 58% 'very disappointed' (src: First Round Review: 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit' (Rahul Vohra))
- Slack PMF score (Hiten Shah open survey, 2015, n=731): 51% 'very disappointed' (src: Cited in First Round Review Superhuman essay and Kromatic Real Startup Book)
- Minimum qualified respondents before Ellis-survey results are meaningful: 30 qualified respondents (users who experienced core functionality, used product at least twice, within last two weeks) (src: Sean Ellis original protocol, documented at Kromatic / Real Startup Book)
- Typical number of hidden assumptions in a single Lean Canvas: 15-30 (src: LeanPivot.ai, citing Ash Maurya's Running Lean framework)
- Number of interviews sufficient to identify major customer-discovery pattern breaks: 5-10 deep interviews (src: Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test)
- Superhuman roadmap allocation between offense (double down on loved features) and defense (close friction gaps): 50% / 50% (src: Rahul Vohra, First Round Review Superhuman PMF case study)

**Origin and evidence:** The RAT was named and argued in print by Rik Higham in "The MVP is Dead. Long Live the RAT." (HackerNoon, September 27, 2016). The underlying intellectual lineage is: Steve Blank's customer development (Four Steps to the Epiphany, 2005), Eric Ries's "leap of faith assumption" and value/growth hypothesis sequencing (The Lean Startup, 2011), and Ash Maurya's Lean Canvas risk-triage using the desirability-viability-feasibility hierarchy (Running Lean, 2012). Rob Fitzpatrick's Mom Test (2013) operationalizes the interview protocol that produces honest signals. The PMF benchmark (40% threshold) derives from Sean Ellis's empirical survey of ~100 startups (published 2010). Evidence quality: the 40% benchmark is empirically derived but from a non-peer-reviewed, self-reported dataset; the Superhuman case study is a single detailed practitioner case. No peer-reviewed RCT exists for the RAT methodology. Evidence is strong at the practitioner level, moderate at the empirical level.

**Canonical sources:** Eric Ries, The Lean Startup (Crown Business, 2011) -- 'leap of faith' assumptions, value hypothesis, growth hypothesis, Build-Measure-Learn; Ash Maurya, Running Lean (O'Reilly, 2nd ed. 2012) -- Lean Canvas, desirability/viability/feasibility sequencing, 90-day experiment cycles; Rob Fitzpatrick, The Mom Test (2013) -- interview protocol to surface honest signals on riskiest demand assumptions; Rik Higham, 'The MVP is Dead. Long Live the RAT.' (HackerNoon, September 27, 2016) -- naming and formal articulation of RAT; Sean Ellis, 'The Startup Pyramid' blog post (2010) -- origin of the 40% 'very disappointed' PMF benchmark; Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit' (First Round Review, 2018) -- most cited practitioner application of Ellis benchmark with segmentation twist

**Failure modes**
- Testing the wrong assumption: founders test the assumption they can most easily confirm (e.g., 'can we build this?') rather than the assumption whose failure would kill the business (e.g., 'do customers have this problem at all?'). This is the single most common sequencing error.
- Designing a test that cannot falsify: a test with no pre-stated falsification criterion produces confirmation regardless of outcome. Post-hoc rationalization of ambiguous results is structurally identical to not testing.
- Social desirability bias in interviews: asking future-tense hypotheticals ('would you use this?') instead of past-behavior questions produces polite lies. Fitzpatrick documents this as 'the world's most deadly inaccuracy: I would definitely buy that.'
- Treating landing-page sign-ups as demand confirmation: an email capture proves curiosity, not willingness to pay, overcome switching costs, or repeat use. Smoke tests without a payment step systematically overstate demand.
- Premature feasibility testing: building a proof-of-concept to test engineering feasibility before confirming customer desirability inverts the risk-sequencing logic and consumes runway on the wrong question.
- Sample contamination in PMF surveys: surveying users who experienced the product only once, or not within the last two weeks, inflates 'very disappointed' scores with users who never formed genuine product dependence (Sean Ellis's qualification criteria exist precisely to prevent this).
- Zombie lead accumulation: prospects who say 'yes' in discovery but never advance to pre-order or payment are structurally misleading. Fitzpatrick calls these 'zombie leads' -- dangerous because they consume attention without providing signal.
- Testing one assumption per cycle but ignoring sequencing: running RATs without the desirability-viability-feasibility ordering means a later-sequence assumption may pass even though the prior-sequence assumption would have killed the business.

**False-positive mechanism:** The canonical false positive mechanism is social desirability bias compounded by non-behavioral measurement: customers are wired to be encouraging, so any test that asks for stated intent rather than observed behavior (past purchases, sign-ups with payment, calendar commitments) will return artificially positive signals. A landing-page test without a payment step measures curiosity, not demand. An interview that pitches the solution before asking about the problem primes the respondent to imagine a world where the product exists, generating enthusiasm for a hypothetical rather than evidence of an existing pain. The result is a set of 'confirmed' assumptions whose confirmation was produced by the test design itself. Fitzpatrick documents this as structurally worse than no testing: 'When you know you are clueless, you tend to be careful; collecting a fistful of false positives is like convincing a drunk he is sober: not an improvement.'

**Examples**
- Dropbox: Drew Houston did not build the product to test the riskiest assumption (that users wanted seamless cross-device file sync). Instead, he produced a three-minute explainer video demonstrating the drag-and-drop sync behavior. The waitlist grew from 5,000 to 75,000 overnight, confirming demand before a line of production code was written. This is a textbook RAT: the test artifact was not a product, it was the minimum artifact needed to falsify the demand assumption.
- Superhuman (Rahul Vohra, 2017-2018): After two years of development, Superhuman scored 22% on the Sean Ellis 'very disappointed' survey -- far below the 40% PMF threshold. Rather than launching or pivoting the entire product, Vohra applied segmentation: he disregarded feedback from users in the 'not disappointed' cohort and built solely toward the 'very disappointed' users' stated desires (speed, keyboard shortcuts, automation). Three quarters later the score reached 58%. The lesson for RAT sequencing: PMF measurement must be segment-specific, not population-averaged, and roadmap allocation (50% offense / 50% defense) must follow the signal, not intuition.

**Interpretation**
- Strong signal: A strong RAT result has three properties: (1) the falsification criterion was stated before the test ran; (2) the signal came from observed behavior, not stated intent (payment, calendar commitment, repeat use, waitlist with confirmed email); and (3) the result holds across a representative segment of the target customer, not early adopters alone. On the PMF survey: a score at or above 40% 'very disappointed' on a qualified sample of 30+ users who used the core product at least twice in the last two weeks is the strongest available leading indicator of organic growth. Slack's 51% at 500k paying users is the canonical strong-result benchmark.
- Weak signal: A weak RAT result is any of: stated-intent data without behavioral confirmation ('I would definitely use this'), a landing-page email sign-up without a payment or commitment step, a PMF survey score below 40% or on a sample below 30 qualified respondents, interview signals from a non-representative sample (friends, family, or superfans who were recruited by the founder), or a positive result on a secondary assumption (feasibility or viability) when the primary assumption (desirability) has not yet been tested. Superhuman's initial 22% score is the canonical weak-result benchmark -- real, quantified, and below threshold -- which nonetheless contained actionable signal once segmented.

**Adversarial verification of claims**
- [confirmed] Sean Ellis benchmarked ~100 startups and found that companies exceeding 40% 'very disappointed' on his PMF survey demonstrated strong traction while struggling companies fell below this threshold -- Multiple independent sources corroborate this. Ellis developed the methodology after working with nearly 100 startups and consistently found the 40% threshold separated companies that scaled from those that stalled. The benchmark is documented in the First Round Review Superhuman essay, Kromatic's Real Startup Book, and Ellis's own writing circa 2010. No authoritative source disputes the ~100 startups figure or the 40% cut-off. (src: First Round Review: 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit' (review.firstround.com); Kromatic Real Startup Book (kromatic.com/real-startup-book))
- [contested] Superhuman's initial PMF score (summer 2017) was 22% 'very disappointed' -- The 22% figure is confirmed as the very first survey result, but the 'summer 2017' date is contested. The First Round Review article and the SaaS Club podcast both describe the journey as starting at 22%, then reaching 33% after segmentation, with 33% being the score documented in summer 2017. The Product School summary explicitly states 'Superhuman's PMF score was 33% in summer 2017.' Rahul Vohra's own blog post says 'within just three quarters of our work... the score nearly doubled to 58%' from the post-segmentation baseline. The claim conflates the initial raw score (22%) with a summer 2017 timestamp when the score was already 33%. (src: First Round Review (review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/); SaaS Club Podcast ep. 342 (saasclub.io); Product School Substack (productschool.substack.com))
- [confirmed] Superhuman's PMF score after segmentation and optimization reached 58% 'very disappointed' -- The 58% final score is directly stated in the First Round Review article by Rahul Vohra: 'Within just three quarters of our work to improve the product, the score nearly doubled to 58%.' This is corroborated by the SaaS Club podcast, Vohra's own blog post at blog.superhuman.com, and numerous secondary sources. No credible source disputes this figure. (src: First Round Review (review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/); Rahul Vohra, blog.superhuman.com)
- [confirmed] Slack scored 51% 'very disappointed' in a 2015 Hiten Shah open survey of 731 users -- Hiten Shah's original article at hitenism.com ('731 Slack Users Reveal Why It's So Addictive', dated April 2015) confirms 731 respondents and 51% answering 'very disappointed.' The survey was distributed via Twitter and volunteers sharing with teams. One nuance: the survey was not strictly 'open' in the sense of being publicly posted; it was distributed through Shah's network and volunteer coordinators. The core numbers (731, 51%) are unambiguous and directly traceable to the primary source. (src: Hiten Shah, '731 Slack Users Reveal Why It's So Addictive' (hitenism.com/slack-product-market-fit-survey, 2015))
- [contested] A Lean Canvas typically contains 15-30 hidden assumptions, of which 3-5 are critical RATs -- The LeanPivot.ai source (which cites Ash Maurya's Running Lean framework) does state exactly '15-30 hidden assumptions' and 'top 3-5 RATs.' However, this specific numeric range does not appear in Ash Maurya's own published books or Medium articles found during this search. Maurya's own writing focuses on identifying the riskiest assumptions without specifying a fixed count. The 15-30 figure appears to be an editorial framing by LeanPivot.ai rather than a direct Maurya claim, making it a third-party interpretation, not a primary-source benchmark. The 3-5 RATs figure is similarly absent from Maurya's primary writings found here. (src: LeanPivot.ai 'Chapter 6: Build Your Lean Canvas' (leanpivot.ai/playbook-01-idea-clarity/lean-canvas-and-risks/); Ash Maurya, Running Lean (primary source does not appear to specify these exact ranges))
- [contested] Rob Fitzpatrick: 5-10 deep customer interviews are sufficient to identify major pattern breaks in customer discovery -- No direct quote attributing '5-10 interviews is sufficient' to Rob Fitzpatrick was found in any source. 'The Mom Test' focuses on interview quality and pattern saturation rather than prescribing a fixed count. Multiple secondary summaries explicitly state that Fitzpatrick's actual guidance recommends dozens to 50-100 conversations before making major decisions, and that most founders who stop at 5-10 do so prematurely. The claim as stated -- that 5-10 is 'sufficient' -- directly contradicts multiple readings of the book. The closest true claim is that 5-10 can reveal early signals, not that they are sufficient. (src: The Mom Test, Rob Fitzpatrick (momtestbook.com); secondary analysis at mtlynch.io/book-reports/the-mom-test/ and looppanel.com/blog/customer-interviews)
- [confirmed] Ellis's PMF survey requires respondents to have used the product at least twice within the last two weeks to qualify -- The 'at least twice within the last two weeks' qualification criterion is consistently documented across the Kromatic Real Startup Book, multiple practitioner guides, and survey tool documentation. Kromatic explicitly states the three conditions: experienced core product, used at least twice, used within the last two weeks. This is treated as the canonical Ellis protocol across all major secondary sources and is not disputed by any source found. (src: Kromatic Real Startup Book (kromatic.com/real-startup-book/6-evaluative-product-experiment/survey-product-market-fit); SurveySparrow, Userpilot, Learningloop secondaries)
- [contested] A minimum of ~30 qualified respondents is needed before PMF survey percentages are statistically meaningful -- The Kromatic Real Startup Book does cite 30 as a minimum floor ('aim for at least 30 qualified responses before treating the score as anything more than directional'). However, most practitioner sources, including Hiten Shah and multiple survey tool guides, recommend 40-100 as the practical minimum for actionable results. No original Sean Ellis primary-source document setting '30' as the threshold was found; it appears to be a Kromatic editorial choice. The range across credible sources is 30-100, making the specific '~30' figure a contested simplification rather than a confirmed primary-source benchmark. (src: Kromatic Real Startup Book (kromatic.com); Formbricks (formbricks.com/blog/product-market-fit-survey-questions) recommends 40-100; FitSignal (fitsignal.com) recommends 30+)
- [contested] Dropbox's waitlist grew from 5,000 to 75,000 after its explainer video (demand RAT), before building production code -- The 5,000 to 75,000 overnight surge is confirmed across multiple sources and traces to the March 2008 Digg video, not the original 2007 Hacker News video. However, the 'before building production code' framing is inaccurate: Dropbox had working prototype code and a functional Mac client at the time of the 2008 video (the video demonstrated a real, working product). The video preceded *public launch* and *scaling*, not initial code. The 2007 HackerNews video predated a full product, but that video produced the initial 5,000 list -- the jump to 75,000 came from the 2008 video when code already existed. The demand-RAT narrative is directionally correct but the 'before production code' claim does not survive scrutiny. (src: Alexander Jarvis 'Dropbox doing things that don't scale' (alexanderjarvis.com); Shortform 'The ORIGINAL Dropbox MVP Explainer Video' (shortform.com/blog/dropbox-mvp-explainer-video/); launchtomorrow.com analysis)
- [confirmed] Superhuman allocated 50% of roadmap effort to doubling down on features loved by 'very disappointed' users and 50% to closing gaps identified by the 'somewhat disappointed' cohort -- The 50/50 split is explicitly stated in the First Round Review article and confirmed by the Product School Substack summary quoting Vohra: '50% of capacity goes toward doubling down on what users already love, and 50% goes toward systematically removing the friction that holds the convertible segment back.' The First Round Review article (fetched directly) confirms the framework allocated half the roadmap to loved features and half to gap-closing for the convertible cohort. Multiple independent secondaries corroborate this exact framing. (src: First Round Review: 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit' (review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/); Product School Substack (productschool.substack.com))

**Citations**
- Rik Higham, 'The MVP is Dead. Long Live the RAT.' (HackerNoon, 2016): https://hackernoon.com/the-mvp-is-dead-long-live-the-rat-233d5d16ab02
- Rahul Vohra, 'How Superhuman Built an Engine to Find Product/Market Fit' (First Round Review, 2018): https://review.firstround.com/how-superhuman-built-an-engine-to-find-product-market-fit/
- Sean Ellis PMF Survey Protocol -- The Real Startup Book / Kromatic: https://kromatic.com/real-startup-book/6-evaluative-product-experiment/survey-product-market-fit
- ModelThinkers -- Riskiest Assumption Test overview (cites Rik Higham, Ash Maurya): https://modelthinkers.com/mental-model/riskiest-assumption-test
- LeanPivot.ai -- Lean Canvas and Risks (Ash Maurya RAT framework, 2x2 risk matrix, 15-30 assumptions claim): https://leanpivot.ai/playbook-01-idea-clarity/lean-canvas-and-risks/
- Verticode -- Leap of Faith Assumptions sequencing (Rent the Runway progressive constraint removal): https://www.verticode.co.uk/blog/leap-of-faith-assumptions
- KeenEthics -- Value Riskiest Assumption Test (RAT) explainer including Dropbox example: https://keenethics.com/blog/the-value-riskiest-assumption-test
- Hacker News thread on original RAT article (community reception and critique, 2017): https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=14067056
